more frequent and extreme 
(Williams et al. 2020)
. Non-native species are the second leading critical problem for terrestrial and aquatic species and habitats in Utah. The combined effects of wildfire and, paradoxically, fire suppression are also a critical problem. Fire is now occurring where it virtually never did before, and it has been long excluded from where it naturally occurred on a regular basis. In addition to the issue of timing, the frequency and intensity of fire also has greatly diverged from long-term norms. Lastly, climate change and data gaps have been identified as major threats. These problems need to be understood better before effective conservation action can take place.
The Utah Division of Wildlife Resources has been working with other management agencies and conservation organizations to revise the UWAP. Species of greatest conservation need have been identified, as have the habitats they require for survival. Threats and limiting factors, as well as data gaps, also have been identified. This document provides strong, clear guidance for developing actions that could be effective for managing all of these problems. If this plan were effectively implemented, the result would be healthier habitats and wildlife populations, thereby reducing and preventing listings under the Endangered Species Act. The 2025 edition of the UWAP is organized into eight principal sections: The introductory materials provide some state background and context and discuss approaches to develop the UWAP as well as progress that has been made toward the conservation of Utah's native species since the 2015 UWAP was approved.
Rare plants and rare insects were fully incorporated into the 2025 UWAP, which increased the number of species and habitats. There are 256 species of greatest conservation need and 19 key habitat types identified (see Chapter 1, Species of Greatest Conservation Need -Distribution and Abundance and Chapter 2, Key Habitats -Location and Condition). ## Introduction

This document provides strong, clear guidance for developing actions that could be effective for managing Utah's native species. If this plan were effectively implemented, the result would be healthier habitats and wildlife populations, thereby reducing and preventing listings under the ESA.
The goal of the UWAP is: To manage native wildlife and plant species, including mammals, birds, fish, insects, mollusks, crustaceans, amphibians, and reptiles, and their habitats sufficient to prevent the need for additional listings under the Endangered Species Act. The scope of work required to achieve this goal is beyond what any single organization can accomplish on its own.
The goal of the UWAP is: "To manage native wildlife and plant species, including mammals, birds, fish, insects, mollusks, crustaceans, amphibians, and reptiles eir habitats, sufficient to prevent the need for additional listings under the Endangered Species Act."
The goal of the UWAP is:
To manage native wildlife and plant species, including mammals, birds, fish, insects, mollusks, crustaceans, amphibians, and reptiles, and their habitats sufficient to prevent the need for additional listings under the Endangered Species Act.
It will require collaborative, creative, solution-based partnerships. These partnerships will provide the mechanisms to develop jointly identified objectives and conservation actions for species and habitats and the basis for investment of time and effort to pursue mutually desired outcomes. Utah also has worked with surrounding states to strategize ways in which to use consistent categorizations to identify regional conservation priorities, species of greatest conservation need (SGCN), ecoregions, key habitats, shared resources, and cross-border management where appropriate.
The UWAP identifies fundamental objectives that help evaluate possible conservation actions and outcomes. These objectives are the cornerstone in native species decision-making processes and represent what the UWAP is trying to achieve:
• Avoid and prevent the need for future ESA listings
• Recover species currently listed as threatened or endangered under the ESA so they no longer need protections under the ESA
• Keep animal and plant management under state-led authority
• Maintain healthy animal and plant populations and healthy habitats to ensure these resources are available for future generations of Utahns
• Maintain healthy lands and water in Utah
• Increase opportunities to support the voluntary stewardship efforts of ranchers, farmers, and private landowners (e.g., keep working lands)

## • Increase community engagement in UWAP implementation

This plan should be viewed as the framework for an inclusive discussion of what the shared priorities and methods should be, focusing on solutions as well as respecting the vital importance of a credible process in creating fair, enduring, satisfying outcomes. The UWAP will guide partnership-driven, landscape-scale conservation work to help maintain the full array of Utah's native species and also improve habitat health. The goal of reducing the number of listed species in Utah requires broad societal support.

## Approach Review and Revision

Every 10 years, each state and the District of Columbia are instructed by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) and Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies (AFWA) to review and, if necessary, revise their SWAPs to receive federal wildlife conservation and restoration account funds. The 2025 UWAP is considered a comprehensive major revision and was reviewed by a regional review team composed of USFWS and state wildlife agency leaders. The UWAP will drive native species management and conservation in Utah for the next 10 years, from 2025 to 2035. SWAPs are developed to identify SGCN and landscape conservation efforts that will improve their status.
Reviewing and revising the 2015 UWAP (Joint Team 2015) required approximately 3 years of hard work by many individuals and organizations. To ensure adequate time for the 2025 UWAP revision, a partner meeting was held in March of 2022 to begin the revision process, and work on the revision began in earnest during the summer of 2022. Having been through the revision process twice, 3 years seems to be the requisite amount of time needed to accomplish this task, and a similar time frame is recommended for the next UWAP revision, which is due in October 2035. A 10-year revision process, however, hinders the UWAP from being a living document that evolves and adapts as needed. Three minor amendments to the 2015 UWAP were completed in 
2020
, 2021
, and 2022
(DWR 2020
, 2022))
. These revisions added and removed SGCN and updated their habitats and threats. Minor amendments are not time intensive, and the UWAP Core Team (Core Team) recommends submitting minor amendments as needed to keep the UWAP updated and relevant between comprehensive revisions.

## National Requirements and Guidance

The 2025 UWAP followed the AFWA/USFWS guidance for SWAP review and revision and incorporated the eight required elements of a SWAP (AFWA and USFWS 2017):
1. Species: The distribution and abundance of species of wildlife, including low and declining populations as each state fish and wildlife agency deems appropriate, that are indicative of the diversity and health of the state's SGCN (see Chapter 1, Species of Greatest Conservation Need -Distribution and Abundance). 3. Threats/Stressors: The problems that may adversely affect SGCN or their habitats and priority research and surveys needed to identify factors that may assist in restoration and improved conservation of SGCN and their habitats (see 
Chapter 4,
Threats,
Data
 Gaps, and Conservation Actions).

## Actions:

The actions necessary to conserve SGCN and their habitats and the priorities for implementing such conservation actions (see Chapter 4, Threats, Data Gaps, and Conservation Actions).

## Monitoring:

The provisions for periodic monitoring of SGCN and their habitats, for monitoring the effectiveness of conservation actions, and for adapting conservation actions as appropriate to respond to new information or changing conditions (see Chapter 5, Monitoring and Adapting).
6. Review and Revision: Each state's provisions to review its SWAP at intervals not to exceed 10 years (see the Review and Revision section above).
7. Partner Involvement: Each state's provisions for coordination during the development, implementation, review, and revision of its SWAP with federal, state, and local agencies and tribal nations that manage significant areas of land or water within the state, or administer programs that significantly affect the conservation of species or their habitats (see Chapter 6, Partnerships, Implementation, and Regional Collaboration).
8. Public Involvement: Each state's provisions to provide the necessary public participation in the development, revision, and implementation of the SWAP (see Chapter 6, Partnerships, Implementation, and Regional Collaboration).
Additional SWAP guidance used during the 2025 UWAP revision consisted of the following: Utah's Perspective on the Utah Wildlife Action Plan
The UWAP addresses an expansive array of native wildlife and plant species, including mammals, birds, fish, insects, mollusks, crustaceans, amphibians, and reptiles, and their habitats across the entire state of Utah. The inclusion of plants and insects is a new addition to the UWAP. The UWAP is intended to be a 10-year strategic plan rather than a prescriptive, short-term action plan. This plan aims to facilitate and guide coordinated action among the various members of Utah's wildlife conservation community. These members include DWR, other state and federal natural resource agencies, and tribal nations as well as interested local governments, industry partners, nongovernmental organizations, and individuals. It also aims to motivate the wildlife conservation community toward deeper engagement with other sectors of society. These other sectors drive Utah's economic growth and development and have their own mandates and interests for delivering crucial goods and services such as energy, water, food, and recreation. These other mandates and interests can at times be a source of conflict with the needs of SGCN and key habitats, and with the interests-and in some cases, the mandatesof the conservation community. There are diverse, competing societal interests, viewpoints, and values in addressing the state's conservation of SGCN and their habitats. Understanding relationships and valuing other valid perspectives minimizes conflict and is essential to effective conservation.
Successful conservation projects often rely on bringing together diverse stakeholders and reaching across agency borders. When common ground is found, partnerships and conservation efforts begin. The UWAP has relied on these partnerships in determining conservation and management priorities and strategies. Community attitudes determine how conservation proceeds. The UWAP is conceived as a conservation guide for Utah's entire community: agricultural producers, wildlife enthusiasts, industry groups, advocacy organizations, and agencies. The guiding principles of this strategy are as follows:
• Acknowledge and respect the pivotal role that local stakeholders play in conservation and encourage participation by all stakeholders in the UWAP revision process.
• Enhance and integrate, not replace or diminish, other planning efforts.
• Maintain an atmosphere of inclusiveness and cooperation among wildlife managers, landowners, private and public land managers, and other stakeholders while planning and implementing conservation actions.
• Maintain enough flexibility to incorporate research and management innovations into conservation actions.
• Manage for healthy habitats, for the benefit of all species.
• Identify and tackle the highest-priority conservation threats and minimize distractions by minor, inessential, or low-priority concerns.
Representatives from agencies such as the Bureau of Land Management, Natural Resources Conservation Service, U.S. Forest Service (USFS), USFWS, Utah Department of Transportation, and the Utah Trust Lands Administration participated in the preparation of this plan.
Nonetheless, those agencies will by necessity consider this plan as noncompulsory guidance as they follow their own planning processes and satisfy their own legal requirements while developing project proposals, preparing decision documents, and implementing collaborative conservation actions.
The UWAP is not a singular plan for one agency or entity. The task of preserving and managing
Utah's SGCN is far too large and complex for one agency to achieve alone. The intention of the UWAP is to facilitate cooperation with adjacent state wildlife agencies as well as with local, state, and regional organizations to more successfully achieve range-wide species conservation within Utah's area of influence.
Over the next decade of UWAP implementation, the Core Team will apply new knowledge and skills to these ongoing tasks as the team continues to learn about the species and habitats at risk; however, there also are new actions that need to be initiated, or substantially expanded, to adequately abate the overarching threats to species and habitats. Most of these new actions are ones for which the wildlife conservation community lacks the authority or the capacity to undertake. DWR has identified agencies and organizations that have the authority and capacity to undertake these actions (see the Likely Authorities, Stakeholders, and/or Partners sections in Chapter 4) and is actively working to build relationships with these organizations. These relationships will be vital to the successful employment of this plan as well as implementing conservation actions.
The UWAP identifies a set of primary conservation challenges, from a statewide perspective, and then offers a framework for addressing them. The framework can be used by anyone as a resource for planning, building partnerships, and designing projects. At a minimum, the framework will help direct the allocation of DWR staff and funding. Ideally, it also will help guide conservation efforts undertaken by DWR's partners and thereby better focus statewide efforts to conserve SGCN and their habitats in Utah.

## Key Utah Wildlife Action Plan Updates and Progress

In 2022, members from the Core Team and other partner organizations developed and approved a plan to review and revise the 2015 UWAP 
(Joint Team 2015)
 and thereby develop the 2025
The intention of the UWAP is to facilitate cooperation with adjacent state wildlife agencies as well as with local, state, and regional organizations to more successfully achieve range-wide species conservation within Utah's area of influence.
edition. Nine subcommittees (Appendix A) were formed to address UWAP deficiencies or larger aspects of the plan that needed to be updated. The 2015 edition was restructured, updated, and expanded, resulting in a major, comprehensive revision for the 2025 edition. Principal updates and changes from the 2015 UWAP, organized by required element, consist of the following:
Required Element 1. Species of Greatest Conservation Need

A total of 36 were reclassified as species of greatest information need, 20 were removed because they no longer qualify as SGCN due to improved status and/or improved knowledge on their conservation status, and seven were removed due to changes in taxonomic status. .

The 2025 UWAP is supported by five appendices. Appendix A lists partners who participated in UWAP subcommittees. A glossary is provided in Appendix B to define certain terms that are used throughout the document. In addition, rather than write out scientific names for species at first mention within the document, all scientific names have been collected and listed in Appendix C. Appendix D provides additional details on how key habitats were ranked and conditions measured in Chapter 2, Key Habitats -Location and Condition. Appendix E provides additional detail on the ranking process for Utah's priority threats in Chapter 4, Threats, Data Gaps, and Conservation Actions.

## Background and Context

## History of Species

In the United States of America, individual states hold primary management authority for most of the wildlife species found within their borders. Native migratory birds, marine mammals, and species listed under the ESA constitute the major exceptions; the federal government holds the principal management responsibility for these species. There also are some exceptions based on land tenure: states do not hold primary management authority for wildlife within national parks, national wildlife refuges, or on tribal lands, though they very frequently partner with these entities to manage wildlife on their lands. Tribal nations are sovereign entities that manage their lands unless there is a nexus with other entities. In Utah, no state entity holds management authority over native plants or insects. Utah law defines wildlife in Utah as crustaceans, mollusks, and vertebrate animals living in nature (Utah Code 23A-1-101(65)). All other members of the animal kingdom are not jurisdictional wildlife in Utah and were not included in previous versions of the UWAP, but because the UWAP is a native species conservation plan for all partners, plants and insects were included in the 2025 UWAP.
State and federal wildlife managers have developed a long and distinguished record of wildlife conservation successes since they began earnest work in the first half of the twentieth century.
This work followed the mid-nineteenth to early twentieth century extinctions of several species or subspecies of native North American wildlife, including the eastern elk, passenger pigeon, heath hen, Carolina parakeet, spectacled cormorant, Caribbean monk seal, and Steller's sea cow. These extinctions preceded modern wildlife management and helped trigger an expanding national awareness of-and eventual response to-the need to actively manage wildlife populations impacted by human activities, such as unregulated harvest or widespread modification or destruction of critically important wildlife habitats.
The record of conservation successes includes many species for which people hunt, trap, or fish and some species that are not harvested by hunters or anglers. Many common species that are pursued by hunters and anglers today would have been considered endangered during the early twentieth century if the current ESA had been in existence. The fact that many thendiminished wildlife species have been recovered so widely and successfully serves as a testament to several important actions:
• The wisdom and efficacy of the state-federal conservation partnership first created with passage of the Wildlife Restoration Act of 1937, later followed by the similarly modeled Sport Fish Restoration Act of 1950.
• The dedication and commitment of generations of local, tribal, state, and federal wildlife management professionals and the public who supported them politically and financially, making their work possible.
• The cumulative rate of return on the diverse and tremendous financial investments made in soil, water, land, and air conservation.
• The willingness and ability of tribal, state, federal, and local agencies, and (more recently) conservation nongovernmental organizations to successfully manage wildlife and habitats.

## Wildlife Funding

Nationwide, much of the funding for these conservation successes has traditionally come from hunter and angler license fees and habitat stamps and from federal excise taxes on shooting, boating, and fishing equipment. These excise taxes are collected at the point of sale, deposited into a dedicated trust fund, and distributed annually to the states in a formula-based process that is off limits to annual Congressional appropriation. These federal distributions are matched with state revenues collected through the sale of hunting and fishing licenses, stamps, and tags to provide dependable support for wildlife conservation and management. Although the amount raised and distributed varies significantly year by year, and there are also longer-term trends at play (for instance, greater urbanization of the human population and fewer license-buying hunters per capita in the population), and the certainty that there will be some level of funding every year has proven critical to long-term conservation.
In most states, the use of this excise tax and license funding has been directed mainly toward the conservation of hunted or fished species; however, the majority of wildlife species are not hunted or fished. Although a few states have chosen to use these federal excise taxes to directly fund the management of diverse wildlife species, in general, most species in Utah have not been the direct beneficiaries of this reliable funding mechanism (though they have often benefitted indirectly from such activities as habitat acquisition and management).
Some states have developed local funding solutions to address the challenge of funding native species management, such as sales taxes, impact fees, or dedicated proceeds from lottery sales (see AFWA 2021b for a more detailed list). Many of these local funding solutions, however, appear out of reach or impractical for many states and tribal nations.
The shortage of dedicated native or nongame species funding has inhibited the development, implementation, and sustainability of long-term state conservation programs for species that are not typically sought by hunters and anglers. One of the unintended consequences of this situation has been a long series of federal interventions, via the specific authority granted under the ESA, to force attention onto these species that previously had not received adequate conservation focus.
In 1997, as part of the state water tax, the Utah Legislature created the Species Protection Account, which significantly expanded the funding base for what are now considered UWAP species identified as SGCN. The Species Protection Account has contributed to the development, implementation, and continuance of species conservation in Utah, both directly and indirectly, by supplying matching funds that have enabled the funds to be leveraged for greater benefit to species management. 

## Species

## Overview of Utah

Utah is the eleventh largest state and one of the most arid states in the nation 
(Sowby 2024)
 with an average of 33 centimeters (13 inches) of precipitation per year. Elevation extremes in Utah range from a low of 664 meters (m) 
(2,178 feet)
 above mean sea level in the Beaver Dam Wash to a high of 4,123 m (13,528 feet) above mean sea level in the Uinta Mountains. Most of the 29 counties in Utah have mountain peaks over 3,048 m (10,000 feet), with valley bottoms below 1,524 m (5,000 feet). Various federal government agencies administer approximately 64% of the land surface; state agencies administer 10%; tribal nations administer 5%; and private owners administer 21%.
Utah's climate varies greatly with elevation and regional terrain and, to a lesser extent, by latitude. Generally, the north receives more precipitation and has lower evapotranspiration than the south. Statewide, winter precipitation is more important and more abundant than summer precipitation; in Utah, most precipitation falls as snow. Eastern Utah, and particularly southeastern Utah, receives more summer rain than the rest of the state, being more influenced by the North American monsoon.
Local climates in all regions are diverse with a range of ecosystems from desert to alpine, often with stark differences over just a few miles. Most of the state's precipitation falls in its mountainous regions, whereas more than two-thirds of the state receives less than 30
The WRI is the primary vehicle for UWAP implementation. centimeters (12 inches) of total precipitation per year. Temperatures in Utah have risen more than 2.5 degrees Fahrenheit since the beginning of the twentieth century 
(Frankson et al. 2022)
.
Droughts have intensified across the state, and it is predicted that more water will fall as rain rather than snow in the near future 
(Hegewisch and
Abatzoglou 2024a, 2024b)
. Drought, as measured by the Palmer Drought Severity Index, has varied substantially over the last few decades 
(Rhee and Carbone 2007)
. In general, the period from 1977 to 1986 in Utah did not have drought conditions, whereas the period from 1987 to 2003 did. The subsequent decade saw a stretch of rather moderate, occasionally wet conditions from 2004 to 2011, drought from 2012 to 2014, wetter conditions from 2014 to 2017, and then drought conditions from 2017 to 2018 and 2020 to 2023. The majority of Utah was under "Exceptional Drought" from 2020 to 2022 
(National Drought Mitigation Center et al. 2025)
. Aquatic habitats within Utah are highly impacted by drought and need to be protected to provide an essential refuge from these climatic shifts.
Utah is globally renowned for the species diversity associated with the GSL ecosystem, which is a high-priority landscape for DWR and many conservation partners. Because of its importance to a diversity of wildlife species, saline lakes (specifically, the GSL ecosystem) were identified as a key habitat in the 2025 UWAP. More than 1,500 square kilometers (579 square miles) of wetlands exist on the margins of GSL, where freshwater flows toward the lake 
(Downard 2024)
, making these wetlands a desert oasis for migrating birds. The lake provides essential stopover habitat for a great diversity of shorebird and waterfowl species, numbering in the millions of individuals. GSL is known as a site of hemispheric importance by the Western Hemisphere Shorebird Reserve Network due to the use of the area by hundreds of thousands of shorebirds, including over 500,000 Wilson's phalaropes during fall migration. The lake has also been used by up to 5 million eared grebes, which at times represents 90% of the North American population; up to 20,000 American white pelicans; and over 21% of the continental population of snowy plovers 
(DWR 2023)
. The water elevation in GSL varies with precipitation and evaporation. Since record keeping began in 1850, its surface elevation has fluctuated from a historic low of 1,276.7 m (4,188.5 feet) in 2022 to a historic high of 1,284 m (4,212 feet) in 1986.
The historic low in November 2022 has raised concern for the fragile ecosystem, which requires water to sustain brine shrimp and two species of brine flies, prevent toxic dust storms, and provide habitat to millions of birds. This vertical displacement can shift the shoreline a dozen miles or more in some areas, which translates to a surface area ranging between approximately 600,000 and 1,500,000 acres. This lake-level fluctuation ensures the long-term survival of the lake's dynamic habitats and the bird species that frequent those habitats. The global conservation significance of GSL cannot be overstated.
The complexities of Utah's topography and climate result in biologically diverse habitats.
Important habitat types in Utah include a diversity of wetlands, sagebrush steppe and shrublands, mountain shrub and pinyon-juniper woodlands, aspen-conifer forests, and desert grasslands and shrublands. Approximately 80% of Utah's wildlife depends on wetlands (Utah Geological Survey 2022). Aspen-conifer communities are second to riparian areas in species diversity and abundance. Utah's habitats support approximately 940 species and subspecies of wildlife, including mollusks and crustaceans 
(DWR et al. 2025
), more than 3,000 plants 
(Welsh et al. 2015)
, and thousands of species of invertebrates. These species are organized into diverse animal communities occupying the habitats mentioned above.

## Ecoregions

Ecoregion designations are an important addition to the 2025 UWAP. The Core Team chose to include ecoregions in this revision to facilitate regional collaboration with SWAPs of other western states. Ecoregions are areas where habitats (and the type, quality, and quantity of environmental resources) are generally similar. The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) and USFS each have defined ecoregions. The Core Team chose to use EPA ecoregions 
(EPA 2023)
 rather than USFS ecoregions 
(Cleland et al. 1997;
USFS 1994)
. The EPA ecoregions are hierarchical with the following four levels:
• Level I -12 ecoregions in the conterminous United States (broadest level)
• Level II -25 ecoregions in the conterminous United States
• Level III -105 ecoregions in the conterminous United States
• Level IV -967 ecoregions in the conterminous United States EPA Level III ecoregions were chosen for the 2025 UWAP for the following reasons:
• EPA Level III ecoregions offer more regional specificity than Level I or II but still provide coarse detail that allows for broader conservation aimed at landscape scales.
• UWAP terrestrial key habitats were identified using LANDFIRE Existing Vegetation Types (EVTs) (LANDFIRE 2020a), which are based on EPA Level III ecoregions.
• Other southwestern states, including Nevada and New Mexico, use EPA ecoregions, and consistency will facilitate regional collaboration.
The EPA ecoregions are multiagency and multinational. The Commission for Environmental
Cooperation, an international organization created by Canada, the United States, and Mexico to support the conservation and protection of the North American environment, identifies North American ecoregions by evaluating patterns and composition in several biotic and abiotic factors, including geology, landforms, soils, vegetation, climate, land use, wildlife, and hydrology 
(Omernik 1987
(Omernik , 1995))
. For more detail on how these ecoregions were created, please see 
Omernik and Griffith (2014)
, 
Omernik (1995
Omernik ( , 2004))
, and Commission for Environmental 
Cooperation (1997)
.
The incorporation of EPA Level III ecoregions into the UWAP paves the way for collaboration with adjacent states that have similar SGCN, SWAP habitats, priorities, and conservation concerns. This action addresses the first principle of AFWA guidance for landscape conservation initiatives: develop and use a common lexicon and classification system for species, habitats, threats, and conservation actions (AFWA 2021a).
Utah is composed of arid deserts and canyonlands, salt flats, wetlands, semiarid shrublands, irrigated valleys, woodlands, forested mountains, and glaciated peaks, resulting in enormous ecological diversity and comprising seven EPA Level III ecoregions, which are shown in Figure 
1
 and described below. 

## Central Basin and Range

The Central Basin and Range ecoregion in western Utah covers 25.1% of the state 
(Soulard 2012
) (see Figure 
1
). This ecoregion has hot summers and mild winters with a dry, desert climate. The ecoregion includes mountains separated by broad, dry basins and valleys that include playas, salt flats, and sand dunes. Common plant species in basins and valleys include Parish's Great Basin sagebrush, shadscale saltbush, winterfat, black sagebrush, Wyoming big sagebrush, Mormon tea, rabbitbrush, Indian ricegrass, and wheatgrass species, with greasewood, Nuttall's saltbush, and alkali sacaton in more saline areas. Lower-elevation mountains may have single-leaf pinyon, Utah juniper, sagebrush species, antelope bitterbrush, serviceberry species, snowberry species, and bluebunch wheatgrass. Higher elevations may contain Douglas-fir, white fir, limber pine, or quaking aspen 
(EPA 2013;
Wiken et al. 2011)
.

## Colorado Plateau

The Colorado Plateau ecoregion in eastern and southern Utah covers 43% (EPA 2013) of the state (see Figure 
1
). The climate in this ecoregion is characterized by hot, dry summers and cold, dry winters. This ecoregion exhibits rugged topography with sometimes abrupt changes in relief. Vegetation in low-lying basins and canyons is sparse, and common species are four-wing saltbush, blackbrush, shadscale saltbush, and galleta. In the uplands and higher valleys, common species include Wyoming big sagebrush, black sagebrush, and pinyon-juniper woodland. Common species in higher elevations include Gambel oak, quaking aspen, mountain mahogany, and Douglas-fir 
(Wiken et al. 2011)
.

## Mojave Basin and Range

The Mojave Basin and Range ecoregion in southwestern Utah covers 1% (EPA 2013) of the state (see Figure 
1
). The climate in this ecoregion is characterized as dry, subtropical desert with hot summers and warm winters. Desert vegetation is sparse, and common species include creosote bush, white bursage, Joshua tree and other yuccas, and blackbrush. Common species on alkali flats include saltbush species, saltgrass, alkali sacaton, and iodinebush. Common species in the mountains include sagebrush species, juniper, single-leaf pinyon, ponderosa pine, white fir, limber pine, and bristlecone pine 
(Wiken et al. 2011)
.

## Northern Basin and Range

The Northern Basin and Range ecoregion in northern Utah covers 2% (EPA 2013) of the state (see Figure 
1
). This ecoregion is arid and has hot summers and cold winters, and the climate is characterized as steppe or desert. Common species in low-lying areas include mountain big sagebrush, Wyoming big sagebrush, low sagebrush, bluebunch wheatgrass, rabbitbrush, Idaho fescue, and Thurber's needlegrass with some scattered juniper. Common species in mountains at low to mid-elevations are mountain big sagebrush, mountain mahogany, juniper, and Idaho fescue. Common species in mountains at high elevations are Douglas-fir and quaking aspen 
(Wiken et al. 2011)
.

## Southern Rockies

The Southern Rockies ecoregion in southeastern Utah encompasses 1% (EPA 2013) of the state (see Figure 
1
). In Utah, this ecoregion consists of exclaves surrounded by the Colorado Plateau ecoregion and is characterized by steep, rugged mountains and some middle-elevation high intermontane valleys. The climate in this ecoregion is characterized as humid with substantial temperature variations between seasons and a subarctic climate at higher elevations. This ecoregion has warm to cool summers and severe winters with no distinct dry season. The vegetation communities display elevational banding. Common vegetation at low elevations consists of grass and shrubs with sagebrush, mountain mahogany, pinyon pine, juniper, or scattered Gambel oak woodlands. Common vegetation communities at low to middle elevations comprise a variety of vegetation types, including juniper-oak woodlands, ponderosa pine, Douglas-fir, and aspen species. Common vegetation at middle to high elevation consists of forests composed of Engelmann spruce, subalpine fir, and quaking aspen. Common vegetation at the highest elevations consists of alpine low shrubs, cushion plants, sedges, and krummholz vegetation of stunted Engelmann spruce, subalpine fir, and limber pine 
(Wiken et al. 2011)
.

## Wasatch and Uinta Mountains

The Wasatch and Uinta Mountains ecoregion encompasses 25% (EPA 2013) of Utah, occupying a band down the center of the state for almost its entire length (see Figure 
1
). The terrain includes high, steep mountains with narrow crests and valleys and dissected plateaus and open high mountains in some areas. The climate in this ecoregion is considered to be humid and severe with great temperature variation between seasons. Typically, the summers are warm to hot and the winters are severe with no distinct dry season. The vegetation communities display elevational banding. Common vegetation communities at low to middle elevations comprise a variety of vegetation types, including juniper-oak woodlands, ponderosa pine, Douglas-fir, and aspen species. Common vegetation at middle to high elevation consists of forests composed of Engelmann spruce, subalpine fir, and quaking aspen, with ponderosa pine at lower elevations.
In contrast to the Southern Rockies ecoregion, the more common middle-elevation species include quaking aspen, juniper-pinyon, and oak. The vegetation at the highest elevations typically consists of Douglas-fir, quaking aspen, subalpine fir, Engelmann spruce, and limber pine. The valleys primarily consist of grasses, sagebrush, pinyon, and Utah juniper 
(Wiken et al. 2011)
.

## Wyoming Basin

The Wyoming Basin ecoregion in northeastern Utah covers 2% (EPA 2013) of the state (see Figure 
1
). This ecoregion has warm to hot summers and cold winters, and the climate is characterized as dry steppe and desert. This ecoregion is a broad basin with some high hills and low mountains. There are areas of badlands. The vegetation is primarily arid grasslands and shrublands. Common species in the sagebrush steppes of this ecoregion include Wyoming big sagebrush, black sagebrush, prairie sagebrush, rabbitbrush, western wheatgrass, needle and thread grass, blue grama grass, and prairie junegrass. Common species in the desert

## Introduction

The Utah Division of Wildlife Resources (DWR) has management authority, as defined in Utah
Code (23A-1-101( 
65
)), over more than 940 species of wildlife, including mammals, birds, amphibians, reptiles, fish, mollusks, and crustaceans 
(DWR et al. 2025)
. Previous Utah Wildlife Action Plans (UWAPs) 
(e.g., 2005 and 2015)
 only considered these DWR jurisdictional wildlife as species of greatest conservation need (SGCN) because DWR leads UWAP planning efforts.
State wildlife action plans (SWAPs), however, are plans for all partners working on native species conservation efforts. Although DWR does not have management authority over plants and insects, these taxa 1) are important to maintain ecosystem health and biodiversity; 2) are being managed by UWAP partners; 3) can often benefit through UWAP wildlife projects if they are considered during project planning; and 4) can impact all Utahns if populations decline and imperiled species become listed as threatened or endangered under the Endangered Species Act (ESA).
A total of 30 plant and five insect species were added to the UWAP SGCN list in a 2021 addendum to the 2015 UWAP and their habitats were added in a 2022 addendum 
(DWR 2022)
.
During the 2025 UWAP comprehensive revision, the Plant and Insect Integration and SGCN Subcommittees ensured that conservation needs for additional at-risk Utah plants (more than 3,000) and insects (more than 300) were considered and, where justified, included as SGCN to fully integrate these taxa into the 2025 UWAP.
An essential feature in the development and evaluation of any SWAP is the equitable how secure their habitat is, and the breadth of their distributions. The challenge is to develop an equitable, transparent, and credible approach that spans all taxa.
A rubric (Figure 
2
) was developed to determine which species were included as SGCN for the 2015 UWAP (UWAP Joint Team [Joint Team] 2015). The three fundamental factors considered during development were the likelihood of an ESA listing, the consequences of such a listing, and the ability of the State of Utah to influence a listing decision. The 2025 SGCN Subcommittee discussed the UWAP rubric in its first meeting and agreed to maintain the same criteria for determining SGCN that have been used since the rubric was developed and agreed upon by UWAP partners.
The subcommittee recognized that the rubric had been useful in developing the 2015 SGCN list, including SGCN additions and deletions in subsequent minor revisions.
Modifications were made to the rubric to accommodate plants and insects, because these taxa were not considered in the 2015 UWAP 
(Joint Team 2015)
.
NatureServe national and subnational conservation status ranks (N-and S-rank, respectively) are integral in evaluating whether a species should be considered a UWAP SGCN.
Ranks range from 5 (secure) to 1 (critically imperiled) and are based on a species' distribution, population abundance and trends, and threats (NatureServe 2020). Wildlife species with ranks of N1, N2, and N3 are eligible for inclusion as SGCN within the rubric; however, too many Utah plants and insects exist with these status ranks to provide a useful prioritization for these taxa. The SGCN Subcommittee decided to predominately consider N1 (critically imperiled) and N2 (imperiled) plants and insects, as well as S1 and S2 plants and insects for inclusion as SGCNs. Umbrella species also were considered for inclusion as 2025 SGCNs.
Umbrella species are those whose conservation confers protection to other co-occurring species, promotes ecosystem function, or facilitates habitat connectivity. Three types of umbrella species were considered for the 2025 UWAP:
• Keystone species -species that have an outsized influence in supporting the populations of other species and ecosystem function.
• Indicator species -species that can represent the health of or specific processes within an ecosystem such as environmental changes, species richness, or population densities of other species. • Ecosystem engineer -species that modify the biophysical environment in ways that increase or maintain habitat diversity and thereby impact species richness in an ecosystem.
Frequently, more information is needed to understand whether species are truly declining on the landscape or if a rank is simply reflecting bias of the available data. For example,
NatureServe ranks can indicate rarity if little distributional information is known for a species.
Several species (e.g., many mollusks) were included as SGCN in the 2015 UWAP 
(Joint Team 2015)
 primarily due to limited information. Many data gaps result from a lack of appropriate monitoring methods or unresolved taxonomy. Data gaps can prevent effective management if they indicate that species need conservation attention that is actually unnecessary. This issue was further highlighted through the process of including plants and insects in the 2025 UWAP, because limited, outdated, or incomplete information exists for many of these taxa. More recently, SWAP revisions are starting to distinguish the species with information needs or data gaps as a subset of SGCNs. The SGCN Subcommittee had several discussions to address the issue of data gaps and agreed that an SGCN category defining species of greatest information need (SGIN) would highlight data-deficient taxa to include in the 2025 UWAP and so prioritize filling data gaps. SGIN criteria were based closely on criteria identified in Idaho's recently finalized SWAP (Idaho Department of Fish and Game 2023).
SGIN are species that qualify as SGCN through the UWAP SGCN rubric largely due to a lack of current scientific knowledge for a given taxon or information critical to understanding its conservation needs. Identifying species as SGIN allows UWAP partners to prioritize actions or complete studies (e.g., surveys or monitoring, genomic analyses, habitat association modeling)
to better understand distribution, taxonomy, or ecology for a given species. As these knowledge gaps are filled for SGINs, they can be re-evaluated through the UWAP SGCN rubric to be considered for SGCN designation or removed entirely if their revised status no longer warrants conservation attention. Species were identified as SGIN due to one or more of the following factors:
• Taxonomic uncertainty (further study is necessary to clarify a species' taxonomy)
Black-Footed Ferret. Photograph by Tory Mathis, DWR, Uintah County, 2019.
• Distributional uncertainty (additional occurrence and inventory data needed to confirm species' distribution and range)
• Ecological uncertainty (e.g., population trends, species-specific response to anthropogenic or environmental threats and stressors, uncertain habitat requirements)
This chapter presents a summary of the distribution and abundance information on the 256 SGCN identified during the selection process and highlights additional species identified as SGIN. Specifically, this chapter presents state and national NatureServe ranks, which provide an integrated, weighted index of rarity, trends, and threats, with rarity (the inverse of abundance) assigned most of the weight. Utah-specific information about rarity, trends, and distribution is also presented in this chapter (threats are discussed in 
Chapter 4,
Threats,
Data Gaps,
and Conservation Actions)
. Important points about these ranks and the way they were used to identify SGCN in Utah are as follows:
• No single rank, index, or score can possibly give the whole picture, but the NatureServe conservation status assessment method (NatureServe 2025) provides a uniform, credible, and documented approach.
• This approach may be applied across taxonomic groups, states, and jurisdictional boundaries.
• NatureServe ranks are updated periodically with new inventory and assessment data from DWR and many partners.
To help interpret the material presented in this chapter, a brief explanation of the various ranks is provided below.

## Global, National, and Subnational Levels

NatureServe conservation statuses are applied at all three geographic levels (the 2025 UWAP uses two of these levels, N and S):
• G-ranks are designated at the global (or range-wide) level.
• N-ranks are designated at the national level for a particular nation.
• S-ranks are designated at the subnational or state level.
Uinta Basin Hookless Cactus. Photograph by Tory Mathis, DWR, Uintah County, 2024.

## Commonly Encountered Conservation Status Ranks

Conservation status ranks mainly consist of numbers, which are sometimes replaced by or used with letters or punctuation marks, as follows (NatureServe 2024a):
• 1 -"Critically imperiled -At very high risk of extirpation in the jurisdiction due to very restricted range, very few populations or occurrences, very steep declines, severe threats, or other factors."
• 2 -"Imperiled -At high risk of extirpation in the jurisdiction due to restricted range, few populations or occurrences, steep declines, severe threats, or other factors."
• 3 -"Vulnerable -At moderate risk of extirpation in the jurisdiction due to a fairly restricted range, relatively few populations or occurrences, recent and widespread declines, threats, or other factors."
• 4 -"Apparently Secure -At a fairly low risk of extirpation in the jurisdiction due to an extensive range and/or many populations or occurrences, but with possible cause for some concern as a result of local recent declines, threats, or other factors."
• 5 -"Secure -At very low or no risk of extirpation in the jurisdiction due to a very extensive range, abundant populations or occurrences, with little to no concern from declines or threats."
Thus, for example, an N3 species is nationally vulnerable" and an S2 species is state imperiled for the state in which the rank is assigned.
According to NatureServe convention, a state numerical rank cannot imply that the species is more secure at the state level than it is nationally or globally (e.g., a rank of N1/S3 cannot occur), but some exceptions have been made in a few cases for the following reasons:
• State ranks are assigned and maintained by state natural heritage programs and conservation data centers. The Utah Natural Heritage Program is located within DWR, which updates state ranks periodically.
• National ranks are assigned and maintained by NatureServe, a nonprofit organization and a network of over 80 state, provincial, and other subnational (e.g., Navajo Nation, Tennessee Valley Authority) natural heritage programs. NatureServe updates its national and global ranks at much longer intervals-often 15 to 20 years-than many state programs.
• Logic dictates that a species' actual (versus reported) conservation status would typically change more rapidly in smaller geographic areas (e.g., states) than in much larger ones (e.g., nations). State programs may prefer to maintain, report, and use current information rather than communicate information known to be outdated or inaccurate while waiting for NatureServe to update its ranks. This is the case in Utah.
The letters and punctuation marks used in conservation status ranks are listed below. These (and others not currently applicable in Utah) can be applied at any scale level and can be used in conjunction with ranking numbers or by themselves. The status ranks and definitions were based on NatureServe (2024b) and adapted for the 2025 UWAP:
• B -"Breeding-conservation status refers to the breeding population of the species." Follows the numeric part of the rank, if used.
• N -"Nonbreeding-conservation status refers to the nonbreeding population of the species in the nation or state/province."
• H -Historical occurrence but not known if recently extant. Possibly extinct or extirpated, but with some reasonable hope of rediscovery. Routinely applied after 20 years of no observations (whether any surveys were conducted). (Used instead of a number.)
• NNR and SNR -Unranked (i.e.
, not yet assessed). (Used instead of a number.)
• U -Unrankable due to conflicting or inadequate information. (Used instead of a number.)
• X -Presumed extinct or extirpated, with rediscovery not reasonably expected. Not located despite extensive and intensive searches. Extinction is a global (range-wide) phenomenon, while extirpation applies to loss within a particular national or subnational area, with the entity still extant elsewhere. (Used instead of a number.)
• S#/S# -Range of ranks due to uncertainty (e.g., S2/S3 indicates a state rank ranging from S2 to S3). Limited to two ranks of difference, beyond which the status would be U for Unrankable (e.g., S1/S4 would instead be listed as SU). Ranged ranks apply to global and national ranks as well.
• ? -Recorded within a nation or state, but local status not available, not yet determined, or indeterminate. (Denotes inexact numeric rank.)

## Utah Wildlife Action Plan Rubric

All species considered for inclusion in the 2025 UWAP SGCN/SGIN list were passed through the UWAP rubric's left side (Status Assessment) and-as necessary-its right side (Consequences and Opportunities) (see Figure 
2
). The following sections describe the rubric and the rationale behind Utah's SGCN listing process.

## Figure 2. Species of Greatest Conservation Need Decision Process Rubric

Note: T&E = threatened and endangered.

## Left Side -Status Assessment

The following three essential questions were asked when considering the potential for a native species to face listing under the ESA:
• Is the taxon legally listable?
• Is the taxon native to Utah?
• What is the vulnerability of the taxon to extinction?
Step 1: Listable entity (valid taxon, or significant portion of range)
Because the intended outcome of the UWAP is to prevent additional ESA listings, if the entity under consideration is not an ESA-listable entity (i.e., a recognized taxon or a listable subdivision of a taxon) then it should not be considered a Utah SGCN.
Step 2: Native to Utah Species that are not native flora or fauna of Utah should not be designated Utah SGCN.
Step 3: Species with federal status Species that are federally listed as threatened or endangered under the ESA, considered candidates for federal listing, or undergoing a status review for a petition should be designated Utah SGCNs. This step was added to the 2025 UWAP rubric to ensure that all ESA-listed, candidate, and petitioned species were included on the SGCN list.
Step The species ranked in this category can be more widespread with some local or long-term concerns.
In general, these species would not meet the criteria to be designated as SGCN, but exceptions can be • What are the ecological impacts (severity) and extent (scope) of a species' listing?
• What is Utah's responsibility in maintaining the viability of a species?
A summary of definitions is provided in Table 
1
. An additional four steps on the right side of the SGCN decision process rubric provide reasoning for adding a species as an SGCN or rationale for excluding a species from SGCN consideration.
Step 7: Sum of Listing Consequences = 3 or 4
This step considers the summed scores of two criteria that assess the scope and severity of socioeconomic impacts to Utah of a species' future or current ESA listing (see Table 
1
).
Possible values range from 0 to 4.
If the existing or potential negative socioeconomic consequences of an ESA listing are in the upper half of the possible range, then that species should be considered a provisional Utah SGCN, subject to the results of Step 9.
Step 8: Sum of Extirpation Consequences = 3 or 4
This step evaluates the combined scores of two criteria that determine the scope and severity of potential ecological impacts to Utah due to a species' current or future severe decline or extirpation (see Table 
1
). Possible values range from 0 to 4.
If the existing or potential negative ecological consequences of a severe decline or extirpation in Utah are in the upper half of the possible range, then that species should be considered a provisional Utah SGCN, subject to the results of Step 9. Step 9: Sum of Responsibility = 4, 5, or 6
This step evaluates the combined scores of three criteria that determine the extent of Utah's responsibility for a species' viability. The three criteria quantify the role Utah plays in a species' abundance, range, and annual life cycle (see Table 
1
). Possible values range from 0 to 6.
If Utah's responsibility for a species' viability falls in the upper half of the possible range, that species should be considered a provisional Utah SGCN or Utah SGIN (if qualification is primarily due to data gaps), subject to the results of Step 10.
Step 10: Sum of Listing Consequences, Extirpation Consequences, or Responsibility = 0
This step revisits the scores given to a provisional SGCN for each of the previous three steps.
Consider the following possibilities:
• Step 7 -Sum of Listing Consequences = 0 • Step 8 -Sum of Extirpation Consequences = 0 • Step 9 -Sum of Responsibility = 0
If any one of these three possibilities are met, the provisional SGCN species will not be added to the final SGCN list.

## Results

In the 2015 UWAP (Joint Team 2015), 141 species were listed as SGCN (Table 
2
). The SGCN list has increased to 256 species in this revision with the addition of plants and insects; however, several species that were originally considered SGCNs are now on the SGIN list. The SGCN list is now more representative of species in true decline versus species for which more information is needed to understand population status. The latter species were moved to the SGIN list. The total species for 2025 include species added in minor amendments in 2020 (DWR 2020) and 2021 and 2022 (DWR 2022). 1963). BCT was petitioned to be listed under the ESA in 1979 and remained as warranted but precluded under the ESA through much of the 1980s.
Although genetically pure populations of BCT were thought to have been lost in the early 1970s, additional populations of BCT that appeared pure continued to be discovered from the late 1970s through the 2000s. As genetic testing techniques emerged and improved, pure BCT populations were confirmed. These pure populations became the source for repatriation efforts that helped increase the number of BCT populations in Utah. BCT was again petitioned for listing under the ESA in the 1990s, which led to the development of the Utah conservation agreement and strategy for BCT 
(Lentsch et al. 1997
) and later a range-wide conservation agreement and strategy 
(Lentsch et al. 2000)
. These documents solidified the need and commitment for conservation actions for this species. BCT was identified as an SGCN in the 2005 UWAP. The U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service found BCT not warranted for ESA protections in 2008 after another petition to list the species. Significant conservation actions were undertaken by the mid-2010s, and the Utah S-rank for BCT improved to S4. These conservation efforts included habitat improvements, isolating BCT populations from non-native salmonids, removing non-native salmonid populations, and restocking and restoring BCT to historical habitat. Due to the recent ESA evaluation, BCT was retained as an SGCN in the 2015 UWAP.
The BCT Utah S-rank was re-evaluated in 2024 while DWR compiled an updated SGCN list for the 2025 UWAP; the S-rank remained an S4. With an additional 10 years of conservation actions, Utah BCT partners believed this species to be secure in Utah and removed it from the UWAP SGCN list. The BCT is a true conservation success story that was realized because partners prioritized conservation of the species over decades of conservation. Utah's management strategy for BCT populations has switched to maintenance, allowing more effort to be put into conservation for other SGCN species with more immediate need. BCT populations will be monitored to ensure this species-Utah's state fish-remains common and secure into the future.

## Introduction

Habitat is an important term used across ecological disciplines, and its definition has been the subject of decades of scientific debate 
(Darracq and Tandy 2019;
Hall et al. 1997;
Hodges 2008;
Kirk et al. 2018;
Whittaker et al. 1973)
. Use of consistent, operational definitions for habitat terminology is paramount for communicating important management needs, developing management goals, and addressing legal issues pertaining to wildlife conservation. Importantly, a key commonality in recent primary literature acknowledges that habitat is fundamentally a species-specific term 
(Garshelis 2000;
Hall et al. 1997;
Kirk et al. 2018;
Morrison et al. 2006)
, which implies the emphasis of context when attempting to understand and define habitat and related concepts. Thus, habitat is generally defined as an area with a combination of resources and environmental conditions that produce occupancy (including survival and reproduction) by a given species (or population). 
Hall et al. (1997)
 further clarifies that an organism's habitat is made up of anywhere that an organism is provided with resources and conditions, even temporarily, that allow it to survive, disperse, migrate, and rest (e.g., migratory corridors, land occupied during breeding and nonbreeding seasons). The presence of a species in an area relates to the sum of specific resources needed by that species, more than just vegetation or vegetation structure. The area is considered suitable habitat if the organism is present. Habitat quality refers to the ability of the environment to provide conditions appropriate for individual and population persistence. Highquality habitat is a measurable variable and should not be equated with suitable habitat 
(Hall et al. 1997;
Murphy and Noon 1991)
. Under Section 3(5)(A) of the Endangered Species Act, critical habitat is used primarily as a legal term to describe the physical and biological components essential to the conservation of a species and to support special management considerations or protections for specific geographic areas occupied by a species at the time of listing consideration. Critical habitat is linked to the concept of high-quality habitat.
Habitat is also a variable concept. The combination of resources (e.g., other organisms, shelter, water) and environmental conditions (e.g., temperature, solar input, precipitation, competition or predation pressure) are subject to changes across space, time, and elevation. Estimating a species' habitat should incorporate an understanding of the drivers of community dynamics.
Biological communities are inherently plastic. Interactions among species present at a given
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 67
time are influenced by changes in species composition at daily, seasonal, and annual scales and are constrained by species' life stages, phenology (timing of biological events), resource preferences, and relative abundances. Species interactions are only possible between species that co-occur (are present at the same time), and efforts to resolve interaction networks and spatiotemporal variation should provide crucial management insights given the rapid environmental changes associated with habitat loss, climate change, and biological invasions.
Related concepts include habitat type, vegetation type, habitat availability, and abundance.
Habitat type is defined by the type of vegetation association in an area, or the potential of vegetation to reach a specified climax state 
(Daubenmire 1968
). Vegetation type refers to the characteristic dominant plant species or a common aspect of the assemblage, such as an elevation range or environmental commonality 
(Ornduff et al. 2003:112)
. Habitat availability is the accessibility and procurability of physical and biological components of a habitat by organisms, whereas abundance refers only to the quantity of resources present in the habitat 
(Hall et al. 1997
). An ecosystem is a geographic area wherein the aggregate physical, chemical, and biological processes and services distinguish it from surrounding areas. Ecosystems are the product of the interacting biodiversity within a given habitat. This term is used interchangeably with key habitat and habitat throughout the Utah Wildlife Action Plan (UWAP).
A best practice provides clear definitions for categories, presents logical methods for classifying observations 
(Hodges 2008)
, and uses precise language wherever possible to facilitate further ecological syntheses, effective conservation policy and planning, and crosssector collaboration. It is essential, therefore, to also consider the appropriate scale when mapping habitat to classify distinct environmental units-or suites of species and conditions that distinguish habitats-for informed decision making.
In this chapter, habitats are characterized at a spatial scale large enough to represent the entire state of Utah and include coarse estimates of ecological and landscape heterogeneity that occur at finer geographic scales. Habitats presented must account for the physical, chemical, and biological resources needs of many co-occurring species (including species of greatest conservation need [SGCN] and species of greatest information need [SGIN]). Therefore, the habitats are categorized by habitat type, mapped by vegetation type, and evaluated at varying spatial scales to determine health and threat factors. Methods used to describe terrestrial and aquatic habitats are presented in this chapter, along with the rationale for these processes.
Terrestrial habitats are named according to vegetation units that define the habitat type(s) that best suit the resource needs of most SGCN and other wildlife species, provided that those habitats are intact and appear to be functioning normally. For instance, mountain sagebrush ecosystems in Utah provide key habitat for the greater sage-grouse and western toad for at least some part of their life cycles. Although these two species may be found in the same location, they have differing food, shelter, and space requirements. The habitat is not defined by their presence alone but by the combined biological inhabitants and the chemical and physical processes that can be identified as one ecosystem in Utah. Therefore, Mountain Sagebrush is identified as a key habitat in Utah for both greater sage-grouse and western toad.
Working closely with partners, the Utah Division of Wildlife Resources (DWR) led the development and implementation of two distinct processes to identify the UWAP key habitats (terrestrial and aquatic). See Appendix D for full documentation of the key habitat process. A total of 14 terrestrial key habitats (Table 
5
 and
Table 6
) and five aquatic key habitats (Table 
7
)
were identified. One of the 14 terrestrial key habitats was defined as other habitats, which consists of Badlands, Cliffs and Canyons, Mesic Meadows, Microbialites, and Sand Dunes.
These habitats were too small to evaluate using data sets or programs such as LANDFIRE, but the UWAP Core Team (Core Team) chose to highlight them within a key habitat due to their importance to SGCNs. This chapter presents an account for each key habitat and its extent and condition, drawing from the processes described in Appendix D. These accounts include maps depicting the location of each key habitat within Utah. Terrestrial Key Habitats

The Core Team and the Utah DWR Wildlife Action Plan Implementation Team decided to exclude disturbed, developed, ruderal, and agricultural habitats from consideration as key habitats. Although SGCN use these habitats, DWR and its partners decided to prioritize native key habitats. Sparse vegetation ultimately encompassed multiple habitats, some of which are included in the Other Habitats Key Habitat; consequently, sparse vegetation was not included as its own terrestrial key habitat. Lower montane chaparral also was excluded because of its very small acreage in Utah, and because it is not typical for the state. After these exclusions, the 2015 ranking process (UWAP Joint Team 2015) was not needed because the candidate habitats (after the exclusions described below) became the key habitats. The extent and condition of these key habitats were then measured in LANDFIRE.

Using the LANDFIRE-assigned distribution percentages for the five reference age classes within each habitat type, a best condition was determined for each habitat type and correlated to the biological communities that depend on them. This working hypothesis was particularly applicable to those species referred to as landscape species, such as greater sage-grouse, golden eagle, and wolverine.
A corollary proposition to this working hypothesis was that the best way to help all species that depend on these coarse-scale habitat types is to manage the habitat types toward their expected age-class distribution. This would occur by means of a very large-scale (perhaps even statewide) program of stewardship and restoration. The following are important points to consider with a large-scale approach to managing terrestrial habitat types:
• This management approach is not meant to be applied to the management of those species with very specific and narrow ecological niches and habitat requirements or with very restricted geographic distributions, such as some invertebrates (e.g., tiger beetle, mountain snail species), shorebirds, many plants, and most of the aquatic SGCN.
• This management approach is not meant to replace or diminish management consideration of fine-scale habitats or habitat features embedded within the coarsescale vegetation units, such as springs, talus slopes, and rock outcrops.
• Specialist species, fine-scale habitats, and habitat features will still need to be managed on a localized, case-by-case basis. Managers will need to take care not to overlook and potentially harm these species when focusing on broader stewardship or restoration efforts that manipulate vegetation classes to achieve coarse-scale habitat goals.
• Many Utah SGCN plants have specialized habitat requirements and/or very limited distribution and are not directly associated with any of the 13 UWAP key habitats identified using LANDFIRE (2020a) (see Table 
6
) (the Other Habitats Key Habitat was not defined using 
LANDFIRE [2020a]
). Key habitats for some of the Utah plant SGCNs are identified in Endangered Species Act recovery plans or state conservation agreements and strategies. Management of the extent and condition of these habitat types will follow recommendations outlined in these plans.
Ecological departure is a measure of change between succession classes; good or bad conditions must be determined on a case-by-case basis. Some SGCN may thrive in an area exhibiting higher ecological departure.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 74
For each terrestrial key habitat description, two maps are provided. Map A outlines the location (extent) of the terrestrial key habitat within Utah, and Map B depicts the habitat's customweighted ecological departure obtained from LANDFIRE (2020b) and modified for more realistic applicability. Custom-weighted ecological departures were used to measure key habitat conditions and presented at the resolution of the Hydrologic Unit Code (HUC) 12 subwatersheds. HUC-12 subwatershed polygons containing a very small portion of the total key habitat were not displayed in order to maintain focus on locations with primary concentrations of key habitats. Custom-weighted ecological departures were calculated for HUC-12 units, binned into five categories, and shown as a percentage of the overall degree of departure from archetypal conditions for that particular habitat. To display bin departures, histogram thresholding was used to select HUC-12 polygons containing a meaningful percentage of the total key habitat. Specifically, only HUC-12 polygons with a percentage of key habitat higher than those in the first interval of an equal-interval histogram were displayed. The ecological departure maps are grouped by percentages into the following five categories: 0% to 20%, 20% to 40%, 40% to 60%, 60% to 80%, and 80% to 100%.
Individual terrestrial key habitat descriptions also include some management recommendations, which are intended to help project managers focus on activities that will help reduce departure and improve landscape-scale habitat condition. The SGCN are not listed by key habitat. Instead, readers are directed to a new tool developed by DWR that will help make the UWAP more actionable by allowing partners (including the public) to spatially view potential projects and their overlap with key habitats and SGCNs. The DWR Wildlife Habitat Analysis Tool (DWR 2024a) allows users to understand the plant and wildlife context for habitat. This tool provides authoritative Utah species information, including information on SGCN, SGIN, and other non-rare plants and animals. DWR compiles this information from a variety of sources, including the UWAP, scientific literature, museum collections, and field surveys. The tool's utility in helping a user to understand the context of a key habitat shines in that a user could, for example, select the Aspen-Conifer Key Habitat as a project area and see the likelihood of SGCN historical occurrence or habitat selection in the project area along with any known migration corridors, game species habitat, and a wealth of other useful information. The report generated from the tool also links to detailed species accounts (DWR 2024a) and the current UWAP.

## Aquatic Key Habitats

The Aquatic Key Habitats Subcommittee first identified the 2025 UWAP aquatic key habitats and geospatial data sets that would be used to define the extent of those habitats (see 
Table 7)
.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 75
Next, they focused on measuring habitat condition. Substantial time was spent identifying threats to the aquatic key habitats.
The Aquatic Key Habitats Subcommittee revised the number of aquatic key habitats from the 2015 UWAP into the five presented in Table 
7
. The Aquatic Key Habitats Subcommittee discussed and agreed upon these key habitats, which were then approved by the Utah DWR Wildlife Action Plan Implementation Team and the Core Team. There was no ranking process for aquatic key habitats; all five habitats that were identified were considered key habitats and included in the UWAP. The subcommittee acknowledged that Utah is one of the most arid states in the nation 
(Sowby 2024;
Utah Foundation Research Brief 2009)
; therefore, most aquatic habitats are extremely important and should be considered key habitats. Each aquatic key habitat's extent in Utah, including acreage value and percentage of land area, and the data sources used to extract this information are presented in Table 
7
. Unlike terrestrial key habitats, no comprehensive geospatial data set exists that will determine the extent for all UWAP aquatic key habitats. Aquatic key habitats were generally smaller than terrestrial key habitats; therefore, it was determined that LANDFIRE was not the best data source. The Aquatic Key Habitats Subcommittee realized that the data sets chosen to measure extent were not perfect, but the group ultimately moved forward with the data sets shown in 
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 77
sources rather than one place. Condition measures were often only available for some regions of the state or narrowly focused on one or a few components of condition.
One of the most important aspects of condition-the presence and timing of water-is difficult to measure without expensive instrumentation or many repeated field surveys. After two meetings, the Aquatic Key Habitats Subcommittee determined that it would not be practical to develop habitat condition metrics that all partners could collect moving forward. Challenges in doing so included the following:
1. No single data set shows the extent or provides the condition of the key habitats.
Consequently, field data rather than modeled data are needed for estimates of habitat condition.
2. Each UWAP partner has different priorities when working in aquatic systems.
3. Specific projects have different priorities in aquatic systems.
4. UWAP partners do not have the time, staff, or funding to measure an extensive list of habitat condition metrics for every aquatic project, survey, or monitoring event.
5. Condition data do not exist at a statewide level. Good condition data are available for some of the aquatic key habitats. For example, Utah Division of Water Quality (DWQ) collects and reports on data related to water quality, and the UGS collects data on departure from natural wetland condition. However, these data are not available statewide or do not include all of the important aspects of condition, or both, which makes them challenging to use.
These habitat condition discussions highlighted that many UWAP partners measure aspects of aquatic habitat condition that are often not well known or shared outside of their agency or organization. The Aquatic Key Habitats Subcommittee provided the following recommendations for measuring the condition of UWAP key aquatic habitats:
1. Make existing aquatic habitat condition data readily available (see the Aquatic Key Habitat Descriptions section for additional resources for specific aquatic habitats).
2. All UWAP partners should use the Springs App to continue determining the extent and condition for the Springs Key Habitat.

## 3.

All UWAP partners that evaluate potential barriers to fish and aquatic SGCN movement should use the DWR's Barrier Assessment Inventory Tool (BAIT) (DWR 2024c). Using this Survey123 app will standardize the assessment of and information collected on potential barriers to aquatic SGCN movement.
4. More aquatic temperature data need to be collected, especially continuous data through temperature loggers. All Utah aquatic habitat temperature data should be uploaded into the Utah Continuous Water Quality Monitoring Dashboard (DWQ 2025).
5. UWAP partner discussions should continue to identify meaningful aquatic habitat condition metrics and standardize collection of these metrics (or a subset of metrics) by aquatic habitat to the extent possible.

Bumble bee species (such as the western bumble bee and high country bumble bee) are cold-adapted, generalist foragers that can carry larger pollen loads over long distances. These species exhibit high visitation rates and are especially crucial for ensuring reproductive success and gene flow among flowering alpine plant populations.
Often, small areas of wind-affected and stunted Engelmann spruce and subalpine fir can occur near or at timberline, where harsh alpine conditions do not allow for normal tree growth forms.

## Ecoregional Characteristics

Alpine Key Habitat occurs in three Utah ecoregions (making up more than 1% of the area of each ecoregion): Central Basin and Range, Southern Rockies, and Wasatch and Uinta
Mountains. Descriptions of alpine vegetation communities in these ecoregions are below.

## Central Basin and Range

Approximately 

## Wasatch and Uinta Mountains

Approximately 96% of alpine habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. True alpine ecosystems in the Wasatch and Uinta Mountains ecoregion include areas in the High Uinta
Mountains and smaller areas in the upper Cottonwood Canyons and the Tushar Mountains.
These areas receive more precipitation than the plateaus, mountain valleys, and subalpine zones of this ecoregion.

## Extent

• LANDFIRE (2020a) was used to determine Alpine Key Habitat extent in Utah. In some localized areas, snowmobiling and skiing can compact snow, which results in colder temperatures at the surface and in the root zone, which can reduce plant biodiversity and reduce the subnivean space available for arvicoline rodents that use alpine habitat.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of alpine habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Promoting policies that reduce inappropriate browsing and grazing by domestic livestock and non-native grazers.
• Managing recreation. This is a fragile habitat that will need appropriate and ongoing management. Managers will need to work with recreationalists to allow recreation but still protect this habitat.

## Priority Needs

• Work with partners and land managers to map highly intact alpine habitats at a fine resolution. Work toward the preservation of those mapped habitats to reduce habitat fragmentation and perforation.
• Work with partners to determine a sound method by which to assess alpine habitat condition.
