## Aspen-Conifer

Aspen-conifer habitats (Figure 
5
 and Figure 
6
) support highly biodiverse ecosystems and ecological functions. These habitats include both stable aspen stands (where aspen permanently dominate) and seral-dominant forests (where aspen coexist with conifer species such as white fir, Douglas-fir, Engelmann spruce, and subalpine fir). Typically found at elevations between 2,133 and 3,048 m (7,000 and 10,000 feet), aspen-conifer forests thrive in areas with sufficient moisture and fertile soils that foster a diverse and productive understory 
(DeByle and Winokur 1985)
. Their open canopy structure allows significant sunlight penetration, which promotes a rich ground layer of vegetation that sustains numerous wildlife species.
These habitats also serve as key water sources, supporting creeks, springs, and wetlands that are essential to supporting biodiversity.
Aspen 
(Schimpf and MacMahon 1985)
. Invertebrates in the aspenconifer understory also play a central role in sustaining forest health and diversity. Forest insects perform essential functions such as decomposition of organic matter, seed and spore dispersal, and population control of other species through predation and parasitism.
In meadows within aspen-conifer landscapes, increased sunlight penetration enables a diverse array of plant life and structural habitat. Pollinating insects such as bumble bees, solitary bees, butterflies, moths, flies, and beetles play a critical role in maintaining plant reproduction and ecosystem resilience. These insects also make up the foundation of the food web, serving as an essential food source for a variety of wildlife, including nesting birds, amphibians, reptiles, bats, small mammals, and predatory invertebrates 
(Yang and Gratton 2014)
.
Natural and anthropogenic disturbances help maintain the ecological integrity of aspen-conifer systems. Wildfires, foraging wildlife, bark beetle infestations, avalanches, and sustainable forestry practices promote aspen regeneration by reducing competition from conifers and creating favorable conditions for new growth. Aspen trees are widely recognized as keystone species due to their outsized role in supporting diverse plant and animal communities 
(Berrill et al. 2017;
Edenius et al. 2011;
Kouki et al. 2004;
Stohlgren et al. 1997)
; however, historical undervaluation of aspen in favor of timber production, combined with limited research and gaps in policy, has contributed to their decline 
(Rogers et al. 2020)
.
The distribution of aspen-conifer habitats is influenced by elevation, temperature, and moisture availability. At higher elevations, cold temperatures limit their occurrence, making aspen-conifer habitat more common on warmer, south-facing slopes. Conversely, at lower elevations, aspen is constrained by limited moisture and higher temperatures, leading to its prevalence on cooler, north-facing slopes and in mesic microsites such as seeps and drainages. Where adequate moisture is available, these habitat types can establish across diverse topographies, ranging from gentle to steep slopes and extending onto plateaus within the montane and subalpine zones. Note: HUC-12 polygons containing a very small portion of the total key habitat are not displayed. Custom-weighted ecological departures are calculated for HUC-12 units, binned into five categories (0%-20%, 20%-40%, 40%-60%, 60%-80%, and 80%-100%), and shown as a percentage of the overall degree of departure from archetypal conditions. 

## Central Basin and Range

Approximately 1.5% of aspen-conifer habitat in Utah occurs within the Central Basin and Range ecoregion. This collective 28,000 acres occurs mostly in the higher elevations of the Oquirrh, Stansbury, and Deep Creek Mountains, where moisture and cooler temperatures are more prevalent. Conifer species such as Engelmann spruce, bristlecone pine, limber pine, and Douglas-fir are common conifer components to aspen-conifer vegetation communities in this ecoregion.

## Colorado Plateau

Approximately 7% of aspen-conifer habitat in Utah occurs within the Colorado Plateau ecoregion. Aspen-conifer habitat within this ecoregion occurs at elevations that receive more precipitation in locations that support mixed aspen-conifer forests with ponderosa pine, Douglas-fir, quaking aspen, and Engelmann spruce. In Utah, the Tavaputs Plateau is a representative aspen-conifer forest in the Colorado Plateau.

## Southern Rockies

Approximately 3% of aspen-conifer habitat in Utah occurs within the Southern Rockies ecoregion. This habitat type is found in the La Sal and Abajo Mountains, where cool and moist conditions prevail in the higher elevations above the surrounding Colorado Plateau. Relatively large, intact aspen stands occur on all aspects of the La Sal Mountains, whereas stands in the Abajo Mountains are generally more interspersed with conifers. Subalpine fir, Engelmann spruce, and Douglas-fir are frequently the most common conifer species in aspen-conifer communities in this ecoregion. The understory of aspen-conifer habitat in this region has strong similarities to those in Colorado, where most of the Southern Rockies ecoregion is located.

## Wasatch and Uinta Mountains

Approximately 88% of aspen-conifer habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion, which hosts the largest area of aspen-conifer communities in the state-from the northern Bear River
Mountains to the Markagunt Plateau in the south. The varied topography, geology, and soils create aspen-conifer ecosystems that vary widely in both forest structure and diversity. This vegetation community often occupies higher elevations than aspen-conifer communities in other ecoregions (approximately 2,400-3,000 m [8,000-10,000 feet]) in subalpine forest areas and Douglas-fir forests, as well as aspen parkland communities with white fir and limber pine 
(Woods et al. 2001)
.

## Extent

• LANDFIRE (2020a) was used to determine Aspen-Conifer Key Habitat extent in Utah.
• Total current mapped area:
o 1,916,531 acres o 3.53% of state surface area (see Figure 
5
) o 7,756 km 2 (2,995 square miles) Absence and/or reduction of wildfire events has also decreased health and vigor of aspen stands because more shade-tolerant conifers are better able to germinate and grow in the understory of aspen. Aspen are considered a mid-seral species, and over time aspen stands are naturally replaced by conifers. Although this is considered a natural process, fire suppression has resulted in conifers completely overtaking many aspen stands in Utah. Given the importance of aspen to wildlife, aspen stand decadence has led to a decrease in the cover and diversity of this important habitat type.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 91
Recently, large wildfires in Utah have burned thousands of acres of aspen-conifer habitat, and secondary succession following fires has reset vegetative communities to grasses and forbs with regenerating aspen communities. Where wildfires burned under high-severity conditions, some aspen clones may have been lost, but in general, extensive aspen regeneration is expected. It will likely take many (100 or more) years for aspen-conifer habitat to re-establish, but the seral grass/forb and aspen habitat types are still high quality and important for wildlife use.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of aspen-conifer habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Increase disturbance from either prescribed or natural fire, particularly in aspen stands with a large component of conifer. Recent studies 
(Krasnow and Stephens 2015)
 have
shown that larger-scale burns (e.g., 5,000 acres or more) that burn more intensely have been the most successful in terms of aspen regeneration. Higher-intensity burns stimulate greater numbers of young aspen per unit area than lower-intensity burns. A larger treatment area distributes the pressure of browsing by ungulates, which allows most young aspen stems to survive to maturity and helps avoid over-browsing of aspen suckers.
• Apply mechanical disturbance agents such as timber harvest to stimulate aspen regeneration and avoid or reduce conifer invasion, reduce fuel loading, and increase conifer diversity to help reduce the risk of wood-boring beetle infestations (e.g., bark beetles).
• Monitor smaller, naturally occurring or human-created disturbances for ungulate damage, and take follow-up actions such as additional habitat work, fencing, hazing, hunting, and/or domestic grazing management. This may be required to prevent or reduce damage caused by domestic, wild, or feral ungulates. Use of larger treatment areas (for timber harvests or prescribed fire) can help disperse the pressure of ungulate browsing.
• Promote policies that reduce improper and intensive browsing and grazing by domestic livestock. Livestock distribution methods that prevent livestock from concentrating in small areas include using alternative water sources (such as stock tanks), salting, fencing, and virtual fencing.
• Reintroduce beaver where appropriate and install beaver dam analogs (BDAs) where beaver introduction is not feasible.

Table 
10
 summarizes the threats to aspen-conifer habitat identified in a statewide assessment (described in more detail in Chapter 4, Threats, Data Gaps, and Conservation Actions). Yellow rows indicate UWAP priority threats that negatively impact many species and habitats, including aspen-conifer habitat. White rows indicate threats that are important to aspen-conifer habitat but do not impact as many species and habitats as priority threats. Chapter 4 focuses exclusively on UWAP priority threats. 

## Priority Needs

• Work with partners and land managers to map and preserve intact aspen-conifer stands to reduce habitat fragmentation and perforation.
• Create a priority map overlaying aspen and aspen-conifer stands, fire history, and important watersheds to help determine priorities for forest management, prescribed fire, or other restoration work (e.g., mowing or brush hogging).
• Use mechanical treatments where prescribed fire risks are too high, given high fuel loading and proximity to residential areas.

## Desert Grassland

Desert grassland habitat (Figure 
7
 and Figure 
8
) typically occurs in relatively small, discontinuous patches that form transitional zones between lower-elevation deserts and higherelevation shrubland and woodland habitats. Typically, these areas are found on alluvial flats between narrow desert mountain ranges, playas, mesas, and plains. Desert grassland habitat occurs at elevations between 1,067 and 1,829 m (3,500 and 6,000 feet) and is the hottest, driest, and sunniest grassland in North America. This habitat receives, on average, between 15 and 41 cm (6 and 16 inches) of precipitation annually. Although considered a grassland, desert grasslands may have limited vegetation and may also include shrubs, succulents, and forbs.
Dominant shrub species that may occur as patches within a mosaic of grasslands include Wyoming big sagebrush, saltbush species, blackbrush, Mormon tea species, rabbitbrush species, broom snakeweed, and winterfat. 
(Hunt et al. 1987;
Martínez-Falcón et al. 2023)

## Wasatch and Uinta Mountains

Approximately 1.3% of desert grassland habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Species in this vegetation community vary according to elevation, soils, precipitation, and temperature. Big sagebrush is commonly found along the drier foothills, whereas perennial bunchgrasses and mixed forbs can be found at the middle elevations 
(Grahame and Sisk 2002)
. Much of this region's grassland communities have been converted to dryland or irrigated agricultural lands.

## Wyoming Basin

Approximately 4.3% of desert grassland habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Desert grassland communities occur in the rain shadow of high mountains on hilly terrain in this ecoregion. In the Wyoming Basin and in the northern portions of the basin and range areas, desert grasslands are typically dominated by cool-season grasses such as bluegrasses and wheatgrasses. Wyoming big sagebrush communities commonly co-occur with desert grassland habitat in a patchwork mosaic.

## Extent

• LANDFIRE (2020a) was used to determine Desert Grassland Key Habitat extent in Utah. Off-highway vehicle (OHV) recreation in these areas can also be high because the low-growing vegetation makes off-road travel relatively easy. Repeated compaction and disturbance from this activity can negatively affect vegetation vigor and cover and facilitate the introduction and spread of other non-native vegetation such as annual grasses or invasive forbs.
Changes in precipitation amount and timing, as well as temperature highs and lows (particularly higher overnight lows), can have variable effects on desert grassland habitat; however, negative effects such as increasing invasive species, decreased plant cover, increased fire return intervals, and potential flooding with higher-intensity storms are more likely.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of desert grassland habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Prioritize use of native grasses in restoration or rehabilitation projects.
• Promote and encourage policies that reduce inappropriate grazing by domestic livestock.
• Develop and deploy techniques to diversify species composition in monoculture or nearmonoculture stands of seeded non-native plants (e.g., crested wheatgrass).
• Continue the use of appropriate methods for reducing the spread and dominance of invasive weeds and annual grasses, including early detection and rapid response (EDRR) programs.
• Continue the development of native plant materials suited to this habitat.
• Enhance the prevention and suppression of ignitions in areas that have burned once, which often retain a component of recovering native vegetation and are good candidates for otherwise passive restoration.
• Support fire suppression during periods of extreme drought when damage to perennial grasses is likely. 

## Threats

## Priority Needs

• Work with partners and land managers to map highly intact desert grassland habitats at a fine resolution and work toward the preservation of those mapped habitats to reduce habitat fragmentation and perforation.
• Work with partners to determine a sound method by which to accurately assess desert grassland habitat condition repeatedly through time.
• Support aggressive fire suppression during drought periods, when fire damage to perennial grasses is most likely.

## Desert Shrub

Desert shrub habitat (Figure 
9
 and Figure 
10
) includes arid communities where shrubs provide a dominant characteristic, but grass and forb cover can also be notably dominant. This key habitat occupies mid-to low elevations between 853 and 2,133 m (2,800 and 7,000 feet) on Note: HUC-12 polygons containing a very small portion of the total key habitat are not displayed. ## Ecoregional Characteristics

## Wasatch and Uinta Mountains

Approximately 3.5% of desert shrub habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Desert shrub habitat in this ecoregion occupies portions of the semiarid foothill and lower-elevation valleys.
Vegetation communities in the Wasatch and Uinta Mountains ecoregion often support basin big sagebrush with greasewood, rabbitbrush species, and broom snakeweed 
(Brooks 2012)
.
Understories are often impacted by annual grasses (e.g., cheatgrass) and agricultural cultivars such as crested wheatgrass.

## Extent

• LANDFIRE (2020a) was used to determine Desert Shrub Key Habitat extent in Utah.
• Total current mapped area: Plant and wildlife species occurring in these habitats exist in very harsh environments. Given this, periods of drought conditions or back-to-back dry years can result in a loss of perennial vegetation cover and a decrease in wildlife abundance. Even the hardiest plants need water, and long periods of drought can quickly reduce plant abundance. Most annual species can persist through a drought (as seeds), but perennial species' recovery from droughts can be prolonged.
Locally, energy development (e.g., oil and gas, renewables) and associated infrastructure, such as roads, pads, power lines, and pipelines, can impact these habitats. Given the difficult growing conditions, revegetation of disturbed areas can take many years, and establishment of nonnative vegetation can be problematic.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of desert shrub habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Create and maintain fuel breaks to prevent wildfire from reaching unburned acres, particularly in Great Basin communities where plant cover is higher and annual grass cover is problematic. In habitat still dominated by native plant communities, enhancing the prevention and suppression of ignitions in these unburned areas should be prioritized.
• Continue the search for herbicidal agents and biological controls to use against invasive annual grasses.
• Work with federal land management agencies (e.g., the BLM and the USFS) on OHV management; engage with user groups to help better manage OHV areas to reduce wind and precipitation erosion and habitat impacts.

## Lower Montane Mixed Conifer

Lower montane mixed conifer habitat (Figure 
11
 and Figure 
12
) includes communities occupying warmer, lower montane sites with ponderosa pine, pinyon pine, and juniper dominating the overstory at elevations between 1,188 and 2,438 m (3,900 and 8,000 feet).
Although this habitat type is dominated by pinyon-juniper woodlands, it can also include notable areas of ponderosa pine woodland types. Douglas-fir, oak brush (i.e., Gambel oak), and aspen can also occur in this habitat type, but Douglas-fir and aspen are usually only present on steeper and higher-elevation, north-facing slopes. Foothills, mesas, gentle-to-steep slopes, plateaus, valleys, and ridges all may support lower montane mixed conifer habitat. In southern areas, summer monsoons generally start in July and continue through early fall, contributing substantial moisture. Snowfall in some areas also provides important moisture, which is especially important for understory forb communities. This is one of the more widespread vegetation communities in Utah.
Pinyon pine (i.e., two-needle pinyon) and Utah juniper often dominate the tree canopy in this community type. On the extreme west side of the state and into the Stansbury, Onaqui, and Sheeprock Mountains (on the Uinta-Wasatch-Cache National Forest), single-leaf pinyon can be more common than two-needle pinyon. In many areas where pinyon-juniper woodlands occur, vegetation can be dominated by somewhat shorter (less than 3.0 m [9.8 feet] tall) pinyon-juniper woodlands, creating large areas with relatively closed-canopy conditions. In more closedcanopy conditions, understory shrubs can be very infrequent but can occur in more open stands or in larger openings within stands. Associated understory shrubs can include mountain mahogany; curl-leaf mountain mahogany; and a variety of big sagebrush species, including black sagebrush, Wyoming big sagebrush, and basin big sagebrush. Other common shrubs include yellow rabbitbrush, sticky-leaf rabbitbrush, blackbrush, cliffrose, sticky laurel, and antelope bitterbrush.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 111
Ponderosa pine forests and woodlands occur at higher elevations and in areas that receive more consistent moisture during the growing season. In Utah, monotypic stands of ponderosa pine are infrequent, but ponderosa pine can be an important forest type, especially in areas such as the Abajo Mountains, Henry Mountains, Aquarius Plateau, Escalante Mountains, Markagunt Plateau, and Kolob Terrace.
The understory can be quite sparse in more closed-canopy conditions but nonetheless can often be dominated by annual invasive grasses such as cheatgrass and Japanese brome. When a native understory component does occur, grass species include needle and thread grass, sheep fescue, Great Basin wildrye, blue grama grass, James' galleta grass, bluebunch wheatgrass, Sandberg bluegrass, or muttongrass.
Understory forb species can be an important habitat component, and most species bloom in the spring to take advantage of post-snowmelt soil moisture. Common understory forbs include Indian paintbrush, a variety of prickly pear species, phlox species, buckwheat species, and manyflowered stoneseed.
Complex insect communities associated with lower montane mixed conifer habitat play a multitude of roles that profoundly influence ecosystem function and resilience. Foliar insect communities, including herbivorous insects such as caterpillars, aphids, and leaf and bark beetles, directly affect tree and shrub health by feeding on leaves, needles, and stems. Although herbivory can sometimes stress vegetation (especially in conjunction with drought conditions), moderate levels often stimulate plant defenses, promote greater genetic diversity, and influence community structure and successional dynamics. Insect herbivores also serve as an essential food source for higher trophic levels, supporting a wide range of insectivorous birds, mammals, reptiles, and other arthropods.
Ground-dwelling arthropods, including beetles, ants, millipedes, and decomposer species such as isopods, hover flies, and springtails, play critical roles in nutrient cycling and soil formation.
These organisms break down humus (horizons of accumulated plant material) and other organic matter on the forest floor, accelerating decomposition processes that return nutrients to the soil. Their activity enhances soil structure, promotes aeration, increases water infiltration, and supports the establishment and health of both conifer and broadleaf vegetation.
Pollinating insects-such as bees, flies, butterflies, moths, beetles, and wasps-contribute significantly to the reproduction and genetic diversity of many understory plants and some flowering trees within these woodlands. Successful pollination enhances plant community richness and resilience, which in turn supports broader food webs and habitat complexity. By linking these multiple pathways-herbivory, decomposition, and pollination-complex insect communities are fundamental to maintaining the diversity, productivity, and long-term stability of lower montane mixed conifer habitat.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 112

## Central Basin and Range

Approximately 21.2% of lower montane mixed conifer habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Lower montane mixed conifer habitat in this ecoregion is in rocky and rugged terrain and generally does not form large monocultures but is interspersed with sagebrush communities. At lower elevations it can transition into more xeric shrublands (e.g., greasewood or saltbush shrublands). Lower montane mixed conifer habitat is positioned between the drier, lower salt deserts and saline-sagebrush basins and the higher, wetter oak brush shrublands or aspen-conifer habitat.

## Colorado Plateau

Approximately 56.1% of lower montane mixed conifer habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Lower montane mixed conifer habitat occupies the northern slopes of the Uinta Basin as well as portions of escarpments surrounding the Tavaputs Plateau, the Abajo Mountains, the Book Cliffs, and Grand Staircase National Monument. High numbers of perennial streams and outwash deposits set the lower montane mixed conifer habitat in this ecoregion apart from the basin floor, sand-shale deserts, and canyonlands occurring on the Colorado Plateau. Climatic events such as snowfall, frosts, and droughts are believed to limit the distribution of pinyon-juniper woodlands to altitudinal belts on mountainsides. Ponderosa pine forests and woodlands are common in this ecoregion.

## Northern Basin and Range

Approximately 1.2% of lower montane mixed conifer habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion on mountain hills, slopes, and alluvial fans. Pinyon pine is generally less common as it reaches the northern extent of its range, and understory grasses become more common.
Lower montane mixed conifer vegetation in this ecoregion occupies the belt between the highelevation forests and lower sagebrush and salt desert shrublands.

## Wasatch and Uinta Mountains

Approximately 19.7% of lower montane mixed conifer habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Lower montane mixed conifer vegetation occupies the lower montane zones of this ecoregion, including lower portions of the high plateaus and the semiarid foothills. Juniper and pinyon are common, although a maple-oak scrub matrix is increasingly common toward the northern parts of the state.

## Condition

Lower montane mixed conifer stands can vary in condition depending on site specificity. In many places they are becoming denser, with relatively uniform closed canopies, shorterstatured trees, and decadent or poor-quality ponderosa pine forests covering large areas. This is due to a number of factors, including a complex relationship between livestock grazing (which in the past has reduced the cover of many understory grasses and forbs and has favored pinyon-juniper seedling establishment) and modified wildfire return intervals. In ponderosa pine forests, areas inaccessible to mechanical harvesting (i.e., logging) or infrequent wildfire or prescribed fire events have resulted in decadent stand types of stunted or smaller pole-sized ponderosa pine, often with pinyon pine, juniper, Douglas-fir, or aspen co-occurring. Historically, grasses and forbs would have carried wildfire through woodlands, creating a more diverse mosaic of this habitat type. Now the uniform, closed-canopy stands are often dominated by annual grass understories in pinyon-juniper woodlands and support high-severity wildfires that can dramatically shift this habitat type to an early seral grassland (often dominated by invasive annuals) or a shrub-scrub habitat type.
Additionally, due to a combination of stand overstocking and more frequent and severe drought events, lower montane mixed conifer habitat is often affected by population outbreaks of bark beetles and other native forest insects. Pinyon pine, mountain pine, and Douglas-fir-along with, less commonly, Utah juniper-can experience significant stress and tree mortality associated with insects such as ips beetles and mountain pine beetles. For example, pine sawflies have recently affected pinyon pines in the Uinta Basin. Although healthy pinyon pines can often recover from sawfly outbreaks, dense, overstocked stands can leave trees more vulnerable to damage, leading to increased mortality from sawfly larvae.
Reintroducing wildfire or prescribed fire to both improve habitat and reduce fuel loading is becoming increasingly difficult due to many stands having high dead fuel loading and closedcanopy conditions. Even with lower-severity prescribed fires, annual grasses can quickly take advantage of improved growing conditions and further decrease native forb, grass, and
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 116
understory shrub cover and diversity. In some areas, long-term and/or severe short-term drought conditions can directly or indirectly cause pinyon pine mortality, mostly due to insect (e.g., bark beetles) outbreaks. These events seem to impact pinyon pine more than junipers.
Recent Utah Department of Natural Resources Watershed Restoration Initiative work to reduce pinyon-juniper cover has reduced the risks of high-severity wildfire across thousands of acres.
Pinyon-juniper management efforts can substantially reduce the risks of high-severity wildfire that inevitably negatively impact other habitats, usually due to post-fire annual grass (and other noxious weed) infestations and erosion and debris flow events. Pinyon-juniper management efforts have focused on the encroachment of pinyon-juniper into sagebrush systems. More can be learned about best management practices for mature pinyon-juniper stands that continue to infill, resulting in closed canopies, less productivity for understory species, and greater fire risk.
Other threats to lower montane mixed conifer habitat include oil and gas exploration and development (which is generally limited to existing, known fields). Impacts associated with oil and gas development include permanent impacts from roads and pad sites as well as shortterm and indirect impacts from pipeline construction and the spread of noxious weeds.
Feral horses can be problematic for understory species cover and diversity in some areas of Utah. Management of wild horses is primarily under the authority of the BLM, and wild horse management is extremely difficult and highly controversial.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of lower montane mixed conifer habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Use a variety of mechanical treatments for closed-canopy woodlands to reduce fuel loading and create canopies that are more open. Lower montane mixed conifer stand management often thins or uproots and kills trees. A variety of mechanical treatments, including chaining and mastication, will be an important tool to reduce fuel loading and create more open canopies. These treatments have been shown to improve habitat for sage-grouse species.
• Support mastication (or chipping/grinding) of dense trees or commercial harvest for forest products (e.g., fence timbers, firewood) to reduce closed-canopy conditions. Once fuel breaks and lower fuel loading are established, lower-severity prescribed fire can help improve stand health and improve understory plant cover and diversity. These treatments can be expensive, and invasive annual grasses will always be a threat; timely and aggressive treatment of annual grasses should accompany any stand management.
• Reduce the risks of wildfire. Although lower montane mixed conifer habitat does not commonly support perennial waters, intermittent tributaries flow through this habitat toward important downstream habitats. High-severity wildfire in this community type can increase the risk of post-fire flood and debris flows, which will negatively impact downstream water supplies and mesic wildlife habitats.
• Continue the use of appropriate methods to reduce the spread and dominance of invasive weeds, including EDRR programs.
• Continue the search for herbicidal agents and biological controls to use against invasive annual grasses.
• Continue the search for effective plant restoration materials and methods that can be affordably translated from the research plot scale to the operational project scale.
• Continue the development of new plant materials, especially native forbs, for understory habitat restoration or post-fire revegetation.
• Promote management that includes seeding a diversity of grasses, forbs, and shrubs that will lead to increased resiliency and resistance in the plant community.
• Promote and fund restoration that reduces monotypic or overstocked stand conditions, including cutting or mulching of pinyon and juniper trees and herbicide or mechanical treatment of non-native invasive species.
• Continue monitoring conditions of pinyon-juniper woodlands that have been thinned or treated to reduce fuel loading.

## Priority Needs

• Work with partners and land managers to map highly intact lower montane mixed conifer habitats at a fine resolution and work toward the preservation of those mapped habitats to reduce habitat fragmentation and perforation.
• Work with partners to determine a sound method by which to accurately assess lower montane mixed conifer habitat condition repeatedly through time.
• Prioritize treatments by understanding where juniper invasion of other habitats (such as sagebrush habitats) is occurring and differentiate legacy stands or stands that have oldgrowth characteristics that are desirable to conserve.
• Work with land managers to help fund fuel loading reduction projects.

## Lowland Sagebrush

Lowland sagebrush habitat (Figure 
13
 and Figure 
14
) encompasses semiarid sagebrush
communities, including open shrublands, steppe, rocky hillslopes, canyons, and ridges. These habitats are commonly found on level benches and in basins, often in proximity to lower montane mixed conifer forests. At lower elevations, they may transition into or overlap with salt desert shrub, desert shrub, and desert grassland communities. At mid-elevations, lowland sagebrush habitats often intermingle with pinyon-juniper woodlands, which may extend to higher elevations. Toward the upper elevational range, they can also intergrade with mountain sagebrush, mountain shrublands, and montane forests dominated by mixed conifer and aspen.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 120
The typical elevational range for lowland sagebrush habitat spans approximately 792 to 1,828 m (2,600-6,000 feet).
Dominant species include basin big sagebrush and/or Wyoming big sagebrush, and on more well-drained sites (with coarser soils), black sagebrush and low sagebrush can occur. Other shrubs can include saltbush species, Mormon tea species, spiny hopsage, bud sagebrush, and horsebrush species. Rubber rabbitbrush and broom snakeweed are common on drier sites and sticky-leaf rabbitbrush and antelope bitterbrush are more common on wetter sites.
The herbaceous layer is often sparse (less than 25% of vegetation cover) and composed of perennial grasses such as Indian ricegrass, blue grama grass, bottlebrush squirreltail, thickspike wheatgrass, sheep fescue, needle and thread grass, Great Basin wildrye, and James' galleta grass. At higher elevations and in wetter sites, western wheatgrass, Sandberg bluegrass, or bluebunch wheatgrass are common.
Given the lower elevations of this habitat, forbs are more seasonal. Blooming and reproduction occur in the early spring, and then most forbs quickly desiccate and do not make up much of the understory later in the season. Drier conditions make this system less resistant and resilient to disturbance such as invasion by annual grasses. Invasive forbs such as halogeton can be widespread. In areas where past mowing or chaining was followed by the planting of crested wheatgrass, understories may now be heavily dominated by crested wheatgrass.
Insect communities within lowland sagebrush ecosystems vary across both temporal and spatial scales, with many species closely associated with specific plant hosts and environmental conditions such as warmer temperatures and adequate humidity. Notably diverse insect groups include gall-forming species that rely on sagebrush and other shrub hosts, as well as a variety of herbivorous, predatory, and pollinating insects 
(Spawton and Wetzel 2015)
. Pollinator communities are particularly diverse and include numerous species of bees, butterflies and moths, beetles, and flies. Importantly, non-bee pollinators contribute valuable pollination services in sagebrush habitats, especially during the hottest periods of the year when bee activity may decline 
(Rohde and Pilliod 2021)
. These insect populations also form a critical component of the food web, serving as a primary food source for many bird speciesparticularly during the nesting season when protein demands are high 
(BLM 2004)
. Additionally, insect and arthropod diversity in sagebrush habitats is shaped by ecological interactions such as moderate grazing by ungulates like mule deer and pronghorn. These grazing patterns can influence plant structure and composition, thereby affecting insect habitat and food availability.
Decomposer insects-such as flies, wasps, and carrion beetles-also contribute to ecosystem function by breaking down carcasses, recycling nutrients, and supporting secondary food webs 
(Torma et al. 2023)
.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 121

## Central Basin and Range

Approximately 56.2% of lowland sagebrush habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion.
Lowland sagebrush sites are dominated by basin big sagebrush and Wyoming big sagebrush, but perennial bunchgrasses do occur and become increasingly common in more northerly areas as available moisture increases. The sagebrush basins and slopes include valleys, alluvial fans, and mountain flanks that are not as saline nor as arid as salt deserts or saline basins. This habitat is generally less rocky, rugged, and wooded than the woodland and shrub-covered low mountains and is not as level as the salt deserts or saline basins. Invasion and conversion to annual grasslands following wildfire events is highly common, and thousands of acres are now effectively converted to annual grasslands.

## Colorado Plateau

Approximately 30.4% of lowland sagebrush habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion.
Lowland sagebrush sites are generally below 1,798 m (5,900 feet) on broad, shrub-covered benches and mesas. Dominant species include Wyoming big sagebrush and basin big sagebrush in swales and depressions. Understory grasses are similar to previous descriptions.
Forbs often include globemallow, cacti, longleaf phlox, spiny phlox, death camas, stone crop, Indian paintbrush, stoneseed, and western sticktight. Annual grasses and crested wheatgrass can also be very common.

## Northern Basin and Range

Approximately 2.6% of lowland sagebrush habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Lowland sagebrush habitat in this ecoregion occurs as sagebrush steppe and occupies gentle slopes of unforested terraces, basin rims, valley bottoms, and alluvial fans, primarily south of the Raft River Mountains. Much of the lowland sagebrush habitat in this area has been converted to annual grasslands (by wildfire and passive invasion) as well as dryland and irrigated agricultural fields.

## Wasatch and Uinta Mountains

Approximately 6.7% of lowland sagebrush habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion and can be found in both the semiarid foothill and mountain valley subregions. Vegetation associates can be variable and may include mountain brush species (e.g., pinyon pine, Utah juniper, Gambel oak) or lower and drier species, such as basin big sagebrush.

## Wyoming Basin

Approximately 2.6% of lowland sagebrush habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Lowland sagebrush habitat occurs in an elevational band below mountain sagebrush communities, generally abutting agricultural fields. This habitat mostly occurs on the lower ends of hillslopes along the Monte Cristo and Wasatch ranges. These sites are typically windy and contain shallow, rocky soils that support mostly short shrubs (less than 30 cm [approximately 12 inches] tall). Cool-season grasses and invasive annual grasses are common.

## Extent

• LANDFIRE (2020a) was used to determine Lowland Sagebrush Key Habitat extent in Utah.
• Total current mapped area: 
o 6,

## Condition

In general, lowland sagebrush habitats are in moderate to poor condition, primarily due to invasion by annual grasses, historic livestock grazing, pinyon-juniper encroachment, and repeated drought events. With annual grass invasion (either passive or post-wildfire), control is difficult and retreatment may be necessary. Annual grass can also reduce recruitment of sagebrush. Repeated wildfire events, particularly in the Great Basin ecoregion, have effectively converted thousands of acres of lowland sagebrush habitat to annual grasslands.
To improve livestock grazing and combat erosion, thousands of acres of lowland sagebrush habitat were historically seeded with crested wheatgrass and other introduced grasses.
Although this perennial grass is palatable to livestock and some wildlife species, it often outcompetes native forbs and perennial grasses. This competitive displacement reduces habitat quality for several nongame wildlife species, as well as for local endemics such as greater sage-grouse and Gunnison sage-grouse, which rely heavily on native forbs and the associated insect community for forage. The resulting decline in plant diversity has led to cascading ecological effects, including reduced ecosystem productivity, resilience, and overall species richness in many sagebrush systems (U.S. Department of the Interior 2017).
Lowland sagebrush communities are particularly vulnerable to drought, because many occur at the lower, drier end of the elevational range for typical sagebrush-associated species, including forbs and perennial grasses. Prolonged drought can cause die-offs of these native species, leading to sparse understories that are more prone to invasion by annual grasses and noxious weeds. In severe cases, sagebrush itself may experience widespread mortality. Drought also increases the frequency and intensity of wildfire, which can further degrade these habitats by converting them to annual grasslands.
In the context of these climatic stressors, livestock grazing can exacerbate declines in perennial forbs and grasses. This further diminishes available forage and cover for wildlife, particularly for wintering big game species such as elk, mule deer, and pronghorn, as well as for sage-grouse species that depend on diverse native understories.
Additional habitat pressures arise from energy development. In areas such as the Uinta Basin and eastern Utah, oil and gas activities-including road and well pad construction and temporary pipeline disturbances-directly impact sagebrush habitat. Given ongoing drought conditions, these disturbances often require extended periods for successful revegetation or reclamation and are frequently complicated by the spread of invasive annual grasses and other noxious weeds.
Renewable energy development, particularly utility-scale solar projects, also poses a threat to lowland sagebrush ecosystems. These projects can result in the direct conversion of large habitat areas to renewable energy development, rendering them unsuitable for native wildlife and reducing overall ecological functionality of the landscape.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of lowland sagebrush habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Support federal and state land managers in aggressively suppressing wildfires in lowland sagebrush habitat. If wildfires do occur, ensure that revegetation seed mixes are appropriate, will not create additional issues, and contain appropriate sagebrush seed.
• Use effective fuel breaks to help protect intact sagebrush areas.
• Continue with pinyon-juniper encroachment mitigation, where appropriate. Early treatment when trees are small is more cost effective and causes less disturbance.
• Work with federal and state land managers to appropriately site oil and gas and renewable projects outside more intact or effective lowland sagebrush habitat.

White rows indicate threats that are important to lowland sagebrush habitat but do not impact as many other species and habitats as the priority threats. ## Priority Needs

• Work with partners and land managers to map highly intact lowland sagebrush habitat at a fine resolution and work toward the preservation of those mapped habitats to reduce habitat fragmentation and perforation.
• Work with partners to determine a sound method by which to accurately assess lowland sagebrush habitat condition repeatedly through time.
• Manage pinyon-juniper encroachment.

• Support research and management of invasion by annual grasses.

## Mojave Desert Shrub

Mojave desert shrub habitat (Figure 
15
 and Figure 
16
) occupies the transitional zone between the Sonoran Desert to the south and the cooler Great Basin Desert to the north. This key habitat
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 128
is characterized by lower elevations, hotter temperatures, and drier conditions compared to other desert regions in Utah. Vegetation is often sparse, with large expanses of barren ground, and plant community composition varies based on elevation, aspect, and soil type.
Dominant vegetation includes creosote bush, burrobush, blackbrush, and, in Utah's southwestern corner, western Joshua tree. Succulents such as species of prickly pear, cholla, and California barrel cactus are especially common on slopes. Other prevalent species include saltbush species and broom snakeweed.
This habitat occurs at elevations between 670 and 1,371 m (2,200 and 4,500 feet) and across a range of landforms, including basins, washes, bajadas, alluvial fans, valleys, rocky canyons, rocky outcrops, and transitional zones between desert lowlands slopes and evergreen shrublands. At lower elevations, creosote bush and burrobush dominate, and blackbrush and turbinella oak become more prominent at higher elevations. Joshua trees are localized to Utah's southwestern boundary. Annual precipitation is highly variable, with summer monsoon rains playing a key role in the emergence of annual forbs. Compared to Great Basin sagebrush communities, Mojave shrublands are more drought-tolerant but less so than pure saltbush-or creosote bush-dominated systems. Adjacent vegetation includes sagebrush shrublands at upper elevations and creosote bush or saltbush-greasewood communities at lower elevations and in more alkaline soils.
Insect diversity in the Mojave desert shrub habitat is closely tied to vegetation composition and shaped by extreme climatic conditions. Many pollinators exhibit specialized relationships with specific plant species. For example, certain bees collect pollen, resin, or plant materials to construct nests and rear offspring. Moth and butterfly larvae, as well as some beetles and gallforming insects, rely on particular host plants to complete their life cycles.
Several plant genera serve as keystone resources, supporting a high number of specialist and generalist insect species. These include rabbitbrush, sagebrush, creosote bush, dogbane, biscuitroot, desert marigold, buckwheat, milkweed, yucca, penstemon, primrose, thistle, fleabane, snakeweed, gumweed, jimsonweed, prince's plume, scorpionweed, locoweed, trefoil, lupine, and globemallow. Notably, the decaying trunks of Joshua trees provide essential nesting for carpenter bees and several rare bee species. The pronuba moth, an obligate pollinator of yucca, is critical for yucca reproduction, with its larvae developing within the flowers it pollinates. Many other plant species characterize Mojave desert shrub habitat, including Nevada Mormon tea, desert almond, winterfat, and redberry desert-thorn, and the herbaceous layer may be composed of big galleta, bush muhly, false fluffgrass, broom spurge, three-awn grass, cat's eye, and other perennial grass species.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 129
Pollination services are provided by a wide array of insects, including bees, butterflies, moths, flies, beetles, and wasps, which together support the rich plant diversity of Mojave desert shrub habitat. Herbivorous insects-such as aphids, grasshoppers, crickets, camel crickets, cicadas, and various beetles-as well as detritivores like millipedes, form an essential food base for a variety of desert wildlife. Predatory and parasitic insects and arthropods, including wasps, flies, cuckoo bees, scorpions, tarantulas, mites, and beetles (including glowworm fireflies), contribute to population regulation and nutrient cycling.
The complex and often ephemeral relationships between insect species and native plants drive ecosystem processes and define the structure and function of Mojave desert shrub communities. These interactions are critical to the resilience and biodiversity of this arid and uniquely adapted ecosystem.

## Central Basin and Range

Approximately 23.5% of Mojave desert shrub habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. In this ecoregion, Mojave desert shrub habitat is slightly cooler in the summer and driven more by winter precipitation than its namesake desert farther south. These areas typically contain more salt-tolerant species, such as winterfat, and often have a slightly more herbaceous understory that includes grasses such as Indian ricegrass, James' galleta grass, and Sandberg bluegrass.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 132

## Mojave Basin and Range

Approximately 74.5% of Mojave desert shrub habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion.
Mojave desert shrub vegetation communities in this ecoregion can be expansive, continuous, and occupy a full range of topography, including basins, foot slopes, and lower woodlands.
Vegetation varies with elevation, with creosote bush and burrobush dominating lower elevations; blackbrush, cholla, and perennial grasses dominating middle elevations; and a strong presence of turbinella oak and pinyon-juniper woodlands in the highest Mojave desert shrub habitat in this ecoregion.

## Extent

• LANDFIRE (2020a) was used to determine Mojave Desert Shrub Key Habitat extent in Utah.
• Total current mapped area: Mojave desert shrub habitat has experienced a rapid increase in annual grasses (e.g., red brome, cheatgrass, common Mediterranean grass). These annual grasses provide continuous fuel for wildfire in this ecosystem, which is not adapted to wildfire, much less wildfire with the high frequency and intensity that has been occurring in the last few decades.
The combination of frequent wildfires, drought, and increasing temperature has decreased the resilience of this habitat. Additionally, restoration efforts are particularly difficult and resource intensive in the harsh conditions of the Mojave Desert.
Long-term, low-density livestock grazing has likely reduced the cover of perennial grasses and some more palatable forb species in this habitat.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of Mojave desert shrub habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Prevent and suppress ignitions in unburned areas.
• Promote zoning, policies, and laws that lead to responsible human intrusion and development.
• Promote policies that reduce inappropriate grazing by domestic livestock.
• Continue to research herbicides and biological controls that may be used against invasive annual grasses. 

## Threats

Priority Needs
• Work with partners and land managers to map highly intact Mojave desert shrub habitats at a fine resolution and work toward the preservation of those mapped habitats to reduce habitat fragmentation and perforation.
• Work with partners to determine a sound method by which to accurately assess Mojave desert shrub habitat condition repeatedly through time.
• Prevent ignitions wherever possible in this habitat type.

## Montane and Subalpine Conifer

Montane and subalpine conifer habitat (Figure 
17
 and Figure 
18
) includes spruces, firs, and pines that have adapted to cold winters and warm to cool summers. These communities generally require cool, relatively humid climates and heavy snow cover 
(Woods et al. 2001)
.
Soils differ for this key habitat but can range from generally shallow, coarse, and rocky soils that originated from sedimentary and metamorphic rocks to limestone or dolomitic substrates in montane to subalpine ridges of the intermountain basins where limber and bristlecone pines can dominate. Annual precipitation in this habitat area varies from 45 to 101 cm (18-40 inches), and elevation ranges between 1,829 and 3,353 m (6,000 and 11,000 feet). Perennial streams located in this habitat provide water to the lower, more arid ecoregions 
(Woods et al. 2001
).
The montane vegetation zone is lower than the subalpine vegetation zone. It typically ranges in elevation from 1,829 to 2,896 m (6,000-9,500 feet) and is dominated by tree species such as Douglas-fir, white fir, and ponderosa pine (National Park Service 2014). Dominant tree species within the higher elevations of this habitat are generally found between 2,682 and 2,896 m (8,800 and 9,500 feet) in elevation and include coniferous species such as subalpine fir, Engelmann spruce, blue spruce, bristlecone pine, limber pine, and lodgepole pine (Utah Division of Forestry, Fire and State Lands 2020 [FFSL]; 
Woods et al. 2001)
. Bands of white fir and clustered stands of quaking aspen are also often found distributed throughout this habitat (FFSL 2020).
The composition of the species overstory of this key habitat is influenced by climate, topography, land use history, fire history, and insect activities (National Park Service 2014). The understory may be abundant and diverse to almost absent depending upon these biotic and abiotic features. Common understory species may include huckleberry, common juniper, snowberry, twinberry honeysuckle, elderflower, kinnikinnick, and western prickly gooseberry.
Common herbaceous species may include species with wide-ranging moisture needs, including elk sedge, heartleaf arnica, sweet cicely, spiny milkvetch, and prairie junegrass.
Many flowering plants at higher elevations have evolved generalized relationships with subalpine pollinator communities, exhibiting traits that attract and reward a wide variety of pollinators. Subalpine meadows and wetlands may also support greater local plant species diversity within areas that provide unique microclimates and favor species adapted to those conditions. Animal pollinators in subalpine environments-including bees (especially bumble bees), flower flies, butterflies, moths, wasps, beetles, and hummingbirds-have adaptations such as thermoregulation for cooler temperatures, shorter flight times, and host plant specialization. These plant-pollinator communities are subject to rapid species turnover during the relatively short growing season.
Diverse insect activity in montane and subalpine conifer habitats serves as an ecological indicator of ecosystem health. The presence and abundance of certain herbivorous terrestrial insect taxa-such as moths, beetles, hemipterans, and grasshoppers-can reflect vegetation diversity and may also predict the species richness of insectivorous predators, particularly nesting birds 
(Bujoczek et al. 2020;
Dar and Jamal 2021)
. Monitoring these ecological indicators can help assess the magnitude of stress and degree of exposure to threats such as drought, habitat loss, and disturbances.

## Ecoregional Characteristics

The Montane and Subalpine Conifer Key Habitat is found within four ecoregions in Utah, each covering more than 1% of the respective region: the Central Basin and Range, Colorado Plateau, Southern Rockies, and Wasatch and Uinta Mountains. A description of this key habitat within the Colorado Plateau ecoregion was not included due to the small extent of the Colorado Plateau ecoregion in Utah. Below is an overview of the vegetation communities characteristic of these ecoregions.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 139

## Central Basin and Range

This ecoregion contains approximately 2.8% of Utah's montane and subalpine conifer habitat.
These forested areas are typically isolated, occurring at higher elevations where conditions are wetter than the surrounding lowlands. The region's rugged terrain supports vegetation such as subalpine fir, Engelmann spruce, bristlecone pine, Douglas-fir, mountain big sagebrush, and aspen. The most extensive forest stands may be found on upper, north-facing slopes, where moisture retention is higher 
(Woods et al. 2001)
.

## Southern Rockies

Montane and subalpine conifer habitat covers approximately 1.2% of this ecoregion in Utah, primarily in the La Sal and Abajo Mountains. Subalpine forests, dominated by Engelmann spruce and subalpine fir, typically occur at elevations between 2,682 and 3,200 m (8,800 and 10,500 feet). These high-altitude forests experience cooler temperatures and substantial snow cover.
At mid-elevations 
(2,286-2,743 m [7,500-9
,000 feet]), Douglas-fir, ponderosa pine, and Gambel oak become more prevalent. Lower-elevation forest stands in this ecoregion often have a dense understory of shrubs, including snowberry, Wood's rose, and thimbleberry, along with a diverse array of perennial forbs such as larkspur, rayless coneflower, American vetch, Engelmann aster, showy fleabane, penstemon, and skunk cabbage.

## Wasatch and Uinta Mountains

This ecoregion contains the vast majority (approximately 81%) of Utah's montane and subalpine conifer habitat. These forests span a wide range of elevations, from subalpine forests 
(3,048-3,353 m [10,000-11,000 feet]
) to coniferous forests 
(2,438-3,048 m [8,000-10,000 feet]
), encompassing the montane zones of the Wasatch Mountains and portions of high plateaus 
(2,438-3,353 m [8,000-11,000 feet]
). Common tree species include Douglas-fir, Engelmann spruce, lodgepole pine, subalpine fir, ponderosa pine, and aspen. The mid-elevation areas of the Uinta Mountains feature deep canyons with high-quality perennial streams, primarily fed by mountain snowmelt 
(Woods et al. 2001)
.
In recent years, large wildfires have burned extensive areas within this ecoregion, resulting in significant aspen regeneration. The understory vegetation in these regenerating forests is similar to that found in other montane and subalpine areas and provides important ecological functions and habitat diversity.

## Extent

## Condition

In general, this habitat type is in moderate to good condition, though certain areas present concerns. The majority of these forests are managed by the USFS. Primary anthropogenic impacts include logging (where terrain allows) and extensive livestock grazing. In some regions, mining has caused concentrated environmental disturbances. Although ski area development is localized, it has notable effects, both directly through the clearing of ski runs and indirectly through nearly year-round recreational use, which can disrupt wildlife and habitat integrity.
Historically, logging was widespread in these forests, as evidenced by remnant stumps and younger tree stands. Today, old-growth forests are largely confined to steep, less accessible slopes and designated wilderness areas. Although logging remains a management tool, it is employed far less frequently than in the past due to environmental regulations and reduced industry capacity.
Decades of fire suppression have contributed to unnaturally high fuel loads in many montane and subalpine conifer forests, increasing their vulnerability to high-severity wildfires. Recent, prolonged drought conditions and rising temperatures have also contributed to widespread
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 141
susceptibility of conifers to outbreaks of (native and introduced) bark beetles and other forest pests-including mountain pine beetle, fir engraver beetle, spruce budworm, Douglas-fir beetle, and spruce beetle-leading to a dramatic increase in dead and dying trees. Consequently, many areas face an elevated risk of severe wildfires.
Over the past decade, several large wildfires have burned through Utah's montane and subalpine conifer forests, converting them into early seral forb and grass communities. In some areas, aspen has resprouted extensively following fire disturbance; however, high-severity wildfires can generate intense heat pulses that alter soil profiles, creating hydrophobic conditions that inhibit natural revegetation. This, in turn, increases post-fire runoff and erosion, leading to significant downstream impacts to stream and riparian systems, sometimes many miles from the burn scar.
According to the Terrestrial Ecological Classifications description in NatureServe ( 
2018
), bristlecone pine and limber pine are experiencing widespread outbreaks of mountain pine beetle populations across much of their range. Under normal conditions, winter temperatures kill up to 98% of the beetle population; however, rising winter and summer temperatures are disrupting the beetle's life cycle, facilitating rapid population growth and range expansion 
(Carroll et al. 2003)
. This, combined with prolonged and more frequent severe droughts, fire suppression, and harvesting practices that have resulted in increased stands of aging pines favored by the beetle, has made conifer forests across western North America increasingly vulnerable to infestation.
Western dwarf mistletoe is capable of parasitizing multiple species of conifers. Although it rarely kills trees, it can stunt growth, cause limb damage, increase fuel loads, and significantly reduce the trees' suitability for timber production. Herbivores like deer, elk, squirrels, and porcupines, however, along with specialized insects such as thicket hairstreaks and certain moth species, can help control its excessive growth on host conifers.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of montane and subalpine conifer habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Promote forest diversity rather than solely managing for timber production. Both species richness and structural diversity are essential for building long-term resilience. Many forests in Utah face a heightened risk of high-severity wildfires due to forest pest outbreaks and the resulting accumulation of dead and weakened trees, making wildfires an inevitable factor in montane and subalpine conifer ecosystem dynamics.
• Reduce the dominance of overstocked, homogenous forest stands to help mitigate the severity and extent of wildfires while fostering a more balanced and ecologically functional landscape.
• Implement selective logging in areas where it is appropriate, alongside efforts to protect old-growth stands and improve overall forest health.
o Logging has its own challenges because many forests affected by pest outbreaks are in areas too steep for mechanical treatment such as salvage logging or are located within designated wilderness areas where active management is restricted.
o Using prescribed fire, a valuable tool for forest health, is particularly challenging in montane and subalpine conifer environments due to narrow windows of suitable weather and fuel conditions, as well as the intensity of fires, even when planned.
o Logging can alter forest structure and composition, potentially leading to longterm ecological impacts such as fragmented habitat from logging roads and skid trails, increased erosion, loss of stream shading, disruption of coarse woody debris in aquatic ecosystems, and other edge effects.
o Logging and forest management practices of today are more environmentally sensitive than past approaches. The most significant long-term consequence remains a reduction in old-growth forest conditions.
• Create mosaics of age classes and fuel breaks to limit the spread of high-severity wildfires. This is a key approach to improving forest resilience. Although species such as aspen benefit from fire in the long term, many wildlife and plant species depend on mature and old-growth forests. Large areas dominated by early seral stages can negatively impact forest-dependent species by reducing habitat availability and continuity.
• Manage invasive plant species and recreational impacts.
o Non-native species such as smooth brome, Kentucky bluegrass, orchard grass, and dandelion can spread widely, outcompeting native grasses and forbs.
o Effects of forest pest outbreaks on tree health can be intensified by warmer and drier conditions caused by changing climate.
o Recreation, especially in ski areas and through the increasing use of OHVs, can contribute to habitat degradation. Many older, decommissioned logging roads are now frequently used by OHVs, leading to localized erosion, sedimentation in streams, and noise disturbances that reduce the productivity of otherwise intact wildlife habitats (through noise and human activity disturbances).

## Priority Needs

• Work with partners and land managers to map and preserve highly intact montane and subalpine conifer stands; reduce habitat fragmentation and perforation.
• Work with partners to determine a sound method by which to accurately assess montane and subalpine conifer habitat condition repeatedly through time.
• Create a priority map overlaying montane and subalpine conifer stands, fire history, and important watersheds to help determine priorities for forest management, prescribed fire, or other restoration work (e.g., mowing or brush hogging).
• Work with federal land managers to encourage forest management that reduces fuel loading and creates mosaics of different age classes to reduce the risk of widespread wildfires converting large areas to early seral conditions all at once.

## Mountain Sagebrush

Mountain sagebrush habitat (Figure 
19
 and Figure 
20
) consists of communities where sagebrush species are dominant in mountainous steppe regions. This key habitat is typically found in semiarid areas, occurring between lowland sagebrush habitats and montane or subalpine aspen-conifer forests. It ranges in elevation from 1,829 to over 2,890 m (6,000 to over 9,480 feet), and is widely distributed across Utah; however, its composition varies significantly depending upon latitude, elevation, aspect, and soil conditions.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 145
This habitat can be dominated by several sagebrush species, including mountain big sagebrush, black sagebrush, and snowfield big sagebrush. In some areas, bitterbrush, mountain snowberry, and yellow rabbitbrush may co-dominate or even become the dominant vegetation, depending on elevation and aspect. Mountain sagebrush habitats with long winters and substantial snowfall tend to support a more diverse forb and grass understory. In southern regions, summer monsoonal moisture extends forb and grass production, often leading to abundant herbaceous cover. A well-developed perennial herbaceous layer is common in many areas.
Dominant forbs in this habitat include longleaf phlox, small-leaf pussytoes, woolly locoweed, hedgehog cactus, daisy species, potato cactus, and Indian paintbrush. At higher elevations, more mesic forbs become prevalent. 

## Central Basin and Range

Approximately 13.6% of mountain sagebrush habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion.
Mountain sagebrush habitat occurs on mountain flanks at mid-elevations. Wyoming big sagebrush and mountain big sagebrush dominate the shrub layer, and perennial bunchgrasses are common, especially in the most northern regions and at higher elevations where precipitation and moisture are higher (NatureServe 2018). Grass cover is chiefly cool-season grasses, but invasive annual grasses are increasing, especially at lower elevations. Non-native species such as smooth brome, orchard grass, dandelion, and other species are not necessarily noxious weeds, but they can be dominant in some areas, outcompeting native grasses and forbs.

## Colorado Plateau

Approximately 16.6% of mountain sagebrush habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion.
Mountain sagebrush habitat occurs on higher slopes and is generally not found in the sagebrush basins (those areas are typed as lowland sagebrush habitat) but can occur higher on slopes and on more north-facing slopes. This vegetation community is often invaded by pinyonjuniper trees and can also see locally problematic invasion by annual grasses (e.g., cheatgrass) at lower elevations. Understory grasses include typical species such as mutton grass, Sandberg bluegrass, and bluebunch wheatgrass, with blue grama grass at lower elevations. Understory forbs are typical sagebrush species such as Indian paintbrush, small-leaf pussytoes, various ball cacti, potato cactus and prickly pear species, mat penstemon, and others. 

## Northern Basin and Range

## Wyoming Basin

Approximately 7.2% of this habitat in Utah occurs in this ecoregion. In the Wyoming Basin ecoregion, this habitat occurs at higher elevations on hills, mesas, ridges, and outwash fans, primarily on the western slopes of the Monte Cristo and Wasatch ranges. Repeated grazing and fires have increased the presence of annual grasses in this ecoregion 
(Woods et al. 2001)
.
Mountain sagebrush communities integrate with aspen stands on these mountainsides.

## Condition

This habitat type ranges widely in condition, from poor to good, depending upon fire history, land uses, and elevation. The encroachment of both pinyon-juniper and annual grasses into this habitat have reduced habitat quality for many sagebrush obligate species. With the higher moisture levels typically occurring in mountain sagebrush habitats, native grasses and forbs can occupy much of the understory space, which makes it difficult for annual grasses to invade these areas without accompanying disturbance. In some areas of the state, pinyon-juniper encroachment is problematic for both decreasing the overall cover and quality of this habitat type and increasing the risk of higher-severity wildfire. With severe fire regime alteration and extended fire suppression, trees, especially junipers and pinyons, have colonized these shrublands and have gradually excluded the shade-intolerant sagebrush (La Puma 2023).
Pinyon-juniper encroachment can also reduce habitat effectiveness for several sagebrush obligates-most notably, greater sage-grouse and Gunnison sage-grouse). In the past, mowing or spraying (with herbicides such as Tebuthiuron) were common methods of reducing sagebrush cover and increasing grass production to benefit livestock grazing. Although these practices are no longer used as often as they once were, sagebrush mowing (or tilling) on private lands still occurs and can impact large areas. Additionally, seeding with non-native grasses (such as smooth brome or crested wheatgrass) to improve livestock grazing can also negatively impact this habitat and result in lower native forb and grass cover in the understory.
In some areas, residential development can convert many acres of suitable habitat to an unsuitable condition, resulting in direct habitat loss.

## Improving Condition

Primary strategies for management of mountain sagebrush habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Manage pinyon-juniper. Early treatment when pinyon-juniper seedlings are small is more cost effective, has less visual impact, and creates less woody debris.
• Continue to research methods to improve sagebrush habitat 
(Doherty et al. 2022
).
• Continue to work with federal land managers to protect larger and healthier stands of mountain sagebrush habitat. This can include identifying the locations of these habitats and driving potential impacts to less intact habitats (e.g., siting renewable or oil and gas projects away from more intact habitats).
• Work with local governments to identify larger and healthier stands of mountain sagebrush habitat to help avoid development in these habitats.
• Identify new noxious weed infestations and treatments.
• Use prescribed fire with caution. Although prescribed fire can be an effective tool for creating various age classes and helping to reinvigorate decadent stands, the risks of weed invasion and long-term loss of sagebrush cover must be carefully considered.

## Mountain Shrub

Mountain shrub habitat (Figure 
21
 and Figure 
22
) includes vegetation communities that occur Wildfire plays an important role in this habitat and contributes to the unique composition and variety of species. In some cases, fires can cause a severe die-back of the existing shrub cover, or conversely, can enable aggressive resprouting of a specific species, such as Gambel oak (NatureServe 2018).
Insect communities in mountain shrublands are comparable in species richness and functional roles to those found in mountain sagebrush habitats; however, variation in dominant plant species, annual precipitation and temperature patterns, and the frequency and intensity of disturbance regimes all influence the composition, abundance, and ecological interactions of these communities. Although the active growing season in mountain shrublands is shorter than in lower-elevation habitats, the spring emergence of vegetation creates a seasonal expansion of habitat resources. This phenological window allows many insect species already active at lower elevations to disperse into mountain shrublands, enabling them to exploit newly available resources and reduce interspecific competition. Furthermore, many shrub species serve as keystone species that support a wide range of specialist insects-particularly oak, willow, boxelder, chokecherry, sumac, wild rose, mountain mahogany, and conifer species-making them vital to supporting insectivorous wildlife.
Pollinator assemblages in these habitats include pollen generalist species-such as bumble bees, solitary bees, flies, and beetles-as well as herbivorous host plant specialists such as moths and butterflies that visit flowers for nectar. Additional floral visitors include ants, wasps, and various predators that may inadvertently transfer pollen between flowers. Beyond pollinators, a wide array of insect taxa-including flies, beetles, wasps, ants, hemipterans, grasshoppers, dragonflies, and others-contribute significantly to animal diversity and ecosystem processes. These insects function as herbivores, predators, parasites, and scavengers, as well as a key portion of the food resources for other wildlife, collectively enhancing the productivity, resilience, and ecological integrity in mountain shrub ecosystems.

Custom-weighted ecological departures are calculated for HUC-12 units, binned into five categories (0%-20%, 20%-40%, 40%-60%, 60%-80%, and 80%-100%), and shown as a percentage of the overall degree of departure from archetypal conditions. Approximately 16.1% of mountain shrub habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Mountain shrub habitats generally integrate with pinyon-juniper woodlands and sagebrush shrublands, with mountain shrub occurring at higher elevations and in moister habitats (such as on northfacing slopes). Mountain shrub habitats often grade into aspen habitats at their upper extents.
Shrubs such as bitterbrush and western serviceberry serve as important browse for mule deer in this ecoregion 
(Woods et al. 2001)
.

## Colorado Plateau

Approximately 20% of mountain shrub habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Landscape features such as the semiarid benchlands, canyonlands, and escarpments all contain portions of mountain shrub habitat. Gambel oak, Utah serviceberry, sagebrush species, snowberry species, and bitterbrush are relatively dominant in mountain shrub habitat in this ecoregion.

## Northern Basin and Range

Approximately 1.3% of mountain shrub habitat in Utah occurs within the Northern Basin and Range ecoregion and is generally found on wetter or higher-elevation slopes of the Raft River and Grouse Creek Mountains. Mountain shrub habitat is bounded on lower elevations by sagebrush and juniper woodlands and at upper elevations by aspen.

## Wasatch and Uinta Mountains

Approximately 59.5% of mountain shrub habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion and supports a wide variety of shrub species (see the extensive list at the beginning of the Mountain Shrub section). Maple-oak scrub is the most common mountain shrub habitat type at the northern extent of this ecoregion and is slowly replaced by pinyon-juniper woodlands farther south and downslope 
(Woods et al. 2001)
.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of mountain shrub habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Enforce a vegetation management plan aimed at protecting curl-leaf mountain mahogany with grazing-specific measures. Native ungulates highly favor curl-leaf mountain mahogany for winter range, which has ultimately led to excessive browsing and reduced regeneration as seedlings try to become established (NatureServe 2018).
• Promote policies and management that allow fire to return to a more natural regime. • Continue funding and support for weed abatement programs, including EDRR programs.

White rows indicate threats that are important to mountain shrub habitat but do not impact as many species and habitats as the priority threats.
Chapter 4 focuses exclusively on UWAP priority threats.
