## Priority Needs

• Work with partners and land managers to map and preserve highly intact mountain shrub habitats to reduce habitat fragmentation and perforation.
• Work with partners to determine a sound method by which to accurately assess mountain shrub habitat condition repeatedly through time.
• Produce a fine-scale, accurate map of remaining mountain mahogany stands in good condition to be able to collectively preserve good quality mountain shrub habitat.
• Implement fire policies that encourage a more natural fire regime to assist in the enhancement of mountain shrub habitat in Utah.
• Manage pinyon-juniper. Early treatment when pinyon-juniper seedlings are small is more cost effective, has less visual impact, and creates less woody debris.

## Riparian

Riparian habitats (Figure 
23
 and Figure 
24
) are ecologically distinct in Utah, spanning from alpine environments above the tree line to the hottest lowland regions. These areas occur along streambanks, rivers, flood zones, lake margins, seeps, spring, alluvial terraces, hillslopes, canyons, and valleys. Despite the diversity in elevation, vegetation, and climate, the defining feature of riparian habitats is the consistent presence of water.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 163
Vegetation varies greatly across this gradient, from alpine forbs and stunted willows to tall Fremont cottonwood trees in desert canyons. In more mesic, lowland areas, riparian vegetation includes netleaf hackberry, velvet ash, desert willow, coyote willow, and skunkbush sumac.
Riparian shrubs also include dogwood, golden currant, silverleaf buffaloberry, and Wood's rose (Salt Lake County 2003). Riparian vegetation often originates in adjacent uplands and may tolerate dry conditions for part of the year, whereas wetland vegetation requires prolonged soil saturation and often includes obligate wetland species that cannot survive outside these conditions. For the purposes of habitat classification, riparian systems are categorized as terrestrial key habitats, with extent and condition evaluated using LANDFIRE data sets (LANDFIRE 2020a, 2020b) (see the
Terrestrial Key Habitats section of this chapter and Appendix D).
Due to the critical importance of water for agriculture, a significant proportion of riparian habitat is privately owned. Water development and diversion projects for irrigation are common and can alter natural stream flows and ecological function. In some areas, however, these modifications may create expanded wetland-like conditions through flood irrigation and increased soil saturation.
Riparian zones are ecological hotspots that support a wide range of wildlife and plant diversity.
In Utah's arid environment, lowland riparian areas are among the rarest and most biologically rich habitat types. These areas provide food, water, shelter, and connectivity for species movement.
Riparian habitat is essential to the majority of wildlife species in Utah, serving as critical habitat for both riparian obligate species and numerous upland species that rely extensively on these
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 164
areas for various life history needs. Bird diversity in riparian areas is notably high, with observed bird counts approximately twice those of neighboring uplands 
(Norvell et al. 2005)
. Additionally, many large mammals, fish, and amphibians make extensive use of riparian corridors. Streams bordered by well-developed riparian vegetation support higher populations of fish and amphibians, and many amphibian species rely on adjacent riparian zones such as those surrounding streams, sloughs, and other saturated areas for foraging.
Streamside vegetation contributes to lower water temperatures through shading, stabilizes banks, traps sediment and pollutants, and increases instream habitat complexity with coarse woody debris. These functions improve conditions for aquatic macroinvertebrates, which are essential prey for many vertebrate species.
Riparian systems support a remarkably diverse assemblage of insect and invertebrate species due to their cooler, moister microclimates and high plant diversity. Insect groups include herbivores such as grasshoppers, katydids, planthoppers, leafhoppers, aphids, crickets, beetles, gall-formers, thrips, and moths, many of which specialize in riparian vegetation. Pollinating insects include bumble bees, solitary bees, flies, beetles, moths, butterflies, and nectar-feeding ants and wasps. Aquatic insects-including non-biting midges, mosquitoes, crane flies, mayflies, stoneflies, salmonflies, snakeflies, dobsonflies, caddisflies, dragonflies, and damselflies-develop in benthic substrates and emerge as flying adults to mate and disperse.
Detritivores and decomposers that process leaf litter and organic debris enhance soil fertility and contribute to water quality. Predators and parasitoids, including spiders, fireflies, tiger beetles, and wasps as well as amphibians, reptiles, birds, mammals, and fish rely on these abundant invertebrates for food.
The presence and diversity of aquatic macroinvertebrates are often used to assess stream health and water quality 
(Faulkner 1999)
. Moth diversity has also been suggested as a potential indicator of localized ecosystem health 
(Duran et al. 2022)
. The abundance of insect prey supports a high degree of the wildlife diversity associated with riparian habitats; this availability of prey resources permits niche partitioning among groups such as aerial insectivores (e.g., nocturnal birds and bats) 
(Bullington et al. 2021)
.
Beyond ecological importance, riparian habitats provide significant ecosystem services. These include flood mitigation, water purification, erosion control, and recreation opportunities such as fishing, birdwatching, kayaking, and hiking. The high biodiversity and ecological functions of riparian areas underscore their value for both conservation and sustainable land management. Note: HUC-12 polygons containing a very small portion of the total key habitat are not displayed. Custom-weighted ecological departures are calculated for HUC-12 units, binned into five categories (0%-20%, 20%-40%, 40%-60%, 60%-80%, and 80%-100%), and shown as a percentage of the overall degree of departure from archetypal conditions. 

## Central Basin and Range

Approximately 45.2% of riparian habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Although riparian habitat is found scattered throughout the state, in this ecoregion specifically, it appears most prominent in the Moist Wasatch Front footslopes and the Malad and Cache Valley subregions.
As noted above, the riparian habitat is found along streams, rivers, and lakes. Dominant riparian vegetation generally comprises a variety of species, including trees such as narrow-leaf and Fremont cottonwood, native boxelder, introduced Siberian elm, and native species such as speckled alder and water birch. Dominant shrubs include the noxious weeds Russian olive and tamarisk, as well as native silver sage, spearleaf rabbitbrush, dogwood, and several willow species, of which coyote willow is often the most common.

)

• Distribution of riparian habitat: mapped in 2,347 of 2,885 HUC-12 subwatersheds that overlap Utah. In mid-to lower elevational ranges, riparian habitat in Utah is subject to more indirect impacts through establishment of noxious weeds (primarily Russian olive and tamarisk, but also white top, Russian knapweed, Canada thistle, phragmites, and reed canarygrass). Tamarisk (and
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 169
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 170
Russian olive, to a lesser extent) creates soil conditions unsuitable for cottonwood seedling establishment. In some areas, riparian habitats have been completely changed from cottonwood stands to non-native systems. In mid-elevation zones, establishment of agricultural cultivars can also change habitat conditions, with smooth brome, orchard grass, Kentucky bluegrass, and crested wheatgrass often more dominant than local native plant species. These shifts in vegetation composition reduce the suitability of riparian habitats for big game species but have significant impacts to nongame species groups, including migratory birds, amphibians, and reptiles. In localized areas, primarily where irrigation flows return, where there is runoff from agricultural practices, and below older water treatment plants, elevated nutrient loading in return waters can create eutrophic conditions where species such as cattail can become extremely prolific and dominate riparian habitats.
Common stressors impacting riparian habitat include non-native and invasive plants, water diversions and irrigation return flows, hydrologic alteration, inappropriate grazing practices, and water quality stressors from urban and agricultural sources.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of riparian habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Identify riparian habitat impacted by land use practices and continue to support funding for low-tech, process-based restoration (e.g., BDAs, one-rock dams). The use of these methods will restore Riparian Key Habitat by reconnecting floodplains, reducing soil erosion and controlling sediment, and increasing flood and wildfire resilience.
• Continue to support funding for weed treatment in riparian habitat, including Russian olive and tamarisk eradication. Use of mechanical treatment, herbicides, and prescribed fire are options to reduce non-native plant species; however, treatment must be followed up with management of other weeds, such as Russian knapweed and white top, which can quickly expand in disturbed areas and negate any benefits of initial treatments.
Securing funding and follow-up treatments can ensure that initial efforts are successful.
• Identify old water control structures (including levees) that channeled rivers and separated floodplains from streams. Remove obsolete structures where possible to help reconnect floodplains and re-establish riparian zones.
• Assist in revegetation and restoration of burn scars.
• Promote zoning, policies, and laws that lead to responsible human intrusion and development.
• Promote development of BDAs and post-assisted log structures. 

## Threats

## Priority Needs

• Carry out systematic replacement of non-native riparian vegetation (e.g., tamarisk, nonnative willows, Russian olive) with native riparian vegetation.
• Work with partners to determine key metrics to monitor the condition of Utah's riparian habitat.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 174
• Develop functional flows for Utah's streams and rivers to provide appropriate flow regimes to sustain riparian habitat.

## Salt Desert Shrub

Salt desert shrub habitats (Figure 
25
 and Figure 
26
) occur in highly saline and sometimes gypsiferous soil types primarily derived from decomposed shales and calcareous sandstones.
Soil types play a strong role in the amount of vegetation cover and the types of vegetation found within this habitat. In some areas, salt desert shrublands have less than 5% vegetation cover.
Due to the low vegetation cover, fires in this habitat do not occur as frequently as they do in other habitats throughout Utah. Fires can occur in conjunction with wet years, possibly once every 100 years on average (NatureServe 2018).
These habitats are typically found at elevations of 1,524 to 2,195 m (5,000-7,200 feet) and can occur either in the bottoms of enclosed basins or on alluvial and alkaline flats, mesas, plateaus, washes, bluffs, and badlands. In some areas, shrubs can have more extensive cover, but in other areas, annual grasses and forbs (invasive and native) can be more dominant than shrubs.
Often, salt desert shrub communities can be interspersed within desert shrub and lowland sagebrush vegetation communities. These key habitats receive on average 12.7 to 33.0 cm (5.0-13.0 inches) of precipitation per year.
The soils in this habitat can be both alkaline and saline and experience high erosion rates when on slopes, such as in badlands. Plant communities are composed of species tolerant of these arid and saline environments, including a variety of saltbush species and greasewood. These landscapes can be dominated by dwarf shrublands composed of relatively pure stands of saltbush, especially mat saltbush and Gardner's saltbush. 

## Central Basin and Range

Approximately 46.2% of salt desert shrub habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Salt desert shrub communities in this ecoregion are arid and occur mostly in saline basins, although some overlap does exist with this ecoregion's desert shrub habitats. Vegetation density within salt desert shrub communities of this ecoregion is often sparse but can be moderate, depending on topography and soil salinity levels. Common vegetation includes salt-tolerant plants such as shadscale, winterfat, and greasewood. More mesic and high-salt-tolerant plants, such as pickleweed and saltgrass, may occasionally occur along waterways and in ponded areas. Annual grasses and introduced perennials found in this ecoregion include cheatgrass, red brome, common Mediterranean grass (crested wheatgrass can also become well established within this habitat), and Russian thistle.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 178
Colorado Plateau
Approximately 52.6% of salt desert shrub habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. Salt desert shrub communities in this ecoregion are sparsely vegetated with large areas of dwarf shrublands. Dominant species include mat saltbush, bud sagebrush, James' galleta grass, shadscale, greasewood, winterfat, Indian ricegrass, horsebrush, various mallows, rabbitbrush, and Mormon tea. Depending on precipitation trends, a variety of forbs can be found in this habitat. The noxious weed halogeton is widespread and can often poison livestock. Tamarisk, another noxious weed, is common along waterways and occurs sporadically along dry washes.
Salt desert shrub soils in this ecoregion tend to be heavy in clay, often with significant amounts of salt and gypsum. The Colorado Plateau ecoregion is home to several national parks and large areas of BLM-administered lands, as well as major natural gas and oil developments.

## Extent

• LANDFIRE (2020a) was used to determine Salt Desert Shrub Key Habitat extent in Utah.
• Total current mapped area: 
o 4,
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 179
Halogeton and annual grasses (e.g., cheatgrass) have become established across large areas.
Like native species in this habitat, these annual species can take advantage of wet years and increase in coverage, while having low cover in drought years.
Given the naturally low vegetation cover, intense precipitation events (i.e., thunderstorms) can produce excessive flooding and erosion events. Therefore, the ability of washes and gulches to support more well-developed riparian areas is naturally limited.
Because of the naturally open terrain, it is easy for OHVs to leave designated trails and cross open deserts, which can result in widespread soil and vegetation disturbance and weed dispersion and establishment. This results in even lower plant cover that can locally increase both wind-and precipitation-driven soil erosion.
Both plants and wildlife species occurring in this habitat exist in very harsh environments. Given this, periods of drought conditions or back-to-back dry years can result in a loss of perennial vegetation cover and a decrease in wildlife abundance. Even the hardiest of plants require some water, and longer periods of drought can quickly reduce plant abundance. Most of the annual species can persist through a drought (as seeds), but perennial species' (both plants and wildlife) recovery from droughts can take many years.
Locally, energy development (e.g., oil and gas and renewables) and associated infrastructure such as roads, pads, power lines, and pipelines can impact these habitats. Given the difficult growing conditions, revegetation of disturbed areas can take many years, and establishment of non-native species can be problematic.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of salt desert shrub habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Create and maintain fuel breaks to prevent fire from reaching remaining unburned acres, particularly in Great Basin communities where plant cover is higher and annual grass cover is problematic. Prioritize the enhanced prevention and suppression of ignitions in unburned areas in habitats dominated by native plant communities.
• Continue the search for herbicidal agents and biological controls to use against invasive annual grasses.
• Work with larger federal land management agencies (e.g., the BLM and USFS) on OHV management; engage with user groups to help better manage OHV areas to reduce wind and precipitation erosion and habitat impacts.
• Continue research for effective plant restoration materials and methods that can be affordably translated from the research plot scale to the operational project scale.
• Continue the funding and support for weed abatement programs, including EDRR programs.
• Continue the development of new plant materials and restoration techniques suited to this habitat.
• Promote management that includes seeding a diversity of grasses, forbs, and shrubs that will lead to increased resiliency and resistance in the plant community. 

## Threats

## Priority Needs

• Work with partners and land managers to map highly intact salt desert shrub habitats at a fine resolution and work toward the preservation of those habitats to reduce habitat fragmentation and perforation.
• Work with partners to determine a sound method by which to assess salt desert shrub habitat condition to allow meaningful comparison through time.
• Work with federal land managers to identify high OHV disturbance areas and better manage OHV use to help reduce soil erosion and loss of sparse vegetation cover.

## Subalpine Grassland

Subalpine grassland habitat (Figure 
27
 Insect communities in subalpine grasslands share many traits with those of alpine ecosystems, due to overlapping plant species and similar environmental conditions above and below tree line. These grasslands, often interspersed with clusters of trees in a meadow mosaic, provide structural complexity and plant diversity that support a rich array of insect species-especially pollinators. The brief but intense growing season fosters a high level of insect activity among flowering forbs and shrubs, including numerous species of bumble bees and solitary native bees, flies, wasps, beetles, butterflies, and moths.
The presence of rocky soils and burrowing rodents create diverse nesting habitats for groundnesting insects such as bumble bees. Several rare and declining bumble bee species appear to have found refugia in Utah's subalpine grasslands, where they persist despite broader declines
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 183
across their range due to habitat loss and fragmentation, drought, and rising summer temperatures 
(Janousek et al. 2023)
.
Throughout the season, plant-pollinator interactions shift rapidly due to overlapping species phenologies. These interactions influence floral traits, maintain genetic diversity in plant populations, and shape ecological processes such as herbivory, parasitism, and predation.
Many pollinators occupy multiple ecological roles-not only as pollinators but also as herbivores contributing to nutrient cycling; prey for higher trophic levels; predators and parasitoids regulating invertebrate populations; and decomposers enhancing soil formation, carbon storage, and nutrient availability.
Other insects common to these habitats include many types of flies, ground beetles, grasshoppers, crickets, hemipterans, damselflies, ants, velvet ants, and many other taxa that occupy a multitude of ecological niches. Despite a relatively short growing season, the high species richness and productivity of subalpine grasslands are sustained in large part by the diversity and functional roles of their insect communities. 

## Central Basin and Range

Approximately 16.9% of subalpine grassland habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion.
Subalpine grassland habitat can be found in valleys, on slopes, and on higher-elevation alluvial fans in the Oquirrh, Stansbury, Deep Creek, and Mineral Mountains. Vegetation in this ecoregion is typically a mosaic and varies in species composition depending on elevation and soils; for example, grasslands often intermix with stands of aspen and mixed conifers in the higher elevations, especially on north-facing slopes. Dominant species include spike fescue, bluebunch wheatgrass, and slender wheatgrass.

## Colorado Plateau

Approximately 11.6% of subalpine grassland habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion, primarily on the Tavaputs Plateau. Precipitation in this habitat tends to be higher than other habitat types within the Colorado Plateau ecoregion, and increased moisture from thunderstorms provides favorable conditions for warm-season grasses. In this ecoregion, it is typical to see Wyoming big sagebrush co-occur with grasses. Dominant species can include Thurber's fescue, Sandberg bluegrass, muttongrass, and Kentucky bluegrass.

## Southern Rockies

Approximately 2.4% of subalpine grassland habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion in the La Sal and Abajo Mountains. Subalpine grasslands in this ecoregion are often dominated by bunchgrasses such as timber oatgrass, Parry's oatgrass, fescues, slimstem muhly, Fendler's bluegrass, slender wheatgrass, or bluebunch wheatgrass.

## Northern Basin and Range

Approximately 4.0% of subalpine grassland habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion, chiefly in the Raft River, Goose Creek, and Grouse Creek Mountains. This key habitat is commonly found on the ridgetops and side slopes of mountains with cold winters. In the Dissected High Lava Plateau area of this ecoregion, vegetation mostly consists of bluebunch wheatgrass, Idaho fescue, Wyoming big sagebrush, and low sagebrush. Crested wheatgrass can also be common.

## Wasatch and Uinta Mountains

Approximately 63.6% of subalpine grassland habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion. The Wasatch and Uinta Mountains are characterized by high, glaciated mountains; dissected plateaus; foothills; and intervening valleys 
(Woods et al. 2001
) that create many opportunities for subalpine grasslands to develop. Subalpine grasslands occur in mosaics with forest stands of fir, aspen, and pine. Species often found in this habitat in this ecoregion include pine dropseed, spike fescue, and oatgrasses and less often, Thurber's fescue.

## Wyoming Basin

Approximately 1.5% of subalpine grassland habitat in Utah occurs within this ecoregion.
Subalpine grasslands are found close to the northeastern border of Utah near Flaming Gorge
Reservoir and to the northwest near the town of Randolph, where several waterbodies bisect these habitats (e.g., Little Creek and Big Creek). Subalpine grassland habitat in this ecoregion tends to dominate high hills and low mountains and can also be drier and less continuous than typical subalpine grasslands. Typical species in this habitat include bluebunch wheatgrass, Sandberg bluegrass, and Fendler's bluegrass mixed with mountain big sagebrush. Exotic annual grasses can also be common because of repeated fires and grazing in this part of Utah.

## Condition

Subalpine grasslands are generally in good condition; these habitats are often high enough in elevation and receive enough precipitation to limit the invasion of annual grasses. Past overgrazing in some areas has likely favored coarser grasses that are less palatable to wildlife, such as Thurber's fescue; therefore, some areas likely have limited species diversity. In mesic depressions, some noxious weeds may be of issue (primarily Canada thistle).
Impacts from wildfires are fairly limited except during the most extreme droughts; most grasses only burn at the top, preserving the root crown, and can resprout relatively quickly. During extreme drought, however, root crowns can become very dry, and fires can burn down into the roots and kill the grass completely.
In some areas, concentrated livestock grazing and feral horse grazing can negatively impact community health and, as mentioned, favor coarser and less-palatable grass species (e.g., Thurber's fescue).
Near roadsides and previously disturbed areas, the establishment of smooth brome (which can form near monocultures) has displaced native grasses and forbs.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of subalpine grassland habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Monitor impacts from feral horses and other species (introduced or native) more closely, especially how those impacts are compounded when combined with livestock grazing (which federal managers have generally better supervised in terms of duration and timing).
• Assess natural perennial grass re-establishment after extreme fire events. If perennial grass mortality has occurred, reseeding with local native grasses should occur to prevent excessive erosion and to help reduce the risk of non-native species establishment (e.g., smooth brome, annual grasses).
• Continue the development of new plant materials and restoration techniques suited to this habitat.

## Threats

Table 
21
 summarizes the threats to subalpine grassland habitat identified in a statewide assessment (described in more detail in Chapter 4, Threats, Data Gaps, and Conservation Actions). Yellow rows indicate UWAP priority threats that negatively impact many species and habitats, including subalpine grassland habitat. White rows indicate threats that are important to subalpine grassland habitat but do not impact as many species and habitats as the priority threats. Chapter 4 focuses exclusively on UWAP priority threats. 

## Priority Needs

• Work with partners and land managers to map highly intact subalpine grassland habitats at a fine resolution and work toward the preservation of those mapped habitats to reduce habitat fragmentation and perforation.
• Work with partners to determine a sound method by which to accurately assess subalpine grassland habitat condition to allow meaningful comparison through time.
• Support grazing management to promote a variety of grass species to occupy the different niches within this habitat type.
• Promote any prescribed wildfire management protocols to enhance diversity across the community versus allowing greater occupancy of introduced grasses.

## Other Habitats

## Badlands

In Utah, the term badlands generally refers to areas with sparse or absent vegetation, primarily due to inhospitable soil conditions. These soils are typically derived from marine shale, mudstone, or other highly erodible, clay-rich parent materials that exhibit slow water infiltration and high surface runoff. As a result, vegetation is limited and often composed of locally endemic species that have evolved specialized adaptations to persist under such extreme environmental conditions over millennia. Despite their harsh conditions, Utah's badlands support an exceptional diversity of insect life.
Many beetles, moths, butterflies, grasshoppers, camel crickets, gnats, and flies have adapted to the unique challenges of this environment. Particularly notable is the richness of groundnesting, solitary, pollen-specialist bees, which have evolved brief flight periods that coincide with the flowering of specific host plants 
(Carril et al. 2018
). These highly specialized bees are often closely associated with equally elusive kleptoparasitic species, such as cuckoo bees, wasps, velvet ants, and certain beetles. Their life cycles are tightly linked to seasonal precipitation, which triggers the sequential emergence of host plants and their pollinators.
These complex ecological interactions are further supported by a suite of cryptic predatorssuch as spiders, scorpions, solfugids, centipedes, and other arthropods-that are rarely encountered but play integral roles in badland food webs. Together, these communities illustrate the remarkable biodiversity and ecological specialization present in Utah's badland ecosystems.

## Mesic Meadows

Mesic meadows most commonly contain some sagebrush, lower montane, and subalpine habitats where finely textured soils, snow deposition, highly moist soils, or windswept conditions limit tree establishment. Many occurrences are small patches that are often found in mosaics within woodlands, dense shrublands, or just below alpine communities. Mesic 

## Microbialites

Microbialites are a key feature of the Saline Lakes/Great Salt Lake Ecosystem Key Habitat and occur specifically within the GSL ecosystem. These organo-sedimentary structures can take several forms: thinly layered stromatolites, clotted thrombolites, and spherical oncolites.
Microbialites are formed through precipitation from mineral-rich springs and form reef-like structures that cover an estimated 20% of the GSL bottom, thus representing the most extensive assemblage of living microbialites on Earth.
GSL microbialites are colonized by complex photosynthetic microbial mats, consisting of both cyanobacteria and algae (diatoms) that contribute fixed carbon supporting a diversity of heterotrophic microorganisms also living within these mats. These diverse microbial communities are also thought to be involved in the formation of carbonate minerals, which can lithify and preserve the microbialite structure. Biomass produced by these complex microbial communities supports a variety of more complex organisms, including brine flies, brine shrimp, and recently discovered nematode worms, though the lake's hypersaline environment precludes most aquatic organisms that would browse on stromatolites. The relative lack of aquatic animals in GSL minimizes stirring of sediments from the lakebed that would otherwise blanket stromatolites from sunlight, and the absence of plants and the scarcity of other algae on the lake floor reduce competition for nutrients and space.
The microbialites of GSL's benthic community contribute to the greater lake ecosystem as an essential support system for brine fly offspring. Brine flies lay eggs on the rough texture of submerged microbialite substrates, where larvae attach and graze on the mat of photosynthetic microorganisms until they can safely pupate and detach into the water column. Brine flies represent key food sources for birds, including an estimated 10 million birds that visit GSL annually 
(Wurtsbaugh 2009)
. This number includes both shorebirds and waterbirds that dive and feed on brine flies and brine shrimp. Brine flies are considered a keystone species of GSL because of their outsized role as a vital food source for many bird species and other insectivorous predators that depend on the lake ecosystem as an important feeding ground.
Changes in the water level, salinity, and biology of GSL have been shown to impact the aquatic community, particularly with respect to microbialite communities and effects on brine flies.
Increases in lake elevation have historically been accompanied by decrease in salinity, shifting phytoplankton communities dominated by halophilic microorganisms to those dominated by phototrophic microorganisms. Decreases in lake elevation accompany increased salinity levels, which result in a decreased abundance of phytoplankton, increased metabolic stress to brine fly larvae due to higher osmoregulation, and potential exposure and desiccation of microbialite communities. 
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 196

## Sand Dunes

Sand dunes are typically small, localized landforms where both stabilized and actively shifting sand dominate the substrate. These environments are characterized by intense heat, aridity, and persistent winds and generally support little to no vegetation due to these harsh conditions.
Localized pockets of higher soil moisture may occur, however, influenced by underlying geology and the presence of springs.
Despite the arid setting, the rapid infiltration of precipitation into dune sands reduces surface evaporation, creating relatively mesic microenvironments. This retained soil moisture contributes to the stability of dune systems and supports a surprising array of biodiversity, particularly below the surface. Ground-nesting insects, burrowing rodents, and reptiles are commonly found in these habitats, benefiting from the moisture and shelter the dunes provide.
Vegetation in sand dune habitats is typically sparse but specialized. Plant species are adapted to survive extreme conditions such as high temperatures, prolonged drought, and the physical challenges of sand burial. Adaptations include rapid stem elongation, radical growth, and, in the case of the federally listed Welsh's milkweed, extensive rhizome networks that both anchor the plant and help stabilize the dune itself. Characteristic vegetation includes drought-tolerant grasses, forbs, and low shrubs.
Variations in sand grain size and texture, along with fluctuating moisture availability, create microhabitat heterogeneity across dune landscapes. Differences in vegetation between dune crests and swales reflect these underlying physical and hydrological gradients, contributing to a mosaic of ecological niches.
Dune systems are often isolated from each other, forming ecological "habitat islands" that support distinct and endemic species 
(Epps et al. 2002;
Ricketts et al. 1999)
. Due to this isolation, many organisms within dunes-particularly insects-have limited dispersal capabilities and are highly adapted to the unique conditions of their local environment. Consequently, they are vulnerable to disturbances that alter dune dynamics or ecological function.
Sand dunes harbor a wide variety of sand-obligate insects, including darkling beetles, tiger beetles, camel crickets, flies, velvet ants, and diverse pollinator assemblages-moths, wasps, butterflies, and bees-often closely associated with the available plant species. These invertebrate communities fluctuate with the seasons, forming ephemeral biological communities during periods of moderate temperatures and increased moisture availability, particularly in spring and autumn. These seasonal peaks in productivity correspond with heightened activity among both invertebrate and vertebrate wildlife.
An example of a dune-restricted and conservation-dependent species is the endemic Coral Pink Sand Dunes tiger beetle in southern Utah, which relies on a combination of vegetated swales with sufficient soil stability and shifting, unvegetated dune areas for different stages of its life cycle. The species' survival depends on the continued availability of invertebrate prey and the maintenance of its specialized habitat 
(Knisley and Hill 2001)
. Aquatic Key Habitat Descriptions

## Lakes and Reservoirs

Lakes and reservoirs habitat (Figure 
34
 Larger natural lakes occupy topographic depressions, such as waterbodies in subalpine cirques, montane lakes, larger oxbow waterbodies, and some spring-fed waterbodies. Reservoirs are typically human-made impoundments and can occur in river and stream channels or on natural lakes impounded by dams; however, off-channel reservoirs have also been created. Reservoir size and shape are determined by topography and range from expansive reservoirs on rivers (e.g., Lake Powell, which has a storage capacity of more than 24,322,000 acre-feet) to larger livestock waterbodies or urban stormwater basins (e.g., those with a storage capacity of less than 5,000 acre-feet). Smaller reservoirs typically serve local communities, providing water for irrigation, recreation, and wildlife habitat, whereas large reservoirs can store substantial amounts of water for purposes such as agriculture, municipal supply, and hydropower generation. Larger human-made impoundments in waterfowl management areas outside saline lakes habitat are also included in this key habitat, however, the NHDPlus and NWI data sets may classify these as either lakes or waterbodies. 

## Ecoregion Characteristics

Lakes and Reservoirs Key Habitat occurs across Utah in all seven ecoregions. Lakes and reservoirs are important because they provide habitat and water for SGCN in dry landscapes across Utah. The Central Basin and Range ecoregion contains two of Utah's largest natural lakes, both of which are the remnants of prehistoric Lake Bonneville: Utah Lake (freshwater) and GSL (saline). The Colorado Plateau ecoregion contains Lake Powell, the largest reservoir in Utah.

## Extent

• Lakes and reservoirs were classified as Open Water Key Habitat in the 2015 UWAP.
• The NHDPlus layer was used to determine habitat extent for lakes and reservoirs habitat in Utah.
• Lakes and reservoirs include perennial bodies of standing water, including natural lakes, reservoirs, and waterbodies greater than 20 acres. In general, waterbodies less than 20 acres in size are classified as wetlands because the NWI captures these habitats better than NHDPlus; however, there is some overlap between these two habitats.
• In general, the extent of reservoirs fluctuates seasonally between wet and dry precipitation cycles and depending on water management, whereas these have less of an impact to the extent of natural lakes (e.g., Bear Lake).
Most remote subalpine lakes and waterbodies are minimally altered by human activities.
Lower-elevation lakes, waterbodies, and reservoirs vary in condition. Of the lakes, waterbodies, and reservoirs monitored by the Utah Department of Environmental Quality, 47% do not support one or more beneficial uses (e.g., the desired uses that the waterbody should support), primarily due to metal pollutants, dissolved oxygen, and nutrient levels that lead to eutrophication and sedimentation. Lakes and reservoirs also can experience harmful algal blooms, with 11 lakes and reservoirs currently listed as impaired due to harmful algal blooms or eutrophication (DWQ 2022).
In general, water quality in lakes and reservoirs is impacted by upstream human land use and water quantity.
The relative condition of most of Utah's smaller lakes is currently unknown; however, some habitat metrics are collected by agencies (see Table 
7
 in the Aquatic Key Habitats section of this chapter).

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of lakes and reservoirs habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Promote policies that maintain conservation pools in lakes and reservoirs.
• Promote policies that limit nutrient input into lakes and reservoirs.
• Promote policies that reduce grazing practices by domestic livestock, feral horses, and wildlife that negatively impact lakes and reservoirs.
• Promote policies that minimize or eliminate the placement of roads in riparian zones.
The condition metrics above are mainly measures of water quality or physical lake conditions, but invasive species can impact lake condition as well. Important strategies to manage invasive species may include the following:
• Continue the use of methods for reducing the spread and dominance of invasive weeds and aquatic vegetation, including EDRR programs.
• Promote policies that eliminate illegal fish introductions.

## Rivers and Streams

Rivers and streams (Figure 
36
 and Figure 
37
) represent flowing water in natural stream channels as identified by the NHDPlus data layer that defines the extent of this key habitat (see Figure 
36
). Rivers and streams vary greatly in size, from small streams (i.e., intermittent and first to second order; see 
Strahler [1957]
 for stream order definition) to very large rivers (i.e., fifth order and larger). Small streams often originate in the headwaters of watersheds or subwatersheds. They can flow seasonally (intermittently) or perennially (year-round), with the greatest streamflow often supported by snowmelt and base summer flows supported by groundwater. The gradient for small streams tends to be moderate to high with boulder and cobble substrates. When water is present, temperature varies by elevation and source (e.g., precipitation or groundwater).
Some small streams originate in semiarid regions or smaller watersheds, where they provide ribbons of aquatic and riparian habitats and there are no larger streams. Small streams, particularly in southern Utah, can carry large volumes of water during summer monsoon rains.
Large streams (i.e., third to fourth order) form as small streams coalesce in wide montane and foothill valleys. They tend to have wider channels and a lower gradient than small streams.
Snowmelt-driven flood pulses can be of high volume, moving sediment and large woody debris.
Summer base flows can be low and are typically sustained by groundwater, with occasional large monsoonal flood events that deliver sediment. Larger streams have greater meandering Aquatic macroinvertebrates in Utah's rivers and streams are small organisms-mostly insect larvae, worms, and snails-that play a crucial role in the health of these ecosystems. These diverse communities fulfill many ecological functions. They are primary consumers of algae and aquatic vegetation; detritivores that break down organic material; predators that prey on other invertebrates and small fish; and, importantly, a major food source for fish, amphibians, reptiles, birds, and mammals. Insects comprise the majority of these macroinvertebrates, including mayflies, stoneflies, caddisflies, beetles, midges, crane flies, dragonflies, and damselflies. Other invertebrates include snails, clams, aquatic worms, and crayfish. These 

## Extent

• The NHDPlus layer (U.S. Geological Survey 2025a) was used to determine habitat extent for rivers and streams habitat in Utah.
• Rivers and streams represent flowing water in natural stream channels that are categorized as perennial, intermittent, or ephemeral based on flow duration and groundwater exchange processes.

## Condition

Intact small stream habitat occurs across the state but is increasingly affected by warming temperatures, drought, livestock grazing, and human land uses that alter streamflow, water temperature, sediment transport, and nutrient cycling. Similarly, intact large stream habitat is present but often is impacted by historical or current human land uses, constrained and simplified floodplains, reduced habitat diversity and function, altered river flows, and impaired water quality. Human activities include water development and livestock grazing. Additionally, many Utah rivers, particularly in the Great Basin, face challenges from minimally buffered developments that alter river flows, constrain and simplify floodplains, reduce riparian habitat, and diminish habitat complexity and function, as well as impact the abundance and distribution of large woody debris.
Diversions and dams fragment habitat and limit movement opportunities for some SGCN to access habitats for life history needs such as spawning.
DWQ's 2024 Integrated Report on Water Quality found that 42% of perennial stream miles (out of 15,820 perennial miles reported) in Utah did not meet water quality standards (DWQ 2024). This is down from 52% reported in 2022 (DWQ 2022). Out of those perennial stream miles evaluated, a total of 31% supported their designated beneficial use. Designated beneficial uses protect water quality for different uses, including drinking water, recreation, aquatic life, and agriculture (DWQ 2024).
The relative condition of most of Utah's rivers and streams is currently unknown; however, some habitat metrics are collected by agencies and other organizations (see Table 
8
 in the Aquatic Key Habitats section of this chapter).

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of rivers and streams habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Promote policies that maintain or restore natural flow and sediment regimes.
• Promote policies that reduce inappropriate grazing by domestic livestock, feral horses, and wildlife.
• Promote policies that minimize or eliminate the placement of roads in riparian zones.
• Promote policies that reduce inappropriate residential and commercial development in floodplains.
• Continue the use of appropriate methods for reducing the spread and dominance of invasive weeds and aquatic vegetation, including EDRR programs.
• Use proven stream restoration techniques to aggrade the channels of highly degraded streams, especially those that are so channelized and/or incised that they are no longer connected to the floodplain, (e.g., incorporation of low-tech, process-based restoration techniques such as BDAs) to restore natural stream conditions 
(Anderson, Goeking, et al. 2024
; Utah State University 2024).

## Saline Lakes/Great Salt Lake Ecosystem

The Saline Lakes/Great Salt Lake Ecosystem Key Habitat (Figure 
38
 and Figure 
39
) is ecologically distinct from freshwater lakes and reservoirs due to its elevated salinity, endorheic nature (i.e., lacking an outlet to the ocean or other drainage systems), and specialized communities of halophilic organisms. Despite these differences, saline lakes similarly influence regional temperature and precipitation patterns and play a critical role in regulating local climates. Typically found in arid regions with high evaporation rates, saline lakes are vital ecological components in Utah's desert landscape.
This habitat type has been newly defined in the 2025 UWAP (it was previously grouped with the Open Water Key Habitat in the 2015 UWAP [UWAP Joint Team 2015]). In Utah, prominent saline systems include Sevier Lake and GSL, with the latter serving as the primary focus of this key habitat due to its high conservation value for SGCNs.
GSL is the largest saline lake in the Western Hemisphere and the eighth largest globally. Its ecosystem encompasses the lake and adjacent habitats-such as wetlands and marshesextending up to approximately 1,286 m (4,218 feet) above sea level. There is considerable overlap between the Wetlands and the Saline Lakes/Great Salt Lake Ecosystem Key Habitats, particularly along the GSL's extensive shoreline wetland systems, which comprises hundreds of thousands of acres of playas, marshes, meadows, and waterbodies that support high species diversity. These habitats are influenced by the hydrological inputs from the Jordan, Weber, and Bear Rivers, with lake elevation and wetland extent largely dependent on water management, snowfall, and spring runoff. Although the hypersaline conditions of GSL limit invertebrate biodiversity, the lake supports immense populations of specialized species such brine shrimp, brine flies, alkali flies, and newly discovered nematodes 
(Jung et al. 2024
). These organisms thrive on abundant phytoplankton and experience little interspecific competition, leading to population sizes in the billions. Salinity and lake temperature are key drivers of primary productivity, population dynamics, and individual development rates for these invertebrates. As a result, they form a critical prey base for millions of migratory and resident birds that rely on the lake throughout the year 
(Barrett and Belovsky 2020)
. Ecosystem Key Habitat is increasingly important to these bird species.

## Extent

• The extent of the Saline Lakes/Great Salt Lake Ecosystem Key Habitat depends on tributary inputs and water management, but for the UWAP, this habitat includes associated wetlands and marshes below approximately 1,286 m (4,218 feet) above sea level.
• Wetlands surrounding GSL that are within this elevation line overlap with wetlands identified in the Wetlands Key Habitat. Data sets collected by agencies to evaluate GSL habitat condition are presented in Table 
8
 in the Aquatic Key Habitats section of this chapter.

## Condition

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of Saline Lakes/Great Salt Lake Ecosystem Key Habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Promote policies that maintain or restore natural sediment regimes and streamflow into saline lakes.
• Improve water delivery inputs (quality and quantity) by promoting and observing water conservation.
• Establish GSL water rights to maintain healthy lake elevations.
• Promote policies that minimize or eliminate the placement of roads in wetlands and riparian zones.
• Promote policies that reduce residential and commercial development in wetlands, riparian zones, and floodplains that impact GSL.
• Continuing the use of methods for reducing the spread and dominance of invasive weeds and aquatic vegetation (especially phragmites), including EDRR programs.

## Springs

Springs are groundwater-dependent ecosystems that occur when water emerges at the surface from an underground aquifer or water table. These systems are sustained by groundwater recharge from precipitation and surface water. Deeper aquifers often contain ancient water, with some dated to thousands of years old. There are 12 recognized types of springs: cave, exposure, fountain, geyser, gushet, hanging garden, helocrene, hillslope, hypocrene, limnocrene, mound form, and rheocrene (SSI 2024a).
Springs are typically formed where water discharges along slopes at geological faults or rock fractures, or where it is forced to the surface by impermeable layers or pressure or thermal gradients, such as in artesian and geothermal springs. These features are distributed throughout Utah's landscape, from low-elevation deserts to high-elevation forests (Figure 
40
).
Their size, physical and chemical properties, and condition vary considerably.
Although many springs in Utah are small and locally significant, some are large and high volume. Springs may be perennial or ephemeral and can emerge in a wide range of environments, including desert basins, forests, and lake beds, and river channels. Spring-fed aquatic habitats are notable for their relatively stable water temperatures and chemical composition, both of which reflect the geological formations through which the groundwater passes. These temperatures may be cooler or substantially warmer than ambient air temperature. Warm and hot (geothermal) springs often exhibit unique chemistry and support specialized aquatic biota adapted to those conditions.
Springs frequently support unique biological communities of aquatic biota, particularly aquatic plants, mollusks, and invertebrates. Due to the often isolated nature of springs in Utah's arid climate, they provide disproportionately valuable and productive riparian and wetland habitats.
Despite some overlap with other aquatic ecosystems, Springs Key Habitat is defined as including isolated springheads, spring-fed creeks, and the associated habitats. Springs that connect with larger flowing systems are categorized under Rivers and Streams Key Habitat, and adjacent vegetated wetlands-such as wet meadows and marshes-are covered under Wetlands Key Habitat. Some larger springheads are mapped within the wetlands spatial layer, but these are still considered part of the Springs Key Habitat (see Figure 
40
; Figure 
41
).
Springs support diverse and often specialized insect communities that are shaped by factors such as water chemistry, temperature, and flow dynamics. These include numerous aquatic and terrestrial insects, such as crane flies, non-biting midges, mosquitoes, hover flies, tachinids, and picture-wing flies, which occupy a variety of ecological niches. Beetles are also well represented, from aquatic diving beetles to predaceous fireflies and tiger beetles in moist soils, and various herbivorous and saproxylic ground beetles.
Pollinators-including bees, flies, butterflies, moths, and wasps-are frequently associated with springs and may consist of rare, endemic, or migratory species (as with monarch butterflies) seeking floral resources. Additionally, aquatic macroinvertebrates are a prominent component of spring ecosystems, with taxa such as mayflies, caddisflies, dragonflies, damselflies, water striders, and occasionally stoneflies, alongside mollusks and crustaceans. These organisms fulfill critical ecological functions, including sediment processing, decomposition, water quality enhancement, and serving as prey for a wide range of wildlife such as fish, amphibians, birds, and mammals. • More than 11,000 springs are mapped within Utah (see Figure 
40
).
The condition of many of Utah's springs is currently unknown. The condition of springs habitat in some areas has been diminished due to stressors, including changes in precipitation and hydrologic regimes, surface and groundwater availability, disturbance, and human land use and extraction.
Some habitat condition information exists for springs in the DWR Springs Database (DWR 2024b) and for springs that contain SGCN springsnails that are managed under a conservation agreement and strategy.
The BLM also has condition information for some springs on BLM-administered lands through
Proper Functioning Condition assessments and the Riparian and Wetland Assessment, Inventory, and Monitoring (AIM) program.
A priority of the Statewide Mollusk Conservation Strategy Working Group (Springsnail Conservation Team 2020) is to develop protocols for monitoring spring habitat condition through time. The Springs App allows users to record spring habitat condition data.

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of springs habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Promote policies that maintain or restore natural flow and sediment regimes within springs.
• Promote policies that reduce grazing by domestic livestock, feral horses, and wildlife that negatively impacts springs.
• Promote policies that minimize or eliminate the placement of roads in riparian zones.
• Promote policies that reduce development that impacts springs.
• Promote policies that reduce dewatering of springs.
• Continue the use of methods for reducing the spread and dominance of invasive weeds and aquatic vegetation, including EDRR programs.
• Follow SSI's restoration and rehabilitation recommendations for heavily degraded springs and springs that contain rare species of springsnails and other mollusks, including removal of exotic weeds and protection from grazing.

## Wetlands

Wetlands encompass a broad range of habitats, including marshes, wet meadows, fens, backwater sloughs, oxbows, and mudflat fringes. These ecosystems are characterized by a high water table and frequent soil saturation at or near the surface. Wetlands may be permanently inundated, intermittently flooded, or seasonally saturated and are classified based on the duration and depth of flooding. Flooding regimes range from deep, permanent inundation to brief, shallow saturation during parts of the growing season 
(Downard et al. 2017)
.
The USFWS defines wetlands as transitional areas between terrestrial and aquatic systems that are inundated or saturated with water often enough to support hydric soils and hydrophytic vegetation (Federal Geographic Data Committee 2013). For the purposes of the 2025 UWAP, small ponds less than 20 acres in size were categorized as wetlands. This distinction aligns with data layers used to identify aquatic key habitats; many features mapped in the NWI either dry out seasonally or have transitioned into vegetated wetlands over time. There is an overlap between the Wetlands Key Habitat and the Saline Lakes/Great Salt Lake Ecosystem Key Habitat with wetlands adjacent to GSL.
Utah wetlands are hydrologically diverse systems receiving water from multiple sources, including groundwater discharge, surface runoff, direct precipitation, snowmelt, and overbank flooding from rivers and lakes. Many wetlands receive input from a combination of these sources (Figure 
42
 and Figure 
43
).
There is often overlap between wetland and riparian habitats; however, they differ in spatial occurrence and hydrologic regimes. Wetlands can occur across the landscape and typically experience longer and more frequent periods of soil saturation than riparian zones. In contrast, riparian habitats are confined to areas adjacent to rivers, streams, and lakes, and are typically flooded only briefly, often during seasonal high-water events such as spring runoff.
Wetland vegetation is dominated by hydrophytes (plant species adapted to saturated soils) and tends to be primarily herbaceous. Riparian vegetation, by contrast, may include both herbaceous and woody species. Wetlands provide habitat for a wide array of plant and animal The regular presence of moisture combined with plant diversity and structural complexity enables wetland systems to support a wide variety of insects and other invertebrates.
Herbivorous insect groups include grasshoppers, katydids, planthoppers, leafhoppers, aphids,
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 230
beetles, gall-formers, thrips, butterflies and moths, many of which are specialized to use wetland and riparian vegetation. Pollinators such as bumble bees, solitary bees, butterflies, moths, nectar-feeding ants and wasps, flies, and beetles are also abundant and essential to ecosystem function. Aquatic insects-such as non-biting midges, mosquitoes, crane flies, mayflies, stoneflies, salmonflies, snakeflies, dobsonflies, caddisflies, dragonflies, and damselflies-develop in submerged or saturated substrates and emerge as aerial adults. These insects contribute to nutrient cycling, decomposition, and food web dynamics. Detritivores and decomposers further enrich wetland soil and water quality by breaking down organic debris and recycling nutrients. Wetlands also support a wide range of predatory and parasitic species, including spiders, tiger beetles, fireflies, and wasps, as well as amphibians, reptiles, birds, mammals, and fish that feed on invertebrates. This abundance of insect prey contributes to the overall biological richness and ecological productivity of wetland ecosystems. 

## Ecoregion Characteristics

Wetlands Key Habitat occurs across Utah in all seven ecoregions. Wetlands filter pollutants and excess nutrients, provide water for SGCN and SGIN in dry climates, and have unique ecosystems that contain species found nowhere else. Depending on where wetlands occur in Utah, they provide different benefits for SGCNs. For example, in the Central Basin and Range ecoregion, some wetlands provide year-round habitat for birds, fish, amphibians, and mollusks, whereas wetlands around GSL (included as part of the Saline Lakes/Great Salt Lake Ecosystem Key Habitat) provide important habitat for migrating and breeding shorebirds, waterbirds, waterfowl, and landbirds. In the Colorado Plateau ecoregion, wetlands along the Green and Colorado Rivers provide critical early life stage habitat for SGCN fish and year-round habitat for many SGCN birds.

## Extent

• The extent of wetlands in Utah is mapped within the NWI (USFWS 2024a). Although NWI coverage of some areas of Utah is outdated, the overall NWI wetland layer for the state has become more accurate over the past 10 years 
(Menuz and Goodwin 2024)
.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 233
• Historical data suggest that approximately 30% of the state's wetlands were destroyed prior to the 1980s 
(Dahl 1990
); however, no comprehensive assessment has occurred since then.
• • Total current mapped area within Utah totals 532,240.09 acres, which is 0.98% of the state's surface area (see Figure 
42
).
• Wetlands are mapped in 2,433 of 2,885 HUC-12 units that overlap Utah.

## Condition

The studies indicate that the condition of a given wetland typically corresponds to its elevational location within its respective watershed; the higher the wetland location, the better the wetland condition. This is likely due to several factors. In general, higher elevations receive more precipitation and fewer land use impacts, whereas lower elevations receive less precipitation and have more land use impacts.
Non-native and invasive plant species are the most widespread stressor and are particularly common in wetlands impacted by other disturbances; around GSL, phragmites is the most problematic 
(Kettenring et al. 2020)
. Also common are stressors to hydrology, including unnatural flow regimes due to water management, decreased flows from water diversion and pumping, and extended periods of drought. Other common stressors are hydrologic alteration, habitat loss and encroachment of land conversion to hardscaped uses, inappropriate livestock grazing, and urban and agricultural sources that affect water quality.
Some habitat condition metrics for wetlands are tracked through the development of conservation agreements and strategies for some SGCN (e.g., 
Bailey et al. 2006;
Least Chub Conservation Team 2014)
.
The BLM maintains condition information on some wetlands on BLM-administered lands 

## Improving Condition

An effective strategy for management of wetlands habitat in Utah may include the following elements:
• Promote policies that maintain or restore natural flow and sediment regimes.
• Promote policies that reduce inappropriate grazing by domestic livestock, feral horses, and wildlife.
• Promote policies that protect wetlands from development and provide a buffer between development and adjacent aquatic resources.
• Continue the use of appropriate methods for reducing the spread and dominance of invasive weeds and aquatic vegetation, including EDRR programs.
• Work to improve wetland mitigation by providing comments on public notices and suggestions for potential mitigation sites and exploring a possible state-run mitigation program.
• Support and expand existing wetland restoration programs (e.g., Zeedyk structures, phragmites removal, Utah Lake shore restoration) and develop additional restoration capacity.
Data sets collected by agencies to evaluate wetlands habitat condition are presented in Table 
7
 in the Aquatic Key Habitats section in this chapter. 

## Threats

## Priority Needs

• Continue updating NWI coverage of Utah wetlands.
• Support efforts by UWAP partners such as the BLM and UGS to continue collecting wetland condition data and work with partners to determine key metrics that could be collected more broadly and to test applicability of methods for targeted decision making.
• Better quantify hydrologic stress to wetlands using information such as climate and remotely sensed groundwater and streamflow data.
• Identify important wetlands that lack existing protections (due to landownership, lack of federal protections, etc.).
• Work to implement protections for high-priority wetlands that lack protection or are experiencing extreme hydrologic stress.
• Survey wetlands for SGCN and SGIN.

## Introduction

The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) defines climate as the average weather over a period of time, or "the statistical description in terms of the mean and variability of relevant quantities over a period of time ranging from months to thousands or millions of years.
The classical period for averaging these variables is 30 years, as defined by the World University and is summarized later in this chapter. The subcommittee also identified long-term goals for addressing climate change that could not be accomplished for the 2025 UWAP but will be incorporated in future amendments or revisions.

## Current Weather and Climate Across Utah

Various resources were reviewed to capture the current weather and climate across Utah, including the following:
• Utah Center for Climate and Weather (2015)
• Utah Geological Survey (UGS) (2024e)
• Weather Atlas (2024)
• National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration National Centers for Environmental Information 
(Palecki et al. 2021)
 This chapter provides a snapshot of current weather in Utah, recent changes to weather, and 
44
) then melts in spring and summer. By acting as a natural water reservoir that is seasonally replenished, snowpack buffers the landscape against dry conditions. Snow cover also acts as a large-scale control on plant growth and animal movement, influencing local ecology. Thus, current and projected shifts in Utah's temperature and precipitation regimes due to climate change will continue to impact its water supply, wildfire risk, and ecosystems. 

## Summer Temperatures

In Utah, July temperatures are rising faster than any other month, increasing by nearly 1°F per decade since 1979 (Figure 
46
). This rate of warming parallels global patterns of increasing temperatures caused by anthropogenic greenhouse gas emissions, particularly carbon dioxide 
(Crowley 2000)
. Because future greenhouse gas emissions depend on policies and actions, scientists consider a range of future greenhouse gas concentration scenarios to predict future 

## Winter Temperatures

Temperatures in winter also appear to be rising, though not as rapidly as summer temperatures.
The trend for January mean temperature in Utah indicates an increase of approximately 0.7°F per decade since 1979, but these data are more variable, and the trend is not statistically significant (P = 0.12). More warming, however, is anticipated. By 2100, under the RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5 emissions scenarios, winter temperatures in Utah are expected to rise by 3.8°F and 8.2°F, respectively (Figure 
48
). 

## Precipitation and Snowpack

The amount of winter precipitation falling in Utah has not meaningfully changed in the last 40 years, nor is it projected to (Figure 
49
) 
(

## Stream Flow

In mountain regions, summer stream flow is linked to the amount of snowpack that accumulated during the preceding winter and the timing of snowmelt. Thus, reduced peak snowpack and shifts toward an earlier peak-a response to warmer temperatures-can change stream baseflows. For example, August discharge in northern Utah's Logan River has declined since 1979 (Figure 
51
). Although this decline has not been directly attributed to climate change, it parallels expectations that consider regional temperature increases and snowpack declines during this period (see Figure 
46
 and Figure 
50
). 

## Wildfire

In addition to reducing water availability, higher temperatures affect fire risk by increasing vapor pressure deficit, which draws moisture out of fuel, making it more flammable (Figure 
53
).
Across the western United States, contemporary climate change accounted for approximately 55% of the observed increase in fuel aridity from 1979 to 2015 
(Abatzoglou and Williams 2016)
.
In July 2007, the largest wildfire in Utah's recent history occurred: the Milford Flat Fire. The fire was ignited by lightning and, in less than 2 weeks, burned approximately 363,000 acres. As temperatures continue to rise and fuel becomes drier, the frequency and severity of large fires in Utah will likely increase. Changes in fire regime affect habitat by altering the structure and type of vegetation 
(Agee 1993)
. Generally, higher temperatures, declining snowpack, and reduced late-summer stream flow in Utah will alter the mosaic of existing habitats. For instance, these conditions will likely extend the Great Basin Desert to higher elevations and expand its geographic range. At the same time, habitats above tree line will contract as downslope tree species shift uphill. Long-term data sets are needed to track these changes and their effects. Overall, it is important to note that research into extreme weather events within the state of Utah is limited and highly uncertain, but it is expected that with increasing average temperatures, drought conditions will increase in severity. Changes in seasonal precipitation may also increase the severity of winter storms. Projections show more precipitation will occur as rainfall during the winter season instead of snow, and this in turn may lead to an increase in flood risks 
(Garfin et al. 2013
).

## Identifying Climate Refugia

The Climate Adaptation Subcommittee worked with Dr. Edward Hammill of Utah State University to identify climate refugia in Utah (Figure 
55
). An analysis using the program Marxan was completed to identify Hydrologic Unit Code (HUC) 12 subwatersheds within Utah that were projected to be the least exposed to temperature increases at RCP 4.5 and also included at least 50% of the distribution of 2025 UWAP species of greatest conservation need (SGCN) and 50% of the UWAP key habitats (see 
Hammill et al. 2024
 for more details on the Marxan analysis). Figure 
55
 presents the analysis results for all SGCNs, as well as for aquatic and terrestrial SGCNs separately. The figure compares outcomes when only SGCNs were evaluated versus when both SGCNs and key habitats were considered. The identified HUC-12s highlight areas in the state that support SGCNs and key habitats while being less exposed to the impacts of climate change. Therefore, focusing landscape restoration projects in these areas should retain the highest benefits over time. 

## Long-Term Climate Goals

The Climate Adaptation Subcommittee identified several long-term goals for incorporating climate adaptation that could not be achieved for the 2025 UWAP. This group will continue to work toward prioritizing and accomplishing some of these goals. Goals that were not achieved are as follows:
• Expand the climate refugia analysis to include other risk layers (e.g., fire, industry, development) and SGCN habitat connectivity and movement needs to better identify true Conservation Opportunity Areas to help focus limited funding into the most important conservation areas identified in the UWAP.
• Evaluate climate vulnerability of SGCNs using the Climate Change Vulnerability Index (NatureServe 2024c).
• Complete climate predictive modeling for key habitats.
• Model the impacts of increased water temperature to aquatic SGCNs and prioritize mitigation through methods such as purchasing or leasing additional water, riparian restoration, and increasing streambank vegetation.
• Align climate threats in Utah Division of Wildlife's Watershed Restoration Initiative (WRI)
with solutions (e.g., incorporate climate resilience into best management practices and/or develop an adaptive climate resilience checklist for restoration projects).

## Threats

The and Appendix E for a full list of threats. These climate-specific threats were used in SGCN and key habitat threat assessments for the 2025 UWAP. Climate change and severe weather directly affect fish and wildlife within Utah by shifting and altering their habitats at many trophic levels.
Climate change affects seasonal temperatures and precipitation (timing, amount, duration, and severity), which in turn shifts the habitats that may be suitable for certain species. This includes rising water temperatures, which threaten sensitive aquatic species, and shifting terrestrial habitats, which are being affected by habitat encroachment due to increased woody vegetation.
Fire is a major climate-related threat with the potential to generate large landscape-level effects on ecosystems throughout Utah. It is linked to climate change, past forest management, and invasive annual grasses. Gaps in available data for Utah are a major concern because they reduce managers' ability to make informed, forward-looking decisions. Climate-specific concerns include the future effects of increasing temperature, precipitation variability, a decreasing snowpack, and timing and availability of snowmelt.

## Conservation Actions

Conservation actions can be taken to better prepare and be more proactive at managing resources and reducing negative impacts from projected changes to climate. Some current actions implemented by Utah Division of Wildlife and partners, including resources, are summarized below.

## Making Climate Actionable Through Utah Division of Wildlife Resources' Watershed Restoration Initiative

The WRI is the primary vehicle for implementing the UWAP and provides the framework for all Utah partners to submit and implement Utah wildlife conservation projects, from big game habitat restoration and wildfire rehabilitation to UWAP project implementation. There have been over 700 partners

UWAP project planning and implementation have been effectively integrated into the WRI project scoring system, which ensures that all projects consider UWAP implementation along with their other project objectives.
