## U.S. Forest Service Climate Strategies

The U. • Keep water temperature from rising beyond thresholds in which aquatic life may survive through streambank and riparian restoration; maintain water quantity and healthy riparian corridors.
• Maintain biodiversity and healthy landscapes to reduce the impact from noxious weeds and lower fuel moisture, which could lead to increased fire activity.
• Conserve and enhance environmental water to benefit SGCNs and their key habitats (see multiple Essential Conservation Actions to Address this Threat sections under waterrelated threats in Chapter 4, Threats, Data Gaps, and Conservation Actions).

## Introduction

Utah's diverse habitats are subject to a wide range of ecological threats. As one of the most arid states in the nation, and with over 3,048 meters (m) (10,000 feet) of elevational variation, Utah encompasses seven U.S. Environmental Protection Agency Level III ecoregions and 37 Level IV ecoregions, each characterized by distinct environmental conditions and unique biological communities. For resource managers, overseeing these ecoregions and understanding the pressures they face is essential. These threats vary by ecoregion and include shifting climatic patterns, invasive species encroachment, and development pressures.
A comprehensive understanding of these threats is the foundation for assessing their impacts, evaluating ecological resilience, and identifying effective mitigation strategies. Effective conservation efforts prioritize minimizing the impact of large-scale threats: preventing ecosystem degradation is more efficient than attempting to restore ecosystems already damaged. Furthermore, restoration efforts are unlikely to succeed if the root causes of degradation remain unaddressed. Given the ecological diversity of Utah's ecoregions and potential severity of the threats they face, a thorough understanding of these challenges is critical to preserving the state's biodiversity for future generations.
Developing a comprehensive list of the threats to species and habitats is a significant challenge due to Utah's vast and varied landscapes as well as the complexity of the threats themselves.
Many early state wildlife action plans lacked consistency in the terminology used to describe threats, data gaps, and essential conservation actions. This inconsistency hindered conservation across state borders and complicated collaboration, coordination, and joint fundraising initiatives. Standardizing the terminology used in conservation strategies fosters more effective and consistent approaches at state and regional levels.
Utah and other states adopted a standardized terminology (at the recommendation of the Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies) ( that can be taken to avoid, reduce, or mitigate threats identified in state wildlife action plans.
Using standardized language to describe and refer to threats and conservation actions during
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 260
the planning process allows state wildlife action plans to more easily identify which threats impact multiple species and habitats-collectively referred to as conservation targets. The standardized threats and conservation actions continue to be updated (Conservation Standards 2025), and future Utah Wildlife Action Plans (UWAPs) will use the updated threats and conservation actions.

## Standardized Threats System

The standardized terminology adopted by numerous states and territories 
(CMP 2016)
 categorizes threats into two hierarchical levels. At the first level (Level-1), a limited number of broad categories encompasses all possible threats, clarified based on their origin (e.g., human disturbance, development, invasive species). These general Level-1 categories are further divided into more specific Level-2 categories. Within this structured system, users can define even more detailed threat categories.
In Utah, an additional level of standardized threats, Level-3, was developed by UWAP partners for the 2015 UWAP (UWAP Joint Team [Joint Team] 2015) to preserve critical local information that might otherwise be lost within the broader Level-2 classifications. For example, presence of diversions and dam/reservoir operation represent distinct Level-3 threats, each requiring different mitigation strategies and involving different stakeholders. Both are categorized under the Level-2 threat, dams and water management/use, which, in turn, falls under the broader Level-1 threat of natural system modification. This standardized terminology significantly enhances the ability to assess threats and conservation actions across multiple conservation targets within Utah, while also facilitating collaboration among states.
The standardized threat classification outlined by 
Salafsky et al. (2008)
 and its subsequent revisions 
(CMP 2016;
Salafsky et al. 2025)
 do not address data gaps. The UWAP treats data gaps as a distinct Level-1 threat, with specific gaps categorized under Level-2 threats. In preparation for the 2025 UWAP revision, the Species of Greatest Conservation Need (SGCN) Subcommittee reviewed all Utah-specific Level-3 threats to ensure their continued relevance.
Some Level-3 threats were updated, and additional ones were introduced to incorporate threats affecting native plants and invertebrates, which were newly included in the UWAP.
Each standardized threat at every level is assigned a numeric code and label. Throughout this chapter, threat labels are used more frequently than numeric codes. Where codes do appear, they follow the format: #.#.#, where the first digit represents the Level-1 category, the second denotes Level-2, and the third indicates Level-3.

## Statewide Threat Assessment

The threat assessment was conducted with a focus on key habitats, wildlife, plants, and invertebrates. Some identified threats also represent essential public services and land uses.
For instance, water development, residential expansion, renewable energy production, mining, and agriculture are critical public interests that provide societal benefits. Although activities such as water diversion for municipal or agricultural purposes may negatively impact certain SGCNs and their habitats, they remain legitimate and necessary uses of natural resources.
Identifying these threats is essential to assess their relative impact and to determine where remedial or preventive actions would be most effective.

## UWAP-prioritized threats have been effectively integrated into the Watershed Restoration

Initiative (WRI) project scoring system, ensuring that all projects align with UWAP implementation while addressing other project objectives. Because WRI funding is highly competitive, project proponents are incentivized to strengthen their proposals by demonstrating how their projects mitigate threats to SGCNs and their key habitats. Additionally, WRI's diverse funding portfolio provides an opportunity to extend limited UWAP funds and secure financial support through nontraditional sources. For further details on the WRI, refer to the case study in Chapter 6, Partnerships, Implementation, and Regional Collaboration.
A summary of the threat assessment process is provided below, with additional details on the methods used to identify, evaluate, and prioritize threats and data gaps available in Appendix E.
Threats were scored based on their scope and severity for each target:
• Threat Scope: The proportion of the population or species distribution (or key habitat) currently affected or projected to be affected within the next 10 years.
• Threat Severity: The extent to which the threat is likely to eliminate populations (or key habitats) or significantly reduce distribution within the next 10 years.
Using a numerical formula (NatureServe 2020), severity and scope scores were combined into a single threat impact measure.
After evaluating all threats to all targets, the data were analyzed to determine the following:
• The number of targets affected by each threat.
• The overall degree of impact (low, medium, high, very high).
A total of 6,594 threat-by-target instances were identified (Table 
28
).
Table 
28
 presents the statewide assessment results, summarizing threats at the broadest classification level (Level-1). If threat 12 (Data Gaps) is the primary reason for a species' designation as an SGCN, that species is now classified as a species of greatest information need (SGIN) (see Table 
28
). This new classification approach is an addition for the 2025 UWAP (see Chapter 1, Species of Greatest Conservation Need -Distribution and Abundance, for further details). 

## Ranking the Threats

The Joint Team developed a threat ranking system for the 2015 UWAP (Joint Team 2015) and applied it to the full threats data set. The goal was to conduct a transparent, repeatable, and objective process for ranking priority threats across the state, rather than focusing on specific threats. This approach also aimed to improve efficiency and provide clear direction for UWAP implementation.
Threats that pose broader, more significant challenges by severely impacting multiple conservation targets over large areas are ranked higher and are considered priority UWAP threats. In contrast, threats that affect fewer targets or cause less severe impacts in more limited areas are ranked lower and are addressed outside the scope of the UWAP.
For the 2025 UWAP, the SGCN, Terrestrial Key Habitats, and Aquatic Key Habitats Subcommittees reviewed and updated threat assessments for new SGCNs, SGCNs that had updated NatureServe subnational conservation status ranks (i.e., S-ranks), and each key habitat.
The Water, Climate Adaptation, and Habitat Connectivity Subcommittees also evaluated and updated threats for their respective resources. After the updates, the Utah Division of Wildlife Resources (DWR) Wildlife Action Plan Implementation Team and UWAP Core Team reviewed the threats and provided feedback to ensure agreement and add relevant insights.
This chapter presents a summary of Utah's priority threats. Additional details on the methods used to identify and assess threats and data gaps, along with a table listing all threats from the full data set, can be found in Appendix E. Below is a description of the threat ranking process:
• Starting with the complete data set (6,594 records), all Level-2 threats relevant to Utah targets (48) had data relating to data gaps (Level-1 threat 12. Data Gaps) removed, eliminating seven Level-2 threats.
• The remaining data (4,557 records for the 41 remaining Level-2 threats) were further filtered by removing instances of low-impact threats (2,183 records), which resulted in the elimination of one Level-2 threat.
• From the remaining data set (2,374 records across 40 Level-2 threats), records with unknown impact levels (117 records) were removed, eliminating three additional Level-2 threats.
• For the remaining data set (2,257 records across 37 Level-2 threats), a count was made of how many targets each threat impacted (a range of 1 to 223 targets). The average was calculated to be 61.
• Twelve Level-2 threats that impacted more than the average number of targets (>61),
were selected as priority Level-2 threats (Table 
29
).
These 12 priority Level-2 threats account for over 27% of the total data set (1,784 of 6,594 records). Only these priority threats were considered for further assessment, and non-priority threats were removed from subsequent tables. For a complete list of all threats, see Appendix E.

265
Note: Priority Level-2 threats are highlighted in orange. Numerical threat codes are part of the hierarchy and standardization.
Following the Level-2 threats prioritization exercise, 70 Level-3 threats remained nested within the 12 priority Level-2 threats. Because Level-3 represents the most actionable level for conservation efforts, the UWAP Core Team determined that addressing all 70 threats would be inefficient and impractical for targeted action and monitoring. As a result, a further prioritization process was conducted (Table 
30
) using the same methodology applied to Level-2 threats:
• Within the 12 priority Level-2 threats, 70 Level-3 threats were identified, encompassing 1,784 records.
• To refine the focus, the number of conservation targets impacted by each threat was analyzed (range 1-217), and the average number of impacted targets was calculated at 25.7.
• All Level-3 threats affecting more than the average number of targets (>25.7) were designated priority Level-3 threats.
• The final set of 20 priority Level-3 threats (including the three Level-2 threats in Table 
30
) accounts for 13.2% of the full data set (872 of 6,594 records).
Only these priority threats were included in subsequent assessments, and non-priority threats were excluded from the following tables. For a complete list of all identified threats, refer to Appendix E.

29

. Table

However, if the Level-3 threats nested within the Level-2 threats did not impact an above-average level of conservation targets on their own, they did not rise to a Level-3 priority threat. In addition, when ranking Level-3 threats, all records for Level-3 threats with low or unknown impacts were removed, and the sum of the remaining conservation targets are presented in Table 
30
. The number of conservation targets presented in Table 
29

30
. This ranking process aimed to identify the threats with the highest impact to the greatest number of SGCNs and key habitats so that conservation resources can be focused more efficiently. The UWAP Core Team found these results satisfactory because they eliminated nearly 78% of the data while preserving all key statewide threats for further review.
Ideally, the wildlife conservation community needs to move toward steady and holistic management for the benefit of many species and habitats at once. By prioritizing threats to focus on those with the largest statewide impact, the UWAP implements a consistent approach with collective benefit. This approach was implemented in the 2015 UWAP 
(Joint Team 2015)
 and carried forward in this revision, because it is efficient and proactive in accomplishing the following:
• Reducing the number of species warranting a listing under the Endangered Species Act (ESA)
• Reducing the number of divisive, expensive conflicts among stakeholders and authorities
• Improving broad ecosystem health and function (i.e., cleaner air and water, more carbon in the soil, more water in the streams and lakes, less soil erosion, and more native wildlife)
• Increasing recreational and economic opportunities and intangible amenities for all Utahns

Although such threats must be mitigated to ensure the conservation of specific SGCN or key habitats, they fall outside the scope of the UWAP and will not be considered further in this document. ## The Standardized Conservation Actions System

Similar to standardized threats, standardized conservation actions (see Table 
E-
 • Level-1 actions: Broad, landscape-scale strategies that address multiple SGCN or key habitats. These actions focus on general intervention types, such as water protection, land conservation, or policy changes.
• Level-2 actions: More specific measures that fall within the most relevant Level-1 category.
• Level-3 actions: Highly targeted efforts nested within the appropriate Level-2 category.
Each standardized action, like the standardized threats, is assigned a numeric code and label.
Throughout this chapter, action codes and labels are used consistently, and are presented in Potential Conservation Actions tables to address each priority threat. The coding format mirrors that of threats, structured as #.#.#, where the first digit represents the Level-1 category, the second corresponds to Level-2, and the third indicates Level-3. (It is important to note that the coding and classification of priority threats and conservation actions are not directly correlated.)

## Essential Conservation Actions

Conservation professionals must understand the biological and human social systems in which they work. The core strategy of conservation is to influence these biological and social systems for the good of SGCNs and their habitats. Actions are essential to bring this strategy to life to improve the conservation status of SGCN and key habitats. For instance, actionable items may include the following:
• Restore and/or improve declining wildlife populations and degraded habitat conditions or functions
• Respond to emergencies
• Take advantage of valuable opportunities
Effective conservation actions must be selected or developed in collaboration with administrators and stakeholders involved in activities identified as priority threats-many of which serve legitimate economic, cultural, or recreational purposes.

## Collaborative Development of Realistic and Acceptable Conservation Actions

A strategic first step in developing conservation actions is to anticipate potential implementation challenges. Some threats can be mitigated through actions that DWR and its partners have full authority to undertake. Others require dialogue and compromise, and DWR and partners will continue to pursue such approaches individually and collectively.
Certain threats, however, present greater challenges. In some cases, no existing UWAP partners have the legal authority to address them. In others, competing resource demands create conflicts with conservation objectives, or the appropriate course of action remains unclear.
Addressing these threats will require working collaboratively with relevant authorities, stakeholders, and partners to identify shared interests and develop mutually acceptable solutions.
The actions outlined in this plan serve as starting points for discussion rather than rigid mandates. Progress will depend on considering a wide range of ideas that can lead to broadly acceptable strategies for meeting the needs of SGCNs, their habitats, and the people of Utah.
Inclusivity is essential in determining the scope, method, location, and timing of conservation efforts. Many key partners and allies have yet to be fully engaged in shaping the final UWAP actions.
Because most SGCNs and key habitats are affected by multiple threats, DWR recognizes the importance of collaboration with agencies and partners beyond its direct authority. When multiple threats impact a conservation target, actions will be implemented strategically to ensure a balanced and diversified approach, allocating funding and resources appropriately to address each threat. Conservation actions will be continuously evaluated to measure their effectiveness in mitigating threats and ensuring an equitable contribution to long-term species and habitat security.
This chapter presents a detailed analysis for each priority threat, including a comprehensive description and assessment. At the core of each priority threat analysis is a set of essential conservation actions, structured around four key components:
• Objective(s) for each priority Level-3 threat.
• Potential indicators of success reaching each objective.
• Potential conservation actions to mitigate the threat.
• Likely authorities, stakeholders, and/or partners involved in addressing the threat.
Although some priority threats present complex and formidable challenges requiring extensive stakeholder collaboration, successful precedents demonstrate that such challenges can be effectively managed. To illustrate this, several case studies are included as examples to guide and inspire those working to address these issues. Additionally, Chapter 6, Partners, Implementation, and Regional Collaboration, offers resources and recommendations for initiating and structuring crucial stakeholder discussions.
Within the analyses for each priority threat below, a table is provided that shows the SGCN species affected by this threat at the very high, high, and medium levels. In some cases, more than one priority Level-3 threat is associated with the Level-2 threat. Consequently, some threat impacts in these tables may have a score larger than one.

## Residential and Commercial Development

Threats from human settlements or other nonagricultural land uses with a substantial footprint.
Within this broad category, housing and urban areas was identified as the sole Level-2 priority threat. A frequently overlooked aspect of urban expansion is the widespread introduction of artificial light in environments where it would not naturally occur, both in terms of timing and intensity.

## Housing and Urban Areas

Artificial light at night is particularly challenging to quantify because its effects extend far beyond the physical boundaries of urban infrastructure. Atmospheric scattering and cloud cover contribute to skyglow, which can spread light pollution hundreds of miles farther than its direct source. Unlike natural light from the sun, moon, and stars, artificial light at night has a distinct spectral composition, with modern lighting technologies-such as light-emitting diodes (LEDs)trending toward whiter light that emits a strong component in the blue spectrum.
The biological effects of artificial light at night vary substantially across species, life stages, habitats, and exposure conditions. However, extensive research consistently demonstrates the harmful impacts of light pollution to gene expression, physiology, foraging, daily movements, migration, reproduction, and overall survival 
(Gaston et al. 2015)
, as well as ecological functions such as pollination 
(Knop et al. 2017)
, and even nighttime cleansing of the atmosphere 
(Stark et al. 2011)
. Although light pollution is identified as a separate Level-3 threat from expansion of wildlife concerns need to be incorporated into planning efforts to achieve the greatest impact.
Although the primary aim of the UWAP is to propose actions that directly benefit SGCNs, there are many benefits of preserving wildlife habitat in and around urban areas. Experiencing nature in an urban context can foster support for ecological preservation and has the potential to improve the quality of life and health of urban and suburban residents.

## Case Study: Balancing Urban Development and Ecological Preservation in Eagle Mountain, Utah

Although mule deer are not designated as SGCN, they are a priority species for DWR. Eagle Mountain, Utah, provides a case study for balancing urban development while preserving a critical migration corridor for mule deer and demonstrates how partnerships can positively protect wildlife habitat. Eagle Mountain is one of the fastest-growing cities in the state of Utah, and its population is expected to double by 2040. This rapid growth presents significant challenges, especially in maintaining a migration corridor for approximately 2,000 mule deer between the Lake Mountains and the Oquirrh Mountains.
Identifying the Challenges • DWR identified an area to provide a high-use migration corridor through the city with minimal impact to landowners. The corridor was strategically designed to follow ridgelines and ephemeral streams, using existing city ordinances and vested open spaces.

## The Power of Communication and Diverse Collaborative Partnerships

• Communication: When DWR presented Eagle Mountain City (EMC) with the mule deer tracking data, the first partnership began to preserve this mule deer migration corridor.
• Community Outreach: The initial joint effort between DWR and EMC was to educate the community about the mule deer migration corridor. Their community outreach resulted in a strong collaborative partnership with several organizations.
• Common Goal: This diverse partnership united around the common goal of minimizing human-wildlife conflict while preserving the mule deer migration corridor.

## Roles of Nonprofit Organizations and Government Entities

• Nonprofit organizations have played a crucial role in advancing the mule deer migration corridor. They bring diverse interests and perspectives, actively engage the community, raise awareness, offer innovative solutions, and leverage local knowledge and resources.
• Government expertise brings professional knowledge, diverse resources, and the ability to implement policy and management strategies, enhancing the impact and importance of the mule deer migration corridor through collaboration and community outreach.

## Partnerships in Action

Diverse partnerships are essential for safeguarding the deer migration corridor from urban development through a multifaceted approach. The commitment from each partner is also exemplified through the following actions:
• Bureau of Land Management (BLM): The BLM follows the Secretarial Order 3362 directive to apply site-specific management activities identified in the UWAP (BLM 2018).
• DWR: DWR has integrated the mule deer migration corridor in the UWAP and have applied for funding for corridor improvements (Utah Department of Natural Resources
[DNR] 2025a; DWR and Wildlife Migration Initiative 2024).
• Utah National Guard Camp Williams and West Traverse Community Partnership: These entities actively contribute by assisting and funding conservation easements, restoration projects, and wildlife-impregnable fencing within their authorized priority zones (Conservation Fund 2025).
• Great Salt Lake (GSL) Sentinel Landscape Committee: The mule deer migration corridor holds integral importance within the GSL Sentinel Landscape because it enhances the potential for federal protection of the corridor (GSL Sentinel Landscape Committee 2024).
• Utah Department of Transportation (UDOT): UDOT continues to collect deer-vehicle collision data and participate in scientific research on the effectiveness of wildlife crossing structures and speed reduction measures to determine driver response (Wildlife Connectivity Institute 2025).
• EMC: EMC has taken proactive steps by passing a wildlife corridor overlay zone and incorporating the corridor into update master plans 
(EMC 2020
(EMC , 2022
(EMC , 2025))
.
• Eagle Mountain Nature and Wildlife Alliance: The Eagle Mountain Nature and Wildlife
Alliance's educational community outreach efforts have garnered numerous letters of support for grant funding from multiple stakeholders. Their research and recommendations contribute to effective policy and planning for the corridor, and they assist with ongoing scientific research and various migration corridor projects.
• Mule Deer Foundation: The Mule Deer Foundation is dedicated to advocating for mule deer and preserving and restoring habitat. They are among the largest financial supporters of wildlife-impregnable fencing along the migration corridor.
The Eagle Mountain, Utah, case study provides a compelling example of successful collaboration among diverse partners. Their efforts to manage urban development while preserving a crucial mule deer migration corridor are achieved through scientific research, community outreach, strategic design, planning, and policy implementation.

## Essential Conservation Actions to Address this Threat

Objective #1 for Housing and Urban Areas
Identify and protect open lands critical to native wildlife, plants, and invertebrates from being developed for housing and urban growth.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching this Objective

• Sufficient acreage of land tracts in key areas are in ownership, easement, or zoning status that precludes housing and urban development and designates conservation to safeguard crucial habitats.
• Monitored populations of indicator species, SGCNs, or other key species demonstrate stable or increasing trends, suggesting that habitat protection efforts are effectively supporting biodiversity.

## Potential Conservation Actions

## CODE ACTION

2.1.1 Manage game species to support sustainable populations that contribute to economic activity and the maintenance of natural resources.
3.1.2 Raise awareness through social media outreach about the importance of insect diversity to ecosystem health, including as an important food source for other wildlife, pollination services, natural pest control, water quality, decomposition, and nutrient cycling.

Threat: Improper Grazing -Livestock (current). Livestock grazing on both private and public lands plays a significant role in Utah's economy and the livelihoods of rural communities. In 2022, agricultural cash receipts totaled $2.8 billion, largely driven by livestock and dairy production, contributing approximately 2.6% of Utah's total gross domestic product (GDP) 
(English et al. 2025)
. This economic impact is particularly pronounced in rural areas, where livestock production supports local businesses and employment. As the predominant land management practice in the western United States, grazing influences nearly three-quarters of the region's land, including wilderness areas, wildlife refuges, national forests, and national parks. Consequently, it has a substantial impact to native ecosystems.
The ecological effects of livestock grazing can be profound, altering ecosystem composition, function, and structure 
(Franklin et al. 1981;
Jones 2000;
Li et al. 2021)
. Across western ecosystems, grazing can have direct and indirect negative consequences for vertebrates, plant communities, and invertebrate populations. Studies examining impacts to pollinators, particularly wild bees, butterflies, and flower flies, indicate that intensive grazing reduces pollinator abundance, richness, and diversity 
(Glenny et al. 2022)
. Although grazing can be managed sustainably, the historical variability of range management practices since the introduction of livestock to the West complicates efforts to assess long-term ecological costs 
(Fleischner 1994)
.
In landscapes and vegetation communities that evolved with natural grazing disturbance 
(Lazaro et al. 2016;
Shapira et al. 2020;
Tonietto and Larkin 2018;
Vulliamy et al. 2006)
.
Conversely, improper grazing has the potential to negatively affect key habitats and SGCNs.
Grazing may have a neutral impact to rapidly regenerating ecosystems (such as grasslands), but many of Utah's xeric habitats are characterized by slow growing vegetation that is negatively impacted by herbivory and soil disturbance. These disturbances risk destruction of insect nesting sites and water resources, a decrease in native plant reproduction, and the spread of invasive plant species 
(Glenny et al. 2022
). These ecological consequences can, in turn, affect the economic stability of ranching operations. Therefore, it is essential to implement grazing practices that balance economic viability with ecological sustainability.
Fortunately, modern livestock practices in Utah have substantially improved over the past century, and a range of stewardship practices exists that promote both economic and ecological sustainability. Many of these practices are eligible for technical and financial assistance, enabling ranchers to minimize environmental impacts while maintaining or even enhancing productivity. In some cases, sustainable practices can offer direct economic benefits; for instance, one study suggests that simply providing cattle with access to clean water can promote greater weight gain 
(Willms et al. 2002)
. By integrating responsible grazing strategies and prioritizing landscape health, it is possible to sustain both healthy ecosystems and resilient agricultural economies.

## Case Study: Three Creeks Grazing Management Changes -Envisioning Sustainable Grazing

The Three Creeks Grazing project was implemented in 2022 through a collaborative effort with the BLM, U.S. Forest Service (USFS), Utah Grazing Improvement Program, TLA, NRCS, Three Creeks Grazing LLC, and Working Lands Conservation. This project was an effort to change grazing management on 138,000 acres of public and private rangelands in Rich County, Utah, in a way that would benefit wildlife species and habitat as well as maintain livestock grazing and the local communities and economies that it supports.
Implementation occurred after years of planning, environmental assessments, and construction of needed infrastructure. Some of the goals of this project were to improve riparian habitat, water quality, and greater sage-grouse habitat and reduce erosion while increasing or maintaining available forage for livestock and continuing to permit livestock grazing at current owned wildlife management areas. Some grazers even purchase equipment and materials for these areas, which allows funding to be used elsewhere.
The areas that cattle graze have been converted from monotypic stands of phragmites to multifunctional pastures. These areas were previously unavailable to nearly all birds and are now heavily used by white-faced ibis, American avocet, and black-necked stilts. Long-billed curlew,
Wilson's phalarope, and snowy plover occasionally use these areas as well. American bittern are also thriving with the return to cattail and other native emergent vegetation types. Intensive phragmites grazing has opened many channels that were previously so overgrown that American white pelicans could not take off or land in them. There are now exponentially more opportunities for piscivorous birds to use these areas.
Grazing not only removes plant biomass but also provides other benefits, including breaking up dead plant material through trampling, stressing phragmites through grazing, and increasing biodiversity. Proper water management in combination with grazing can drought stress the areas, which allow salts to come to the surface. These salty conditions further stress the phragmites and allow native vegetation like saltgrass to come back and outcompete phragmites. Using this mutually beneficial method, state agencies around GSL have been able
to manage almost 20,000 acres that are being grazed each year with minimal effort beyond managing grazing contracts.
Cows Grazing Phragmites. Photograph by Rich Hansen, DWR, Weber County, 2024.

Grazing is managed such that ecological conditions in key habitats show measurable improvement in various indicators of rangeland health.

## Case Study: The Invasion of Cheatgrass

After years of grazing in the 1800s and early 1900s with little to no management, most Utah rangelands were overgrazed. Historic overgrazing and other factors such as altered fire regimes and climate change have contributed to current habitat challenges. Cheatgrass was introduced to Utah in the mid-1800s by European settlers and was able to fill the void left by overgrazing.
Since its introduction, cheatgrass has spread to most of Utah's rangelands. Similar issues have occurred in Utah's sagebrush key habitats. Historic grazing degraded the sagebrush ecosystem by removing the native bunchgrass and forb understory, which allowed the invasion of cheatgrass. Most range management projects in Utah address some of the effects of historic overgrazing and need to consider how the project could increase the potential for an invasion of annuals such as cheatgrass.
A recent successful project completed by the BLM, DWR, and others was to reseed approximately 12,000 acres of rangeland that burned in the 2020 Turkey Farm Road Fire in the Mojave Basin and Range ecoregion. A variety of reseeding efforts were needed to ensure success of the project. Rangeland in this ecoregion supports several SGCN species, including the Mojave desert tortoise and Gila monster, and soils are highly susceptible to wind and water erosion, invasion of noxious weeds, and the spread of cheatgrass after a fire. The primary objective of the seeding effort was to reintroduce perennial grasses, forbs, and shrubs after the fire that would establish and compete with invasive annual grasses. Aerial seeding was the primary method of treatment for the project burned area. The seed mix was prepared by the Great Basin Research Center and comprised seeds that were ecoregion specific and beneficial to the Mojave desert tortoise. In addition to aerial seeding, hand planting of seeds and seedlings were used to ensure successful coverage. Following the seeding, chaining was implemented,
where the terrain allowed, on 420 acres. In addition, state-owned lands in the treatment area received an herbicide treatment of Plateau to prevent cheatgrass invasion. Herbicide was sprayed via helicopter on 2,439 acres. Rehabilitation is a slow process in Utah's dry climate, but early monitoring results have been promising with the establishment of native vegetation over non-native vegetation. The project will be monitored for multiple years (for more information, see the 2020 Turkey Farm Road ESR, Project No. 5499 in the WRI database [DNR 2024b]).

## Essential Conservation Actions to Address this Threat

Collaborate with partners to restore landscapes degraded by historic grazing, particularly in areas where natural processes alone cannot achieve recovery or restore the landscape to a desired state. Restoration efforts should be prioritized in areas where ongoing degradation continues to negatively impact SGCNs and their key habitats.

Since its inception, the Penstemon Conservation Team has completed a mitigation plan, population monitoring plan, weed management plan, livestock management plan, and a As of 2024, the 6,802 in-kind hours and $364,256 spent on penstemon work (does not include hours and funding prior to 2017) has been an enlightening learning experience for all partners.

Largely because of this wide-ranging partnership and its accomplishments, the USFWS announced that the two penstemons were not warranted for listing under the ESA in 2022.

Fat-whorled pondsnail -1 -conflicts. The DWR WHAT was developed in 2024 to provide resources to environmental professionals (e.g., private consultants, developers, energy sector members), conservation partners (e.g., NGOs, federal and state agencies), educators, and the public (DWR 2024a).
This geographic analysis tool promotes improved wildlife planning efforts and environmental review through better access to data earlier in the decision-making process. Informed management actions in turn aid efforts such as optimal site selection, habitat restoration, land conservation, species listing prevention under the ESA, sustainable trail design, and improved wildlife connectivity.
The tool has an intuitive user interface and leverages holistic data sources to provide a one-stop shop for authoritative Utah wildlife and plant species information. The tool provides comprehensive reporting that goes beyond wildlife information to provide mitigation recommendations and important site and landscape context. It has the potential to serve as a portal for data ingestion allowing for analysis of private species survey data and user reference layers. Data can be tailored to individual user roles allowing for various levels of data sharing and custom uses. The WHAT can be found on the DWR website (DWR 2024a).

miles) of interstate highways and approximately 3,318 km (2,062 miles) of U.S. highways, along with 5,887 km (3,658 miles) of Utah state highways. These major roadways serve as the primary means for transporting goods and people throughout the state. Additionally, Utah's transportation network includes thousands of miles of lighter-duty roads, ranging from paved two-lane county roads to dirt or gravel backways that are maintained periodically.
Roads significantly impact wildlife in various ways. Historically, roads were constructed in locations that offered the most cost-effective and straightforward development, often in canyon bottoms or along creeks and rivers. This approach necessitated bridges and culverts to cross waterbodies, inadvertently creating barriers to fish passage. Furthermore, the stabilization of meandering waterways to prevent lateral movement has hindered natural flood events, which are essential for maintaining riparian vegetation. Such vegetation provides critical habitat for numerous aquatic and terrestrial wildlife species, native plants, and invertebrates.
Roadways can also fragment natural habitats, isolating wildlife populations and restricting their access to essential resources such as food and breeding grounds. Collisions with vehicles pose a major threat to wildlife, resulting in significant mortality rates for mammals, reptiles, birds, amphibians, and invertebrates. These fatalities impact population dynamics and gene flow.
Many animals experience elevated stress because of traffic noise and the presence of vehicles.
Avoidance of roads can influence community composition and dynamics, alter wildlife movement patterns, and result in reduced productivity in roadway-adjacent habitat.
The development and expansion of road networks facilitate the spread of non-native plants and animals, primarily due to disturbances and removal of native vegetation. Areas adjacent to roadways often experience altered vegetation, microclimate shifts, and other edge effects that disrupt species reliant on specific habitat conditions. Pollutants from roads directly degrade the habitat that supports wildlife. Chemical pollutants associated with high traffic volumes and deicing treatments can accumulate in the soils and vegetation of roadside habitats; these degraded resources in turn expose invertebrate wildlife to potentially toxic levels of these pollutants 
(Mitchell et al. 2020)
. Wildflowers near dirt roads exhibit reduced pollen loads as a result of dust deposition, reducing their ability to serve as a food resource for native pollinators 
(Waser et al. 2017)
. Traffic noise further interferes with wildlife behavior, communication, foraging, breeding, and migration patterns. Reduced landscape connectivity and restricted movements caused by roads contribute to increased mortality, lower reproduction rates, disrupted migratory routes, and ultimately, smaller and less viable wildlife populations.
Roadways are also significant contributors to light pollution-also known as skyglow-a form of habitat degradation that has cascading effects on entire ecosystems, particularly where natural
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 312
darkness is essential 
(Cieraad et al. 2022;
Gaston et al. 2021)
. Artificial lighting along roads and highways can both attract and repel wildlife, altering critical nocturnal behaviors such as migration, foraging, hunting, and courtship 
(Burt et al. 2023;
Cao et al. 2024;
Hillón-Salas et al. 2024;
Horton et al. 2023;
Van Doren et al. 2017)
. These disruptions extend to physiological processes, because artificial light interferes with circadian rhythms, navigation, reproduction, metabolic activity, and overall health across many species across both terrestrial and aquatic systems 
(Emmer et al. 2018;
Stanton and Cowart 2024;
Touzot et al. 2020)
.
Insects and migratory birds are particularly vulnerable. They are disoriented by bright lights, and millions are harmed or killed each year, making light pollution of key driver of both insect and avian declines 
(Adams et al. 2021;
Avalon et al. 2019)
. Moths, fireflies, beetles, katydids, stoneflies, mayflies, caddisflies, midges, and many other nocturnal insects may suffer from exhaustion, heat exposure, increased predation risk, and disrupted courtship behaviors.
Likewise, bats and other small mammals tend to avoid artificially lit areas or may be drawn to certain light wavelengths, resulting in functional habitat loss or alteration and a subsequent reduction in food availability and other critical resources 
(Beier 2006;
Hermans et al. 2024;
Rydell 2006)
.
Despite these impacts, light pollution can be effectively mitigated without compromising human Gardner Policy Institute 2017). This growth will likely result in significant modifications to existing roadways as well as the construction of new roads. As infrastructure expands, collaborative efforts can help avoid, minimize, or mitigate habitat fragmentation to the benefit of both wildlife and human populations. Potential strategies include the implementation of wildlife crossings, roadside fencing to guide wildlife to designated crossing points, habitat restoration in degraded areas, and reduced speed limits in wildlife-prone zones to decrease the risk of collisions.

## Case Study: Wildlife Crossings Create Opportunities for Safe Passage for all Wildlife While Bringing Partners Together to Provide Connectivity for People

Southern Utah is home to spectacular scenery that attracts thousands of visitors annually.
Although these landscapes attract tourists for their natural beauty, the area is also home to many wildlife species, including the premier Paunsaugunt interstate migratory deer herd that moves between Utah and Arizona. U.S. Highway 89 bisects the spring and fall migration routes, and hundreds of wildlife-vehicle collisions were occurring annually along that route.
In 

## Essential Conservation Actions to Address this Threat

Objective #1 for Roads -Transportation Network
New roads are planned and sited in areas where there is limited impact to native wildlife, plants, and invertebrates. When existing roads are maintained, barriers to wildlife movement are removed to allow for movement, and roadside habitats are carefully managed to minimize further degradation.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching this Objective

• UDOT works with DWR and other wildlife stakeholders to site and design new highways to avoid, minimize, and/or mitigate wildlife-vehicle collisions.
• UDOT works with DWR and other wildlife stakeholders to improve and maintain existing highways to avoid, minimize, and/or mitigate wildlife-vehicle collisions.
• UDOT, counties, and private landowners work with DWR and other wildlife stakeholders to allow and improve fish passage at road/stream crossings to avoid or minimize barriers to aquatic SGCN movement.
• UDOT works with municipalities, tribal nations, and other stakeholders to create an action plan to limit and reduce light pollution and build support for protecting dark skies.

## Potential Conservation Actions

## CODE ACTION

1.1.1 Maintain a zone (1.5-3.0 m [4.9-9.8 feet] wide) of closely mowed vegetation bordering road pavement to reduce plant and invertebrate exposure to roadway toxins; vegetation removal should ideally be limited to this zone (except for selective removal of invasive weeds).
1.1.1 Limit the frequency of roadside vegetation removal to no more than twice a year; mowing and similar vegetation removal should be conducted only in early spring or late fall.
1.1.1 Use arthropod-friendly mowing techniques (e.g., mow during the warmest time of the day, use flushing bars, use a minimum mow height of 30 to 40 centimeters (12-16 inches) in areas further from the road).

## CODE ACTION

1.1.1 Install (or retrofit) shielded streetlights with long-wavelength bulbs (i.e., warmer colors) in order to reduce light pollution and impacts to wildlife.
1.1.2 Use selective spot-treatments of pesticide to remove invasive species rather than blanket application; limit pesticide use to early in the growing season before plants have flowered.
1.2.3 Plant seed mixes along roadways that use local ecotype native seeds with a diversity of flowering plants. Prioritize planting after construction projects and at the periphery of existing habitat; limit creation of new habitat to areas where planting will be highly effective (i.e., sites that experience low pressure from invasive weeds, are situated on low-traffic roads, and connect areas of existing wildlife habitat). 3.1.3 Develop informational materials to help design (e.g., gradient, stream velocity) road and stream crossings for aquatic SGCN movements.
4.3.2 Enforce existing regulations on stream alteration.
6.1.1 Develop a list of priority locations for site or area protective designations.
6.1.1 Establish protected areas of roadside with remnant native plant communities that provide habitat for pollinator SGCN species.
6.3.1 Apply protective zoning designations to identified critical wildlife areas.
6.4.2 Develop a list of priority locations for restoration of habitats and natural processes.
6.5.1 Design and locate recreational infrastructure and facilities in appropriate locations that avoid or minimize adverse impacts to SGCNs and key habitats.
Threat: OHV Motorized Recreation. With its beautiful and diverse landscapes featuring dramatic alpine mountains, iconic red rock deserts, shimmering salt flats, and stunning rivers and lakes, Utah is a virtual mecca for outdoor recreation enthusiasts. From birdwatching and nature photography to skiing and snowmobiling, in 2022, outdoor recreation contributed more than $11.98 billion in direct visitor spending, employed more than 98,600 direct jobs, and led to $23.38 billion in total output for Utah's economy 
(Leaver 2024)
.
When enjoyed responsibly, OHV motorized recreation can have minimal impacts to wildlife populations and their habitats and can provide opportunities for people to observe and appreciate wildlife. OHV use without adequate consideration for its negative potential, however, can have severe impacts to wildlife, including changes to wildlife abundance and community composition, degraded habitats, and conflicts between wildlife stakeholders. The effects of recreation activities on wildlife can be roughly categorized as impacts to individuals and impacts to habitats.
Recreation impacts to individuals are direct effects from activities that may harm, kill, or displace an individual plant or animal, such as striking them with a vehicle. This also includes activities that unintentionally or intentionally harass individuals. Obvious behavioral responses to disturbance (such as a bird flushing from a nest) are often accompanied by invisible physiological responses (such as elevated levels of stress hormones). Although a single incident may not harm an individual, repeated disturbance can negatively impact survival or reproduction. Chronic exposure to recreation-imposed disturbance can be particularly harmful when critical life stage habitats such as dens, nests, maternity colonies, and hibernacula are disturbed, leading to reduced reproductive fitness, abandoned offspring, and increased mortality. Recreation activities can also directly impact individuals through the introduction of problematic plants, pathogens, and predators, and by creating artificial conditions that subsidize native predator populations.
OHV use can be a significant source of noise pollution, particularly from vehicles with twostroke engines and modified exhaust systems, which produce noise levels in high (>90) decibel ranges. Prolonged exposure to loud noise from OHVs directly impacts animal behavior, stress levels and heart rates, breeding and migratory patterns, and predator-prey interactions-leading to shifts in biological communities in popular OHV use areas. OHVs also contribute to air pollution by stirring up dust and particulate matter, and gas-powered vehicles tend to produce excessive exhaust. Leaking fluids from OHVs can be toxic to wildlife and persist in soil and waterways. Recreation impacts to habitats include changes to structural features and ecosystem function that in turn affect the behavior, survival, reproduction, and distribution of individuals. Common impacts are trampling plants, exposing roots, soil compaction, erosion (which introduces sediments that pollute water and reduce oxygen availability), sparking accidental wildfires, and creating faux microhabitats that could potentially lure animals into harm's way. Illegal trails created by OHV ruts fragment habitat and facilitate the dispersal of invasive species in sensitive areas. The impacts can be more significant in rare and or vulnerable habitats that are important to wildlife, such as wetlands, riparian areas, dune systems, desert soils, and alpine meadows, which are highly sensitive to disturbance and may have limited natural recovery processes.
For both forms of impact, the significance and magnitude of effect are related to the scope, intensity, and timing of the recreational activity. Individual responses, even for organisms within the same species, may vary greatly due to factors such as habitat condition, habituation, and life stage.
In general, recreation was not ranked as a high-impact threat to many individual species or habitats. Recreational activities rose to a priority threat because they are pervasive on the landscape and have myriad impacts to many species. In most instances, recreation alone is unlikely to drive a species to an ESA listing or extinction but can compound or exacerbate the effects of other threats to imperiled populations. OHV motorized recreation commonly serves as a vector for other priority threats, including invasive species and diseases, and is also a frequent source of fire ignitions.
As recreation continues to increase with the growth in both population and tourism, finding ways to manage it while providing for the needs of wildlife will be a large and growing challenge for the foreseeable future. In 2022, the Utah State Legislature recognized the growing importance of the outdoors to Utah's economic prosperity and quality of life and created the Utah Division of Outdoor Recreation. The conversion to electric-powered OHVs can mitigate many types of pollution (noise, air, soil), and educating OHV users about negative environmental impacts and responsible riding practices can greatly minimize harms to habitats and sensitive species. DWR has been coordinating closely with the new Division of Outdoor Recreation to minimize OHV and other outdoor-related conflicts with wildlife.
of partner commitments and rigorous annual population monitoring, the endemic Coral Pink Sand Dunes tiger beetle and its habitat are protected, allowing OHV access to safely continue in most other areas of the park.

## Essential Conservation Actions to Address this Threat

Objective #1 for Recreational Activities
Recreational opportunities are designed and presented in ways that encourage and promote responsible participation while also ensuring that wildlife and habitat impacts are kept at acceptably low levels.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching this Objective

• New trails and recreation facilities are designed to minimize wildlife impacts.
• New trails and recreation facilities are located in areas of existing disturbance and avoid areas of unfragmented wildlife habitat.
• SGCN populations remain stable in areas of high recreation interest, despite growth in the recreation sector of Utah's economy.
• Consumer preferences for electric OHVs and equipment conversions to electric powertrains reduce noise and emissions, reflecting the growing demand for sustainability, peaceful outdoor experiences, and reduced long-term operating costs.

Responsible recreation is promoted and encouraged via effective education and enforcement.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching this Objective

• Decrease in the mileage of user-created illegal trails.
• Decrease in law enforcement citations issued to outdoor recreationists.

## Natural System Modifications

Threats from actions that convert or degrade habitat in service of managing natural or seminatural systems, often to improve human welfare.
Within this very broad category, two Level-2 threats were ranked as priorities: fire and fire suppression and dams and water management/use. Each has been given its own table and associated threat and action descriptions.

Threat: Inappropriate Fire Frequency and Intensity. This includes all cases of fire regimes that have been disrupted from their historical range of natural variation. Some habitats are now burning with greatly increased frequency. These tend to be lower-elevation deserts and shrublands, and the cause is most commonly the presence of a continuous bed of fine fuels, which readily ignites and carries fire across vast distances. Continuous fine-fuel beds leave no
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 331
unburned mosaics (as burned, native bunchgrasses would have) and allow fire to jump significant obstacles such as rivers and interstate highways. These unnatural fire patterns often burn with a sterilizing thermal intensity, generating heat sufficient to kill all native seed stored in the soil bed, and potentially impacting soil microbes and mycorrhizae, which are important for native soil and plant ecology. Siltation and other water quality issues pertaining to fire pose a significant threat to aquatic invertebrates, particularly freshwater mussel populations, which
have been documented to suffer potential extirpation due to wildfire runoff (Lawrence et al.

## 2022).

Invasive, non-native annual grasses such as cheatgrass and red brome create these fine, continuous fuels. The most apparent landscape effects of increased lower-elevation fire frequency are a reduction in shrub and forb cover and an increase in the extent of areas dominated by annual grasses. These areas will remain at an ecological dead end until active restoration is conducted to restore habitat functions. Many wildlife and invertebrate species are negatively impacted by these landscape changes due to reductions in food and cover, while very few tolerate or benefit from the changes.
Other habitats are now burning with greatly decreased frequency. These tend to be mid-and high-elevation forests. The principal cause of less-frequent fire in upper-elevation landscapes was the suppression of almost every ignition under the "10 a.m. Policy" (in effect from , which decreed that every fire should be suppressed by 10 a.m. on the day after it was first spotted 
(Loveridge 1944
). Another cause is policy requirements created or imposed upon local, state, and federal land fire management agencies. These policies have been implemented nationally through a highly effective interagency wildfire response system. This system has tremendous logistical and tactical capacities and is enabled by massive public expenditures; however, before 2002, when the Wildland Fire Leadership Council was created (USFS 2025), there was no coherent, national strategy for allowing some natural fire progression and no recognition of the risk, unsustainability, and unnaturalness of the degree of fuel accumulation that the wholesale use of this response and suppression system had caused. The National Strategy: The Final Phase in the Development of the National Cohesive Wildland Fire Management Strategy described its "vision for the next century: To safely and effectively extinguish fire, when needed; use fire where allowable; manage our natural resources; and as a Nation, live with wildland fire" (Wildland Fire Leadership Council 2014).
A secondary cause of reduced fire frequency is land cover fragmentation caused by human developments such as reservoirs, subdivisions, and highways. Fragmentation facilitates fire suppression by preventing fires from burning as far as they once would have. Fragmentation complicates and impedes use of prescribed fire by introducing numerous risks and complications to fire management.
The most apparent vegetative effect of decreased mid-and high-elevation fire frequency is a reduction in the area occupied by young stands of deciduous tree species and by shadeintolerant conifers, shrubs, and herbaceous plants. Concurrently, there has been an increase in the density and acreage of shade-tolerant and/or fire-intolerant conifer forests and an aging and eventual replacement of deciduous forests. As with excess low-elevation fire, many wildlife species are negatively impacted by the extensive landscape changes resulting from too infrequent mid-and high-elevation fire.
Another negative consequence of less-frequent fire at higher, more productive elevations is excessive fuel buildup, which can result in larger and hotter fires. When a fire ignites and suppression is not successful, it can be catastrophically damaging to various values and interests. Of particular concern are effects on aquatic ecosystems and developed water supplies, both of which can be destroyed by catastrophic floods and debris flows triggered by heavy rains following severe fire events.
Fire suppression is necessary in some circumstances to protect property and human life. The unmet need is to operationalize, at a multi-decadal and continental scale, a recognition that not every fire needs to be suppressed. Excessive fuel buildup prohibits simple reversion to a let-itburn policy. The growing problem of catastrophic megafires can be solved by a systematic campaign of active restoration via mechanical fuel-reduction treatments and prescribed fire to safely return wildfire as a viable, natural, cost-effective means of maintaining necessary patterns of ecological succession across the landscape. The fire and land management policy environment will require significant change to enable this solution to occur.

## Case Study: Prioritizing Fuels Treatments with Landscape Conservation Forecasting

In 2007, TNC, in collaboration with federal agencies and technology contractors, expanded the national LANDFIRE program's state-and-transition simulation models of reference vegetation to develop a planning tool known as Landscape Conservation Forecasting (LCF) 
(Low et al. 2010;
Provencher et al. 2013;
Provencher et al. 2021)
. LCF enhances the ecological health of large landscapes by assisting managers of national forests, national parks, BLM field offices, and private entities such as mining companies to identify cost-effective land management strategies. These strategies are designed to achieve the lowest ecological departure from reference condition, maximize ecological returns on investments, and optimize other stakeholders' priorities, such as wildlife habitat suitability and carbon stock-and-flow metrics.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 333
The foundation of LCF consists of two primary maps: 1) the distribution of ecological systems (i.e., dominant vegetation types expected based on the physical environment); and 2) the current vegetation classes within each ecological system, categorized by succession, structure, and whether they align with or deviate from reference (pre-settlement) conditions.
Ecological integrity is assessed with various metrics, and the primary indicator is ecological departure. Ecological departure measures the differences between: 1) expected distribution of vegetation classes under reference conditions, and 2) the actual distribution of vegetation classes in the current landscape 
(Blankenship et al. 2015;
Blankenship et al. 2021;
Hann and Strom 2003;
Provencher et al. 2024;
Swaty et al. 2022)
. Although the goal is to guide management toward reducing this dissimilarity, achieving a perfect match with reference conditions may not be financially feasible.
LCF operates using computerized ecological state-and-transition simulation models 
(Daniel et al. 2016
) that depict how each ecological system functions naturally and in response to human management. These models simulate the effects of natural disturbance (e.g., fire) and human activities (e.g., cattle grazing) on vegetation dynamics over time. Each management action is associated with a known cost per unit area and success/failure rates derived from experts' knowledge and literature 
(Provencher et al. 2021)
. The complexity of these simulations can be enhanced by incorporating spatial climate time series, and factors such as topography, wind direction lightning strike data, land management constraints, and equipment availability 
(Daniel et al. 2016;
Provencher et al. 2021)
.
Once maps, models, and metrics are established, TNC collaborates with agency staff and stakeholders to evaluate outcomes of various management strategies over medium-to longrange periods (typically 20 or 50 years). The principal focus is on reducing vegetation classes with excessive acreages, such as overabundant native or uncharacteristic classes, while increasing those that are underrepresented with the goal of aligning more closely with the refence conditions.
Model outputs are primarily expressed in terms of predicted ecological departure and can be compared to scenarios in which no active management occurs. This comparison quantifies the improvements in ecological integrity resulting from active management versus minimal management approaches. Additionally, vegetation dynamics over time can be analyzed to deepen the understanding of ecological responses to different treatments.
The benefits of active management, as predicted by simulations, include healthier vegetation conditions and reduced risks of large, high-severity wildfires, which threaten wildlife and infrastructure. The cost of achieving these benefits is also reported, allowing for an evaluation of the cost effectiveness of management actions over 10-or 50-year periods. The Ecological Return on Investment (E-ROI) metric, developed by TNC, quantifies this cost effectiveness by calculating the difference in ecological departure between active and custodial (control) scenarios multiplied by the size of the ecological system and divided by the total cost of management 
(Low et al. 2010;
Provencher et al. 2013)
. Comparing E-ROI values across different management scenarios provides a standardized measure to guide decision making. Although selecting actions with the highest E-ROI is generally recommended, other considerations, such as public safety through hazardous fuel reduction, may influence final management decisions.
To date, approximately 20 large-scale LCF applications have been completed or are underway, primarily in Nevada, eastern California, and Utah. Each new LCF project builds on previous implementations and incorporates scientific advancements and innovations. As LCF projects are completed across the Intermountain West, they collectively contribute to large-scale planning efforts aimed at improving ecological health across the region.

## Essential Conservation Actions to Address this Threat

Objective #1 for Inappropriate Fire Frequency and Intensity
Fire is suppressed in habitats that are now prone to large and destructive fires that impact native vegetation; habitats are being actively managed (i.e., treated) to reduce components or factors that promote risk of catastrophic fire, such as cheatgrass, excessive conifer encroachment, or unnaturally large stands of mature Gambel oak.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching this Objective

• Many land and resource management agencies are conducting long-range planning to identify where and when to implement fuel-reduction treatments aimed at invasive annual grasses and pervasive conifer encroachment, among other treatments.
• The distribution of vegetation classes in susceptible habitats is becoming more similar to (less departed from) their natural reference distribution, especially due to reduction of uncharacteristic, annual-grass-dominated classes.
• Annual acreage treated for fuels reduction is adequate and appropriate.
• Treatment unit sizes are appropriate for the habitat type and its natural disturbance regime.
