## Dams and Water Management/Use

Table 
38
 summarizes the numbers of species and habitats impacted by this priority Level-2 threat by all nested priority Level-3 threats and the level of impacts (very high, high, or medium).
This Level-2 threat is further subdivided into numerous Level-3 threats, 12 of which have impacts above the low level. Six of these were ranked as priorities: presence of diversions, dam/reservoir operation, channelization/bank alteration (direct, intentional), groundwater pumping, agricultural/municipal/industrial water usage, and water allocation policies. Note: This Level-2 threat has more than one priority Level-3 threat; consequently, some threat impacts have a score larger than one.
The threat of dams and water management/use includes diversions and withdrawal of both surface and groundwater from natural systems. This is a broad-scale threat that encompasses To provide sufficient water supply to Utah's human population, a complex network of dams, diversions, canals, wells, and pipelines has been built to store and supply water to meet agricultural, municipal, and industrial needs. As Utah's population grows, conservation of water will become increasingly important to ensure sufficient water for human use as well as for healthy wildlife and habitats.
DWR's stake in water conservation is that water is essential for all wildlife. This is most obviously a need for fish and rivers, but all wildlife and all habitats rely on water. DWR has worked with many partners on water-related issues. This has been accomplished through endangered species recovery programs, conservation agreements, water rights regulations, stream alteration permits, water acquisition, and many other processes.
Many of the priority UWAP threats are not clearly bounded, simple, or linear with respect to cause and effect. The threat of dams and water management/use exemplifies this challenge.
To present and address the water-related problems facing species and habitats as cleanly and simply as possible, actions have been organized in the following way:
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 341
• Actions related to dams and diversions will focus on correcting barriers to fish movement and other mechanisms of habitat and population fragmentation.
• Actions related to water use will focus on retaining or acquiring water to sustain the habitat at critical times.
• Actions related to dam and reservoir operation and sediment transport imbalance will focus on maintaining or restoring appropriate temperature and sediment regimes and timing of flows.
To minimize repeating the same objectives and actions multiple times, actions are mostly listed under just one threat (though it would not be incorrect to list them under multiple threat categories). For example, the action of removing a diversion (listed under Level-3 threat 7.2.2
Presence of Diversions), would affect fish movement, but it could also affect the amount, and thus temperature, of water in the system; therefore, it could also qualify as an action under dam/reservoir operation. Changing the amount of water in a stream affects a stream's capacity to transport sediment; therefore, removing a diversion could also qualify as an action under dam/reservoir operation.
Managers who are planning projects in streams or rivers to address one specific threat should examine the actions under all dams and water management/use Level-3 threat categories to determine if actions identified under other threats could help address their threat and improve their project.
The case studies and objectives for more specific Level-3 threats discussed below provide examples of cases where DWR and partners have worked together to address specific threats.
This collaboration has allowed for the development of more comprehensive approaches to identify and prioritize threats and actions on a broader scale. Comprehensive, watershed-scale management and restoration plans were first developed for the Weber River (USU 2014) and San Rafael River 
(Laub et. al. 2013)
, and community watershed groups have been established in many other locations. The Weber and San Rafael Rivers are examples of two very different rivers and watersheds (with different outlets, levels of development, degree and kind of fishing interest and pressure, native species communities, etc.); however, these two plans were both developed in close consultation with local stakeholders to identify native species' needs and broadly acceptable, yet still specific, goals and objectives. These plans discuss all the Level-3 threats discussed below as well as additional watershed-specific threats that are not statewide priorities but are very important locally. They also pull together information known about the watershed and species needs, fishing pressure, agricultural and municipal water needs, and other information specific to each watershed. With this information and buy-in from the local
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 342
stakeholders, the working groups can prioritize efficient work areas where threats can be addressed most effectively. These watershed-scale plans were the model upon which additional plans have been developed.
Threat: Presence of Diversions. Dams and water diversions are a broad category of structures that can remove water from a stream and/or create a physical impediment to the movement and passage (upstream or downstream) of fish, sediment, and coarse woody debris. Even small water diversions can completely fragment a previously intact natural system that depends on migratory movement of fishes. Some diversions also have design features that may entrain or entrap fish in canals, or otherwise remove them from the natural channel, so that a potentially significant portion of the breeding population is lost through annual water management actions.
Some diversions have been abandoned and are falling into disrepair. Although these diversions are no longer actively used to remove water from a stream, they often still present a bidirectional impediment to fish passage and may also restrict the downstream movement of sediment or coarse woody debris, both of which are fundamentally important in aquatic ecology.
This threat, like all the threats nested within dams and water management/use, disproportionately impacts aquatic species and their habitats. Aquatic species represent 70% of the SGCN targets with medium, high, or very high impacts from this threat.
Effective conservation of water for both humans and wildlife demands careful monitoring of the subsequent effects of water control, including thermal, hydrologic, and sedimentary alteration of waterways downstream of dams and diversion structures. Aquatic invertebrate SGCNs are particularly vulnerable to alterations in waterways from diversion and dam presence. One such species is the giant salmonfly, a keystone species that provides a vital prey and nutrient base for native aquatic and terrestrial species across multiple trophic levels 
(Lecerf and Richardson 2011;
Walters et al. 2018
) such as native fish, migratory birds, and other insectivores. Changes in host fish migration and general presence of host fish due to water diversions and dams are also significant threats for freshwater mussel SGCNs 
(Vaughn and Taylor 1999
) such as western pearlshell and winged floater.
Water diversion structures are much smaller than dams and are typically more widely distributed throughout a watershed. Although a single dam may have a greater negative impact to wildlife than a single diversion, diversions are vastly more numerous and widely distributed and have the potential to cause a greater scope of impact for many species. Diversions tend to be less permanent structures than dams, and there are frequent opportunities across the landscape to influence how and when they are built, removed, or renovated.
Although DWRe records both dams and diversions, they have not been fully mapped in relation to their impact to fish passage. UWAP partners are beginning to do this in some watersheds.
For example, diversions and other fish barriers have been mapped in the Weber River watershed 
(Thompson and Burnett 2019)
 (Figure 
56
).
This map highlights the diversity, abundance, and extent of diversions in this watershed and was created for prioritizing which diversions most urgently need to be altered to permit fish passage. This type of mapping still needs to occur for many other Utah watersheds. All new diversions on fish-bearing streams should be made passable to the desirable aquatic species that are present or planned for reintroduction; however, water diversion structures are already abundant in the landscape and retrofitting them for fish passage is generally very costly.
Therefore, assessment and prioritization of existing diversions for fish passage retrofittingwhether diversions are abandoned or still in operation-needs to occur. Recently, the Barrier Assessment and Implementation Tool (BAIT) has been developed to standardize the process to evaluate instream structures to determine their level of impact to fish passage. BAIT will allow UWAP partners to address this threat in a consistent manner. spawning and overwintering habitats. Water and water management has been identified in the UWAP as the most pervasive threat to aquatic SGCNs and their habitats, and this is especially true in the Weber River. The habitat for BCT and green suckers has been fragmented by more than 300 barriers; most of these barriers are from irrigation diversions, but some are from road and utility crossings 
(Thompson and Burnett 2019)
 (see Figure 
56
).
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 345
In 2010, DWR and Trout Unlimited began undertaking barrier removal in the Weber River for native fish as a priority conservation action. Initially, the effort to reconnect habitat was slow and the lack of relationships with stakeholders such as water users, government agencies, private landowners, and utility companies was hampering progress with habitat reconnection.
New barriers were being built at a faster rate than barriers were being removed. To build these relationships, a steering committee, composed of Utah agencies working in the Weber River watershed, was formed to secure a small grant, hire a consulting firm, organize stakeholder meetings to identify broad stakeholder priorities, and write a watershed plan (USU 2014) that ultimately identified BCT and green sucker as priority conservation targets. The watershed plan and subsequent stakeholder meetings developed a framework for the Weber River Partnership.
This partnership holds an annual symposium where larger watershed issues are discussed. The symposium has provided a platform where fisheries managers have been able to tell the story of these two SGCN and the importance of habitat connectivity 
(Thompson and Burnett 2019)
.
Through collaborative relationships with nontraditional partners, the relevance of fisheries in the Weber River has been realized. Further relevance to the watershed is evidenced by the development of a wide range of on-the-ground actions. Fish passage has been re-established at multiple mainstem Weber River diversions and with most fluvial BCT spawning tributaries.
Largely due to good data and a newly formed partnership with PacifiCorp, a large, multimilliondollar fish ladder is scheduled to be built in 2026 as part of a Federal Energy Regulatory
Commission relicensing project at a small hydroelectric dam in Weber Canyon. Additional projects are in various stages of development, and water users continue to contact fishery managers to discuss irrigation diversion reconstruction projects that incorporate fish passage 
(Thompson and Burnett 2019)
.

## Essential Conservation Actions to Address this Threat

Objective #1 for Presence of Diversions
Native fish can move past water diversion barriers where needed or desired to allow fulfillment of necessary life cycles.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching this Objective

• Water districts and irrigation companies work with DWR and other wildlife stakeholders to design, construct, or reconstruct irrigation diversion to allow upstream and downstream SGCN movement at priority barriers and minimize entrainment in irrigation ditches.
• Number of diversions removed or made passable for fish.
• Increased miles of connected or accessible habitat.
• Number of diversion intakes having screens or other technology to prevent or reduce fish entrainment.
• Repeated, site-specific monitoring of aquatic macroinvertebrate communities that demonstrates improved ecosystem health (e.g., clean and oxygenated water, sufficient shelter substrates, adequate food sources, cold water temperatures), and stable populations of keystone species (e.g., stoneflies, caddisflies, mayflies).

## • Universities

Threat: Dam/Reservoir Operation. One significant and ongoing impact of dams is the alteration of the timing and magnitude of the natural hydrograph. Many fish species rely on natural flow patterns for key life history events. For example, the rising limb of the hydrograph caused by snowmelt runoff often triggers spawning movements and leads to seasonal flooding of floodplains and backwaters, which provide essential rearing habitats for juvenile fish species.
Dams also substantially affect water temperature and downstream sediment transport. By trapping the majority of stream sediment, dams contribute to the degradation and downcutting of downstream channels, often causing more extensive habitat loss than the upstream impoundment itself. These changes can severely limit native species' ability to grow and survive in these altered conditions and simultaneously create favorable conditions for non-native cold water predatory fish, which can severely impact juvenile native fish densities. Deposition of fine sediment downstream of dams and diversions can cause significant reproductive and recruitment failure in freshwater mussels 
(Williams and Searles Mazzacano 2022)
.
Water storage for agriculture and municipal use remains a critical need for communities that depend on these resources. In some cases, DWR and partners have successfully collaborated with water users and dam managers to purchase or lease water and adjust water release schedules to better mimic a more natural hydrograph while still fulfilling essential water supply needs. With GSL reaching a record low level of 1,276.7 m (4,188.5 feet) in November 2022, the State of Utah has enacted additional water conservation measures and purchased or leased water rights for the lake. As water continues to be allocated throughout the GSL watershed, new opportunities may emerge to time water releases in ways that benefit stream ecosystems.

## Case Study: Identifying Functional Flows for the Great Salt Lake Basin

California has developed functional flows for many of their river basins (California Environmental Flows Technical Team 2021). This approach characterizes and quantifies stream
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 348
flow targets that are ecologically critical to the support and maintenance of habitat, water quality, and aquatic life. Functional flows are essentially the stream flow needed by stream reach to maintain functional river processes that maintain water quality and healthy fisheries.
The development of functional flows for the GSL basin was funded through the Great Salt Lake Basin Integrated Plan (DWRe 2024).
There are many competing interests for water in the GSL basin. Ideally, the timing and magnitude of water deliveries to GSL also would maximize water quality, habitat, and aquatic life in upstream tributaries. The appropriate magnitude, timing, and rate of change of flow to maintain or restore these benefits, however, has yet to be determined. Functional flows represent distinct aspects of the annual stream flow hydrograph that sustain ecological, geomorphic, or biogeochemical functions and that support the life history and habitat needs of aquatic biota. The functional flow metrics are not directly linked to individual or specific organisms or groups but are associated with specific biological and ecosystem processes 
(Yarnell et al. 2020)
. Functional flows are characterized using a suite of flow metrics calculated at reference stream flow gages. These flow metrics can be used to help establish flow-based conservation objectives for GSL basin streams and wetlands. Delivering water to GSL can also support upstream water quality and aquatic life uses for multiple ecosystem benefits.
Generating reach-scale estimates of natural functional flows for all streams in the GSL basin will quantify ecosystem water demands and enable deliveries and tradeoffs with human water uses to be modeled. This provides needed information to develop more efficient and effective restoration strategies in degraded waterbodies. Understanding the ecological functions of stream flow along a longitudinal gradient from streams to rivers to wetlands to the lake will help identify locations throughout the basin where altering the timing or quantity of water delivery can improve ecological conditions. Finally, functional flows will be evaluated for wet, moderate, and dry year conditions so that water and other natural resource managers can take advantage of wet years or mitigate adverse effects in dry years 
(Anderegg et al. 2023)
. Functional flow metrics will provide critical input about how limited water supplies can be more effectively managed to be ecologically protective 
(Null et al. 2022)
.
The Functional Flow Project will support stakeholders by developing functional flows for Utah's GSL basin. To support the Great Salt Lake Basin Integrated Plan (DWRe 2024), the Functional Flow Project will provide tools and data sets to 1) identify key functional flows and associated ecological indicators, 2) develop a geodatabase of reach-scale natural functional flow metric predictions, and 3) provide a library of regional flow-ecology relationships. Future applications of the Functional Flow Project include evaluating hydromodification from functional flows and evaluating water management alternatives for delivering functional flows. Applying a functional flows framework will enable stakeholders to estimate the nature and extent of ecological modifications resulting from hydrologic alterations and make recommendations about flows that would most benefit GSL basin rivers, streams, wetlands, and the lake itself.

## Essential Conservation Actions to Address this Threat

Objective #1 for Dam/Reservoir Operation Natural hydrographs 
(e.g., timing, duration, temperature)
 are restored or mimicked in priority stream reaches below dams and reservoirs.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching this Objective

• Water temperatures are appropriate.
• Sediment transport regime is sufficient to maintain essential downstream habitats.
• The downstream hydrograph is sufficient to promote suitable habitat conditions and characteristics that allow for the complete life cycle of the target SGCNs.
• Demographically adequate fish reproduction and recruitment are occurring.
• Decreased frequency of dewatered systems.
• Stable or increasing water levels in GSL.
Objective #2 for Dam/Reservoir Operation
Opportunities are found and taken to modify or remove reservoir infrastructure or modify outflow management to simulate or return natural sediment transport.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching this Objective

• Working to restore a more natural hydrograph (spring peak) is part of reaching sediment balance.
• Sediment transport supports natural ecology or sufficiently promotes native species reproduction.
• Rewetting drained wetlands creates a supply of permanent artificial lentic pools, that support diverse and abundant aquatic macroinvertebrate communities and contribute to habitat value of these restored ecosystems.
• Build new or retrofit old dams with proven features designed to pass sediment.
• Work with water users and reservoir operators to shepherd water in larger pulses to mobilize sediment and transport.
• Work with water users and reservoir operators to remove (and pass) sediment from reservoirs where loss of storage due to sediment input is a concern.
Objective #3 for Dam/Reservoir Operation Aquatic invasive species are restricted in distribution to the locations they occupy in 2025.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching this Objective

• Avoid unintentional promotion or spread of existing invasive and problematic species through unrelated actions.
• Avoid the spread of existing diseases and pathogens or establishment of new diseases and pathogens through education, planning, management, and/or regulation.
• Conduct targeted predator management and/or screen outflows to prevent escapement from fisheries.
• Allocate more enforcement resources to illegal species introductions. • USFS

## • Tribal nations

Threat: Channelization/Bank Alteration (direct, intentional). This threat is one of the most significant to the Streams and Rivers Key Habitat, affecting a wide range of taxa and habitats; however, the overall impact is generally lower than other water use threats, because no taxa or habitats have been assigned a very high impact rating. Fish are the most affected taxonomic group, with 43% of all medium, high, and very high water-related threats impacting them. Many fish species rely on complex habitats-such as oxbows, pools, side channels, and backwatersto meet essential life stage requirements, including temperature regulation, food availability, cover, and water quality conditions. Channelization and bank alteration have also been shown to reduce habitat viability for keystone aquatic invertebrate species 
(Birrell et al. 2019)
.
Channelization and bank alteration projects are commonly implemented for flood control, water delivery facilitation, and the protection of homes, roads, land, and other resources. However, these modifications often reduce essential habitat complexity, making it more challenging for many species to forage, evade predators, seek refuge from high or fast flows, and successfully reach adulthood. Additionally, terrestrial SGCNs are also affected, because these alterations degrade or eliminate riparian areas: critical habitats on which more than 75% of bird species in Utah partially or entirely depend 
(Norvell et al. 2005)
. Riparian zones also provide vital roosting and foraging habitat for bats.
Although stream alteration permits are now required for such projects, many waterways have already been significantly degraded due to past activities. Stream alteration permits can be denied or modified based on potential impacts to species, but managers are not always consulted, and the permitting agency may not always be aware of species-specific concerns.
Although flood control and resource protection remain necessary, these projects must be approached more holistically to support healthy wildlife populations and habitats and prevent future listings under the ESA. Improved coordination between permitting agencies and wildlife managers is critical to achieving these goals.

## Case Study: The Provo River Delta

June sucker, an ESA-listed species recently downlisted from endangered to threatened, is endemic to Utah Lake. This species primarily relies on the tributaries to Utah Lake for spawning in May to June. Historically, June sucker larvae would drift down into deltas at the interface of the river and lake where they would experience cover for predator avoidance and warmer temperatures that resulted in abundant food and conditions conducive to fast growth. These constructed through the lowered dike (JSRIP 2023a). This reconnected the lake with historical Skipper Bay, allowing Utah Lake to expand eastward toward its historical shoreline. On March 2, 2023, the Provo River was diverted from a point just downstream of Lakeshore Bridge into the newly constructed delta (JSRIP 2023b). The river eventually made its way through the delta and new outlet channels into Utah Lake over the next week.
In the first June sucker spawning event after the river was diverted, a near record number of June suckers migrated through the new delta into the Provo River. Nearly 6,300 tagged June suckers were detected entering the delta, from April 1 through July 12; however, because only a small portion of adult June suckers were tagged, the total number that entered the delta is likely five to 10 times greater. DWR is monitoring the delta and during the first few years, juvenile June sucker have been documented.

## Essential Conservation Actions to Address this Threat

Objective #1 for Channelization/Bank Alteration (direct, intentional)
Complex habitats and floodplain connections are restored or maintained in selected rivers/streams.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching this Objective

• Reduction in artificially channelized or straightened stream miles.
• When making decisions on stream alteration permits, DWRe solicits and, when feasible, incorporates suggestions from DWR and other agencies or organizations with natural resource management or wildlife conservation interests.
• When planning emergency watershed protection projects, the NRCS coordinates with DWR to ensure those projects are not being planned with objectives in conflict with the purposes of a restoration project carried out in the same location or negatively impacts stream processes.
• Maintain ecologically relevant flow regimes that support healthy habitat conditions required for diverse aquatic macroinvertebrate communities (as a key food source for aquatic and riparian wildlife).
• Private landowners are encouraged to consider the downstream impacts from a single stream alteration permit project and make better voluntary decisions on projects permitted through the process, reducing erosion and incursion into the floodplain downstream. Many UWAP aquatic key habitats rely on groundwater, and some will be lost if groundwater levels continue to decline. Wetland habitats in Utah's west desert are especially reliant on groundwater; these habitats contain multiple mollusk and amphibian SGCNs and are the habitats that all remaining extant least chub rely upon.

## Case Study: Snake Valley Hydrologic Monitoring

Spring and wetland complexes in the Snake Valley of western Utah provide critical habitats for several SGCNs, including Columbia spotted frog, least chub, and several mollusk species. All are fully aquatic and depend on spring-fed, perennial pools or rills for refuge from temperature extremes, droughts, low oxygen levels, and protection from predators 
(Arkle and Pilliod 2015;
Sáenz 2014
). Additionally, least chub and Columbia spotted frog depend on shallow, seasonally flooded areas with dense vegetation 
(Grover 2019;
Hovingh 1993)
 for spawning and larval development. The extent and quality of these refuge and spawning habitats within the Snake Valley wetlands depend on surface water extent, which in turn depends on surrounding groundwater levels 
(Grover 2019)
. Declining groundwater levels 1) reduce the amount of suitable refuge or spawning habitats, 2) limit the duration of seasonal flooding impacting the development of Columbia spotted frog and least chub larvae, and 3) isolate and fragment populations 
(Goodwin et al. 2021;
Grover 2019)
.

## • Landowners

Threat: Agricultural/Municipal/Industrial Water Usage. This is the main driver for all the dams and water management/use threats. All people need water, and society has created a legal framework and a physical delivery system that prioritizes getting water to people. The result is surface water ecosystems that no longer function naturally in terms of water and sediment transport regimes or as fish and wildlife habitat. In addition, groundwater-dependent aquatic ecosystems often no longer provide the conditions necessary for the species that historically occurred there, due to reductions in spring discharge and stream baseflows because of groundwater pumping. Organic pollution from agricultural water usage can also contribute to hypoxia in oxygen-sensitive aquatic invertebrate species 
(Frakes et al. 2021)
, which reduces ecological viability for native aquatic species.
This threat is also directly tied to the threat of water allocation policies, which was seen in the UWAP threat assessment as an enabling factor affecting numerous SGCNs and key habitats in all areas of the state. The threat assessment implicates water policy, and the heavy use it permits, in complete dewatering of some stream systems, a pervasive state of diminished flows and levels, and consequent widespread habitat loss and fragmentation. The alteration of flow regimes is responsible for several extirpations of prey species for native trout and migratory birds occurring in the lower Blacksmith Fork and Spanish Fork Rivers and possibly the Provo River. Future extirpations from dewatering are likely if trends continue 
(Birrell et al. 2019;
Frakes et al. 2021)
.
Case Study: Coordinating Water Deliveries to Achieve Fish Passage and Maintain Fish Habitat
On the lower Duchesne River, the Central Utah Water Conservancy District, DWR, irrigators, and water user groups coordinate to ensure the delivery of instream flows targeted to achieve fish passage requirements and maintain habitat. This same collection of partners also completed a candidate conservation agreement with an assurances/safe harbor agreement to provide regulatory assurances to landowners and water users prior to the recent restoration of fish passage around the Myton Diversion, which allows fish access to an additional 32 kilometers (20 miles) of riverine habitat.

Aquatic key habitats (especially at those locations important for SGCNs) contain sufficient water to maintain a functioning aquatic ecosystem that supports the conservation target(s).

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching this Objective

• Implement the functional flows framework for the GSL basin streams.
• Develop and implement functional flows for the remainder of Utah beyond the GSL basin.
• Water flows mimic natural conditions.
• Water quality is sufficient to maintain ecosystem health.
• Quantity and timing of flows, groundwater inputs, and surface water levels are appropriate for the aquatic system.
• Healthy aquatic ecosystems (invertebrates, native fish, etc.).
• All age classes of the native fish community are represented.
• Stable or increasing water levels in GSL. Many SGCNs and key habitats are impacted by water allocation policies; however, the worstaffected SGCN are the fish, with 43% of all the medium, high, or very high impacts.

## Case Study: Acquiring Instream Flow to Sustain the Habitat of Multiple Species of Greatest Conservation Need

The least chub was a candidate for federal listing under the ESA until August 2014, when the USFWS made the decision to remove the species from candidate status. Many actions (implemented under a candidate conservation agreement) supported the USFWS's decision, including the acquisition of an instream flow for the largest existing population of the species at the Bishop Springs Wetland Complex in western Utah's Snake Valley.
A small spring-fed reservoir is the primary water supply for Bishop Springs Wetland Complex, which also supports populations of Columbia spotted frog and northern leopard frog, which are SGCNs in Utah. A single property owner had water rights associated with this reservoir for agricultural purposes. When the landowner exercised these water rights, up to two-thirds of the wetland complex was dewatered, resulting in the mortality of thousands of least chub as well as impacts to Columbia spotted frog. DWR worked closely with the landowner and USFWS to develop a candidate conservation agreement with assurances that allowed for DWR to purchase and install infrastructure to convert the inefficient flood irrigation operation into a more efficient pressurized irrigation system in return for the landowner agreeing to supply the saved water for least chub. Through this agreement, the landowner also received assurances from the USFWS that if least chub were federally listed in the future, the landowner would not be required to take additional conservation measures on this property. In October 2008, the Utah State Engineer approved an exchange application and granted an instream flow to DWR for the Bishop Springs Wetland Complex.

• Agencies and NGOs are aware of and use all instream flow authorities for which they are already eligible.
• Agencies and NGOs inventory and communicate any necessary instream flow authorities for which they are not yet eligible.

Aquatic key habitats (especially those with occurrences of SGCNs) contain sufficient water to maintain a functioning aquatic ecosystem that supports the conservation target(s).

• Water distribution considers functional flow targets.

• All age classes of the native fish community are represented.

## Potential Conservation Actions

Threat: Invasive Wildlife Species (non-native). Invasive non-native animals are a significant threat to many of Utah's ecosystems. Not all non-native (introduced) species are considered invasive. Some species have been intentionally and legally introduced to support hunting and sport fishing and, when properly managed, their impacts to native species and habitat can be minimized. A species is classified as invasive when it becomes dominant over one or more desirable species or significantly alters key environmental aspects such as water flow, community composition, or disease transmission. In addition to their economic and occasional human health consequences, invasive species disrupt ecological systems through various mechanisms, including direct predation and competition for resources. Although the specific impacts may vary, UWAP objectives and management actions remain largely consistent across different invasion scenarios.
The management approach for an invasive species-whether prevention, eradication, or suppression-depends on the stage of its introduction. Invasive species can enter ecosystems through various pathways. Some introductions are deliberate and legal but have unintended ecological consequences. Others result from illegal activities, such as the unauthorized transfer of live sport fish between waterbodies or the release of exotic species imported through the pet trade, including American bullfrogs, velvet longhorned beetles, and apple snails 
(Lockwood et al. 2019)
. Many introductions occur unintentionally, often through passive transport, as seen in the spread of quagga mussels, Japanese beetles, and red imported fire ants. In some cases, the mode of introduction remains unknown.
Identifying the source of introduction is crucial for implementing effective management strategies. Once an invasive species is established, control and removal efforts depend on factors such as the affected habitat (e.g., type of waterbody) its connectivity to other susceptible habitats (e.g., tributaries, corridors) the presence of native species, and the feasibility of suppression or eradication measures.
Invasive species control can pose environmental risks. Methods such as insecticide applications can harm non-target native species if not carefully applied, disrupting food webs and affecting other beneficial organisms. Mechanical removal and improper handling of invasive species during removal can disrupt habitat structure and unintentionally facilitate their further spread to new areas. Choosing control strategies that specifically target the invasive species while minimizing harm to native organisms and their habitat, combining multiple methods that maximize effectiveness and reduce reliance on any single method (such as integrated pest management), and regularly monitoring the impact of control efforts on both invasive and native species can inform adaptive management as needed.

## Case Study: Eradicating Invasive Fishes from the Virgin River

Eradication of red shiner has been the primary goal for Virgin River native fish recovery in Utah for more than three decades. Between 1996 and 2021, chemical treatment phases were designed to effectively eradicate red shiner from the Virgin River in Utah. Problem reaches were partitioned into manageable units, and eradication phases were implemented in succession between fish barriers with each phase repeated until 100% red shiner eradication was achieved.

Objective #2 for Invasive Wildlife Species (non-native)
Invasive non-native wildlife species are removed or controlled through functional eradication or their adverse impacts are reduced in priority habitats or locations.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching these Objectives

• Public messages and policies are in place to aggressively manage the risk of introductions.
• Native species are occurring, reproducing, and recruiting.

## Case Study: Managing Tamarisk to Improve Hydrological Function and Habitat Complexity

The San Rafael River is a tributary of the Green River in southeastern Utah. In some stretches it still supports several SGCNs and key habitats, but viewed as a whole, it is a severely degraded river system. There are multiple factors that have led to the current state, including dams and diversions, water withdrawals, altered flow regime, and invasions by non-native plant and animal species. For much of its length, the San Rafael River resembles a ditch, with little connection between the river and the floodplain and little resemblance to the dynamic, perennial desert river it once was.
The BLM, DWR, and USU developed a restoration plan for the San Rafael River that addresses many of these issues at the watershed scale 
(Laub et al. 2013)
. Initial phases of implementation have focused on tamarisk removal on DWR property. formed, and the channel was able to erode some of its levee-like banks 
(Keller et al. 2014)
. By 2012, significant natural recruitment of native vegetation also was occurring.
This fortunate timing of a natural flood following the mechanical vegetation removal highlighted which methods were most effective for achieving the project goals and provided insights into how human-made floods might be employed to accelerate habitat restoration. This knowledge will be applied to future phases of this project, increasing the efficiency and effectiveness of the San Rafael River Restoration Plan as it is implemented up-and downstream of DWRadministered reaches.

Note: This Level-2 threat has more than one priority Level-3 threat; consequently, some threat impacts have a score larger than one.
Threat: Natural Rarity and Small Isolated Populations. Natural rarity and small isolated populations are not threats per se; rather, they are more of a limiting factor. Nevertheless, they are often a contributing factor to species imperilment and ESA listing; therefore, they demand management awareness and sometimes action to identify and manage threats before they become pervasive. Therefore, these two factors were included in the UWAP threat assessment and ranking process, and they emerged as priorities. Both natural rarity and small isolated populations were identified as threats for several species that are naturally rare in Utah or occur in small, isolated populations. There are several ways natural rarity and small isolated populations can manifest. They are presented in order of increasing management priority from a listing-prevention perspective:
• A small portion of Utah may be at the extreme end of a much larger range for a species.
In these cases, there may (or may not) be a greater risk of losing the species in Utah, even though the species is secure elsewhere in its extensive range. The common chuckwalla is one example.
• In other cases, a species may be widespread within Utah, and often well beyond, and its natural condition is to occur at low densities. Spotted bat is one example.
• Finally, the species may occur in only one or very few locations in Utah, and nowhere else in the world. It may still occur at natural densities, or it may be depleted within its current distribution. This small range may have always been the case for this species, or this may be all that is left of a historically or prehistorically larger range. Many springsnails are good examples.
Actions that managers may choose to take to address these threats depend on how they manifest, as well as any additional threats the species may face. Each species identified as potentially impacted by one or both of these threats should be evaluated on a case-by-case basis to determine what, if any, action can or should be taken to address the threat. As stated above, these threats (which are limiting factors rather than threats) demand management awareness; however, management action is not universally warranted. Deciding to take no action can be technically justified yet still controversial among stakeholders, particularly if the reasoning has not been discussed and communicated.

## Case Study: Sub-Globose Snake Pyrg

The sub-globose snake pyrg is a springsnail that occurs in three springs that make up Gandy While conducting monitoring surveys in 2019, DWR discovered the presence and establishment of a non-native fish, the Loricariid (armored) catfish, throughout the majority of the sub-globose snake pyrg habitat. The potential threat (i.e., competition and predation on pyrgs) was a high concern for conservation partners because the sub-globose snake pyrg only occurs in a single location. A conservation agreement and strategy was developed for the sub-globose snake pyrg in 2020, and armored catfish was identified as the largest threat. The conservation team developed a non-native fish eradication plan and began implementation the same year. More frequent monitoring indicates that armored catfish removals have been successful, at least temporarily, and the sub-globose snake pyrg densities are remaining within historical levels.
Following the completion of a species status assessment, the USFWS concluded that the conservation agreement and strategy was implementing the necessary conservation actions for this species, and it was found not warranted for protection under the ESA (USFWS 2020).

## Essential Conservation Actions to Address this Threat

Objective #1 for Natural Rarity and Small Isolated Populations
Naturally rare species and critical small, isolated populations remain extant in Utah through the 10year life of the UWAP.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching this Objective

• Numbers of populations and/or abundance of individuals and/or area of occupancy of naturally rare species and small, isolated populations show an upward or static (not declining) trend.
• Inventory and monitoring efforts for species richness, abundance, and habitat condition are included criteria for restoration project planning and success metrics.

2.1.1 Develop rare and priority pollinator species distribution maps, habitat connectivity, and migration maps for diverse land managers using existing data sources to assist with identifying inventory and monitoring sites for priority pollinator species. • The impacts associated with improper grazing are more severe under drought conditions.
• Drought increases the severity of impacts associated with dams and water management/use, and human population growth will increase the demand for limited water supplies.
• Drought and rising average temperatures increase the risk of inappropriate fire frequency and intensity, and an increased human population likely will elevate humancaused ignitions.
• Altered climate conditions may increase the vulnerability of SGCNs to threats posed by invasive non-native species and may promote invasion, establishment, and competitive advantage of alien species to the detriment of SGCNs.
• SGCNs that fall into the realm of natural rarity or small isolated populations are likely to be most vulnerable to impacts associated with climate change, and mechanisms to promote their survival (e.g., facilitated translocations to establish refuge populations) may run into political resistance (for example, the resistance to moving Utah prairie dogs across county borders).
Environmental change appears certain, but the specifics are still unclear. Existing laws, regulations, and policies present challenges or limitations that must be overcome to address threats (e.g., water management), for the benefit of conservation targets and human society.
From a conservation planning perspective, however, abundant opportunities are available to address climate change-related impacts. Continued diligence in addressing existing threats will minimize the compounding effects associated with climate change.
Within this broad category, two Level-2 threats were deemed priorities: ecosystem encroachment and changes in precipitation and hydrological regimes. Each has been given its own table.

## Ecosystem Encroachment

Table 
41
 summarizes the numbers of species and habitats impacted by this priority Level-2 threat by all nested priority Level-3 threats and the level of impacts (very high, high, or medium).
This Level-2 threat is further subdivided into three Level-3 threats, all of which have impacts above the low level. One of them, increasing stream temperatures, was ranked as a priority. The title of this threat refers to major changes in habitat composition and location, possibly leading to species' inability to shift their ranges to follow the changes of their needed habitat conditions.
Threat: Increasing Stream Temperatures. Many aquatic species have a narrow temperature range in which they can persist. Stream temperatures have been rising throughout the West because of drought, diversions, reduced snowpack, decreased spring runoff, and loss of riparian habitat that provides shade. As temperatures warm beyond tolerable ranges, populations of aquatic insects and other macroinvertebrates are particularly impacted, both in abundance and diversity. Many fish distributions retract from the warmer lowlands and are limited to cool temperature refugia such as higher elevations or reaches with cold spring inflows, and thus available habitat and connectivity between drainages may be lost. This results in smaller, more fragmented populations that may not be able to recover naturally from catastrophic disturbances. Aquatic invertebrate populations are in decline due to stream temperature increase 
(Anderson et al. 2019
) and the alteration of host fish presence due to rising stream temperatures poses a significant threat for freshwater mussel reproduction 
(Hastie and Young 2003)
.
With continuing climate change, drought and water use are expected to increase and continue, temperatures will continue to warm, and additional cold and cool water fish habitat will be lost.
This will necessitate a major intensification of the management of SGCNs and key habitats as well as of all the other threats that impinge upon them (e.g., recreational activities, improper grazing, disease, problematic native species).
Impediments to species movement can happen for various underlying reasons. Two possible response strategies to this scenario are as follows:
• Improve resilience: remove or reduce impediments to species movement as their habitats shift.
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 385
• Improve resistance: reduce the rate or severity of habitat shifting itself, so that species can remain in place longer if it is difficult or impossible for them to move.
Many of the coarse-scale terrestrial habitat management actions practiced in Utah support resistance strategies. It is hoped that these actions continue and are accelerated as a component of implementing the UWAP. Atmospheric carbon dioxide concentrations are still increasing rapidly, however; resistance would be a risky standalone strategy for wildlife and habitat managers to adopt. Fortunately, resilience is also an option:
• Utah's wide range of elevational diversity presents numerous opportunities for plant, insect, and animal species to shift their ranges upslope in response to warmer temperatures, specifically increasing stream temperatures.
• Similarly, with more than 70% of the state's total acreage in public ownership, northward migrations of species and communities in response to climate change are a real possibility.
• Utah's public lands also establish boundaries for urban growth and development while providing landscape-level opportunities to manage conservation targets and their opportunities for migration and range shifting.
Therefore, implementing a mixed strategy, including an increasing proportion of actions to improve species' resilience (i.e., their ability to move as circumstances on the ground require it) is a real possibility.

## Case Study: Climate Change and Increasing Stream Temperatures Limit Native Trout Distribution

One impact from climate change is warmer stream temperatures. Studies indicate that suitable habitat for native trout could decline by as much as 60% by 2080 
(Wenger et al. 2011)
. Native trout management will need to consider warming stream temperatures when prioritizing projects. Some native trout habitats are already marginal for stream temperatures, consequently, putting time and money into these reaches will ultimately be a waste of resources if these habitats are lost in the near future from rising stream temperatures.
In Utah, additional fine-scale modeling is needed to determine more realistic impacts from rising stream temperatures. These analyses will allow resource managers to better focus on habitats where native trout will be able to persist into the future. Additional restoration of these habitats such as improvement of riparian vegetation and riffle-to-pool ratios, non-native trout eradication, and restoring connectivity will allow native trout to persist in in light of climate change and increasing stream temperatures.

## Essential Conservation Actions to Address this Threat

Objective #1 for Increasing Stream Temperatures
Affected aquatic habitats are adaptively managed such that geomorphic and hydromorphic features and associated native plant communities are improved or maintained for SGCN resilience to increasing stream temperatures.

## Objective #2 for Increasing Stream Temperatures

Aquatic species' populations and community structure, composition, and genetic diversity are improved and maintained to establish resiliency to persist on the landscape despite suitable habitat location and distribution shifts.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching these Objectives

• Dissolved oxygen, pH, turbidity, conductivity, sediment, flow, and temperature levels are within a range that typically support native fish and aquatic species.
• Suitable habitat physical conditions (e.g., appropriate-sized substrates, essential channel features) exist for fish and aquatic species to occur.
• Associated surrounding riparian and wetland plant communities occur in aquatic habitat areas, mitigating increasing stream temperatures and abiotic fluctuations associated with shifting climatic conditions.
• Umbrella species and/or SGCN reproduction and recruitment are occurring.
• Viable populations of riparian and aquatic umbrella species occur. threat by all nested priority Level-3 threats and the level of impacts (very high, high, or medium).

## Potential Conservation Actions

This Level-2 threat has not been further subdivided into Level-3 threats.
and severity of drought. These challenges are particularly acute for aquatic species and their habitats, especially in isolated or terminal drainages. Decreased stream flows and warmer temperatures can be harmful to species richness and abundance at multiple trophic levels, leading to overall aquatic habitat degradation. Drought, compounded by other effects of climate change and existing anthropogenic pressures-such as water depletion and inappropriate grazing practices-exacerbates the impact to aquatic and riparian ecosystems, threatening the diverse wildlife that depend on them.
Drier conditions and reduced vegetation moisture can have serious impacts to unique and sensitive ecosystems such as alpine meadows and springs-complex habitats that often have relatively high plant and aquatic insect species richness, as well as endemic species found in these habitats and nowhere else 
(Bonelli et al. 2021;
Claret and Marmonier 2019)
. Periods of severe drought contribute to delayed spring phenology 
(Li et al. 2023
) and an increased risk of large and intense wildfires 
(Kitzberger et al. 2007)
, which further hinder ecosystem recovery and resiliency.
Downward trends in overall abundance and diversity of pollinators-especially wild bees and butterflies-are largely driven by the effects of increased drought and altered precipitation patterns 
(Forister et al. 2010;
Forister et al. 2023;
Kammerer et al. 2021)
. Drought especially leads to reduced floral resources for at-risk pollinator species, as well as common species that maintain healthy and diverse plant communities.

## Case Study: Cascading Effects of Drought on the Great Salt Lake Ecosystem

GSL is an ecologically vital yet highly sensitive A key impact of lower lake levels is rising salinity, which disrupts the delicate balance of life in GSL. As water recedes, microbialites-carbonate
Great Salt Lake at Record Low Water Levels in 2022.
Copernicus Sentinel data 2022. Map data from OpenStreetMap.

## 2025

-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 398
structures layered with microbial mats-become exposed, leading to their desiccation and loss of photosynthetic communities that sustain brine fly larvae. Simultaneously, increased salinity forces brine shrimp and brine fly pupae in the benthic water column to expend more energy on osmoregulation, reducing brine shrimp populations and shrinking the adult body size of brine flies. These changes significantly decrease the available biomass of both key prey species, threatening millions of migratory birds that depend on them.
One species at immediate risk is the Wilson's phalarope, which relies on GSL as a crucial stopover to refuel before its long winter migration south. More than one-third of the global population of Wilson's phalaropes depends on the lake for an abundant brine fly population, which allows the phalarope to gain weight during its stopover before migration. A GSL ecosystem collapse due to declining water levels and increased salinity was highlighted as a threat in a recent petition to list the species as threatened under the ESA.
Beyond its ecological role, the declining GSL poses serious risks to public health and regional climate stability. As the lakebed is exposed, windblown dust degrades air quality, affecting human health throughout the Salt Lake Valley. Additionally, the lake's influence on weather patterns and snow deposition is critical for maintaining water resources along the Wasatch Front, as well as a draw for winter recreation that drives the state's tourism.
Recognizing the urgency of this crisis, Utah has taken significant legislative and collaborative action. In 2023, the Utah Legislature established the GSL Commission to coordinate response efforts, followed by the creation of the GSL Strike Team, the GSL Advisory Council, and the GSL Watershed Council-all focused on mitigating public health, economic, and ecological impacts.
Current strategies include policy reforms, funding allocations, and water reallocation initiatives aimed at restoring thousands of acres of GSL wetlands, managing lake salinity, and securing water flows through voluntary water transactions and shifts toward native, drought-resilient landscaping.
Although these efforts represent progress, the long-term sustainability of GSL remains uncertain, requiring continued commitment to conservation, responsible water management, and climate adaptation strategies.

## Case Study: Extended Droughts Will Become More Frequent

Utah was in an extended drought that peaked in 2022. During this drought, some streams dried up or fisheries suffered from poor water quality due to low water levels, terrestrial wildlife and plants suffered without rain and water sources, and GSL recorded an all-time elevational low of 1,276.7 m (4,188.5 feet). These extended drought conditions are forecasted to become more frequent into the future due to climate change. This recent drought highlighted the need to change management in Utah to accommodate extreme conditions. Many DNR divisions, water conservancy districts, and cities developed drought contingency plans to highlight necessary management changes. During extreme drought conditions, for example, DWR will reduce big game permits, redirect fish stockings away from waters being impacted by low water levels, increase fishery creel limits in waters that likely will be lost due limited water, and transport water to guzzlers in dry habitats to ensure terrestrial wildlife have water.

## Essential Conservation Actions to Address this Threat

Objective #1 for Droughts
Terrestrial SGCNs and key habitats persist on the landscape, despite increasing drought conditions.

## Objective #2 for Droughts

Aquatic SGCNs and key habitats persist on the landscape, despite increasing drought conditions.

## Potential Indicators of Success Reaching these Objectives

• Effects of habitat fragmentation and loss from the effects of drought conditions are identified and minimized through partner coordination.
• Dissolved oxygen, pH, turbidity, conductivity, sediment, flow, and temperature levels persist within a range that typically support native fish and aquatic species.
• Suitable physical habitat conditions (e.g., appropriate-sized substrates, essential channel features) exist for fish and aquatic species to occur.
• Suitable physical habitat conditions (e.g., appropriate vegetation structures and compositions, essential water sources) exist for diverse terrestrial communities to occur.
• Surrounding riparian and wetland plant communities occur within historical aquatic habitat areas.
• Viable, indicator riparian, aquatic, and upland wildlife populations occur.

## Introduction

In wildlife and habitat management, there are two basic categories of information that monitoring programs are intended to create and communicate:
1. The status of a conservation target.
2. The effectiveness of a management action.
Evaluating conservation effectiveness requires some measurement of the change in target status from current or baseline condition resulting from management actions. In natural resource management, indicators are often used to detect changes in targets and threats; thus, they are vital to status assessment and effectiveness monitoring. They are also important to implementation because they are used to make management decisions.
Indicators are meant to integrate many ecological functions and represent aspects of the larger ecosystem that may be either too expensive or time consuming to monitor. The best indicators address one or more of the following types of values:
• Ecological values -how well the indicator represents targets and threats, and how those respond to natural and human-driven changes on the landscape.
• Practical values -how readily the indicator can be monitored and modeled, based on existing programs and resources.
• Social values -how well the indicator resonates with partners, stakeholders, and other audiences.
Indicators always have at least one scale that is used for measurement. A common example from game management is fawn:doe ratio, which is an indicator of fawn production and survival. This indicator's measurement scale (or measure) is the number of live fawns per 100 doe deer. This measure is derived by a particular set of people, using a defined method.
The purpose of this chapter is to define the indicators, measures, and methods that will be employed to monitor 1) the status of the Utah Wildlife Action Plan's (UWAP's) monitoring targets and 2) the effectiveness of management actions to define, maintain, or improve target status.

## Periodic Status Assessments of Species of Greatest Conservation Need Indicators

For each one of Utah's species of greatest conservation need (SGCNs), the fundamental properties to be assessed are "distribution and abundance . . . indicative of the health of wildlife." The UWAP indicators that measure these properties on a statewide basis for each species are the three conservation status factor categories of the NatureServe Conservation Rank Calculator 1 (NatureServe 2020):
• Rarity
• Threats

## • Trends

Measures for all status factors that make up each of these three factor categories 
(Master et al. 2012
) are contained in the Conservation Rank Calculator. Users have some choice of which status factors they can use.
The status factor chosen to represent the indicator Rarity in Utah is Area of Occupancy. Table 
44
 shows the measure used for a species' Area of Occupancy (larger grid cells are the default option, whereas the smaller grid is used for species inhabiting linear habitats, such as river fishes) 
(Master et al. 2012)
. NatureServe updates national ranks on their own schedule, typically on the order of 15 to 20 years. Species may be added to or removed from the Utah SGCN list as a result of these updates to state and/or national ranks. Such changes would prompt a consultation with U.S.
Fish and Wildlife Service (USFWS) on the need for a major or minor revision of the UWAP.
Several of Utah's SGCNs have significant data gaps, which impinge on DWR's ability to confidently assess Rarity (and thus short-term trends) and Threat scope. These data gaps must be filled to improve the accuracy and precision of status assessments as well as enable development of effective management actions and associated effectiveness monitoring. The intent of the inclusion of a species of greatest information need (SGIN) list in the 2025 UWAP was to highlight three of the most pervasive data gap threats that impact understanding and management for SGIN species and larger SGIN taxonomic groups: Inadequate Understanding of Ecology and Life History, Inadequate Understanding of Distribution or Range, and Taxonomic Debate.

## Species Distribution and Habitat Suitability Models

Species Distribution and Habitat Suitability Models (SDHMs) are being developed for many Utah SGCNs. SDHMs show a predicted suitable habitat for a species based on various biotic and abiotic environmental factors. Consequently, SDHMs also depict a possible geographic distribution of the species, which can be particularly useful when surveying for species that are not well described. As more information is used in the development of SDHMs, the models will become more useful in UWAP implementation. Assessments of S-ranks were developed to use As is the case for SGCNs, there are also some significant data gaps that interfere with assessing the status of terrestrial and aquatic key habitats. These data gaps must be filled to improve the accuracy and precision of status assessments as well as to develop effective management actions and to monitor the effectiveness of those actions.

## Water Quality Data

States must submit biennial reports to the EPA on water quality of surface water to comply with the Clean Water Act. States also are required to submit a list of waterbodies that do not meet the state's water quality standards. Surface waters that are measured for water quality are placed into discrete assessment units. Lakes, reservoirs, ponds, flowing surface waters, and canals are distinguished as individual assessment units (see DWQ 2024 for more details).
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 5 | 414
Although assessments do not cover the entire state, they do cover all areas with perennial streams and lakes. DWQ's most recent report for Utah was submitted in 2024 (DWQ 2024) (Table 
46
). Water quality was evaluated in five categories: 1) waterbodies fully support their beneficial use (e.g., recreation, drinking water); 2) waterbodies support some but not all designated uses, but there is no evidence of impairment; 3) waterbodies with insufficient information to make a support determination; 4) at least one designated use is not being supported but a total maximum daily load (TMDL) (a measure that accounts for all sources of pollutants that caused the waterbody to receive an impaired determination) is not needed; or 5) at least one designated use is not being supported and a TMDL is needed (impaired waterbodies). For waterbodies listed as impaired, the Clean Water Act requires a TMDL to be developed. Most impairments in all waterbodies are linked to exceeding the maximum criterion for dissolved oxygen, pH, or temperature (DWQ 2024). 

## Bioassessment Data

The challenge of quantifying environmental and ecological impairment to aquatic ecosystems was addressed in 1992 through a partnership between aquatic scientists from Utah State University (USU), the U.S. Forest Service, and the Bureau of Land Management, who developed a biological monitoring and assessment process using aquatic invertebrate assemblages. The NAMC evaluates the functional components or biological integrity of aquatic ecosystems (meaning, "the capability of supporting and maintaining a balanced, integrated, adaptive
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 5 | 415
community of organisms having a composition and diversity comparable to that of the natural habitats in the region" 
[Frey 1977]
) that are measured as a departure from reference conditions with the least disturbance on the landscape. The NAMC works with agencies and academic partners to quantify environmental and biological conditions using benthic macroinvertebrate and water chemistry samples collected from streams, rivers, lakes, springs, and reservoirs.
Water quality and physical habitat can be modeled at local and regional scales by assessing biological indicator traits such as tolerance values, functional feeding groups, and thermal preferences. Aquatic biological assessments quantify species richness and community composition. As sampling is repeated at sites, these data may be able to identify macroinvertebrate trends in the future. NAMC maintains the Aquatic Invertebrates Database, a spatial database that contains nearly 4,000 unique records for aquatic invertebrate taxa in Utah associated with samples processed by its laboratory and data incorporated from other collection surveys. These publicly available data, as well as field protocols, are provided on the NAMC website, hosted by USU's Quinney College of Natural Resources (USU 2025a).

## Hydrologic Data

## Stream Gages

Water diversions have had a significant impact to Utah's aquatic resources; water management has been identified as the most pervasive threat (in scope and severity) to SGCNs and their habitats. U.S. Geological Survey stream gages monitor many of Utah's stream resources (U.S.

## Geological Survey 2025b

). Utah Division of Water Rights (DWRi) provides additional gaging information, and some stream flow information is monitored by individual water districts.
Despite the existing stream gaging network, many streams and stream segments remain ungaged. The Utah Division of Water Resources' Great Salt Lake Basin Integrated Plan (2024) identified the lack of stream flow gages as an impediment to effective water monitoring. During 2023 and 2024, Utah Division of Water Resources worked with USU to complete a stream flow gap analysis to identify priority stream reaches or canals that lack stream flow gaging 
(Lukens et al. 2024
). This study recommended the consolidation of all stream flow information and, as of 2025, this information is being compiled.

## Groundwater Monitoring

DWRi manages groundwater resources in Utah, and groundwater management plans exist for most basins impacted by water withdrawals (DWRi 2025). To better understand and monitor groundwater resources, DWRi works closely with the UGS to monitor groundwater resources.
The UGS conducts water budget studies and monitors groundwater levels in wells and springs
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 5 | 416
to provide data to support DWRi decision making. The UGS also has developed a groundwater dashboard that compiles data from previous UGS water budget studies and showcases water level trends for wells across the state over intervals of 
5, 10, and 30 years (UGS 2025)
. In 2024, the UGS analyzed water levels in wells measured by the U.S. Geological Survey for 265 wells in Utah with 30 years of groundwater measurements between 1994 and 2024 and found that 6% of the wells had a significant increasing or stable water level trend (0-0.4 meter [0-1.3 feet] per year); 86% had a significant moderate rate of decline (-0.6-0 meter [-2.0-0 feet] per year); and 8% had a significant large rate of decline (-1.5 to -0.6 meters [-4.8 to -2.0 feet] per year) (UGS 2025).

## Remotely Sensed Products

Remote sensing data have also been used to study patterns in surface water extent over time.
Both the Wetland Evaluation Tool (USFWS 2025a) and Global Surface Water Explorer (2020) provide resources that indicate declines in surface water extent from the early 1980s to more recent years. It is worth noting, however, that the 1980s were a historically wet time in Utah.

• Make existing aquatic key habitat condition data readily available (see the Aquatic Key Habitat Descriptions section in Chapter 2, Key Habitats -Location and Condition, for additional resources).

## Periodic Status Assessments of Threats

The status of the threats impacting conservation targets needs to be measured periodically for several important reasons. Threats are
• an integral component of the five-factor analysis that the USFWS uses to make its decisions about threatened and endangered species listing and recovery;
• one of three factor categories used in the NatureServe Conservation Rank Calculator (NatureServe 2020), which is how the UWAP proposes to periodically determine SGCN performance and conservation status;
• an integral part of the UWAP's habitat condition assessments; and
• what the UWAP should seek to prevent or reduce with conservation management interventions.
The fundamental property to be assessed for threats is threat impact. An earlier section of this chapter, Periodic Status Assessments of Species of Greatest Conservation Need, has more detail on the target-by-target scale of inquiry and how impact is calculated. This section, however, is concerned with aggregating threats to characterize their impact across the state and across the entire suite of SGCNs and key habitats.

## Indicators

An evaluation of the statewide scope, severity, timing, and reversibility of every standardized threat for every target resulted in the statewide threat assessment. This assessment's data set was then subjected to a prioritization scheme. The prioritization scheme used a screening and averaging algorithm to yield a set of priority Level-2 and Level-3 threats (see Chapter 4, Threats, Data Gaps, and Conservation Actions, and Appendix E for details). Each priority threat has a defined, categorized degree of impact (very high, high, or medium) to a quantified set of targets.
A few priority threats were only defined at Level-2; in those cases, the indicator is its Level-2 
2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 5 | 418 impact category. Refer to

## Measures

The sole measure for the indicator impact category is the number of targets impacted in each category organized by each priority threat. Conservation targets are SGCN and key habitats. The number of SGCN and key habitats impacted by threats are summarized for the ranking process.
The impact categories used to prioritize threats are very high, high, and medium. Threats considered to have a low or unknown impact to conservation targets were removed from the prioritization process.

## Methods

To accurately measure the scope (and thus derive the impact) of a threat, it is necessary to have accurate, current distribution maps of both the target and the threat. Many existing spatial data sets for threats serve as model inputs for Terrestrial and Aquatic Intactness. These existing data sets are maintained and updated by several organizations, including DWR. Some data sets are housed on DWR or other UWAP partners' servers, but many are housed at the Utah Automated Geographic Reference Center, which makes them available to the public. Comparing current and older versions of these spatial data sets enables DWR and partners to track changes in the distribution of targets and threats and thus judge the implementation and effectiveness of the UWAP. As the previous section illustrates, the realm of spatial data sets for both threats and targets is one of constant change and improvement.
Similar to SGCNs and key habitats, there are some significant data gaps that interfere with assessing the status (scope and severity) of certain threats. These data gaps-most often, the absence of an initial baseline-must be filled to improve the accuracy and precision of periodic status assessments. In some cases, such data gaps are due to existing data not yet having been developed into spatial data sets. In other cases, the raw data have yet to be collected, or they only exist for limited areas and are unsuitable for statewide use. In the absence of appropriate data, the best judgment of subject-matter experts will be used.

## Periodic Status Assessments of Data Gaps

The status of the data gaps impinging upon the management of conservation targets also needs to be measured periodically. Fundamentally, data gaps can pose severe impediments to vital conservation, sometimes to the degree that nothing useful can be done until the data gap is filled. Conversely, data gaps can also cause undue concern for cryptic or unstudied species that may in fact be secure. These are the extremes on a continuum. Discerning where reality falls on this continuum in any given situation cannot be clarified without some cost and effort.
Data gaps impede status assessment and effectiveness monitoring in the following general ways:
• Obscuring the true abundance and/or distribution of SGCNs
• Obscuring the true location and/or condition of key habitats
• Obscuring the true scope and/or severity (thus impact) of threats
• Obscuring the actual need for, and/or the effectiveness of, conservation actions
The fundamental question to be assessed for data gaps is, "Do they still exist?" As in the previous section on threats, the concern here is with aggregating data gaps to characterize their statewide status across the entire suite of SGCNs and key habitats and the threats thought to be impacting them.

## Indicators

There is still a lot to learn about most conservation targets, but not all the data gaps prevent action, and not all data gaps are crucial. This is an important distinction that needs to be accounted for when applying the following two indicators of progress toward filling data gaps:
• The number of UWAP SGIN
• The number of data gaps remaining

## Measures

Measures for these two indicators are embodied in the indicators themselves (see the Methods section below and Table 
43
 in Chapter 4, Threats, Data Gaps, and Conservation Actions for more information).

## Methods

The UWAP SGCN Subcommittee identified the following three Level-2 data gaps as significant and ones that hampered the ability to effectively manage species: Collectively, these data gaps make up nearly half of the data gaps identified in Table 
43
. To better address these three data gaps, the SGIN list was developed and included in the 2025 UWAP. If species qualified as SGCN through the UWAP SGCN rubric primarily due to one or more of these three data gaps, they were moved to the SGIN list. The SGIN table (see Table 
4
) identifies which of these three data gaps are relevant by SGIN (see Chapter 1, Species of Greatest Conservation Need -Distribution and Abundance for more information).
Through time, tracking the number of SGIN will provide an assessment on these three data gaps. Measures of success demonstrating that these data gaps are being addressed include the following:
• Fewer broad taxon groups on the SGIN list. Currently, the SGIN list contains some higher taxon levels (e.g., genus, family) rather than individual species, which demonstrates larger fundamental data gaps for these species' groups. As these data gaps are addressed, a better understanding of which species in these broader taxon groups need conservation attention will be achieved.
• Fewer species on the SGIN list. As these data gaps are addressed, a better understanding of which species need conservation attention will be known. With better knowledge, species that are not declining or in need of conservation attention can be removed from the SGIN list. Conversely, as these data gaps are filled, if the new information does indicate that species need conservation attention, these species can be elevated to the SGCN list.
Tracking the total number of data gaps is another measure of progress toward filling those gaps. As the number of data gaps decreases, UWAP partners will be in a better position to understand which species need conservation attention and what actions will result in effective conservation. Beyond the three data gaps used to highlight SGIN, the remaining data gaps have been recorded through the species threats assessments as part of the S-rank process. These data gaps can be referenced and tracked when S-ranks are updated. Similarly, threats to key habitats can be tracked through time as key habitat threat assessments are updated.
For example, the 2025 UWAP threat assessment highlighted that uncertainty related to climate change is a data gap that has become more pervasive since 2015 and now encompasses 30% of the data gaps identified in Table 
43
. The UWAP Climate Adaptation Subcommittee has committed to meeting into the foreseeable future to better address UWAP climate needs.
