## Measuring the Effectiveness of Conservation Actions

There are hundreds of potential conservation actions outlined in Chapter 4, Threats, Data Gaps, and Conservation Actions, that can be taken to implement the UWAP. Each project and action will be evaluated differently to determine the effectiveness of the desired conservation action.
Evaluating UWAP projects and actions will be critical in determining Utah's progress in native species conservation efforts.

## Verifying and Tracking Utah Wildlife Action Plan Implementation

Before the effectiveness of actions can be determined, it is necessary to first document that the actions were implemented as intended and designed. There are several reasons for this, including the following:
• If an action has not been implemented, then looking for a resulting effect might be a waste of resources.
• If no action has been implemented, but an effect that would be expected to result from that action is still observed, further investigation would be warranted. Specifically, the problem-action-effect-solution results chain might need to be refined.
• If some action other than the intended action has been implemented, then making inference to the efficacy of the intended but untaken action would be inaccurate.
• Time lags between actions and outcomes are very common. 

## Indicators

• Projects that address UWAP priority threats or data gaps.
• Programs conducting periodic status assessments of SGCNs, key habitats, and priority threats.

## Measures

• Numbers of projects/conservation actions completed.
• Expenditures on such projects/conservation actions.
• Number of partners submitting implementation data formatted to standard threats and actions and to status assessment indicators.
• Adapting and incorporating new information or changing conditions that impact threats and conservation actions.
• Quantity of data submitted.

## Methods

By adopting the standardized naming system for threats and conservation actions and reporting their efforts via the online Watershed Restoration Initiative project database, any current or future UWAP partner can contribute to the efforts to monitor some aspects of UWAP implementation-mainly on-the-ground, direct actions. The Core Team will meet each year to coordinate UWAP implementation and prioritize conservation actions. If new threats to SGCNs or key habitats arise, these threats will be incorporated into the appropriate threat assessments.
The same applies to conservation actions. If new information or changing conditions impact and change priority threats to SGCNs and key habitats, the conservation actions to address those threats will to be reviewed and adapted. Changes in priority threats will be incorporated into the Watershed Restoration Initiative to ensure that conservation projects/actions remain relevant with new information and changing conditions.
Relying solely on indicators and measures related to completed projects and expended funds as indicators and measures of implementation, however, might be inadequate and problematic. As described in Chapter 4, Threats, Data Gaps, and Conservation Actions, some of the highestimpact threats to Utah SGCN and key habitats are especially challenging to address. Several factors contribute to this. For example, there may be questions or confusion surrounding who has legal authority to address a threat, conflicting resource demands, and influential factors that are outside the purview of conservation organizations.
Taking effective action against such complex, challenging threats will require cooperation among the agencies and organizations that are the administrators and stakeholders of those threats. Those actions currently listed in the UWAP for which DWR lacks full capacity and authority to undertake alone have been presented as starting points in an inclusive discussion, not as requirements or decisions that have already been made. Not being cooperative and inclusive is, in effect, deciding that the most-challenging, highest-impact threats are not going to be addressed. The statewide, all-targets threat assessment demonstrates that failing to address such threats to wildlife would likely lead to numerous Endangered Species Act listings, a disastrous outcome for Utahns and an abdication of many UWAP stakeholders' public responsibilities.
This raises some difficulties for traditional natural resources professionals. Besides the indicators of natural resources outcomes described elsewhere in this chapter, it could be beneficial to note indicators that assess and can improve joint performance navigating social processes such as facilitating meetings, involving stakeholders in planning and decision making, optimizing communication among stakeholders, and two-way learning. These processes would have their own indicators, measures, and methods.

## Measuring and Verifying Overall Utah Wildlife Action Plan Effectiveness

UWAP partners still need some way to determine whether they are being effective.
Effectiveness is ultimately defined as meeting the stated purpose of the UWAP: The purpose of Utah's 2025 Wildlife Action Plan is to manage native wildlife species and their habitats to help prevent listings under the Endangered Species Act.
The target status indicators help detect degraded or precarious wildlife populations and habitats that are often precursors to Endangered Species Act listings. Measures for all indicators have already been obtained; in 2025, all chosen target status indicators have baseline values. If filling the data gaps and taking action to address the priority threats succeed, then those successes will be reflected in future measurements of the chosen indicators. It will be evident that the status of targets has improved and that degraded or precarious SGCN populations and habitats have been restored and conserved. This is the conceptual model for preventing Endangered Species Act listings and the resulting negative impacts to Utah. This is how overall UWAP effectiveness should be measured, verified, and judged.

## Introduction

The task of preserving and managing Utah's fish and wildlife is far too large and complex for one agency to achieve alone. invertebrates and their habitats depends on many organizations working together and across disciplines. While the UWAP Core Team included many of the UWAP partner agencies/organizations and was the overarching partner team that helped guide the UWAP revision, many other partners and stakeholders worked collaboratively to develop this version of the UWAP (Table 
47
). These groups incorporate the UWAP into their decision making and daily workflows and collaborate with DWR when developing and implementing projects across the state. The UWAP is intended to facilitate cooperation with adjacent states, as well as local and regional conservation organizations and tribal nations, to achieve range-wide species and habitat conservation.

## Regional Initiatives Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies Initiatives

## Southern Wings

To prevent migratory species from becoming endangered, and recover those already endangered, researchers must understand and address the totality of threats facing the species throughout their full life cycle and Utah's role in supporting their populations. This is particularly important for neotropical migratory birds.
Case Study -Southwestern Collaboration: Western Yellow-Billed Cuckoo
State wildlife managers face a persistent structural challenge to the effective conservation of wide-ranging and migratory SGCN: coordination among states. The threats these SGCN face accumulate across migrations and seasons and are rarely confined to state or national jurisdictions. Research shows that effective conservation actions must be coordinated across the full annual cycle (FAC) of these species 
(Marra et al. 2015)
, meeting species' needs when and where they occur. The FAC encompasses a species' full range of habitat associations and biological activities throughout a year, including breeding, nonbreeding, and migration or dispersal periods 
(Marra et al. 2015;
Schuster et al. 2019)
. Thus, conservation actions that focus on migratory birds need to span political boundaries and emphasize approaches with the greatest return on investment. The AFWA Southern Wings program has been designed to facilitate cross-boundary work focused on neotropical migratory birds.
This section explores one migrant that is a priority and was listed as an SGCN ca. Since 2020, 12 states, including several southwestern states (i.e., Arizona, California, Colorado, Nevada, New Mexico, Utah, and Texas), have been participating in a competitive STWG Program project to develop a range-wide distribution model, implement a range-wide survey, and investigate the use of autonomous recording units in detecting this species. As an example of the cross-boundary collaboration this STWG Program funding catalyzed; the participating states completed over 300 surveys, which was more than initially proposed. The western subspecies is the focus of a working group that promotes yellow-billed cuckoo science, conservation, recovery, and partnerships. This working group has also adopted the Road to Recovery process, which integrates biological and social science to identify and address specific causes of yellowbilled cuckoo decline throughout its annual cycle (Road to Recovery 2023).
As the above examples show, conservation and management of SGCN regularly require working beyond state and national borders. Watersheds (such as the Gila River watershed, which contains western yellow-billed cuckoo critical habitat) and flyways (important regional partnerships for bird conservation) often encompass all or parts of multiple states and require complex coordination among many partners and jurisdictions to improve conditions and provide needed habitat. Recovery of declining species that have large ranges, like the western yellow-billed cuckoo, requires coordination with partners and agencies with interest and jurisdiction throughout the species' range. This includes partnerships between neighboring states and with regional or international organizations and alliances (e.g., Partners in Flight) that can accomplish needed conservation actions to achieve shared goals.
Threats to landscapes and ecosystems used by birds, such as the western yellow-billed cuckoo, during the migratory and nonbreeding season vary by country and region but include deforestation, commodity agriculture (palm oil), illegal logging, contaminants, and insufficient enforcement in protected areas. Poverty across this region of the world adds to the dire need for support from international partners that have a common interest in the protection and conservation of shared avifauna. Projects also need to consider the needs and interests of local communities to be successful. International conservation actions intended to curb these threats include the acquisition and protection of lands used as migratory pathways and nonbreeding sites; education of landowners on regenerative agricultural and ranching practices, including shade-grown coffee farming; the creation and maintenance of native tree nurseries;
and reforestation efforts.
To address these threats, multiple southwestern states have committed to participating in cross-border FAC conservation for migratory species, including the western yellow-billed cuckoo. This commitment is critical to the improvement and long-term sustainability of species like the yellow-billed cuckoo that migrate and winter south of the United States (Figure 
58
). Western Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies Initiatives

## Sagebrush Conservation Design and Sagebrush Conservation Strategy

The Western Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies (WAFWA) Sagebrush Conservation
Strategy is an international collaborative effort to provide guidance for conservation efforts across the sagebrush biome, which includes 14 western states and two Canadian provinces. It was created by a team of scientists and specialists from a mix of federal and state agencies, universities, and nongovernmental organizations (NGOs). DWR participated in the production of the strategy. The WAFWA Sagebrush Conservation Strategy provides a biome-wide model to measure the ecological integrity of sagebrush ecosystems. This effort has also identified the major threats to the sagebrush ecosystem, which include invasive annual grasses, conifer encroachment, and human modifications. The ecological integrity index quantifies the trend over time of the ecosystem and the individual threats. The Sagebrush Conservation Strategy is a tool that project managers throughout the sagebrush biome can use to strengthen and justify habitat restoration projects that mitigate the threats to sagebrush ecosystems and can inform where projects can be done to protect core sagebrush habitats (WAFWA 2020).

## Southwest State Wildlife Action Plan Cross Borders Group

Regional collaboration occurs across the West, but the large size of western states can often make coordination of native species conservation efforts across state boundaries challenging, align SWAPs. The primary recommendation that materialized from the workshop was the need for a WAFWA Wildlife Diversity/SWAP Committee to allow a space for western states to better discuss and align SWAPs. The WAFWA Nongame/Endangered Fish and Wildlife Species
Committee discussed this need at WAFWA's 2023 summer meeting and approved the formation of a WAFWA Wildlife Diversity/SWAP Committee. Since its formation, the WAFWA committee has met three times as of March 2025. In addition, the Southwest SWAP Cross Borders Group has continued to meet monthly to share ideas specific to the southwestern United States.

## Western Monarch and Native Insect Pollinator Working Group

The Western Monarch and Native Insect Pollinator (WMNIP) Working Group is a multistate group organized within WAFWA that identifies regional strategies to benefit declining insect pollinator populations. One of the goals of this group is to avoid the need for federal Endangered Species Act (ESA) species listings for native insect pollinators by benefiting multiple target species through landscape-level, data-driven conservation actions. The approved 2023-2026 work plan specifically prioritizes western state and federal agency coordination and identifies shared species priorities, conservation needs and opportunities, and best management practices for native insect pollinators and their habitats. 

## Utah's Wildlife Migration Initiative

Utah's Wildlife Migration Initiative is a statewide campaign to identify, preserve, and enhance wildlife movement. This initiative's purpose is to identify migration and movement patterns, seasonal habitat and wildlife stopover sites, priority areas that can reconnect fragmented habitat ranges, and locations that allow terrestrial and aquatic wildlife species throughout Utah 

## Public Involvement

Since the development of the 2005 and 2015 UWAPs, the public has become more involved in native species conservation in the state. DWR relies heavily on partners, and several community science projects have been developed to better engage the public and implement the UWAP. A few of the communications and event outreach efforts and recent community science projects are listed below.

## Communications and Outreach Efforts

## Blog Posts

DWR publishes a monthly blog post on its website highlighting native wildlife, including these topics (DWR 2024i):
• Conserving wild Bonneville cutthroat trout in the midst of cattle country

• Ask a herpetologist

• The wonderfully weird world of mollusks projects that improve rangeland health, productivity, and sustainability. Eligible projects include water developments, fencing, grazing management planning, and vegetation treatments, with grants available for both large and small livestock operations (Utah Department of Agriculture and Food 2025). GIP also plays a critical role in advocating for and facilitating collaborative, landscape-scale conservation among agricultural producers.
One of GIP's most recognized achievements is its pivotal support of the Three Creeks Grazing Project in Rich County. Facing the threat of grazing permit reductions due to impaired streams and regulatory pressure, 38 ranchers formed the Three Creeks Grazing LLC. With technical guidance and advocacy from GIP, these ranchers developed and implemented a new, sciencebased grazing management plan across 146,000 acres of public land managed by both the BLM and the USFS 
(Hemken 2021)
. The plan, inspired by successful models on neighboring private lands, consolidated 10 separate grazing allotments into a single, coordinated system using high-density, short-duration, time-controlled grazing.
GIP's involvement was instrumental in navigating the complex federal permitting process, securing funding, and building consensus among producers and agencies. The collaborative approach enabled the implementation of infrastructure improvements, active grazing management, and robust monitoring of rangeland health and water quality. The results have been significant: improved water quality, enhanced wildlife habitat (including for greater sagegrouse), increased forage productivity, and greater economic sustainability for participating ranchers.

## Implementation Mechanisms

Utah has a variety of mechanisms that are vital to UWAP implementation and public involvement. One of the goals of the UWAP is to help prevent species being listed under the ESA. UWAP is a tool for prioritizing SGCN species and key habitats for management and conservation. The UWAP enables partners (e.g., USFWS, USFS, BLM) to prioritize projects that would benefit those species and habitats. The UWAP also provides guidance for tools such as the DWR's WRI to help implement the plan. Descriptions of the important tools used to implement the UWAP are provided below.

## Resource Development Coordinating Committee

The Utah Legislature created the Resource Development Coordinating Committee (RDCC) (Utah Code 63L-11-401) to
• assist the state planning coordinator in fulfilling the responsibilities of reviewing and coordinating technical and policy actions that may affect the physical resources of the state; and
• facilitate the exchange of information on those actions among state agencies and other levels of government.
Federal law provides for state collaboration and participation throughout the federal project planning process. The RDCC includes representatives from state agencies, which are generally involved in public lands management. Committee members communicate through the RDCC by submitting projects for review and commenting on federal, state, or local proposed actions.
State agency comments make known a project's potential for positive or adverse impacts and provide recommendations for mitigation. When necessary, RDCC staff coordinate and facilitate state agency comments by publishing a unified state comment. Comments may be most important in accomplishing successful outcomes. The RDCC coordinates the review of many proposed public land planning and development projects such as
• forest health and watershed improvement plans,
• wetland reviews,
• use of public range resources by livestock operators and wildlife,
• oil and gas and mining development, and
• use of public lands by off-highway vehicles (OHVs) and other recreational opportunities.
The status of every project can be viewed through the RDCC Project Management System (Public Lands Policy Coordinating Office 2025). The 2015 UWAP was submitted to the RDCC for review.
The following state agencies are represented on the RDCC:
• Office of the Governor The formal RDCC process and preliminary informal consultations are a very important mechanism for local, state, and federal projects or developments to come to the attention of DNR and DWR. This awareness is crucial, because these projects or developments may be a substantial source of threats to wildlife or their habitats. 

## Public Meetings

DWR's Regional Advisory Councils (RACs) and Utah Wildlife Board (Board) together are the policy arm of DWR. As the ultimate decision makers for matters over which the agency has sole or lead authority, they play a vital role in adapting state wildlife management to new realities.
In each of DWR's five administrative regions, a RAC exists to recommend actions and advise the Board in wildlife and habitat management decisions. Each RAC includes representatives of agriculture, sportsmen, watchable wildlife, local government, federal land agencies, tribal nations (where appropriate), and the public at large. The RACs gather and compile information from DWR staff, the public, and government agencies before making recommendations to the Board.
The Board establishes policies designed to accomplish the purposes and to fulfill the intent of all laws pertaining to wildlife and the preservation, protection, conservation, perpetuation, introduction, and management of wildlife in Utah. In developing wildlife policy, the Board considers the recommendations of DWR personnel and each RAC but may reject recommendations with written explanation. Like RACs, the Board has open meetings where public comment is welcome prior to the finalization of any policy decisions. The governorappointed Board is composed of seven members with expertise or experience in at least one of the following: 1) wildlife management or biology; 2) habitat management, including range or aquatic habitats; 3) business, including knowledge of private land issues; or 4) economics, including knowledge of recreational wildlife uses such as hunting and fishing.
The 2005 UWAP was directed through these channels as it was developed and finalized.
Because the 2025 UWAP is an updated version of an approved plan, an informational presentation was given to the Board to highlight important updates and changes to the plan.
resources of agencies and organizations with long histories of ecosystem management and restoration endeavors in Utah into a single, functional partnership. From 2005 through 2024, the WRI has leveraged well over $377 million to affect the management of over 2.5 million acres. In recent years, approximately $25 to $30 million in diverse public and private funding has been applied to dozens of projects, averaging 110,000-150,000 acres annually.
The WRI has developed three general approaches to address the risks to the shared interests of the partnership:
• Ecosystem restoration through physical manipulations such as seeding, reconstruction, vegetation management, and other means
• Administrative changes in land management made through permitted or allowed uses and management prescriptions
• Communication and team building among the public, stakeholders, and the WRI to promote the understanding of risks to natural resources and values and to improve cooperation and problem solving across boundaries
The partnership is represented at four levels of organization:
• Director's council: Agency and organization administrators meet regularly.
• Statewide core teams: Each member has a representative on a state-level team that meets regularly with conservation organizations to monitor the effectiveness of each group's involvement in the partnership, share information about new programs, discuss problems, and address resource-allocation needs.
• Regional teams: Organized in each of DWR's five administrative regions, these teams consist of Utah Partners for Conservation and Development members, conservation organizations, and stakeholders who meet to discuss priority conservation focus areas, identify potential projects, and pool resources (e.g., funding, technical assistance, logistics support) needed to implement restoration projects.
• Local conservation work groups: These work groups identify local conservation concerns and develop strategies to meet local needs.
Regional teams fill the important role of acting as clearinghouses for conservation priorities and actions. Each team defines focus areas for their region and habitat restoration objectives for those areas. Objectives are developed collaboratively while focusing on management plans, the best available science, and targeted public outreach.

## Member Affiliation

Paul Biotic: Anything living, from plants and animals to microbes.
Channel downcutting: An overall geological process in which hydraulic action deepens a stream or valley by removing material from its floor.
Channelization: Occurs when a river or stream channel has been modified (e.g., straightening, widening, deepening, clearing, or changing location). Reasons a stream may have been modified include flood control, navigation, and drainage.
Climate adaptation: Actions that aid in reducing current or potential foreseeable impacts from climate change.
Climate change: Change in climate patterns (e.g., those resulting from increased atmospheric greenhouse gases like carbon dioxide) and/or events outside the natural range of variation.
Climate refugia: Areas relatively buffered from contemporary climate change over time that enable persistence of valued physical, ecological, and sociocultural resources. More broadly, climate refugia are habitats that components of biodiversity retreat to, persist in, and can potentially expand from under changing environmental conditions.
Community (ecological community): An assemblage of species that coexist in a similar environment.
Conservation Opportunity Areas: Delineated part of the landscape where conservation efforts can have significant benefits to species of greatest conservation need.
Decadence: Forestry term for dead, broken, or deformed tops and/or bole or root rot (U.S.
Forest Service 2007).
Ecoregion: Areas at the spatial scale of ecosystems similar in type, quality, and quantity of environmental resources, including biotic, abiotic, terrestrial, and aquatic ecosystem components (U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 2023).
Ecosystem: A physiogeographically defined area wherein the aggregate physical, chemical, and biological processes and services distinguish it from surrounding areas. Ecosystems are the product of the interacting biodiversity within a given habitat.
data across the United States. LANDFIRE was used for the UWAP to identify terrestrial key habitats and their associated condition.
Landsat imagery: Satellite imagery that captures high-quality images of Earth's surface. This Morphological: Relating to the branch of biology that deals with the form of living organisms and relationships between their structures.
Perennial: Things that are enduring or continually recurring.

## Phenology:

The study of phases or events in biological life cycles such as bird migration or emergence of leaves and flowers.
Population: A collection of individuals of a species in a defined area that may or may not breed with other groups of that species in other places 
(Mills 2013)
.
Problematic native plants and animals: Harmful plants and animals that are originally found within the ecosystem(s) in question but have become out of balance or been released directly or indirectly due to human activities (e.g., overabundant native deer, overabundant algae due to loss of native grazing fish, plague that affects rodents, invasive grasses).
Propagation: Captive breeding to aid the recovery of or augment species with declining populations or help maintain genetic diversity in small, isolated populations.

## Regime:

The conditions or characteristic behaviors of scientific or natural processes.
• Fire regime: The frequency, duration, intensity, extent, and seasonality of wildfire disturbance at a location.
• Flow regime: The pattern of flow or fluid distribution of one fluid phase relative to another. 

## ↑ ↑ ↑ ↑

Types of habitats are comprehensive: every acre in Utah can be assigned to a type of habitat (some will be non-natural [e.g., urban, agriculture]).

## ↑ ↑ ↓ ↓

The number of habitats, which is related to how finely or coarsely they are defined, is considered appropriate for the whole-state scope of the UWAP.

## ↔ ↔ ↓ ↔

The habitats are definable, have broad support and utility (management usefulness), and match or can be cross-walked with habitat schemes of major partners.

## ↔ ↔ ↓ ↔

Habitat data exist in spatial format; maps of habitat locations can be made.

## ↑ ↑ ↔ ↑

Habitat spatial data cover all of Utah from border to border and are consistent throughout that range.

## ↑ ↑ ↓ ↓

Habitats and their spatial data allow for the assessment of the habitats' condition, both current and in the future, all at locations or occurrences within Utah (without extensive fieldwork). The 2015 UWAP used LANDFIRE version 1. 
2 (2012)
. In 2016, LANDFIRE received updates, improvements, and a remap. Ultimately, LANDFIRE version 2.2 (2020a) was used for the 2025 UWAP. One reason LANDFIRE was chosen in 2015 was to measure changes in habitat extent and condition from the 2015 to 2025 UWAP. Some issues prevented the DWR geographic information system (GIS) team from comparing extent and condition in the 2015 UWAP with that of terrestrial key habitats in the 2025 UWAP. However, the DWR GIS team has determined that comparisons will be feasible between revisions using LANDFIRE going forward. These issues are discussed in more detail below.
LANDFIRE was not suitable for aquatic habitats and could only be used for terrestrial habitats for the following reasons:
• The coarse-resolution 30 × 30-meter LANDFIRE pixels do not capture the common types of aquatic spatial features very well (e.g., lines, small polygons, long narrow polygons). One result of this is a systematic under-representation of pixels classified as these kinds of aquatic habitats. The pixels are instead classified as their dominant feature-typically, a terrestrial habitat.
• Two of the key habitats that were considered-Wet Meadow and Wetland-had no corresponding LANDFIRE-defined biophysical settings (BpS). LANDFIRE also does not fully consider purely aquatic habitats (e.g., streams and rivers, ponds and lakes). Better data sets were identified for all aquatic habitats, and these are described below.
Utah Geological Survey partner Diane Menuz contributed a significant amount of time to help identify appropriate data sources for aquatic habitats. Riparian habitat was evaluated with both National Hydrography Dataset Plus and LANDFIRE, and LANDFIRE was determined to be adequate for this habitat.
For the 2025 UWAP, DWR wanted to understand how UWAP partners were using UWAP key habitats and if there was a need to revisit these habitats for the 2025 UWAP. In preparation for the 2025 UWAP comprehensive revision, a UWAP partners meeting was held in March 2022.
Through this meeting and partner surveys conducted before and after the meeting, the UWAP partners indicated a need for some revision of the key habitats. These changes are briefly outlined below. The Core Team was formed from the UWAP partners to help guide the EVTs represent the current distribution of plant communities on the landscape. They are mapped within similar ecological processes, substrates, and/or environmental gradients using decision tree models and Landsat imagery along with elevation, disturbance, biophysical gradient, and field observation data. EVTs also include ruderal or semi-natural vegetation types.
EVTs are described by LANDFIRE in four ways:
1. A name is assigned to each classified habitat community. EVT categories. LANDFIRE BpS categories only represent the vegetation types that would be present in the absence of human impacts. Moreover, BpS represent the vegetation composition and structure that would be present under historical undisturbed conditions rather than what is present today. Given this, BpS serve best as a target for restoration and a standard to describe historical losses rather than a description of current conditions, which is the purpose of the EVT. The partners believed that it was important to consider the current vegetation composition and structure that Utah's wildlife is experiencing and do their best to manage that rather than trying to reach an unrealistic goal of managing for the vegetation composition and structure present prior to Euro-American settlement. The UWAP partners recognize that this represents a significant shift in how UWAP progress is measured, and if EVT's descriptions of current conditions are endorsed as the best expression of remaining habitat potential or taken at face value as the target of restoration, there is a danger that the shift from BpS to EVT as the means of describing vegetation baselines codifies a shifted baseline. Nonetheless, the Terrestrial Key Habitat Subcommittee, the Utah DWR Wildlife Action Plan Implementation Team, and the Core Team discussed this potential change and agreed to use EVT LANDFIRE categories for the 2025 UWAP. The grouping of habitat categories for the 2015 BpS and 2025 EVT data sets were similar, and, in both instances, these broader habitat groupings were then analyzed to ultimately determine key habitats. The Terrestrial Key Habitat Subcommittee considered the extent of landcover in the state, the presence of SGCN, and the feasibility of management to help decide which habitats would be prioritized as key habitats for the 2025 UWAP.

## A Three-Step Process to Determine Extent and Condition of Terrestrial Key Habitats

Using the LANDFIRE data downloaded for the state of Utah-each 30-meter pixel having both its BpS identity and its corresponding EVT-the Terrestrial Key Habitat Subcommittee followed three steps:
1. Identify the habitats in Utah that make up the full candidate set (candidate habitats) is the overall similarity between the Reference Amount and Current Amount columns. Therefore, the dissimilarity between the two columns is the ecological departure of the Current Amounts from the Reference Amounts (in this case, 61%).
This ecological departure analysis is a common approach when working with BpS-based key habitats (as was done in the 2015 UWAP), but it carries issues for the 2025 UWAP because key habitats are defined using EVTs. Because ecological departure is given on the BpS scale, and because a single BpS is likely to contain many EVTs within it, these ecological departure scores become meaningless because they cannot be directly summarized into key habitats. Moreover, ecological departure analyses describe departure only as departure from archetypal or natural successional stage distributions (proportional breakdown of succession classes A-E) and does not address to what extent each of the existing habitats is different from those natural BpS.
Therefore, the 2025 UWAP adopted a solution using weighted means of ecological departures that could be assigned to each EVT. A weighted mean is based on the proportional area of each EVT within a BpS within a HUC-12. In other words, ecological departures were calculated for each BpS within each HUC but also identified the acreage of each EVT within that area unit. The area acreage was used to approximate how much of each ecological departure score was accounted for by each EVT. Doing so provided weighted ecological departure scores for every EVT that could be grouped into terrestrial key habitats and summarized at the HUC-12 or statewide scale. Notes: Aside from the first five rows, unassigned succession classes were removed from the table and are not necessary for analysis. Because some habitat succession classes are not observed by the LANDFIRE model, their reference percentages may not be known, such as with BpS:10190, HUC-12:29083665; however, those values are not necessary for the ecological departure calculations. Given this, aside from BpS:10190, HUC-12:29083665, which is represented with a "?," unobserved succession classes have been excluded from this table for brevity.
An en dash (-) indicates that there is no value for that particular row because there are multiple rows for each BpS composing the lowland sagebrush key habitat and only one ecological departure calculated for each BpS and for the lowland sagebrush key habitat.
* Succession class is the general successional stage that the current habitat is classified within, from A (early successional stage) to E (late successional stage). † Columns three through five represent arbitrary area units for the key habitat at statewide, HUC, and local scales, respectively. ‡ Reference percentage is the LANDFIRE-modeled percentage of the sampling unit that would fall within the corresponding successional stage under archetypal conditions. § Columns 10 and 11 were calculated by dividing column five (Key Habitat Area per HUC-12, BpS, Succession) by columns three and four, respectively. ¶ Weighted ecological departure in column 12 was calculated using the equation presented above. Overall, this condition assessment provides an expanded understanding of Utah's key habitats' current level of naturalness. This assessment addresses key habitats as LANDFIRE-modeled

## 2025

EVTs and provides a weighted ecological departure score for each key habitat that falls within each BpS and HUC-12 sampling unit, thereby summarizing ecological departures at the HUC-12 or statewide scales in a more representative way. As a result, this assessment, as described above, satisfies the condition assessment described under State Wildlife Action Plan Required Element 2 (Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies and the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service 2017).
There are several components that could be improved to gain more insight and accuracy in future analyses. First, this approach is lacking because it does not identify the amount of departure of an EVT or key habitat from natural BpS habitat types. For example, a Desert Shrub BpS and a Mojave Desert Shrub EVT are similar habitats to one another, but a Desert Shrub BpS and an Alpine EVT are very distinct from one another. Furthermore, those BpS-to-EVT differences that result in large changes to the combined height of the vegetation community lead to increased biases over habitats similar in height. For example, comparing Desert Shrub BpS to a pinyon-juniper-dominated habitat would result in greater height discrepancy than comparing Desert Shrub BpS to a sagebrush-dominated habitat. The more drastic change in height between Desert Shrub BpS and a pinyon-juniper-dominated habitat would result in a larger ecological departure score and, consequently, more bias.
Because ecological departure is calculated at the community level within a sampling unit, key habitats that present more drastic changes (compared to BpS) in canopy height will be more costly in final departure scores. Therefore, the extent of these differences in habitat type and habitat height should be accounted for in future analyses to improve condition assessments.
A key area for improvement in future habitat condition assessments is the integration of climate To address these shortcomings, future assessments would benefit from incorporating expertcurated inputs such as refined acreage targets, detailed successional stage distributions, quantified penalties for invasive species, and models tailored specifically to Utah's ecosystems.
In addition to improving the accuracy of ecological evaluations, these enhancements would help clarify the causes of departure and distinguish between degraded and naturally evolving

[FIGURE: Congress passed legislation authorizing the State and Tribal Wildlife Grants (STWG) Program in 2001 in recognition of the federal need to support states with additional native species funding. The intent of the STWG Program was to help slow the rate of new listings under the Endangered Species Act (ESA). To ensure that the funds are well spent, this legislation requires that each participating state and territory develop a state wildlife action plan (SWAP) by 2005. All 56 U.S. states and territories chose to participate in this new conservation program, and each completed a SWAP by the 2005 deadline. In addition, Congress conditioned ongoing participation in the STWG Program on states' reviewing and revising their plans at least once every 10 years. Accordingly, the Utah Division of Wildlife Resources (DWR) has led a conservation planning partnership to update the Utah Wildlife Action Plan (UWAP) in 2015 and to create this 2025 iteration. Although DWR compiled and edited the UWAP, broad partner engagement has created a 2025-2035 UWAP that is truly a plan for everyone working on native species conservation efforts in Utah. This partnership includes state and federal natural resource agencies, academics, tribal nations, and conservation and agriculture advocates. Through 2024, DWR has received over $19 million in federal STWG Program appropriations to implement the 2005 UWAP (Sutter et al. 2005) and]

[FIGURE: Habitats: The location and relative condition of key habitats and community types essential to the conservation of each state's SGCN (see Chapter 2, Key Habitats -Location and Condition).]

[FIGURE: Protection Account funding and funding from many other sources are now routed through the Utah Department of Natural Resources' Watershed Restoration Initiative (WRI). The WRI was established in 1993 and reconvened in 2003 but has gained the most traction in terms of dollars invested, partners involved, and projects completed over the last 15 years. The WRI is a partner-based program that allows partners (i.e., state, local, and federal agencies, tribal nations, and nongovernmental organizations) to pool dollars for projects that improve watershed health and biological diversity, water quality and yield, and opportunities for sustainable uses of natural resources. Since 2005, the WRI and partners have completed over 2,700 projects and provided over $350 million dollars in funding that has improved 2.5 million acres of priority watersheds in Utah (Utah Department of Natural Resources 2024a). The WRI is the primary vehicle for UWAP implementation. Over the past 30 years it has become apparent that reliable funding sources for the conservation of fish, wildlife, plants, and invertebrates are needed if additional species are to be kept from becoming listed as threatened or endangered under the ESA. Throughout the mid-and late 1990s, a national coalition of conservation-minded agencies, organizations, and businesses lobbied Congress for passage of legislation required to allow for such funding. This lobbying campaign, known as Teaming with Wildlife, was partially successful. The U.S. Department of the Interior and Related Agencies Appropriations Act of 2002 created the federal STWG Program, which enables Congressional appropriators to consider funding wildlife and habitat conservation on a year-toyear basis. This law requires that each state has a current, approved SWAP to remain eligible for any STWG Program funding that Congress appropriates. States that choose to participate in the STWG Program must review and revise their SWAPs at least once every 10 years to maintain their eligibility. Utah's initial SWAP was completed and approved in 2005 (Sutter et al. 2005), revised in 2015 (Joint Team 2015), and again in 2025.]

[FIGURE: (2015). † DWR(2020, 2022). Since the 2015 UWAP, 78 species have been retained as SGCN, and 62 species were completely removed as SGCNs. Of these 62 species, 35 were reclassified as SGIN. Of the remaining species, 20 were removed because they no longer qualify as SGCN due to a change in their conservation status. Recent genetic work is redefining mollusk taxonomy in Utah (Linscott and Lucid 2024). Five mollusk species were removed from the SGCN list because they are no longer considered valid species. Genetic work is ongoing, and additional changes to the SGCN/SGIN lists will be likely in the near future. One reptile and one mammal species also were removed due to taxonomic changes. There were several changes in S-ranks for SGCNs. Of the 78 species retained between the 2015 and 2025 revisions, the S-ranks changed for 22 species: five SGCN became more secure, and 17 species became more vulnerable.Case Study: Bonneville Cutthroat Trout -Back from the Brink of ExtinctionBonneville cutthroat trout (BCT) is one of seven recognized subspecies within the Rocky Mountain cutthroat trout interim species classification that historically occurred in the western United States(Markle 2018). BCT occurs in the Bonneville Basin of Wyoming, Idaho, Nevada, and Utah, with over 90% of its range in Utah. Like all cutthroat trout, BCT populations have declined due to competition and hybridization with non-native salmonids and habitat loss. In the 1960s and 1970s, genetically pure BCT were thought to have been extirpated because of more than 100 years of stocking of non-native salmonids, particularly rainbow trout and Yellowstone cutthroat trout, into BCT-occupied streams and lakes(Holden et al. 1974; Sigler and Miller   Woundfin. [FIGURE: 409,738 acres o 9.96% of state surface area (see Figure 9) o 21,892 km 2 (8,453 square miles) • LANDFIRE EVTs (NatureServe 2018) included in this key habitat are as follows: o Colorado Plateau Blackbrush-Mormon-Tea Shrubland o Inter-Mountain Basins Semi-Desert Shrub-Steppe o Mojave Mid-Elevation Mixed Desert Scrub o Southern Colorado Plateau Sand ShrublandConditionDesert shrub habitats are in fair to good condition given the harsh growing conditions; invasive or weedy species sometimes have difficulty becoming established in these habitats. There are exceptions where soil conditions differ, and species such as halogeton, non-native annual grasses, and crested wheatgrass have become established. Impacts from improper historic grazing have reduced the cover of more palatable forbs and grasses, and, in some areas, improper grazing may still be limiting the cover of grasses and forbs, particularly around stock tanks and natural water sources. Given the naturally low vegetation cover, intense precipitation events (i.e., thunderstorms) can produce excessive flooding and erosion events. The ability for washes and gulches to support more well-developed riparian areas is therefore naturally limited.Due to the naturally open terrain, OHVs can easily leave designated trails and cross open desert areas, leading to significant localized soil and vegetation disturbance. This disturbance reduces plant cover and increases susceptibility to both wind-and water-driven erosion. Existing road networks may be affected by severe erosion events and may also cause localized damage to surrounding vegetation as a result of soil erosion and deposition.]

[FIGURE: , flooding, and erosion all impact mountain shrub habitat. A decrease in curl-leaf mountain mahogany occurred from 1954 through 1997 because of increased fire incidences linked to increased cheatgrass dominance; curl-leaf mountain mahogany is easily killed by fires and does not resprout (NatureServe 2018). Other species, such as birch leaf mountain mahogany, depend on fires to reproduce (via seeding). The open spaces created by shrub die-backs can allow for tree growth; however, the xeric characteristics and well-drained steep slopes of these lands have limited growth potential for trees.]

[FIGURE: also common for roads to follow rivers (and thus riparian habitat). Some road placement has led to increased sediment deposition into streams and riparian habitat and indirect pollution impacts from road runoff. Additionally, riparian areas have been impacted directly by large landscape-scale wildfires through the burning of vegetation and indirectly through post-fire flooding, which can scour vegetation and cause stream downcutting and sediment deposition and completely bury riparian habitats. Over time, most post-fire impacts will be resolved through re-establishment of more natural flow regimes, but aggressive weed management will need to occur to allow natural development of seral stages.In montane and lower elevational zones, historical water developments have channelized rivers and streams and have reduced widths of historical floodplains and riparian habitat. Many of these developments have eroded and/or washed away, but many remain, thus simplifying the floodplain and narrowing potential riparian habitats. With less development occurring near streams and rivers, and diminished risks of flood events primarily due to large water storage projects, opportunities exist to identify old stream channelization structures and restore riparian habitat.]

[FIGURE: and Figure28) occurs at elevations between2,195 and   3,353 m (7,200 and 11,000 feet), where finely textured soils, persistent snow accumulation, or windswept dry conditions limit the establishment of trees and shrubs. These grasslands often appear in small pockets across a range of elevations and vegetation types, where topographic and soil conditions favor increased moisture retention and soil deposition. Soils in subalpine grasslands are generally well drained, slightly acidic, and rich in organic material-similar in character to prairie soils. Dominant grasses in this habitat include pine dropseed, timber oatgrass, Parry's oatgrass, Idaho fescue, Thurber's fescue, pullup muhly, and slender wheatgrass. Thurber's fescue is especially common at higher elevations, and Parry's oatgrass dominates on thinner, coarser soils. Forbs and low flowering shrubs also occur frequently in these habitats, including geranium, violet, buttercup, western blue flag, columbine, scarlet gilia, showy fleabane, cinquefoil species, larkspur, monkshood, mountain bluebell, harebell, biscuitroot, false hellebore, monument plant, Penstemon, elephant's-head lousewort, clover, arrowleaf balsamroot, and a variety of daisy-like composite flowers. Exotic species like Kentucky bluegrass and dandelion frequently occur near heavily grazed areas and along roadsides (NatureServe 2018).]

[FIGURE: Badlands-or "sparsely vegetated habitats" as classified by the LANDFIRE EVT model (LANDFIRE 2020a; NatureServe 2018)-are widespread across Utah, largely due to the prevalence of saline-affected geological substrates in many of the state's desert regions. Notable examples of plant species adapted to these conditions include the three milkvetch species under the Milkvetch Conservation Agreement: Cisco milkvetch, Isely's milkvetch, and Stage Station milkvetch (USFWS et al. 2022). Additional characteristic species include dwarf bear-poppy, numerous saltbush species, and other wildflowers whose seeds remain viable for several years until adequate precipitation prompts their germination, rapid growth and flowering, and return to the soil seed bank.]

[FIGURE: meadows occur on gentle to moderate-gradient slopes and in relatively moist habitats. Soils are typically seasonally moist to saturated in the spring but dry out later in the growing season. At montane elevations, soils are usually clays or silt loams, and some occurrences may have inclusions of hydric soils in low depressional areas. At subalpine elevations, soils are derived2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 194from a variety of parent materials and are usually rocky or gravelly, with good aeration and drainage and a well-developed organic layer. Some occurrences are more heavily dominated by grasses, whereas others are more dominated by forbs and occasional shrubs. Mesic meadows differ from wet meadows in that their soils dry out by midsummer. Many SGCN, including the greater sage-grouse, rely upon mesic meadows for water as well as food because these areas provide abundant insect life for critical life stages of young birds.]

[FIGURE: and Figure35) includes perennial bodies of standing freshwater such as natural lakes and reservoirs. For the purpose of the 2025 UWAP, small ponds less than 20 acres in size were categorized as Wetlands Key Habitat because they are more effectively mapped in the NWI source data set. Most lakes and reservoirs are permanently or semipermanently inundated and have wave-formed or bedrock shorelines. Emergent vegetation often occupies the littoral zones, but vegetative cover of the surface area is generally less than 30% (U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 2024a). Clear lakes and reservoirs often support submerged or floating aquatic vegetation. Lakes and reservoirs vary in depth from Lake Powell at 178 m (583 feet) and Bear Lake at 63 m (208 feet) to Utah Lake at approximately 5.5 m (18 feet)(Abbott et al. 2022). Larger and deeper reservoirs share many features of lakes, including thermal stratification; in contrast, smaller waterbodies have relatively uniform water temperatures.]

[FIGURE: Figure 34. Lakes and Reservoirs Key Habitat Statewide Distribution]

[FIGURE: potential than small streams, which allows floodplain features such as alluvial bars, side channels, and overhanging banks to develop. Riparian habitats along large streams can be diverse and productive and can include forests, shrublands, and meadows. Large woody debris is more abundant in large streams; further diversifies stream habitat; and is an important labile2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 206substrate for periphyton, microbes, and macroinvertebrates. Water temperatures are generally cool to cold, although temperatures rise during the summer.Rivers (fifth order and larger) include all mainstem rivers and their major tributaries. Streamflow is characterized by high-volume spring and summer flooding from snowmelt and summer base flows that are sustained by groundwater and larger perennial tributaries. Foothill and lower montane rivers have moderate gradients with boulder and cobble rapids and riffles; cobble and gravel runs; and large pools with sand, gravel, and silt. The Colorado and Green Rivers have wide, dynamic floodplains with complex aquatic habitats, although tamarisk and reduced spring flow due to reservoirs and diversions (i.e., human water demand) reduce the connection with the floodplain. High flows move sediment and woody debris that form side and braided channels, backwaters, oxbow waterbodies, and wetlands. Canyon reaches tend to have rapids created by large boulder inputs from side canyon fans and cliffs, followed by large and deep pools and sandy beaches in eddies.Larger rivers and streams with higher volumes and velocities also have greater stream capacity (i.e., ability to transport sediment) and stream competence (i.e., movement of larger sediment particles). Rivers with greater stream competence have the ability to shape aquatic habitat by undercutting banks, establishing side channel habitat, and moving boulders and large woody debris. Streamflow, sedimentation, and geology collaboratively shape aquatic habitats through various mechanisms. Streamflow dynamics, including velocity variations, give rise to distinct habitat types such as pools, riffles, and runs, each supporting different aquatic species and ecological functions. Sedimentation processes contribute to habitat formation by depositing sediments to create features such as sandbars and gravel beds, whereas erosion can undercut banks and deepen pools. Additionally, sediment transport introduces large woody debris into streams, enhancing habitat complexity. Geology influences substrate composition, channel morphology, and the formation of features like undercut banks, collectively shaping habitat structure and providing essential niches for aquatic organisms (McCabe 2011).]

[FIGURE: Drought, human demand for water, changes to hydrology from development, forest management, and invasive species (e.g., phragmites) have reduced the amount of habitat available for migratory birds. GSL fell to a record low water level in October 2021 and again in2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 2 | 218November 2022. See the target elevation set in the Final Great Salt Lake Comprehensive Management Plan and Record of Decision for the severity levels of possible drought (FFSL 2013).GSL's salinity varies with the lake's water volume. Increased salinity observed during the historic low lake levels in the South Arm in November 2022 threatened an ecosystem collapse. With the increased frequency of droughts and increased demand for water due to human population growth in the areas surrounding the GSL ecosystem, increased salinity levels and a future ecosystem collapse will remain a threat(Steed 2024).Non-native, invasive phragmites covers much of GSL's wetlands. Phragmites is an aggressive noxious weed that can quickly colonize ecosystems by seeds, stolons, and rhizomes. This dense species can alter the habitat, resulting in negative effects on birds and other species(Hazelton et al. 2018). Phragmites disrupts hydrology and sediment transport and increases evapotranspiration rates(Alminagorta 2017), which reduces water availability for wetlands. In addition, phragmites converts diverse native habitats of sparse, patchy, low vegetation and open water (i.e., good migratory bird habitat) to impenetrable, dense, homogenous, fire-prone, 3.7-m-tall (12.0-foot-tall) vegetation stands with no open water, which eliminates migratory bird habitat. The State of Utah has been aggressively treating and removing phragmites (FFSL 2024).]

[FIGURE: Organization" (IPCC 2018). Climate change is projected to have profound effects in Utah, characterized by a reduced snowpack due to more precipitation falling as rain than snow, more snow melting during the winter, and earlier snowmelt each spring; more frequent heat waves; and a decrease in the flows of Utah's rivers (U.S. Environmental Protection Agency 2016). Changes in these climatic indicators have the potential to impact Utah's habitats, altering the distribution and composition of fish, wildlife, plants, and invertebrates. Changes to the composition of Utah's ecosystems will then have consequences for resource-driven economic sectors and associated stakeholders. The impacts of climate change extend beyond environmental shifts; these impacts are crucially affecting resources essential for Utah's communities and health, including food production, water availability, and biodiversity (IPCC 2023). Additionally, climate change influences the spread of infectious diseases, exacerbates extreme weather events, alters species' ranges and seasonal timing, and disrupts ecosystem structures (IPCC 2023). Notably, global surface temperatures have increased faster since 1970 than in any other 50-year period over the last 2,000 years, underscoring the urgency of addressing these interconnected challenges (IPCC 2023). Given this, understanding and mitigating these impacts is vital for preserving Utah's natural heritage and ensuring the well-being of its residents. The Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies (2022) outlines the foundational issues that fish and wildlife face because of climate change and provides resources to implement adaptive responses to this threat. This guidance is broken into seven principles for incorporating climate change into state wildlife action plans: 1) fully integrate climate change into state wildlife action plans; 2) adopt forward-looking goals; 3) explicitly link actions to climate vulnerabilities; 4) manage for change, not just persistence; 5) consider broader landscapes and longer time frames; 6) address uncertainty by considering future scenarios and use of adaptive management; and 7) engage diverse partners with climate experience and expertise. 2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 3 | 239 The Utah Wildlife Action Plan (UWAP) Climate Adaptation Subcommittee has undertaken tasks to begin incorporating climate considerations throughout the plan, particularly engaging diverse partners to identify short-and long-term goals for making progress on addressing climate. The group decided that identifying climate refugia in Utah would be the first step in addressing climate for the 2025 UWAP. This work was completed by Dr. Edward Hammill from Utah State]

The original four RCPs areRCP 2.6, 4.5, 6, and 8.5. The higher the RCP value, the higher the greenhouse gas concentration. Two commonly used scenarios are RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5. RCP 4.5 is a lower emissions scenario based on a future where the concentration of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere stabilizes before the year 2100; RCP 8.5 is a higher emissions scenario where greenhouse gas concentrations continually increase(RCP Database 2009). Looking ahead to 2100, under RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5, summer temperatures in Utah are expected to rise by 3.4°F and 9.0°F, respectively (Figure47).]

From 1916 to 2014, stream flow in the Upper Colorado River Basin, which includes eastern Utah, declined by over 16%, despite a slight increase in precipitation over the same period(McCabe et al. 2017;Milly and Dunne 2020;Xiao et al. 2018). Forest Service continues to develop climate change vulnerability assessments and engage in climate adaptation efforts, furthering the incorporation of climate science and climate adaptation principles, strategies, and tactics into project designs and management decisions.2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 3 | 257The Intermountain Region of the U.S. Forest Service partnered with the research branch of the agency, along with many other partners, and successfully collaborated in a region-wide climate assessment effort from 2015 to 2018. This was published as a general technical report through the Rocky Mountain Research Station in 2018 and provides a comprehensive foundation for the agency and partners to continue to build on. The general technical report is titled Climate Change Vulnerability and Adaptation in the Intermountain Region and consists of 15 chapters, including chapters on climate science, hydrology, vegetation, terrestrial species, recreation, and ecosystem services. The successful effort took an all-hands, all-lands approach and welcomed the input of partners(Halofsky et al. 2018a(Halofsky et al.  , 2018b)). The region and agency continue to build on this work though climate scorecards, research, reports, and workshops.The Nature Conservancy Strategies: Resilient Sites and Connected LandscapesIn 2016, The Nature Conservancy (TNC) released the first version of the resilient and connected network (RCN) analysis tool for terrestrial ecosystems(Anderson et al. 2023; TNC 2025a). The RCN is a spatial tool that provides an analysis at reasonable resolution (30-180 m [approximately 98-590 feet]) and identifies areas on the landscape that provide landscape resilience to climate change, areas with connectivity and flow, areas with recognized biodiversity, and a compilation of all three as the RCN for the purposes of planning and conservation. This mapping tool has been periodically updated and increased in spatial extent.In 2024, a freshwater version of the RCN tool was released that focused on freshwater ecosystems of the United States(Anderson, Olivero, et al. 2024; TNC 2025b). The landscape resilience analysis shows a gradient (e.g., from low to high) of landscape resilience to climate change. Landscape resilience is partially based on topographic diversity and landscape connectivity. For example, a conical-shaped mountain without disturbance would have high resilience because species that are able to move can shift their range to the appropriate slope and aspect. Connectivity and flow identify areas with high landscape connectivity (e.g., no development, interstates, natural boundaries, etc.) and are split categorically into gradients of diffuse flow and concentrated flow. Areas with recognized biodiversity were identified in TNC's ecoregional planning efforts from the 2000s but rely heavily on state-level data sets from natural heritage programs. The RCNs are areas with high values of landscape resilience, flow, and recognized biodiversity. The analysis was generally biophysical and not species oriented but was developed to identify areas with high conservation value based on their climate resilience, biodiversity, and connectivity. 2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 3 | 258 General Strategies Obtain continuous water temperature data from streams important to SGCNs and upload data to the Utah Division of Water Quality continuous water quality monitoring dashboard (Utah Division of Water Quality 2025).]

Annual monitoring determined changes to length of time livestock spent in each allotment. Because of its negative impacts to wildlife, including SGCN birds, land managers around GSL seek to manage or contain phragmites. Past approaches to manage phragmites included herbicide applications, mowing, burning, water management, and revegetation. On public lands, encourage collaborative problem solving and monitoring among agency staff, permittees, and interested parties.6.4.1 Use cost-share and technical assistance programs administered by NRCS, UFBF, UDAF, USU Extension, and other organizations to improve landscapes that are still impacted by historic improper grazing practices.to better understand the full extent of environmental consequences, UWAP partners believe that these impacts will increase over the next 10 years as Utah continues to expand its energy portfolio.Case Study: Developing a Conservation Agreement for Two Species of Penstemons Two of Utah's SGCN plants, the White River beardtongue and the Graham's beardtongue, are herbaceous perennials found growing in the Uinta Basin on white shale ledges and talus slopes of the Green River Formation. Among and under those white shale ledges and other areas within the Green River Formation lie large deposits of both oil shale and tar sandstwo resources often sought for extraction for energy needs. Due to the rarity of these plants and the threat of widespread oil shale and tar sands extraction, these two plants were proposed to be listed under the ESA. In 2013, representatives from federal, state, and local entities (USFWS, Uintah County [Utah], DWR, Rio Blanco County [Colorado], Utah Public Lands Policy Coordinating Office, BLM, and TLA) formed the Penstemon Conservation Team. The purpose of the Penstemon Conservation Team is to identify, avoid, minimize, and mitigate potential threats to these two penstemons and their habitats and to promote the species' long-term persistence to prevent the need to list either species under the ESA. Hundreds of person hours of meetings with the above stated partners were conducted to address plant mortality and habitat fragmentation due to energy development as well as indirect disturbance, population vulnerability, climate change, and lack of range-wide protection as well as the cumulative effects on these plants due to these stated factors. The resulting penstemon conservation agreement and strategy (Penstemon Conservation Team 2014) details the information and the process with which the Penstemon]

[FIGURE: 2009, multiple state, federal, local, and private entities came together to develop a solution to improve motorist safety and reduce wildlife-vehicle collisions while maintaining habitat connectivity for mule deer. DWR and UDOT worked together with multiple partners, including Arizona Game and Fish Department, the Federal Highway Administration, Kane County, the BLM, Grand Staircase-Escalante National Monument, Sportsmen for Fish and Wildlife, the Mule Deer Foundation, and others to fund and implement the U.S. Highway 89 Kanab-Paunsaugunt Wildlife Crossing Project. Wildlife exclusionary fencing and associated infrastructure such as cattleguards, gates, and escape ramps were installed along 20.1 km (12.5 miles) of roadway to guide mule deer to four existing structures and three newly constructed wildlife underpasses completed in 2013. DWR, UDOT, and Arizona Game and Fish Department initiated a 5-year postconstruction monitoring study with USU. During monitoring, various other wildlife species were observed using the crossings, such as jackrabbit, badger, black bear, bobcat, coyote, and mountain lion, which indicates that SGCNs are benefiting from the project. The study found the crash rate had decreased by 53%, and overall success rate at all seven structures during the entire study was 77%. Six of the structures had mule deer success rates over 90% by year five of the study. Phase I of the U.S. Highway 89 Kanab-Paunsaugunt Wildlife Crossing Project was reported as "one of the most successful mule deer mitigation projects in all of North America" (Cramer and Hamlin 2019). The project is a great example of what partners can accomplish when they come together to collaborate to find solutions. The Wildlife Crossing Pilot Program was established in 2021 under the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law. Under this program, the Federal Highway Administration allocated funding for grants to reduce wildlife-vehicle collisions while improving habitat connectivity for terrestrial and aquatic species. To help match this federal grant opportunity, the Utah State Legislature provided UDOT $20 million, with support from many different organizations. The partnership saw this as an opportunity to continue with Phase II of the U.S. Highway 89 Kanab-Paunsaugunt Wildlife Crossing Project. The partnership collaboratively applied for the Wildlife Crossing Pilot Program grant to construct more wildlife exclusionary fencing and crossing along U.S. Highway 89. Phase II was awarded $5 million from the Wildlife Crossing Pilot Program grant by leveraging $5 million of matching funds provided by the Utah State Legislature. It is anticipated that Phase II will begin in 2025/2026 and will include an additional 11.6 km (7.2 miles) of exclusionary fencing, and the upgrading of two existing culverts for wildlife passage, and the construction of a new wildlife underpass. Additional funding contributions include $300,000 from DWR's successful National Fish and Wildlife Foundation grant and $100,000 from conservation organizations.]
