[FIGURE: delta habitats were completely lost over time due to channelization of the rivers to accommodate growing development. Approximately 80% of the annual June sucker spawning migration occurs in the Provo River, Utah Lake's largest tributary. Dredging and channelization transformed the lower reaches of the Provo River into a deep, cold canal with minimal cover. Although abundant June sucker larvae would drift down into this habitat each year, recruitment was essentially zero because of these habitat conditions. The Provo River Delta Project was initiated by the June Sucker Recovery Implementation Program (JSRIP) (2024), led by the Utah Reclamation, Mitigation, and Conservation Commission to create complex delta habitat to promote June sucker recruitment (Utah Reclamation, Mitigation, and Conservation Commission et al. 2025a). The delta project officially broke ground on June 11, 2020 (Utah Reclamation, Mitigation, and Conservation Commission et al. 2025b), and the next 3 years were mostly spent excavating a system of braided channels and wetland ponds in 260 acres of acquired agricultural land just north of the original Provo River channel. Major revegetation efforts followed construction each fall, with stewardship activities taking place throughout the year to cultivate and protect plantings.In 2022, the north end of Skipper Bay Dike, which was initially constructed in the 1940s to keep Utah Lake from flooding the area, was lowered (JSRIP 2022), and four outlet channels were Provo River Delta. Photograph by UtahByAir, Utah County, 2024.]

[FIGURE: Work with landowners requesting stream alteration permits to voluntarily improve their project design or plans; ultimately decline permits that debilitate the floodplain and downstream habitat.Likely Authorities, Stakeholders, and/or Partners• DNR (including DWRi, DWRe, UGS, and DWR) City and county stakeholders2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 355Threat: Groundwater Pumping. As Utah's population continues to grow, additional water resources will be needed. Some groundwater resources have been used for decades, and others have been developed more recently. DWRi has created groundwater management plans (DWRi 2025) for areas throughout Utah to promote wise use of the groundwater, protect existing water rights, and address water quality issues and over-appropriation of groundwater. The creation, requirements, management, purpose, and effects of these plans are explained in Section 73-5-15 of the Utah State code. UGS monitors groundwater levels and conditions that DWRi relies upon for these management plans (UGS 2024f).]

[FIGURE: 2009, the UGS established a monitoring network of 55 shallow wells (0.9-1.8 m [3.0-6.0 feet] deep), 68 deeper wells(3.1-45.7 m [10.0-150.0 feet] deep), and five spring gages to collect water level and other hydrologic data throughout Snake Valley. The primary purpose of this monitoring project is to provide baseline data on the magnitude and timing of surface water and groundwater fluctuations in wetlands crucial to SGCNs. Initially conceived in response to a nowwithdrawn groundwater pumping project proposed by the Southern Nevada Water Authority, local applications for increased groundwater pumping continue, and groundwater levels in monitoring wells near Garrison, Utah, have declined by 0.06 to 1.83 m (0.20-0.60 feet) per year since the mid-1990s(Hurlow and Inkenbrandt 2016). Based on these measured water level2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 356declines and anecdotal evidence of SGCN habitat decline in Snake Valley wetlands, biologists and other stakeholders have identified a clear need for continued monitoring. Water level monitoring serves as proxy habitat monitoring, providing biologists with a quantitative data set, including more locations and observations than would be feasible through a typical field effort. This quantitative data set can be used to identify long-term trends, plan and measure habitat improvement success, and justify management or water rights protests. UGS, DWR, and the BLM have collected considerable hydrologic and species data in wetland complexes throughout Snake Valley over the past 15 years. In 2021, UGS published a 10-year summary report evaluating 10-year trends in water levels for individual wells and whole wetland complexes(Goodwin et al. 2021). From 2010 to 2021, the wetland complexes with largest significantly declining trends were in southern Snake Valley (nearest to areas with dense irrigated agriculture) at Big Springs and Burbank Meadows, with trends of -0.025 and -0.029 m (-0.082 and -0.096 feet) per year, respectively(Goodwin et al. 2021). Over the same 2010 to 2021 time period, 10-year trends at Leland-Harris, Bishop Springs, and Gandy Salt Marsh wetland complexes were either insignificant or too contradictory to reliably estimate long-term trends across the entire complex; however, 15-year trends (2009-2023) calculated from early spring readings at deeper wells(3-8 m [10-25 feet]) located near source springheads for Leland-Harris, Bishop Springs, and Gandy Salt Marsh identify declining water levels at rates of -0.012, 0.012, and -0.027 m (-0.04, -0.04, and -0.09 feet) per year, respectively (UGS 2025). UGS continues to collect hydrologic data in Snake Valley and provides current water level data and 15-year trends for deeper wells in the Utah Groundwater Data Hub (UGS 2025).]

[FIGURE: Authorities, Stakeholders, and/or Partners • DNR (including DWRi, DWRe, UGS, FFSL, DWR, and Division of State Parks) A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 360Threat: Water Allocation Policies. In Utah, the right to use water is based on the prior appropriations doctrine, which originated in the 1870s. A core principle of this doctrine is that rights are conditional on beneficial use (i.e., agricultural, industrial, or urban). For over a century, maintaining ecosystem services or values was not considered a beneficial use of water, and attempts to do so could result in loss of water rights.In 1986, the Utah State Legislature passed a bill enabling DWR and the Division of State Parks to acquire and hold instream flow rights under certain circumstances. Although the statutory definition of beneficial use remained unchanged, as a practical matter, state agencies began recognizing the beneficial use of instream flows for specific environmental or recreational purposes.The 1986 law was amended in 2008 and 2013 to allow private, nonprofit fishing groups to lease water to protect or restore stream flows for three native trout taxa. This is an area where there are still great needs, but some progress has been made by working with owners of water rights and state and federal regulations to find mutually beneficial solutions.Most recently, in 2022, the Utah State Legislature passed House Bill 33 (Instream Water Flow Amendments), which included FFSL as an entity that can hold environmental water rights. Most significantly, this bill recognized instream flow or environmental water as a beneficial use and allows private water right holders to work with one of the three DNR divisions to modify or change their water right beneficial use to instream or environmental purposes. Prior to this legislation, the seniority of water rights was lost if the beneficial use of a water right was changed to instream flow or environmental purposes, but now the water right retains its original priority date, which ensures the water right will provide the environmental benefits that are intended.]

[FIGURE: Phased chemical treatments successfully eradicated red shiner from the Virgin River in Utah in 2021, when the final phase was successfully completed between the Stateline Barrier in Utah and the Virgin River Gorge Barrier in Arizona. Although the eradication of red shiner from Utah is a significant achievement, it is imperative to continue distribution monitoring, barrier function and maintenance, and downstream eradication to prevent recolonization and facilitate native fish population response. The successful treatment of the Virgin River in Utah provides a buffer between red shiner-free reaches (upstream of Stateline Barrier) and occupied reaches (downstream of Virgin River Gorge Barrier), greatly reducing the likelihood that red shiner will recolonize the Virgin River in Utah. The eradication of red shiner will aid in the expansion of viable populations of woundfin and Virgin River chub, but it is imperative to maintain a buffer reach that is free of red shiner as well as continuing downstream eradication efforts to continue to support recovery of these endangered native fish. Prevention is often cheaper and more effective than either eradication or suppression. Therefore, due to the ongoing threat of recolonization and illegal introductions, intensive early detection monitoring has been implemented biannually to evaluate the status of red shiner in the Virgin River Basin in Utah. Early detection monitoring in 2024 confirmed red shiner are not present in the Virgin River Basin in Utah.]

[FIGURE: A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 371]

[FIGURE: DNR (including DWRi, DWRe, FFSL, DWR, and Division of Outdoor Recreation) Plant Species (non-native). Non-native plants that dominate vegetation communities can significantly alter the composition, structure, and function of habitats. In particular, their spread can degrade native biodiversity, reduce soil stability, and disrupt hydrological processes. Additionally, the dominance of invasive plants can fundamentally alter ecological processes such as fire or flood regimes, accelerating the decline or even local extinction of SGCNs. Beyond their ecological impacts, invasive plants also impose economic burdens by increasing agricultural production costs, reducing crop quality, depleting water and fertilizer resources, and heightening wildfire risks.Many invasive plant species were initially introduced for beneficial purposes, such as erosion control, windbreaks, or ornamental landscaping, but have since spread uncontrollably, threatening Utah's waterways and ecosystems. For example, tamarisk (or saltcedar), a deciduous shrub originally planted to control erosion along river corridors in the Southwest, is now widespread across Utah's floodplains, stream courses, salt flats, marshes, and irrigation2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 4 | 372ditches. This species consumes large amounts of water, provides little value to wildlife, increases fire frequency, and disrupts biodiversity and riparian hydrology, creating significant ecological and management challenges. Other invasive species, such as periwinkle and bermudagrass, remain commercially available for landscaping despite their aggressive growth and harmful impacts to native ecosystems.Certain land use practices unintentionally facilitate the spread of invasive plants by creating disturbances that suppress native biological communities and provide open ecological space for invasion. For example, livestock grazing and road development have contributed to the spread of cheatgrass by eliminating native bunchgrasses and damaging biological soil crusts.]

[FIGURE: Tamarisk dominance on the San Rafael River has created a vicious cycle by trapping sediment along the river's banks, causing them to rise. This leads to channel incision and narrowing, which leads to further entrapment of the river within its banks and disconnection from its floodplain. The goals of tamarisk removal on the San Rafael River were to increase channel movement laterally, increase instream habitat complexity, recover native vegetation, and increase hydrologic connectivity of the river to its floodplain. DWR began whole-tree and root-wad removal on the San Rafael River in 2009. Tamarisk was piled and left for future burning. In 2011, a very high snowpack led to a sustained springtime flood, where the river connected with the floodplain. [FIGURE: A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 6 | 428 Twenty years ago, Congress created the State and Tribal Wildlife Grants (STWG) Program, which led to the development of SWAPs nationwide. Many of the first SWAPs from 2005 were inconsistent in the terminology used to describe threats, data gaps, and essential conservation actions. This inconsistency impeded conservation across state borders, and challenged efforts toward collaboration, coordination, and joint fundraising within states. Recently, the Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies (AFWA) President's Task Force on Shared Science and Landscape Conservation Priorities assembled the SWAP and Landscape Conservation Work Group to develop recommendations on how SWAPs can be made even more effective at improving range-wide conservation of SGCN (AFWA 2021a). The work group adopted guiding principles and provided recommended actions to facilitate implementation. Utah is implementing this]

[FIGURE: 2015 in SWAPs for six southwestern states and Texas (U.S. Geological Survey 2015): the western yellow-billed cuckoo. This section outlines current and potential future collaborative efforts to better conserve this important species. Conservation of wide-ranging, migrant species at the state level is critical; however, it is also important to understand the value of coordinating each state's resources and conservation initiatives in the context of delivering population-level conservation successes. The recovery and sustainability of regionally important SGCNs, such as the federally threatened western yellow-billed cuckoo, depends on cross-border (state and international) collaboration. Western states encompass important breeding habitats for this subspecies of yellow-billed cuckoo (Birds of the World 2024), including federally designated critical habitats within Arizona, California, Colorado, Idaho, New Mexico, Texas, and Utah (U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service [USFWS] 2021) (Figure 57). River corridors represent especially important habitat for this subspecies across its range; xeroriparian habitat is consistently used in southeastern Arizona. Western Yellow-Billed Cuckoo. Photograph by Chrissy Kondrat, Arizona Game and Fish Department, 2018.]

[FIGURE: Figure 57. Habitat Suitability and Critical Habitat for Western Yellow-Billed Cuckoo in the United States Sources: NatureServe and Heritage Network Partners (2023); USFWS (2024b).]

[FIGURE: Figure 58. Yellow-Billed Cuckoo Abundance Map Across its Full Annual CycleSource:Fink et al. (2022).Actions that require implementation of FAC conservation and those that were identified by the western yellow-billed cuckoo working group as important to implement on wintering grounds for this subspecies include conserving the Gran Chaco (scrub-forest formation); identifying and conserving critical stopover sites in tropical dry forests and other habitats; and identifying population-specific migratory routes. There are at least four Southern Wings projects that have addressed, or currently are addressing, some of these and other important actions for the yellow-billed cuckoo (Southern Wings 2024). The urgent need for additional work in the yellowbilled cuckoo's wintering grounds, such as a Southern Wings project that focuses on the Gran Chaco, presents new collaborative opportunities for states to advance FAC conservation of the species.]

[FIGURE: especially with limited funding. Effectively implementing SWAPs within state boundaries has been the focus in the West, and wildlife diversity and SWAP coordination has suffered accordingly. Other AFWA regions have Wildlife Diversity/SWAP committees (e.g., Northeast Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies, Midwest Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies) and they have been effectively aligning their SWAPs over the past decade. Seeing the need for better SWAP coordination, the states of Arizona, California, New Mexico, Nevada, and Utah began meeting monthly in 2021 (Colorado and Texas joined this group in 2023) to share information and better coordinate SWAP alignment. These meetings facilitated cross-border discussions on aligning SWAP SGCNs, habitats, and threats. The Southwest SWAP Cross Borders Group organized a WAFWA Wildlife Diversity and SWAP workshop in January 2023 to discuss opportunities to further collaborate across borders and 2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • C H A P T E R 6 | 434]

[FIGURE: Utah's representative to the WMNIP Working Group served as chairperson and steered the activities accomplished by the WMNIP Working Group. The chairperson coordinated monthly meetings, reported minutes and accomplishments, developed the WMNIP Working Group Shared Priority Species database, recruited additional state involvement beyond the seven original member states (Arizona, California, Idaho, Nevada, Oregon, Utah, and Washington), and led both the working group charter revision and the development of the 3-year work plan. The chairperson engaged in extensive public outreach to promote awareness of the Western Monarch Butterfly Conservation Plan 2019-2069 (WAFWA 2019) and the conservation needs of insect pollinators, virtually and in person, through multiple presentations to stakeholders, partners, and professional societies. The chairperson also facilitated partnerships with academic and nonprofit organizations to benefit western monarch and native insect pollinator data collection, information sharing, and community building. As a result, the WMNIP Working Group priorities and approach closely resemble those of Utah's Rare Insect Conservation Program, effectively setting a standard model that can be followed by other state insect conservation efforts (WAFWA 2025).the Bureau of Land Management (BLM), the Natural Resources Conservation Service, and the U.S. Forest Service (USFS), worked together to help fund this project through DNR's Watershed Restoration Initiative (WRI) to map ecological systems and current vegetation classes and estimate departure from reference and desired management conditions (similar to LANDFIRE). This particular LCF is completed at discrete sites when funding is available, with the intention of mapping more high-priority sites across Utah. The first LCF site covered a large area of high conservation value for all partners, allowing for more comprehensive management of wildlife and vegetation across jurisdictional boundaries. For example, this tool facilitated management and reconnection of the Hamlin Valley and Pine Valley greater sage-grouse populations(Provencher et al. 2022). As of 2024, there are a total of nine LCF sites that have been completed, totaling 3.0 million acres across Utah (Figure59).The switch from using LANDFIRE Biophysical Settings (BpS) to LANDFIRE Existing Vegetation Type (EVT) categories for defining terrestrial key habitats (see Chapter 2, Key Habitats -Location and Condition) has created an issue with how terrestrial key habitat condition will be measured. If LCF could be extended across the entire state of Utah, this tool would allow the use of current vegetation types to define key habitats with the added benefit of a built-in condition metric.]

[FIGURE: to move safely from one habitat area to another. Through extensive tracking efforts to document and analyze wildlife movement and an award-winning, user-controlled database, Wildlife Migration Initiative's partners have implemented projects across jurisdictional boundaries to improve habitat connectivity, reduce highway safety concerns, and assist with land use planning.The effort to document wildlife movement in Utah has already amassed over 43 million animal locations, and the amount of data is growing at an exponential rate. To manage, analyze, and share big data, DWR developed cloud-based data storage and processing tools that can scale as the amount of and demand for data increases. DWR is also automating the flow of data so that it can be accessed and analyzed in near real time by approved users. Dashboards are being developed that allow biologists and researchers to rapidly analyze and mine data. As data increase for SGCN species, Wildlife Migration Initiative data systems can be used to improve understanding of movement patterns (DWR 2025c).]

[FIGURE: development of the 2025 UWAP revision. The Terrestrial Key Habitat and Aquatic Key Habitat Subcommittees were then formed to revise key habitats for the 2025 UWAP. For the 2025 UWAP, the DWR GIS team downloaded LANDFIRE version 2.2 (2020a) data for Utah from the LANDFIRE website (LANDFIRE 2024b). Specifically, for determining both the extent and condition of habitats, the DWR GIS team obtained the BpS and Existing Vegetation Type (EVT) data products shown in Table D-1. LANDFIRE BpS are derived from the current biophysical environment but also from an approximation of the historical disturbance regime. They are a modeled representation of the vegetation that might have been dominant on the landscape before Euro-American settlement (LANDFIRE 2020b) rather than the existing vegetation on the landscape. See the discussion of weighted departure scores in the A Three-Step Process to Determine Extent and Condition of Terrestrial Key Habitats section below.]

[FIGURE: A modeled spatial distribution is provided for each habitat community within the landscape. Each EVT is further classified into strata of successional stages (largely determined by vegetation height [Smith 2024]) designated by the letters A, B, C, D, and E (although D and E are not always used); these generally correspond with advancing age or succession status (i.e., A→B→C→D→E = Young → Middle-Aged → Old = Early → Mid → Late Succession). 4. Some portions of most EVTs are identified as uncharacteristic and are designated by the letter U. These uncharacteristic EVT identities reflect altered conditions resulting from post-settlement human activities, either unnatural disturbances such as agriculture and development or proactive management or restoration. The uncharacteristic class can include situations such as dominance of invasive annual grasses (e.g., cheatgrass), shrublands where the herbaceous understory is depleted, a former floodplain with a decreased gradient and energy flow because of stream-channel downcutting, urban infrastructure, or even a range or wildlife seeding with crested wheatgrass. The 2015 UWAP used LANDFIRE BpS categories to define terrestrial key habitats. For the 2025 UWAP, some UWAP partners encouraged a re-examination of the reasons for using BpS over 2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • A P P E N D I X D | D -6]

[FIGURE: From among the candidate habitats, design and run a process to pick out the key habitats Determine the current extent and condition of the key habitats For Step 1, the Terrestrial Key Habitat Subcommittee assessed the candidate habitats identified in the 2005 and 2015 UWAPs and revised the candidate habitat list. For Step 2, LANDFIRE BpS habitat classifications were used and aggregated into broader groups to create candidate habitats in 2015. For Step 2 of the 2025 revision, EVT habitat classifications were aggregated into broader habitat groups to ultimately create candidate habitats. Some refinements to the boundaries are not the same, so the 2015 UWAP key habitat analyses were not directly applicable to the 2025 UWAP key habitats. Furthermore, 2015 and 2025 UWAP key habitat statistics could not be directly compared against each other to show changes in habitat scale or structure over the past decade because LANDFIRE underwent a data remap(LANDFIRE 2016)    to improve its mapping performance. The impacts of that remap were broad enough to cause noticeable changes in habitat areas that could not be explained biologically. This led the Terrestrial Key Habitat Subcommittee to exclude these comparisons from analysis. Despite the infeasibility of analyzing a comparison from 2015 to 2025, DWR plans to present comparisons between 2025 and 2035 UWAP revisions as metrics of change and progress for more complete habitat condition assessments. The 2025 and 2035 comparison analyses will be more feasible in future revisions because there will be a more direct comparison of EVT categories across decades, combined with a (presumptively) more consistent LANDFIRE model on which to base the comparisons versus the previous 2015 to 2025 comparison analysis.Despite not being able to analyze meaningful comparisons between 2015 and 2025 terrestrial key habitats, the DWR GIS team still provided an assessment of habitat condition for each key habitat using a modification to the ecological departure calculations performed in the 2015 UWAP. To describe this modified condition assessment, it is essential to grasp the concept of ecological departure in the LANDFIRE context. Below are two definitions of ecological departure using different words to describe the same concept.The ecological departure of a BpS is the dissimilarity between 1. the amounts (proportion) of vegetation successional classes (age classes) expected under reference conditions; and 2. the amounts (proportion) of vegetation successional classes (age classes) that are currently present on the landscape.Ecological departure summarizes, in a single number, how out of balance a BpS is in terms of dissimilarity between 1. the current distribution of a habitat's successional classes that are present in an area, and 2. the amounts of those classes that would be expected to occur under a reference baseline of natural disturbance regimes and climate.Ecological departure is fundamentally a comparison of current habitat coverage and successional age classes compared to archetypal conditions, or, conditions prior to Euro-American settlement. The departure scores are calculated within specifically defined geographic areas. In the 2025 UWAP, ecological departure metrics are summarized at both the statewide and 12-digit Hydrologic Unit Code (HUC) landscape parcels (known as subwatersheds) (HUC-12) that overlap the state of Utah. This is a modification from the 2015 UWAP, which summarized metrics at the 8-digit HUC scale. In all, 2,558 of the HUC-12 subwatersheds were used for UWAP analyses.To maintain consistency with the 2015 UWAP, the condition assessment began by calculating ecological departure values for every BpS within a HUC-12 subwatershed in the same way the 2015 UWAP did with HUC-8s. This step was calculated as the magnitude of differences in the BpS reference proportions of each successional stage (A-E; see EVT discussion in the Revising Terrestrial Key Habitats for the 2025 Utah Wildlife Action Plan section above) and LANDFIREmodeled proportions of each existing successional stage. The equation for this calculation is provided below and described in further detail in Figure D-1. 𝐸𝑐𝑜𝑙𝑜𝑔𝑖𝑐𝑎𝑙 𝐷𝑒𝑝𝑎𝑟𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 = 100% -∑ min{𝐶𝑢𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑛𝑡, 𝑅𝑒𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑒𝑛𝑐𝑒} 𝑛 𝑖 =1 Current = Existing proportions of each succession class within a HUC-12 subwatershed and BpS Reference = Reference proportions of each succession class within a HUC-12 subwatershed and BpS]

[FIGURE: Total key habitat area (could be either statewide or per KH A2 = Total key habitat area within each The result of these calculations was a large body of tabular data. A small fragment of the Lowland Sagebrush Key Habitat is illustrated in Table D-4. All necessary variables for calculating a 2025 UWAP weighted ecological departure are present in Table D-4; however, the data have been trimmed to save space. Although the example in Table D-4 informs the way that habitat condition can be assessed, it does not represent either the entirety of this HUC-12 or the majority of the Lowland Sagebrush Key Habitat overall. Nonetheless, in this example, there are multiple ecological departure scores that combine to an average of 71.67 for the HUC-12, which vastly overestimates the amount of departure by not accounting for the area of land that receives those scores. When area-based weights are considered, the overall ecological departure for that key habitat within that HUC-12 drops to only 19.34%, which would be considered a low amount of departure. This behavior is common throughout the complete UWAP data set and highlights the importance of factoring habitat area into the ecological departure value for a more accurate depiction of departure for EVT-based key habitats. 2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • A P P E N D I X D | D -18]

[FIGURE: change impacts and expert input to enhance model accuracy and interpretive value. The current assessment does not sufficiently account for how shifting climate conditions may render some BpS unsuitable or obsolete over time, nor does it evaluate whether LANDFIRE's modeled successional proportions and BpS classifications continue to represent natural or desirable ecological states. As a result, high ecological departure scores may reflect either genuine habitat degradation or simply a transformation into a different, potentially more appropriate habitat under changing conditions. In such cases, reliance on legacy standards may lead to mischaracterization of habitat quality, portraying adaptive or transitional ecosystems as ecologically deficient. 2025-2 0 3 5 U T A H W I L D L I F E A C T I O N P L A N • A P P E N D I X D | D -21]

| Beyond pollination, insect populations form the foundation of many food webs in mountain |
| sagebrush habitats. Insects serve as a vital food source for the majority of bird species- |
| particularly during the nesting season when protein demands are high (BLM 2004)-and many |
| other insectivorous wildlife species. Herbivorous insects feed on a wide variety of native forbs, |
| grasses, and shrubs, while predatory and parasitoid insects help regulate herbivore populations |
| and maintain ecological balance. Insect and invertebrate diversity is further influenced by |
| grazing disturbance by native ungulates-such as mule deer, elk, antelope, and bighorn sheep- |

* Herbaceous vegetation can include several agricultural cultivars that have become established in riparian areas, including smooth brome, orchard grass, yellow sweet clover, and alfalfa.Natives are dominated by arctic rush and a number of sedges. The noxious weed phragmites and the invasive reed canarygrass can be common in many areas. Wetlands in this ecoregion contain rushes, reedgrasses, and areas of open water, providing important habitat for migratory birds and wildlife refuges. Overall, lands within this ecoregion are heavily used for cropland and farming, which has affected stream quality and flow. Herbaceous vegetation can be diverse but is generally dominated by arctic rush, water sedge, Nebraska sedge, beaked sedge, black sedge, and other sedge species. In montane areas, quaking aspen and river hawthorn are common overstory species along riparian areas. In lower-elevation sites, vegetation is similar to Central Basin and Range They are characterized by deep canyons, reservoirs, and some natural lakes. Like the Alpine Key Habitat, snowmelt in these subregions provides water to the lower and more arid ecoregions via perennial streams. Much of the land use in this ecoregion is administered by federal land management agencies. Uses include seasonal livestock grazing,*

[TABLE:  Streamflow conditions can be broadly categorized based on flow duration and groundwater exchange processes. Perennial streams flow year-round, mainly sustained by base flow during low runoff periods. Intermittent streams flow when groundwater discharge or precipitation is sufficient, with occasional dry periods when the water table drops. Ephemeral streams only flow during runoff events, because the water table typically remains below the channel bottom. Rivers and streams transport water, sediment, and nutrients from springs and snowmelt at higher elevations or elevated areas. Geologically speaking, sediment transport has shaped river channels, altered landscapes, and influenced aquatic habitat(Wampler 2012).Rivers and streams have carved through rock layers, creating canyon reaches (Utah State University Extension 2020), especially in the Colorado River system. Nutrient transport is essential for forming the base of the food web (e.g., aquatic plants, algae), which provides important habitats for many different life stages of aquatic and terrestrial SGCNs.]

[TABLE:  Currently, GSL is approximately 121 kilometers (km) (75 miles) long and 56 km (35 miles) wide.It is a shallow terminal lake, with an average depth of approximately 4 m (14 feet) and a maximum depth near 11 m (35 feet). The lake is a remnant of ancient Lake Bonneville and once covered approximately 4,403 km 2 (1,700 square miles) (DWR 2023). Since 1875, the lake's elevation has declined by approximately 2.6 m (8.5 feet), resulting in a 44% reduction in surface area-approximately 2,460 km 2 (950 square miles) (DWR 2023). The tectonic activity that characterizes Utah's Basin and Range Province-specifically, faulting and crustal extensionhas shaped the topography and structural basin that holds the lake.A notable anthropogenic feature of the lake is the Lucin Cutoff, a railroad line crossing GSL from east to west near the south end of Promontory Point. Constructed on a mostly solid causeway, the Lucin Cutoff divides the lake into the North Arm and South Arm, allowing only limited water flow between the two. With no major freshwater inflows, the North Arm has become a hypersaline environment, reaching salinities up to 28% (330 grams per liter). In contrast, the South Arm receives riverine inputs and exhibits lower salinity levels, which are actively managed by the FFSL. Target salinity levels for the South Arm range from 120 to 160 grams per liter]

* Utah is one of the fastest-growing states in the nation.Utah's population reached 3 million in 2015 and is expected to double by 2065(Kem C. Gardner   Policy Institute 2017). That growth will necessitate more places for people to live, work, shop, and recreate (often at the expense of SGCNs and their habitats). For every new resident, open space is lost due to the construction of housing, roads, schools, commercial buildings, and other infrastructure. In 2020, Utah's 25 urban areas were home to 2.94 million people,*

| Increased habitat shifting and extreme weather patterns associated with climate change |
| exacerbate existing threats that are already challenging to address under current conditions. |
| Coupled with projections of a doubling of the state population by 2065 (Kem C. Gardner Policy |
| Institute 2017), the prospects for SGCN conservation have never been more challenging. This |
| population increase will likely impose additional demand on Utah's already limited natural |
| resources, limitations that, under existing levels of use, have already impacted conservation |
| targets and resulted in their status as SGCNs. The list below contains some examples of |
| existing threats being exacerbated by the results of climate change: |
| 2.1.1 Support, implement, and promote vegetation mapping using the National Vegetation |

| for rare pollinator and wildlife habitat condition assessment and mapping. |

It is used extensively by state natural heritage programs, which collect and evaluate data for species and ecosystems of concern using a common methodology. The Conservation Rank Calculator tool facilitates the accurate application of this method and promotes greater accuracy and consistency of the assessments. Threat Impact Based on Threat Severity and Threat Scope Methods Scores for these three factor categories are integrated using the NatureServe Conservation Rank Calculator to derive each SGCN's S-ranks and N-ranks. S-ranks are subnational ranks assigned and maintained by state, territory, provincial, or tribal NatureServe network programs. N-ranks are national ranks assigned and maintained by NatureServe. Those ranks were a core component of the process for selecting SGCNs from the entire list of jurisdictional wildlife (see Chapter 1, Species of Greatest Conservation Need -Distribution and Abundance, for more details). Utah Division of Wildlife Resource's (DWR's) Natural Heritage Program operates the Conservation Rank Calculator to create and update state S-ranks. Most SGCNs will have their state Rarity and Threat status assessed every 10 years, but this could be more frequent if biologists believe there have been recent factors that are affecting the species. Data are managed with Biotics 5 (DWR et al. 2025) and are regularly shared with NatureServe.*

* 5 | 410observed distributional species information, and although it would not be appropriate to use SDHMs as species-occupied habitat in these rankings, these models do demonstrate where additional surveys could and should be conducted to improve the knowledge of SGCN distribution on the landscape. SDHMs are present in the DWR Wildlife Habitat Analysis Tool to help inform project managers (DWR 2024a). SDHMs assess habitats' spatial arrangement and connectivity, identify crucial habitats, or describe the environmental conditions a species selects. SDHMs provide an understanding of the impacts of invasive species spread and identify suitable areas for species translocations or reintroductions. SDHMs can inform wildlife management decisions such as habitat protection, enhancement, and restoration. Some of the indicators and measures selected to monitor terrestrial habitat condition were borrowed directly from the national LANDFIRE program. Other indicators relate more directly to threats than to condition. Some threats already have statewide spatial data sets, whereas others still need to have such data developed.*

[TABLE]
* and the UGS collect comprehensive data about Great Salt Lake's wetland condition, which accounts for the greatest density of wetland area in Utah. UGS continues work that was started byDownard (2021) to test and develop vegetation-based condition assessments of Great Salt Lake and Great Basin wetlands. After the Clean Water Act was enacted in 1972, the EPA, in partnership with states and tribal nations, began a nationwide water quality monitoring effort for lakes, rivers and streams, coastal condition, and wetland condition once every 5 years called the National Aquatic Resource Surveys (EPA 2024a). These surveys established a national water quality baseline and tracked changes over time. The data can be broken down into regions, such as the Western Mountains and Xeric region, which includes Utah (EPA 2024a).*

| The National Wetlands Inventory mapping provides baseline information about wetland |
| --- |
| distribution and extent by mapping individual wetland features visible in aerial imagery; |
| however, National Wetlands Inventory mapping accuracy varies greatly across Utah with much |
| of the state reflecting conditions present in the original mapping from the 1980s. Since the |
| publication of the 2015 UWAP, UGS, DWR, the Bureau of Land Management, and many other |
| mapping organizations have been working to update National Wetlands Inventory mapping |
| within Utah. As of November 2024, these organizations have succeeded in updating mapping |
| across 35% of Utah and expect to complete projects across 70% of Utah by the end of 2025. |

[/TABLE]

[TABLE:  Although Utah Division of Wildlife Resources (DWR) is the lead agency for the Utah Wildlife Action Plan (UWAP), the UWAP is not a singular plan for one agency or entity. DWR does not bear the sole responsibility or authority for its implementation; in fact, UWAP partners have authority over most of the pervasive threats to Utah species and habitats (see Chapter 4, Threats, Data Gaps, and Conservation Actions) and have their own sensitive species lists and mandates for species conservation. To address this challenge, DWR has actively pursued new partners (e.g., other Utah Department of Natural Resources [DNR] divisions, water managers, industry, and transportation) since the development of the 2015 UWAP. Ultimately, success in preserving and managing Utah's fish, wildlife, plants, and]

## CONSERVATION ACTION DESCRIPTION

* CMP = Conservation Measures Partnership; 
IUCN = International Union for Conservation of Nature
. † Level-1 actions: Broad, landscape-scale strategies that address multiple SGCN or key habitats. These actions focus on general intervention types, such as water protection, land conservation, or policy changes. Level-2 actions: More specific measures that fall within the most relevant Level-1 category. Level-3 actions: Highly targeted efforts nested within the appropriate Level-2 category. The coding format is #.#.#, where the first digit represents the Level-1 category, the second corresponds to Level-2, and the third indicates Level-3.

Last Chance townsendia 1 -- Threat: Mining and Quarrying. There are four Level-3 threats identified under mining and quarrying: hardrock minerals, sand and gravel, oil shale, and tar sands. In general, the scope of these Level-3 impacts to individual SGCNs and specific key habitats was small, but collectively they elevated this Level-2 threat to a priority. Mining operations introduce bright artificial light into environments adapted to dark nighttime conditions, altering the sky brightness and, consequently, the physiological and behavioral responses of many wildlife species.
Furthermore, effluents from mineral extraction can contain high concentrations of heavy metals that are toxic to aquatic communities-particularly to the macroinvertebrates that comprise a significant prey base for other wildlife 
(Frakes et al. 2021)
 Threat: Renewable Energy. There are five Level-3 threats identified under renewable energy:
geothermal power facilities, solar power facilities, wind power facilities, hydropower facilities, and nuclear power facilities. In general, the scope of these Level-3 impacts to individual SGCNs and specific key habitats was small, but collectively they elevated this Level-2 threat to a priority. In addition, UWAP partners believe that these impacts will increase over the next 10 years as Utah continues to expand its energy portfolio.

## Case Study: The Wildlife Habitat Analysis Tool

Renewable energy is growing at an accelerated pace 
(Olano 2024)
. As with all industry, renewable energy can impact SGCNs and their habitats, especially if they are not considered in the project scope and design. Many of these conflicts can be avoided if project managers have information indicating where species occur and have resources on how to minimize the Threat: Roads -Transportation Network. Terrestrial Key Habitats 9
Historically, fish and wildlife management has been based on stable, long-term climate conditions within a predictable range of variation; however, credible climate projections for Utah indicate a departure from historical norms, with increasing climatic extremes and variability.
Ensuring the long-term preservation of fish, wildlife, plants, and invertebrates will require a fundamental shift in management strategies-one that prioritizes population and habitat resilience in the face of future uncertainties. This shift must be realized through targeted conservation actions aimed at maintaining, conserving, connecting, restoring, and enhancing species' habitats, thereby enabling them to persist in or adapt to rapidly changing environmental conditions.
Threat: Changes in Precipitation and Hydrological Regimes. Utah, one of the most arid states in the nation 
(Sowby 2024)
, faces significant challenges in addressing the increasing frequency

## Location and Condition of Aquatic Habitats

In common with terrestrial habitats, the condition of aquatic habitats can be assessed and monitored at varying scales depending on the management goals, the variables of interest, and the resources available to perform the assessments. Aquatic habitats are dynamic, however, and comprehensive monitoring to obtain meaningful condition metrics is time intensive.
Recognizing the difficulties of developing a comprehensive statewide approach, aquatic habitat monitoring and condition assessments will be conducted on a case-by-case basis. This marks a departure from the 2015 UWAP, which assessed the total extent of aquatic key habitats (in acres) and an aquatic intactness metric developed from Utah Geological Survey (UGS) models 
(Menuz 2015)
 as a high-level approach to monitoring aquatic habitat condition across Utah.
Shifting to a case-by-case monitoring approach provides several benefits to biologists and resource managers: 1) flexibility to develop monitoring programs specific to SGCN species, region, or availability; 2) adaptability to changing priorities or management concerns; and 3) easy adoption of existing condition data for monitoring.
Five main types of condition data are available for aquatic habitats in Utah:
1. Comprehensive measures that take into account multiple factors to assign condition ratings (applicable to all key habitats). 3. Bioassessment data for aquatic ecosystems that estimate ecological integrity, collected by the National Aquatic Monitoring Center (NAMC) (applicable to all key habitats). There was an open door to participation, and additional partners were continually added to the subcommittees during the revision process. To keep all UWAP partners appraised of the revision progress, solicit input, and provide opportunity for review, a UWAP Core Team with representation from all partners began meeting quarterly in January 2023 and continued meetings through the fall of 2025.

## Intermountain West Joint Venture

Joint ventures are collaborative, regional partnerships that bring together government agencies, nonprofit organizations, corporations, tribal nations, and individuals under the guidance of national and international bird conservation plans to design and implement landscape-scale conservation efforts in support of bird management plans. Utah is located within the Intermountain West Joint Venture and is a partner in regional planning and management initiatives for this group (Intermountain West Joint Venture 2025).

## U.S. Geological Survey National Species of Greatest Conservation Need List

Utah has participated in an AFWA effort with the U.S. Geological Survey to update the national SGCN list (U.S. Geological 
Survey 2015)
. Pulling SWAP information together into a national list provides a better picture of SGCN across the country. This database is a useful tool for states, territories, and partners to inform management actions for species most in need. The national SGCN list is currently updated with SWAP information through 2015 and updated state SGCN lists to seamlessly incorporate those lists as more current information becomes available.

## Regional Distribution Modeling

Regional species distribution habitat models are being developed for an increasing number of SGCN species. Utah is committed to working with partners to provide data for these modeling efforts, which are an iterative process of creation, use, and refinement. Two recent efforts were the development of distribution models produced from the USFWS Science Applications program in Legacy Region 2 (USFWS 2025b) and a range-wide model for the western yellowbilled cuckoo that was a result from a competitive STWG Program project (USFWS 2024c).

## Local and Statewide Initiatives

## Landscape Conservation Forecasting

Landscape Conservation Forecasting (LCF) is a land mapping project effort led by The Nature Conservancy. After LCF was initiated by The Nature Conservancy, Utah partners, including DWR,

## Events

DWR promotes native wildlife and educates the public about the unique species found in Utah through a variety of wildlife viewing events hosted around the state each year. These events, a few of which appear below, build public appreciation and support for Utah wildlife and conservation efforts (DWR 2025e):
• Hummingbird banding and viewing near Vernal
• Desert tortoise viewing in southern Utah
• Bat survey and viewing in central Utah
• Osprey viewing at Flaming Gorge Reservoir

## Social Media

As well as sharing the aforementioned examples on its social media platforms (Facebook, Instagram, Threads, X [formally Twitter], YouTube, Pinterest, and LinkedIn), DWR also creates original content for social media, much of which highlights the native species mentioned in the UWAP:
• Bat surveys and ways to help protect bats.
• Celebrating World Snake Day with fun facts about snakes.
• Impacts to woundfin habitat and programs that are addressing the issue.
• The ongoing successful recovery of the Virgin spinedace: this species was once reduced to 60% of its historical range, but due to conservation actions, this species has recovered to occupy 90% of its historical range.

## Community Science Projects

Western Toad Project Utah's Hogle Zoo and Sageland Collaborative's Western Toad Project have assisted with surveys and monitoring of western toad in Utah for over 10 years. These efforts provide valuable information to help manage this species and allow DWR biologists to focus limited time and resources on other species and native conservation projects. In addition, Utah's Hogle
Zoo tells the conservation story of the western toad in its Wild Utah exhibit to engage the public and raise awareness of the species at the zoo, where they are repatriated to new and existing western toad habitats in Utah (Sageland Collaborative 2022a).
Utah Herp Search Sageland Collaborative's Utah Herp Search uses community science to highlight unique native amphibians and reptiles in Utah where limited locality information is available. This kind of reporting has increased the number of midget faded rattlesnake observations beyond the limited survey work completed by DWR, leading to the realization that the species' distribution is larger and likely more abundant than originally believed (Sageland Collaborative 2022b). If sightings continue to be reported for this species, it likely will be removed from the SGCN list when its S-rank is next updated.

## Utah Pollinator Pursuit

Utah Pollinator Pursuit encourages the public to become involved in species conservation in two key ways: 1) join a program to visit and monitor specific pollinator habitats across Utah, and 2) record pollinator sightings through a phone application to help better understand the distribution of pollinators across Utah (Utah Pollinator Pursuit 2025).

## Rosy-Finch Project

The 3-year Rosy-Finch Project brought Utah community scientists together to conduct graycrowned rosy-finch feeder counts throughout Utah (Sageland Collaborative 2022c).

## Riverscape Restoration Project

## Partnerships with Agricultural Producers

In Utah, farmers, ranchers, and private landowners play a large role in the implementation of the 

## Heart of the Rockies

The Heart of the Rockies is a regional partnership of 30 land trusts across the Rocky Mountain
West that keeps family farms, ranches, and timberlands in family hands through capacity assistance, collaborative fundraising, data sharing, and networking.

## Partners for Conservation

Partners for Conservation embodies a grassroots movement of private landowners working with agencies, nonprofit organizations, and policymakers to collaborate on conservation projects to sustain working landscapes for present and future generations. Partners for Conservation supports working landscapes through voluntary, incentive-based public and private programs.

## Utah Grazing Improvement Program

A standout example of successful public-private partnership in Utah is the Utah Grazing Improvement Program (GIP). Established by the 2006 Rangeland Improvement Act (Utah House Bill 145), GIP provides cost-share grants and technical assistance to livestock producers for

## Species Protection Account

The Species Protection Account is a native species conservation fund established during the 1997 Utah general legislative session; the account is administered by DWR. The purpose of the Species Protection Account is to facilitate the conservation of fish, wildlife, plants, and invertebrate species and their habitats in greatest need of conservation attention. An underlying concern is to avoid, minimize, or mitigate the impacts of ESA listings to the people of Utah. The primary Species Protection Account objective is to fund projects that
• protect, conserve, and recover ESA-listed species; and
• help preclude the need for listing additional species under the ESA by implementing the UWAP.
From 1997 to 2025, the Species Protection Account was funded through a portion of a 1/16thpercent sales tax on water and by a tax provided for in the Brine Shrimp Royalty Act (Utah Code 

## State and Tribal Wildlife Grants

The STWG Program provides federal grant funds for developing and implementing SWAPs.
Grant funds must be used to address conservation needs identified in a state's SWAP, such as research, surveys, species and habitat management, and monitoring. These funds may also be used to update, revise, or modify a SWAP. 

## Utah's Watershed Restoration Initiative

Utah's WRI is the primary tool for implementing the UWAP and for tracking conservation actions.
The WRI is a partnership-driven effort administered by DNR to conserve, restore, and manage ecosystems in priority areas across the state to enhance Utah's
• wildlife and biological diversity,
• water quality and yield for all uses, and
• opportunities for sustainable uses.
The WRI is an initiative sponsored by Utah Partners for Conservation and Development that serves as a clearinghouse to coordinate and share participants' conservation concerns and priorities; to discuss and implement solutions; and to promote an atmosphere of collaboration among landowners, private organizations, and state and federal agencies.
A unifying concern and priority for all Utah Partners for Conservation and Development and WRI participants is to preclude the need for ESA listings.
The WRI is committed to providing novel and dynamic solutions for statewide issues of soil, water, and species health. In an unprecedented collaborative effort, the WRI combines the Each year, resource managers within the partnership propose projects to the regional teams.
Proposals are entered into a statewide projects database and reviewed at regional team meetings. This approach helps the partners share information and resources, and many of the projects receive support from several partners. The following three criteria are used to evaluate the merits of project proposals:
• How well the project proposal is tied to meeting goals and objectives in plans such as the UWAP, which have been approved through a public process
• How well the project mitigates threats to land, water, and species health
• Whether the project maximizes the return on an investment made to implement the project Part of the implementation vision for the 2025 UWAP is that most on-the-ground habitat work will be accomplished via projects initiated within or routed through the WRI. This will strengthen and formalize the consensus that has developed since approval and adoption of the 2005 UWAP, which coincided with the formation of the WRI (DNR 2024a). WRI funding is competitive, so all project managers are striving to submit the best projects that will receive the most points during the ranking process. WRI projects receive points for implementing the UWAP, specifically if the project benefits SGCNs, improves UWAP key habitats, and alleviates priority threats.
SGCNs, key habitats, and priority threats are updated in the WRI each year (if needed) to ensure that UWAP projects/conservation actions are responding to new information or changing conditions.
Appendix A Aquatic Key Habitats Subcommittee Alkali flats (alkali soils): Alkali land, also known as alkaline land, refers to soils with a high pH level (above 7.0) and often high levels of soluble salts. These soils are typically found in arid and semiarid regions where evaporation exceeds precipitation, leading to salt accumulation. Alkali soils can be saline-alkali, meaning they have both high salt and exchangeable sodium content.
Alpine: Refers to a mountainous region with high elevations. Alpine climate is typically cold, snowy, and windy.
Appropriation: Money or assets devoted to a special purpose, such as wildlife protections, in regard to the Utah Wildlife Action Plan (UWAP).
Aquaculture: Breeding, raising, and harvesting fish, shellfish, and aquatic plants.

## Archetypal:

The historic or reference habitat condition.

## Atmospheric deposition:

A process in which particles and/or substances from the atmosphere are deposited onto the Earth's surface. In UWAP, this term describes the threat of air pollutants being returned to the Earth that can negatively affect land and water resources.
Badlands: Colloquial term for dry terrain that is typically characterized by steep slopes, minimal vegetation, and extensively eroded soft sedimentary rocks (e.g., mudrock and sandstone) and clay-rich soils. Badlands often encompass different-shaped landforms such as buttes, which are small hills with steep sides and a flat top. Typically unsuitable for agriculture.

## Biological community (ecological community):

Encompasses a group of interacting species (populations) living in a prescribed area (delineated habitat) at the same time that form a network of relationships within a particular environment, interact with each other and with the available resources, and influence subsequent biological interactions and population dynamics.
Biological communities are essentially the living constituents of a given ecosystem 
(Stroud et al. 2015)
.

## Biophysical gradient:

A characteristic or property that varies from one point to another, physically or chemically (e.g., temperature, pH, concentration, or pressure).
Edge effect: Edge refers to the boundary or transition zone between two adjacent land cover types 
(Cadanasso et al. 2003)
, such as the boundary between riparian habitat and forest or agriculture and shrub-steppe. Edges can affect several ecological parameters, including species distributions and interactions 
(Fagan et al. 1999;
Ries et al. 2004
), which have implications for the structure of habitat and the support habitat provides for species (e.g., providing appropriate temperature and cover) as well as implications for the function and management of ecosystems 
(Porensky and Young 2013)
. Edge effects can be affected by and associated with habitat fragmentation and habitat connectivity (see the definitions for these terms in this glossary), which can alter the impacts of edge effects 
(Porensky and Young 2013)
.
Effluent: Liquid waste that is discharged usually from sewers into a waterbody.
Endangered species; Endangered Species Act-listed species: Species of plants or animals that are listed or proposed for listing as endangered or threatened and are protected under the Endangered Species Act of 1973.
Endemic: A plant or animal that is native to a certain ecosystem.
Environmental gradients: A change in abiotic factors over time such as altitude, depth, temperature, and soil humidity.
Evapotranspiration: Loss of water from soil by evaporation from the soil surface and by transpiration from the leaves of plants.
Exceptional Drought: According to the U.S. Drought Monitor, Exceptional Drought is the most intense or severe drought; it generally occurs once or twice in a 100-year period. During this type of drought, losses of crops and pastures as well as widespread water shortages occur. This type of drought is also known as D4 within the categories of drought. These range from D1 (the least intense) to D4 (the most intense) (U.S. Drought Monitor 2025).

## Existing Vegetation Type (EVT):

Modeled representations of the current distribution of habitat types across the landscape defined as plant community types that tend to co-occur in similar landscapes with similar ecological processes, substrates, or environmental gradients (LANDFIRE 2024b).
Extent: Location or geographic distribution within the state along with the amount of coverage, both of which can be represented via maps or associated tables.
Fluvial: Found in or related to a river or flowing water.

## Habitat connectivity (landscape connectivity):

The degree to which the landscape facilitates or impedes species dispersal movement among habitat patches, which may allow movement of genes, individuals, populations, and species and mitigate population isolation in fragmented habitat. Connectivity focuses on structural or functional corridors that increase dispersal rates between habitat patches across unsuitable, poor quality, or matrix (nonbreeding) habitat and depends on species' movement abilities in crossing landscape mosaics. Habitat connectivity can be associated with and impacted by edge effects and habitat fragmentation (see these terms in this glossary).

## Habitat fragmentation:

The breaking up of a large intact area of a single land cover type into smaller patches 
(Lord and Norton 1990
). In the case of UWAP, this term refers to breaking up once large and contiguous areas of a land cover type into less contiguous areas as a result of anthropogenic disturbances 
(Franklin et al. 2002)
 such as conversion of a native land cover to agriculture. Habitat fragmentation can be associated with and alter the impacts of edge effects (see this term in this glossary) and can impact habitat connectivity (see this term in this glossary).

## Hydrologic Unit Codes (HUCs):

A hierarchical numbering system developed by the U.S.
Geological Survey specifically for watersheds. Each HUC number references a specific watershed. This numbering system enables agencies to agree on watershed boundaries and pinpoint issues such as water quality problems. Invertebrates: Animals that do not possess or develop a spinal column. Includes insects and crustaceans.

## Imperiled

## Key habitat:

In this case, key habitat refers to habitats that are prioritized within Utah. These habitats are based on EVT categories in LANDFIRE (2020a).
LANDFIRE: Also known as Landscape Fire and Resource Management Planning Tools 
(LANDFIRE 2025)
. This regularly updated geospatial data source aids in strategic fire and resource management planning and analysis and provides landcover and associated condition
• Geochemical regime: The relative abundance, distribution, and movement of the Earth's chemical elements.
• Hydrologic or flooding regime: The timing, duration, volume, and pattern of flow or flood events during a defined period of time.
• Precipitation regime: The characteristics of the seasonal distribution of precipitation at a location.
• Sediment regime: Refers to the size, quantity, and distribution of sediments.

## Restoration:

The action of returning something to a former owner, place, or condition.
Riparian: Relating to, on, or along the banks of a river.
Ruderal: Plants that grow on disturbed areas where vegetation cover has been interrupted.

## Seral:

A specific vegetation community occurring on a site at some point in time. For example, a vegetation community that grows after a disturbance event (Old Growth Forest Ecology 2025).

## N-rank and S-rank:

NatureServe's national (N-rank) and subnational (S-rank) levels are part of NatureServe's larger conservation status ranking system to determine level of vulnerability; they are used to help determine which species should be considered a species of greatest conservation need for the UWAP. N-ranks for species of greatest conservation need were determined by NatureServe and partners; S-ranks were determined by the Utah Division of Wildlife Resources.
Stakeholders: Individuals, groups, organizations, or their representatives (interested parties) that work closely with Utah Division of Wildlife Resources or that may have an interest in or be affected by the activities and decisions included within the UWAP. Interested parties may also seek to influence Utah Division of Wildlife Resources' future direction.

## State and Tribal Wildlife Grants Program: "Funds states and territories [and tribal nations] to

help develop and implement programs that benefit wildlife and wildlife habitat through two programs: 1) a competitive national grants program and 2) a formula, or mandatory, grant
program" (National Wildlife Federation 2025). This program funds more than half of all nongame work in Utah when matched with state funds.
Steppe: A semiarid grassland that occurs in temperate climates.
• Sagebrush steppe: A vegetation community in the Intermountain West dominated by sagebrush that includes other native shrubs, grasses, and flowering plants.
Subspecies: A taxonomic category that ranks below species.
Substrates: Of or relating to sediment, soil, bedrock, or other materials that make up the floor or bed or a waterbody; an underlying layer of rock or other materials beneath the soil.

## Successional (ecological succession):

The gradual change in species composition causing the structure of a biological community to change over time.

## Suppression:

In this case, the act of reducing, controlling, or preventing wildfire events.
Taxonomic: Scientific classification of organisms.
Terrestrial: In this case, species or habitats found on land, versus those found in water (aquatic).
Threatened species: Species of plants or animals of concern that have the potential to become endangered and are protected under the Endangered Species Act of 1973.
Translocation: Capturing and moving a free-ranging animal from one location to another.

## Urbanization:

The process of transforming rural areas or areas with no human settlement to cities with a higher density of infrastructure and human populations.

## Vegetation type:

The characteristic dominant plant species in a community (see this term in this glossary) 
(Ornduff et al. 2003:112)
.
Vertebrates: Animals that have a spinal column.
