## Revising Terrestrial Key Habitats for the 2025 Utah Wildlife Action Plan

In 2015, the Joint Team evaluated several existing habitat data sources and selected the source best suited to describe the habitat's extent and condition. Four existing data sources were evaluated for terrestrial habitats in the 2015 UWAP according to eight selection factors. Table 
D
-1 shows these data sets and selection factors along with the evaluation results. Included in • The data source used for the extent of habitats must be in spatial format and cover Utah consistently from border to border, statewide.
• The data source must include both the current and future condition of habitats to detect change over time.
The arrows in Table 
D
-1 show the results of the Joint Team's evaluation: up arrows indicate that existing data meet the criteria well; down arrows indicate that data meet the factor poorly or not at all; and sideways arrows indicate that data meet the factor neither well nor poorly and are somewhere in the middle. When the Joint Team worked on the 2015 UWAP revision, LANDFIRE (La Puma 2023) was the only candidate data set with all the selection factor arrows either up or sideways (i.e., it had no serious flaws from the perspective of the Joint Team's selection criteria). All other candidate data sets contained at least one serious flaw (down arrow). For the selection criteria that assess condition of habitats (bottom row in Table 
D
-1), LANDFIRE had the only adequate data set-in the Joint Team's judgment-for the entire state and could be used again in the 2025 revision. In 2025, the Landscape Conservation Forecasting data set was added for consideration, and the data sources were reassessed (see Table 
D
-1), but LANDFIRE was again determined to be the best available data source by the UWAP Core Team (Core Team) and the Utah Division of Wildlife Resources (DWR) Wildlife Action Plan Implementation Team.
habitats were made to identify broadly defined, user-friendly names that made sense for how DWR biologists manage habitat and to ensure these habitats represented plant and insect SGCN that are new in the 2025 UWAP. The candidate habitats were names only, without usable substance or a way to determine their extent and condition, until the EVT categories that make up each were identified using LANDFIRE. Following discussion with the Utah DWR Wildlife Action Plan Implementation Team and the Core Team, the Terrestrial Key Habitat Subcommittee decided to exclude disturbed, developed, ruderal, and agricultural habitats from consideration for key habitats. Although some SGCNs use these habitats, key habitats are more natural and have larger numbers of SGCN that depend on them. The Sparse Vegetation Key Habitat ultimately encompassed multiple habitats that were included in the Other Habitats category; consequently, sparse vegetation was not included as a terrestrial key habitat.
The third and final step was to determine the current condition of the terrestrial key habitats. In 2015, the Joint Team used the LANDFIRE data to calculate a measure of condition known as ecological departure. The calculation of ecological departure uses both the BpS and the associated vegetation successional class assigned to pixels in the LANDFIRE data set.
Terrestrial key habitats for the 2025 UWAP were developed using EVT categories rather than BpS, however, and there is no condition metric directly associated with EVTs. BpS and EVT

## Appendix E Threat Methods

Creating the 2015 Utah Wildlife Action Plan Threat Assessment
Identifying threats and stressors is one of the eight required elements of a state wildlife action plan because they are the problems that adversely affect species of greatest conservation need (SGCNs) and/or their habitats (Association of Fish and Wildlife Agencies and the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service 2017). In Utah, these threats are incorporated into the Utah Department of Natural Resources Watershed Restoration Initiative (WRI), which is the primary implementation tool of the Utah Wildlife Action Plan (UWAP). Therefore, successfully funded conservation projects must demonstrate how the projects will alleviate stressors to SGCNs and the UWAP key habitats (see Chapter 6, Partnerships, Implementation, and Regional Collaboration for more on WRI). In addition, threats to SGCNs are part of NatureServe's ranking methodology (Faber-Langendoen et al. 2012), which is the cornerstone of the rubric used to develop the UWAP SGCNs (see Chapter 1, Species of Greatest Conservation Need -Distribution and Abundance).
The UWAP Joint Team worked through a structured and thoughtful process of developing threats for the 2015 UWAP, including pros and cons of the process they adopted. Because the 2015 threats performed well for UWAP implementation in the WRI, the 2025 UWAP threats list was built from the existing 2015 list (Table 
E-1
). The following is a brief overview of the 2015 UWAP threats process rationale and rubric (UWAP Joint Team 2015):
• 
Salafsky et al. (2008)
 was used for Level-1 and Level-2 threats language (see Table 
E
-1).
A threats calculator spreadsheet was created to capture results of the species and habitat threats assessments.
o The standardized threats were updated slightly (Conservation Measures Partnership 2016), but the majority of the species and habitat threat assessments had been completed before this newer version was discovered. For continuity, the same Level-1 and Level-2 threats that were used for the 2015 UWAP were used for the 2025 UWAP 
(Salafsky et al. 2008)
. The newer standardized threats version will be used for future UWAP threat assessments.
• A twelfth Level-2 threat was created: Data Gaps (see Table 
E-1
). This threat acknowledges that the lack of actionable information can in itself be a roadblock to effective conservation.
• A third level of detail (Level-3 threats) was created (see Table 
E
-1) and built into the threats calculator to allow users to incorporate additional threat details and specificity to Utah. The number of Level-3 threats per Level-2 threat, including the scope and severity of those threats, were evaluated and aggregated to provide the Level-2 threat impacts to SGCNs or key habitats.
To update threats for the 2025 UWAP and use the updated threats list, the following was completed:
• The SGCN subcommittee reviewed all of the Level-3 threats, and the Climate Adaptation Subcommittee reviewed the Level-3 climate-related threats to ensure new and/or emerging threats were represented in the UWAP threats list and calculator. The updated threats list was presented, discussed, and adopted by the UWAP Core Team (Core Team).
• The updated threats list was used for any NatureServe S-ranks that were calculated to help establish the 2025 UWAP SGCN list. NatureServe methodology was used to aggregate Level-3 threats into Level-2 threats 
(Master et al. 2012
).
• The updated threats list (see Table 
E
-1) was used to complete a threat assessment for the 2025 UWAP key habitats. The Aquatic Key Habitat Subcommittee completed the threat assessments for the 2025 aquatic key habitats. A subset of the Utah Division of Wildlife Resources Wildlife Action Plan Implementation Team completed the threat assessment for the 2025 terrestrial key habitats. The key habitat threat assessments were presented, discussed, and adopted by the Core Team.
• The threat assessments were analyzed using the prioritization process outlined in the 2015 UWAP (UWAP Joint Team 2015). The results were reviewed to ensure they made sense as priority threats across the state. The priority threats (see Table 
E
-1) shifted with the addition of plant and insect SGCNs. Many water-related threats that were prioritized in 2015 were no longer prioritized because they were washed out with the addition of numerous plant SGCN. The Core Team decided to add these water-related threats back into the priority list given the importance of water in the state.
• The updated SGCN threats and key habitat threats will be integrated into the WRI upon approval of the 2025 UWAP. 

## 2.4

Marine and Freshwater Aquaculture: Aquatic animals raised in one location on farmed or nonlocal resources; also hatchery fish allowed to roam in the wild (e.g., shrimp or fin fish aquaculture, fish ponds on farms, hatchery salmon, seeded shellfish beds, artificial algal beds).

## 2.4.1

Unintentional Spread of Non-Native Species 3

## Energy Production and Mining

Threats from production of non-biological resources.

## 3.1

Oil and Gas Drilling: Exploring for, developing, and producing petroleum and other liquid hydrocarbons (e.g., oil wells, deep sea natural gas drilling). 

## 2025-

Natural System Modifications Threats from actions that convert or degrade habitat in service of "managing" natural or semi-natural systems, often to improve human welfare.

## 7.1

Fire and Fire Suppression: Suppression or increase in fire frequency and/or intensity outside of its natural range of variation (e.g., fire suppression to protect homes, inappropriate fire management, escaped agricultural fires, arson, campfires, fires for hunting). Agricultural and Forestry Effluents: Water-borne pollutants from agricultural, silvicultural, and aquaculture systems that include nutrients, toxic chemicals, and/or sediments, including the effects of these pollutants on the site where they are applied (e.g., nutrient loading from fertilizer runoff, herbicide runoff, manure from feedlots, nutrients from aquaculture, soil erosion). 

## Geological Events

Threats from catastrophic geological events.

## 10.1

Volcanoes: Volcanic events (e.g., eruptions, emissions of volcanic gases).

## 10.2

Earthquakes/Tsunamis: Earthquakes and associated events (e.g., earthquakes, tsunamis). 

## Climate Change

Change in climate patterns (e.g., those resulting from increased atmospheric greenhouse gases like carbon dioxide) and/or events outside the natural range of variation that could wipe out a vulnerable species or ecosystem.

## Conservation Actions

The structure of the standardized conservation actions used for UWAP is detailed in The Standardized Conservation Actions System section in Chapter 4, Threats, Data Gaps, and Conservation Actions. The Core Team and UWAP partners reviewed and updated the conservation actions recommended in this UWAP revision after the priority threats for the state were updated. The UWAP conservation actions then were assigned a three-digit numeric code to best match the closest nationally standardized actions. The entire list of nationally standardized actions are presented in Table 
E-

## CONSERVATION ACTION DESCRIPTION 1

Land/Water Management Actions to directly restore a target or mitigate a stress. Actions directly managing or restoring sites, ecosystems and the wider environment.
1.1 Site/Area Stewardship: Cutting invasive vines off trees, liming acid ponds, using microbes to clean up oil spills, use of noise makers to scare off birds, gating caves to protect hibernating bats, routine maintenance in a protected area, use of traditional fire management practices by Indigenous peoples, releasing daughterless male mosquitoes, managing park visitors to reduce disturbance.

## 1.1.1

Restoring targets or mitigating stresses via mechanical actions (e.g., removing invasives, mowing).

## 1.1.2

Restoring targets or mitigating stresses via chemical actions (e.g., herbicides, liming ponds).

## 1.1.3

Restoring targets or mitigating stresses via biological control (e.g., oil-eating microbes).

## 1.1.4

Restoring targets or mitigating stresses via sensory control (e.g., predator urine, noise deterrents).
1.1.5
Restoring targets or mitigating stresses via separation or border actions (e.g., fencing, gating caves).

## 1.1.6

Restoring targets or mitigating stresses via ecological management (e.g., prescribed fire).
1.1.7
Restoring targets or mitigating stresses via genetic manipulation (e.g., sterile males of an invasive species).

## 1.1.8

Restoring targets or mitigating stresses via visitor management (e.g., routing trails around wetlands).
* CMP = Conservation Measures Partnership; IUCN = International Union for Conservation of Nature. † Level-1 actions: Broad, landscape-scale strategies that address multiple SGCN or key habitats. These actions focus on general intervention types, such as water protection, land conservation, or policy changes. Level-2 actions: More specific measures that fall within the most relevant Level-1 category. Level-3 actions: Highly targeted efforts nested within the appropriate Level-2 category. The coding format is #.#.#, where the first digit represents the Level-1 category, the second corresponds to Level-2, and the third indicates Level-3. Ecosystem and Natural Process (Re)Creation: Creating forest corridors, grasslands, or mangroves on degraded lands, creating artificial oyster reefs, breaching levees to restore wetlands, managing dams to simulate natural hydrological regimes, using livestock to simulate herbivore grazing.

## 2025

## 1.2.1

Building or restoring key ecosystem structural components.

## 1.2.2

Building or restoring key ecosystem abiotic functions and processes. † Level-1 actions: Broad, landscape-scale strategies that address multiple SGCN or key habitats. These actions focus on general intervention types, such as water protection, land conservation, or policy changes. Level-2 actions: More specific measures that fall within the most relevant Level-1 category. Level-3 actions: Highly targeted efforts nested within the appropriate Level-2 category. The coding format is #.#.#, where the first digit represents the Level-1 category, the second corresponds to Level-2, and the third indicates Level-3. Establishing and supporting organizations through developing or providing organizational management tools.

## 10.3

Alliance and Partnership Development: Convening meetings of local stakeholders in a community reserve, an international forum to share information about wildlife crimes, a membership services association of land trusts, a regional learning network, an international academic society.

## 10.3.1

Creating or maintaining partnerships focused on coordinating conservation implementation.

## 10.3.2

Creating or maintaining partnerships focused on knowledge generation and sharing.

## 10.4

Financing Conservation: Door-to-door fundraising efforts, private foundation or government grants, foundation investments in green businesses, corporate philanthropy, national debt-for-nature swaps.

Regimes: Broad-scale changes in the geochemical conditions of ecosystems, including ocean acidification (e.g., ocean acidification, changes in atmospheric carbon dioxide affecting plant growth, loss of sediment leading to broad-scale subsidence)
Changes in Temperature: Broad-scale changes in temperature mean, variability, seasonality and extremes, including changes in temperature extremes, increased average summer temperature, and decreased minimum winter/spring temperature
heat waves, cold spells, oceanic temperature changes, melting of glaciers/sea ice
Changes in Precipitation and Hydrological Regimes: Broad-scale changes in precipitation mean, variability, seasonality, and extremes, including decreased or increased precipitation, changes in timing of precipitation, changes in form of precipitation (e.g., snow versus rain; snow cover and snowpack where applicable), changes in evapotranspiration rates and hydrological cycles, and droughts and floods
e.g., droughts, changes in timing of rains, loss of snow cover, increased severity of floods
Species Management Actions directly managing or restoring specific species or taxonomic groups
1 Species Stewardship: Culling ungulates, heating bat roosts to reduce white-nose syndrome effects, artificial bird nesting boxes or platforms, supplementary feeding, planting food trees, manual pollination of orchids, vaccinating wild dogs, guiding elephants on migrations, shooting or trapping invasive competitors
using false brood parasite eggs to reduce brood parasitism
Assisting specific taxa in-situ via population management
Assisting specific taxa in-situ via shelter management
Assisting specific taxa in-situ via nutrient or water management
Assisting specific taxa in-situ via reproduction management
Assisting specific taxa in-situ via disease or injury management
Assisting specific taxa in-situ via movement or migration management
Species Reintroduction and Translocation: Reintroduction of wolves, translocation of species imperiled by climate change to new suitable locations, benign introductions of top predator fish to an ecosystem, transferring monkeys to improve genetic diversity
Facilitation of reintroduction of species where they previously existed
Facilitation of translocation of species to new places
benign introduction
Facilitation of transfer of individuals or genes to increase genetic diversity
Ex-Situ Conservation: Off-site rehabilitation of injured raptors, captive rearing of mussels for reintroduction, captive breeding of an endangered frog to avoid disease, seed banking, genetic restoration of mammoths from DNA, restoration of tortoises from back-crosses, synthetic species creation
Providing ex-situ protection to species via support within the life of an individual
Providing ex-situ protection to species via support or captive breeding over generations
Providing ex-situ protection to species via gene banking
e.g., seeds, sperm, DNA
Providing ex-situ protection to species via genetic reconstruction
e.g., restoring extinct species
Providing ex-situ protection to species via synthetic species creation
Outreach and Communications: Briefing newspaper reporters on conservation issues, social media campaigns, public service radio ads, zoo exhibits about threats to animal habitat, recording conservation songs, producing puppet shows with conservation message, door-to-door engagement, taking children on nature walks
Raising awareness via reported media (e.g., newspapers, TV, radio
curated blogs
Raising awareness via social media
Facebook, personal blogs)
Raising awareness via ads and marketing (e.g., mail campaign, celebrity media ads)
Raising awareness via displays (e.g., museum or zoo exhibits, park signs)
Raising awareness via art (e.g., paintings, recorded music)
Raising awareness via performances
Raising awareness via person-to-person engagement
Raising awareness via experiential learning (e.g., nature walks
outdoor education
Protests and Civil Disobedience: Organizing protest marches against oil drilling, investigative journalism naming and shaming polluting companies, impeding whaling vessels, sitting in trees to prevent logging
sabotaging wildlife traps
Organizing or engaging in protests (confrontations or refusing to engage)
Organizing or engaging in public identification of wrong-doers
Organizing or engaging in impeding activities (legal or illegal passive civil disobedience)
Organizing or engaging in sabotage (illegal active monkeywrenching)
Law Enforcement and Prosecution Actions monitoring and enforcing compliance with existing laws and policies at all levels to deter threats or compel conservation action
1 Detection and Arrest: Monitoring wildlife trafficking across borders, investigating reports of illegal grazing, setting up informer network against tiger poaching
interdicting an illegal fishing vessel
Reducing or deterring illegal behaviors through arrest and interdiction
Criminal Prosecution and Conviction: Collecting evidence to prosecute a wildlife trader, holding trials for alleged law breakers, monitoring to ensure criminals serve time and pay fines
Deterring threat behaviors through prosecuting alleged crimes
Deterring threat behaviors through trying alleged crimes
Deterring threat behaviors through punishing proven crimes (prisons, fine collection, rehabilitation)
Non-Criminal Legal Action: Suing an agency to take action to protect an endangered species, suing a company to stop illegal logging, citing homeowners for sewage violations, agency review of policies or projects
Changing behavior through civil lawsuits
Changing behavior through agency enforcement
Changing behavior through agency or judicial review
Economic, and Moral Incentives Actions using livelihood or other economic and moral incentives to directly influence attitudes and behaviors
Livelihood
Linked Enterprises and Alternative Livelihoods: Non-timber forest product harvesting business, wild salmon fishery, subsistence hunting and gathering, training loggers to be ecotourism guides, training loggers for factory jobs
Creating incentives to change behaviors through linked ecological service-using enterprises (nonconsumptive)
Better Products and Management: Practice competition to develop sea turtle-friendly fishhooks, research to develop low water use crops, providing access to weed-free hay, setting up recycling services, training in chicken production as a substitute protein source for bushmeat, setting up grass and forest banking systems to mitigate farmer risk
engagement with corporations to "green" their supply chains
Changing behaviors by developing better products and practices
2 Changing behaviors by promoting better products and practices
Market-Based Incentives: Promoting responsible palm oil production, educating consumers about seafood sustainability, boycotts of non-dolphin safe tuna, creating carbon market for forest conservation, providing loan guarantees to green businesses, campaign to get universities to divest from fossil fuel companies
Direct Economic Incentives: Compensation payments for elephant crop-damage, giving or raising money for "green" political candidates, providing medical clinic in return for conservation, tax incentives to promote conservation, punitive taxes on high-fuel consuming cars, using economic valuation of wetland flood protection services to promote wetland conservation
Creating incentive to change behaviors through monetary awards and prizes
Non-Monetary Values: Linking conservation to human health, linking conservation to food security, developing religious or cultural arguments for conservation
moral, or cultural benefits
6 Conservation Designation and Planning Actions to create the conditions necessary for other conservation efforts to succeed; actions directly protecting sites and/or species
Protected Area Designation and/or Acquisition: Gazetting a national park, demarcating a town wildlife sanctuary, purchasing a land trust property, establishing tribally owned hunting ground
Easements and Resource Rights: Convincing landowners to establish easements restricting development rights, purchasing water or instream flow rights, securing resource tenure rights for local communities
Use Zoning and Designation: Zoning development versus conservation areas; designating a wild and scenic river or an important bat conservation area
without formal protection
Conducting or doing land use zoning
where to put plantations, housing
Conducting or doing conservation area designation
beyond protected areas
Conservation Planning: Developing a management plan for a national park, revision of an existing plan for a migratory bird species across its range, spatial planning and prioritization effort to identify important bat areas
Planning for managing sites and protected areas
Planning for managing species and taxonomic groups
Planning for managing thematic projects and programs
5 Site Infrastructure: Creating guard posts, border fences, roads, recreational areas
Building or maintaining infrastructure to support site direct management
e.g., greenhouses, supply sheds
Building or maintaining infrastructure to support site resource extraction
e.g., sawmill, fish processing building
Building or maintaining infrastructure to support site transport (e.g., roads, airstrips)
Building or maintaining infrastructure to support site tourism and recreation
boat ramps
Building or maintaining infrastructure to support site learning and research
e.g., student dorms, labs
Building or maintaining infrastructure to support site administration
e.g., headquarters, power plants
Legal and Policy Frameworks Actions developing and influencing legislation; policies and voluntary standards affecting conservation
Advocating for strengthening international wildlife trade laws, writing national legislation for endangered species conservation, proposing state or provincial ballot initiatives to fund conservation, making the case for town sewage regulations, enacting tribal laws governing hunting seasons, creating a roundtable of key soy producers to create standards for responsible soy production

providing support to tribal leaders on environmental matters

Providing conservation capacity development through hands-on coaching and technical assistance
Providing conservation capacity development through workshops and professional development training courses
